--- Page 4 ---
Godan  
A Priceless Treasure of Hindi Literature

Such was the magic of Munshi Premchand’s pen that the events unfolding in society seemed to be your own lived experiences. It was this deep identification with the common people that earned him a place in the world of novels unmatched by any other.

The Emperor of the Novel, Munshi Premchand, not only took a firm stand against the social evils prevalent in society and struck at them with unflinching force, but also sought out the very essence of Indian life.

Munshi ji’s works are a mirror to the life of the Indian people. These priceless treasures of Hindi literature speak of ideals rooted in the soil, grounded in reality.

While reading 'Godan', you will feel as if it is your own story, or the story of those around you. Perhaps this is why it has been translated into almost every language in the world.

We are confident that this most authentic edition of 'Godan' will surely be to your liking.

—The Publisher

--- Page 6 ---
: 1 :

After feeding and watering both his bullocks, Horiram said to his wife, Dhaniya, “Send Gobar to weed the sugarcane. I don’t know when I’ll be back. Hand me my staff.”

Dhaniya’s hands were full of cow dung. She had just come from making cow-dung cakes. She replied, “At least have something to eat and drink first. Why such a hurry?”

Hori wrinkled his furrowed brow and said, “You care about food and drink, but I’m worried that if I’m late, I won’t catch the malik. If he starts his bath and prayers, I’ll be waiting for hours.”

“That’s why I say, have a little breakfast. And what harm if you don’t go today? You just went the day before yesterday.”

“Why do you meddle in things you don’t understand, woman? Give me my staff and mind your own work. It’s only by keeping up these visits that we’ve managed to survive this long—otherwise, who would know where we’d have ended up? There are so many people in the village, but who hasn’t faced eviction, who hasn’t had their property seized? When your neck is under someone else’s foot, it’s best to massage those very feet.”

Dhaniya was not so practical. She thought, We till the zamindar’s fields, he’ll take his rent, that’s all. Why should we flatter him, why should we lick his boots? Though, in these twenty years of married life, she had learned well enough that no matter how much you scrimp and save, how much you tighten your belt, even if you hold on to every single coin with your teeth, it is nearly impossible to pay off the rent in full. Still, she would not give in, and over this matter, husband and wife quarreled day after day. Of her six children, only three were alive now: a son, Gobar, about sixteen years old, and two daughters, Sona and Rupa, twelve and eight. Three sons had died in childhood. Even now, her heart told her that if they’d had medicine, they might have survived, but she hadn’t been able to buy even a single dose. She herself was still young—just thirty-six—but all her hair had turned gray, wrinkles creased her face, her once beautiful wheat-colored skin had grown sallow, and her eyesight had begun to fail. All because of worry over how to fill their bellies. She had never known a moment’s happiness. This endless, grinding hardship had dulled her sense of self-respect into indifference. For someone who can barely get enough to eat, why so much flattery? Her heart rebelled against this situation, but after a few harsh words from Hori, reality would settle in.

Defeated, she threw down Hori’s staff, his jacket, shoes, turban, and waistband.

--- Page 7 ---
Hori glared at her and said, “Are you going to your in-laws’ house, that you’ve brought all five sets of clothes?”

Dhaniya replied, “Even at my in-laws’ place, there aren’t any young sisters-in-law or co-sisters sitting around, whom I’d go and show off to.”

A gentle smile flickered across Hori’s deep, dusky, sunken face. Dhaniya, blushing, said, “As if you’re such a handsome young man that the sisters-in-law would swoon at the sight of you!”

Hori carefully folded his torn mirzai and placed it on the cot, saying, “So you think I’ve grown old? I haven’t even turned forty yet. A man is in his prime at sixty.”

“Go look at yourself in the mirror. Men like you aren’t in their prime at sixty. You can’t even get enough milk, ghee, or ointment to rub on yourself, and you talk of being in your prime! Seeing your condition makes me even more anxious—how will we survive old age? Whose doorstep will we beg at?”

That fleeting gentleness on Hori’s face seemed to wither in the harsh fire of reality. Gathering the firewood, he said, “We won’t even live to see sixty, Dhaniya. We’ll be gone before then.”

Dhaniya rebuked him, “Enough, don’t speak such ominous words. Even when someone says something nice, you start cursing.”

As Hori slung his staff over his shoulder and left the house, Dhaniya stood at the doorway, watching him for a long time. His words, heavy with despair, sent a tremor of fear through her wounded heart. It was as if, with all the austerity and devotion of her womanhood, she was granting her husband the gift of fearlessness. From the depths of her soul, a host of blessings seemed to rise up and envelop Hori, hiding him within their embrace. In this boundless ocean of poverty, her marital bond was the single straw she clung to, crossing the waters with its help. These discordant words, though close to reality, seemed to snatch even that straw from her grasp with a jolt; indeed, it was their very closeness to reality that gave them such power to wound. Who can understand the pain of being called one-eyed, except the one who is one-eyed himself?

Hori walked on, step by step. Seeing the lush, swaying green of the sugarcane plants on either side of the path, he thought to himself, “If God would only send rain from the village, and if the stalks remain healthy, I’ll surely buy a cow. The local cows neither give milk nor are their calves of any use—at best, they end up turning the oilman’s press. No, I’ll buy a crossbred cow. I’ll take good care of her. Even if nothing else, she’ll give four or five seers of milk. Gobar is always longing for milk. If I don’t eat and drink well at this age, then when will I? If I could drink milk for a year, I’d look presentable. The calves would grow into fine bullocks. The cow won’t cost less than two hundred rupees. And besides, a cow is the pride of the household. To see the cow first thing in the morning—what could be better? Who knows when this wish will be fulfilled, when that auspicious day will come!”

Like every householder, Hori’s longing for a cow had been cherished in his heart for as long as he could remember. This was his life’s greatest dream, his deepest yearning. Grand ambitions like living off bank interest, buying land, or building a mansion could never fit into his humble heart.

The sun of Jeth was rising higher, emerging from the mango grove and intensifying the crimson glow that spread across the sky with its silvery brilliance. The air was growing warmer. Farmers working in the fields on both sides greeted him with a “Ram Ram” and respectfully invited him to share a chillum, but where did Hori have the leisure for that? The desire for respect that sat within him brought a glimmer of pride to his parched face at such honor. This was the reward of keeping company with the landlords—otherwise, who would pay him any heed? What worth does a farmer with five bighas of land have? It was no small honor that even the Mahatos, who owned three or four ploughs, bowed their heads before him.

--- Page 8 ---
Now he left the narrow footpath between the fields and entered a low-lying patch, where rainwater would collect during the monsoon, making the ground moist, and in the blazing month of Jeth, a touch of green would appear. The cows from neighboring villages would come here to graze. Even now, the air here felt fresher and cooler. Hori took two or three deep breaths. He felt like sitting here for a while. All day long, he would have to toil in the scorching heat anyway. Several farmers were willing to take a lease on this patch of land. They offered good money, but may God bless Rai Sahib, who had clearly said that this land was reserved for cattle grazing and would not be leased out at any price. If it had been a selfish zamindar, he would have said, “Let the cows go to hell! If we can get money, why should we leave it?” But Rai Sahib still upheld the old tradition. What kind of master is he who does not care for his people?

Suddenly, he saw Bhola coming this way with his cows. Bhola was the cowherd of the neighboring hamlet, attached to this village, and traded in milk and butter. If he got a good price, he would sometimes sell cows to the farmers as well. Hori’s heart yearned at the sight of those cows. If only Bhola would sell him that cow in front, what more could he ask for? He would pay the money little by little. He knew there was no money at home. The rent was still unpaid, and he still owed Bisesar Sah, on which interest kept piling up. But poverty brings with it a kind of shortsightedness, which is not daunted even by shamelessness, abuse, or beatings; it emboldened him now.

Approaching Bhola, he said, “Ram-Ram, Bhola bhai! Tell me, how are things? I heard you brought new cows from the fair this time?”

Bhola replied curtly, having guessed Hori’s intentions. “Yes, I brought two heifers and two cows. The old cows had all dried up. If the cattle don’t give milk, how can one make a living?”

Hori placed his hand on the haunch of the cow in front and said, “She looks like a good milker. How much did you pay for her?”

Bhola boasted, “The market was very high this time, Mahto. I had to pay eighty rupees for her—my eyes nearly popped out! Thirty rupees each for the two heifers. And on top of that, the buyer demands eight seers of milk for every rupee.”

“You people have such brave hearts, brother! But even so, the kind of cattle you bring—there’s no match for them in ten or fifteen villages around.”

Bhola began to swell with pride. “Rai Sahib offered a hundred rupees for her. Fifty each for the two heifers. But I didn’t sell. If God wills, I’ll make a hundred rupees from her in this very season.”

“No doubt about it, brother! What can the masters do? If they get something as a gift, they’ll take it, otherwise, they leave it. It’s only you people who have the courage to count out handfuls of rupees, trusting to fate. I feel like just gazing at her. Blessed is your life, that you serve cows so devotedly! I can’t even get cow dung for my home. For a householder to not have even a single cow—what a shame! Years go by, and we don’t even see a drop of milk. My wife keeps saying, ‘Why don’t you ask Bhola Bhaiya?’ I tell her, ‘If I meet him, I’ll ask.’ She’s very fond of your nature. She says she’s never seen a man like you—when you talk, you keep your eyes lowered, never raise your head.”

The pride that was rising in Bhola was deepened by this overflowing cup of praise. He said, “A real man is one who sees another’s wife or daughter as his own. Any scoundrel who looks at another’s woman with evil intent should be shot.”

“You’ve spoken a priceless truth, brother! Indeed, a true gentleman is one who guards another’s honor as his own.”

--- Page 9 ---
“Just as a woman becomes an orphan when her husband dies, so too does a man lose the use of his hands and feet when his wife passes away. My home is ruined now, Mahto—there’s not even a soul left to hand me a cup of water.”

Last year, Bhola’s wife had died of heatstroke. Hori knew this, but he had never realized that this fifty-year-old, withered Bhola was so tender within. The longing for a wife had become vivid in his eyes. Hori found his seat. His practical, peasant mind grew alert.

“The old saying isn’t false—‘Without a woman, a house is a haunt for ghosts.’ Why don’t you arrange a match somewhere?”

“I’m on the lookout, Mahto, but no one’s willing to get caught so soon. I’m ready to spend a hundred or a hundred and fifty rupees, too. As God wills.”

“I’ll keep an eye out as well. If God wishes, your home will be settled soon.”

“That’s all I hope for, brother. God has given me plenty to eat at home. I get four seers of milk every day, but what use is it?”

“There’s a woman in my in-laws’ village. Three or four years ago, her husband left her and went off to Calcutta. The poor thing makes a living by grinding grain. She has no children either. She’s good-looking and well-mannered. Think of her as Lakshmi herself.”

Bhola’s shriveled face brightened. How much nectar there is in hope! He said, “Now I’ll depend on you, Mahto! If you get a chance, let’s go see her one day.”

“I’ll let you know once everything is arranged. Sometimes, rushing things spoils the work.”

“Go whenever you wish. What’s the hurry? If your heart is set on this cow, then take her.”

“This cow isn’t worthy of my honor, Dada. I don’t want to cause you any loss. My principle isn’t to squeeze my friends! Just as I’ve managed all these days, I’ll manage some more.”

“You talk as if we’re strangers, Hori. Take the cow, pay whatever price you wish. She’ll be in your house just as she was in mine. I bought her for eighty rupees, just give me the same. Go on.”

“But I don’t have the cash, Dada, you know that.”

“Who’s asking you for cash, brother?”

Hori’s chest swelled with pride. The cow wasn’t overpriced at eighty rupees. Such a fine build, six or seven seers of milk both morning and evening, so gentle that even a child could milk her. Each of her calves would fetch a hundred rupees. Tied at his door, she’d add to the house’s glory. He still owed nearly four hundred rupees elsewhere, but he considered debts as good as nothing. If Bhola’s marriage was arranged, for a year or two he wouldn’t even ask for the money. And if the marriage didn’t happen, what harm could come to Hori? At most, Bhola would come again and again to demand payment, get angry, hurl abuses. But Hori felt little shame in this. He was used to such dealings. This was the very fruit of a peasant’s life. He was deceiving Bhola, and this transaction suited his sense of propriety. Even now, written agreements or the lack thereof made no difference to him in matters of lending and borrowing. The constant threat of drought and famine kept his heart timid. The wrathful face of God was always before him. Yet, this deceit was not deceit in his eyes; it was simply self-interest, and that was no sin. Such tricks he played day and night. Even if he had a few rupees at home, he would swear before the mahajan that he didn’t have a single paisa. Wetting the hemp a little, or mixing some seeds in the cotton—these were all acceptable in his code. And here, it wasn’t just self-interest—there was a kind of mischievous amusement in it too. The foolishness of old men is a laughable thing, and such—

--- Page 10 ---
If you squeeze a little out of the old folks, there’s no sin in that.

Handing Hori the cow’s tether, Bhola said, “Take her, Mahto. You’ll do the right thing. Just make sure to take six seers of milk as soon as she calves. Come, I’ll walk you to your house. They might trouble you on the way, thinking you’re a stranger. Now, let me tell you the truth: the landlord offered me ninety rupees, but what do they care for cows? They’d just take her from me and hand her over to some official. And what do officials know of serving a cow? They only know how to suck the blood of others. They’d keep her as long as she gave milk, then sell her off to anyone. Who knows whose hands she’d end up in? Money isn’t everything, brother. There’s some duty too. She’ll be at peace in your home. It won’t be that you eat your fill and let the cow stand hungry. You’ll care for her, caress her, and she’ll bless us. What can I say, brother? There’s not a handful of fodder left at home. All the money is spent at the market. I thought I’d borrow from the mahajan and buy some fodder, but I haven’t paid off my last loan. He refused. How can I feed so many animals? That’s what’s killing me. Even if I feed them just a pinch each, it’s a maund of fodder every day. Only God can see me through this.”

Hori said sympathetically, “Why didn’t you tell me before? I just sold off a cartload of fodder.”

Bhola struck his forehead and said, “Why should I go around crying about my troubles to everyone, brother? No one shares your burden; they all just laugh. I don’t grieve for the cows that have dried up—I’ll keep them alive on leaves and scraps. But this one can’t go without proper feed. If you can, give me ten or twenty rupees for fodder.”

There’s no doubt that a peasant is a hardened self-seeker. It’s hard to get a bribe out of his pocket, he haggles over every price, he pleads with the mahajan for hours to save a single paisa of interest, and he won’t be fooled unless he’s absolutely sure. Yet his whole life is in constant cooperation with nature. Trees bear fruit, and the people eat them. Grain grows in the fields, and it serves the world. Milk fills the cow’s udder, but she doesn’t drink it herself—others do. The clouds bring rain, and the earth is satisfied. In such company, where is there room for base selfishness? Hori was a peasant, and he had never learned to warm his hands at someone else’s burning house.

As soon as he heard Bhola’s tale of hardship, his attitude changed. Returning the tether to Bhola, he said, “I don’t have any money, Dada. But I do have a little fodder left—I’ll give it to you. Come and take it. You’re selling your cow for fodder, and I’d be the one to buy her? My hands would be stained.”

Bhola replied, his voice choked, “Your oxen will never starve! You have plenty stored up too.”

“No, Dada, this year the fodder was good.”

“I shouldn’t have brought up the fodder.”

“If you hadn’t said anything and I’d found out later, I’d have felt terrible—that you thought I wouldn’t understand. If a brother doesn’t help in time of need, who will?”

“But at least take the cow.”

“Not now, Dada. I’ll take her another time.”

“Then take the fodder in exchange for the cow whenever you like.”

Hori said, with the humility of hard times, “If you ever need a handful of grain, would I refuse you, brother?”

--- Page 11 ---
“But won’t your oxen starve to death?”

“God will surely find some way. Asadh is upon us. I’ll manage somehow.”

“But this cow is now yours. Come and take her whenever you wish.”

“To take a brother’s ox seized for debt is a sin, but to take your cow at this moment would be even greater.”

If Hori had the strength to split hairs, he would have gladly taken the cow home. When Bhola asked for cash, it was clear he was not selling the cow for fodder, but for some other reason. Yet, just as a horse shies away at the mere rustle of leaves and refuses to move even under the lash, so was Hori’s state. To take something in another’s time of crisis is a sin—this truth had become a part of his soul over countless lifetimes.

Bhola said, his voice choked with emotion, “Shall I send someone for the fodder, then?”

Hori replied, “I’m just going to the Rai Sahib’s mansion. I’ll be back in an hour or so—send someone then.”

Tears welled up in Bhola’s eyes. He said, “Today you have saved me, Hori bhai! Now I know I am not alone in this world, that I too have a friend.” After a moment, he added, “Don’t forget your promise.”

As Hori walked on, his heart was light. A strange vigor surged within him. What if five or ten maunds of fodder were gone? At least the poor man would not have to sell his cow in distress. When I have fodder, I’ll bring the cow home. God willing, may I find a wife to help me—then all will be well.

He looked back. The speckled cow, swishing flies away with her tail, shaking her head, swayed along with a queenly gait, as if she were a rani among maids. What a blessed day it would be when this Kamadhenu would be tied at his own door!

Semri and Belari are both villages in the province of Awadh. There is no need to mention the district. Hori lives in Belari, Rai Sahib Amarpal Singh in Semri. There are only five miles between the two villages. In the last Satyagraha movement, Rai Sahib had won great renown. He had resigned his council membership and gone to jail. Since then, the tenants of his estate held him in great reverence. Not that the tenants in his estate enjoyed any special concessions, or that the severity of taxes and forced labor had lessened, but all the blame for these ills fell upon the estate managers. No stain could touch Rai Sahib’s reputation. After all, he too was a slave to the same system. The business of administration would go on as it always had; Rai Sahib’s goodness could not alter it. Thus, even though his income and authority had not diminished in the least, his fame had only grown.

He would speak to the tenants with a smile—was that not enough? The lion’s work is to hunt. If, instead of roaring and growling, he could speak sweetly, he would get his prey without leaving home, without wandering the jungle in search.

Though a nationalist, Rai Sahib maintained cordial relations with the authorities; his glances, his gestures, and his officials’ customary bribes all continued as before. He was a lover of literature and music, fond of drama, an eloquent speaker, a fine writer, a skilled marksman. It had been ten years since his wife passed away...

--- Page 12 ---
His hair had turned gray, yet he had not remarried. He would laugh and joke, lightening the burdens of his widower’s life.

When Hori reached the threshold, he saw that grand preparations were underway for the Dhanush-Yajna, held every year on the occasion of Jeth’s Dussehra. Here, a stage was being built; there, a pavilion; elsewhere, a guesthouse for the visitors, and shops for the merchants. The sun was blazing, but Rai Sahib himself was hard at work. Along with his father’s estate, he had inherited devotion to Ram, and by giving the Dhanush-Yajna the form of a play, he had turned it into a refined source of entertainment. On this occasion, his friends and acquaintances, even government officials, would be invited, and for two or three days the entire region would be abuzz with activity.

Rai Sahib’s family was vast. Nearly a hundred and fifty relatives dined together. There were several uncles, dozens of cousins, many brothers, and scores of kin. One uncle was a devoted follower of Radha and lived permanently in Vrindavan. He had composed many poems in the nectar of devotion and would have them printed from time to time to present as gifts to friends. Another uncle was a great devotee of Ram and was translating the Ramayana into Persian. All of them received stipends from the estate. None had any need to work.

Hori stood in the pavilion, wondering how to announce his arrival, when suddenly Rai Sahib appeared and, seeing him, called out, “Ah! You have come, Hori. I was just about to send for you. Listen, this time you must play the gardener of Raja Janak. Do you understand? When Shri Janaki goes to the temple for worship, you will stand there with a bouquet and present it to her. Don’t be late. And see, make sure to tell all the tenants to come and perform the auspicious rites. Come with me to the kothi; I have some things to discuss with you.”

He walked ahead toward the mansion, and Hori followed behind. There, in the shade of a thick tree, Rai Sahib sat on a chair and motioned for Hori to sit on the ground. Then he spoke, “Did you understand what I said? The steward will do what he must, but a tenant listens to another tenant with a different heart than he does to a steward. We have to arrange for twenty thousand rupees within these five or seven days. How it will be done, I cannot fathom. You must be wondering, why is the master sharing his troubles with a man of my station? Whom else can I speak my heart to? For some reason, I trust you. I know at least this much: you will not mock me in your heart. And even if you do, I can bear your laughter. But I cannot bear the laughter of those who are my equals, for their laughter is filled with envy, sarcasm, and malice. And why shouldn’t they laugh? I too laugh heartily, clapping my hands, at their misfortunes, their calamities, their downfall. There is enmity between wealth and compassion. We too give alms, perform acts of charity. But do you know why? Only to put our equals to shame. Our charity and piety are nothing but pure arrogance, nothing but pride. If any one of us is served with a decree, or his property is attached, or he is summoned for arrears of rent, or his young son dies, or his widowed daughter-in-law leaves, or his house catches fire, or he is duped by a courtesan, or ruined by his own tenants—then all his brothers will laugh at him, slap their thighs, as if they have inherited the wealth of the whole world. Yet when they meet, it is with such affection, as if they are ready to shed blood instead of sweat for each other. If those cousins, uncles, aunts, who are living in luxury and gambling, drinking, and indulging in debauchery thanks to this very estate, were to see me in distress, they would light lamps of ghee in celebration. There is no one who truly understands my sorrow, no one who believes I have any right to be unhappy. If I weep, it is considered a joy; if I fall ill, it is a delight. If I change my ways…”

--- Page 13 ---
If I get married, it would be called indulgence. If I start drinking, it would be said I am consuming the blood of my subjects. If I do not indulge in pleasure, and instead turn to corruption, then what more is left to say! These people have done everything in their power to entrap me in luxury, and their efforts continue to this day. Their only wish is that I become blind, that I see everything and yet see nothing, that I know everything and yet remain ignorant.

Raisahab chewed two betel leaves to urge the carriage forward, and looked at Hori’s face as if he wished to read his thoughts.

Raisahab, his mouth full of betel, said, “We used to think such things happened only among our kind, but it seems even among the great, such people are not lacking.”

Hori, mustering his courage, replied, “We used to think such things happened only among our kind, but it seems—”

Raisahab, his mouth still full, interrupted, “You think I am a great man? My name is great, but my deeds are not. If there is envy or enmity among the poor, it is for self-interest or for bread. Such envy and enmity, I can forgive. If someone snatches the morsel from our mouth, it becomes our duty to snatch it back from his throat. If we let it go, we are saints. But the envy and enmity of the great is only for pleasure. We have become so great that we find selflessness and supreme joy only in meanness and cunning. We have reached that level of divinity where the tears of others make us laugh. Do not think this is a small achievement. When the family is so large, someone or the other will always be ill. And the illnesses of the great are also great. What kind of great man would fall ill with a minor ailment? If we get a simple fever, we are given medicine for delirium; if a small boil appears, it becomes a case of blood poisoning. Now, small surgeons, medium surgeons, and great surgeons are being summoned by telegram; a man is being sent to Delhi to bring the Messiah of the land, and to Calcutta for the chief physician. Meanwhile, in the temple, the Durga recitation is underway, Jyotipacharya is studying the horoscope, and the Tantric masters are absorbed in their rituals. There is a race to snatch the Raja Saheb from the jaws of Yama. The vaids and doctors wait eagerly for the moment when his head aches, so that gold may rain in their homes. And all this money is extracted from you and your brothers, at the point of a spear. I only wonder why the wildfire of your sighs does not reduce us to ashes. But no, there is nothing to wonder at—being reduced to ashes takes no time, and the pain lasts only a moment. Inch by inch, limb by limb, we are being consumed, and to escape that outcry, we seek refuge in the police, the officials, the courts, the lawyers, and become playthings in everyone’s hands, like a beautiful woman. The world thinks we are very happy—we have estates, palaces, carriages, servants, debts, courtesans—what do we lack? But if there is no strength in the soul, no pride, then whatever else one may be, he is not a man. He who cannot sleep at night for fear of his enemies, whose suffering is laughed at by all and for whom no one weeps, whose self-respect is crushed under the feet of others, who in the intoxication of pleasure has completely forgotten himself, who licks the boots of the officials and sucks the blood of his subordinates—I do not call such a man happy. He is the most wretched creature in the world. If the Sahib comes for hunting or on a tour, it is my duty to run after his every sorrow. A frown on his brow, and my life dries up. To please him, what do I not do? But if I start telling you all that, perhaps you would not believe it. The doctors—well, it is a blessing if we are spared till tomorrow. We are even ready to prostrate ourselves. This habit of living off others has destroyed us. We have no faith in our own effort, only in the favor of officials.

--- Page 14 ---
To somehow remain in their good graces by constant flattery, and to use their help to instill terror among our own people—this is our only endeavor. The sycophancy of our hangers-on has made us so arrogant and irritable that we have lost all gentleness, humility, and spirit of service. Sometimes I think, if the government were to take away our estates and force us to earn our bread by honest labor, it would be a great favor to us. And it is certain now that even the government will not protect us anymore; we are of no further use to them. All signs indicate that very soon our class will cease to exist. I am ready to welcome that day. May God bring it soon! That will be the day of our deliverance. We are victims of circumstance; it is this very circumstance that is leading to our ruin. And until the shackles of property are removed from our feet, until this curse hovers over our heads, we shall never attain that station of humanity which is the ultimate aim of life.

Raisahab once again took out his betel box and stuffed several betel leaves into his mouth. He was about to say something more when a peon arrived and said, “Sarkar, the begars have refused to work. They say that unless they are given food, they will not work. We threatened them, but they left their work and went away.”

Wrinkles appeared on Raisahab’s forehead. His eyes bulged as he spoke, “Come, let me deal with these scoundrels. When have we ever given them food? Why this new demand today? They will get their usual wage of one anna a day, as always, and for that wage they must work, whether they like it or not.”

Then, turning to Hori, he said, “You may go now, Hori. Make your preparations. Remember what I have told you. I expect at least five hundred from your village.”

Raisahab left in a huff. Hori thought to himself, just now he was talking of policy and dharma, and suddenly he has become so angry.

The sun was now overhead. Overwhelmed by its brilliance, the trees had drawn in their branches. A dusty haze covered the sky, and the earth ahead seemed to tremble in the heat.

Hori picked up his staff and set out for home. Where would he find the money for the auspicious offering? This worry weighed heavily on his mind.

:3:

As Hori neared his village, he saw that Govind was still in the field weeding the sugarcane, and both girls were working alongside him. The hot wind was blowing, dust devils were rising, the earth was ablaze—as if nature had mixed fire into the air. Why were they still in the field? Were they determined to sacrifice themselves for the sake of work? He walked toward the field and shouted from a distance, “Why aren’t you coming, Gobar? Will you keep working forever? The afternoon is half gone—do you have no sense?”

At the sight of him, all three picked up their hoes and joined him. Gobar was a tall, dark, slender youth, who seemed to have no real interest in the work. Instead of cheerfulness, his face showed discontent and rebellion. He worked only to show that he was indifferent to food and drink. The elder girl, Sona, was a modest maiden—dusky, well-shaped, lively, and quick. Her coarse red sari, which she had folded at the knees and tied at her waist, seemed almost burdensome on her slight frame, lending her a dignity beyond her years. The younger, Rupa, was a little girl of five or six, dirty-faced,

--- Page 15 ---
A nest of hair sat atop his head, a single loincloth tied around his waist, he looked both stubborn and forlorn.

Rupa clung to Hori’s legs and said, “Kaka! Look, I didn’t leave a single clod behind. Didi says, ‘Go sit under the tree.’ But if we don’t break up the clods, Kaka, how will the earth be leveled?”

Hori lifted her into his arms and, caressing her, said, “You did very well, beti. Come, let’s go home.”

After suppressing his rebellious feelings for a while, Gobar spoke up, “Why do you go to flatter the landlords every day? If the dues aren’t paid, the peon comes and hurls abuses. We have to do begar, pay all the tributes and gifts ourselves. Then why salute anyone?”

These very thoughts were running through Hori’s mind at that moment, but it was necessary to quell this rebellious spirit in his son. He replied, “If we don’t go to salute, where will we live? When God has made us slaves, what power do we have? It’s only because of this salutation that I was able to put up a hut at the door, and no one said a word. Dhure put up a post at his door and the officials fined him two rupees. We dug so much earth from the pond, the official said nothing; if someone else dug, they’d have to pay a bribe. I go to salute for my own needs. There’s no pleasure in it, nor do I have a Saturday in my feet. You have to stand for hours before the landlord even hears of you. Sometimes he comes out, sometimes he sends word that he’s busy.”

Gobar retorted, “There must be some pleasure in agreeing with the big men, otherwise why would people stand for the membership?”

“When trouble falls on your head, son, then you’ll know. For now, say what you like. I used to think the same things once, but now I know that our necks are pressed under other people’s feet; you can’t live with your head held high.”

Having vented his anger at his father, Gobar calmed down and walked on in silence. Sona saw Rupa sitting in their father’s arms and felt jealous. She scolded, “Why don’t you get down and walk on your own? Are your legs broken?”

Rupa, wrapping her arms around her father’s neck, replied cheekily, “I won’t get down, go away. Kaka, Didi teases me every day, saying, ‘You’re Rupa, I’m Sona.’ Give me another name.”

Hori, feigning seriousness, said to Sona, “Why do you tease her, Sonia? Sona is only for show. It’s Rupa that keeps things going. If there were no Rupa, where would the rupees come from, tell me?”

Sona defended herself, “If there were no Sona, how would there be gold necklaces, nose rings, or ornaments?”

Gobar too joined in the playful argument. He said to Rupa, “You should say that Sona is yellow like a dry leaf, but Rupa is bright, like the sun.”

Sona replied, “At weddings, people wear yellow saris, no one wears a white sari.”

Rupa was defeated by this argument. Neither Gobar nor Hori could counter it. She looked at Hori with tearful eyes.

A new idea struck Hori. He said, “Sona is for the rich. For us poor folk, Rupa is enough. Just as the landlords call wheat ‘gehun’ and chamars call it ‘ji’—because the rich eat wheat, but we eat ‘ji’.”

Sona had no answer to this strong argument. Defeated, she said, “You’re all ganging up on me. Otherwise, I’d have put Rupa to shame.”

Rupa, full of hope, began to chant, “Oh Ram, Sona chamar—Oh Ram, Sona chamar.”

--- Page 16 ---
The joy of this victory was so great that she could not stay in her father's lap. She jumped down to the ground and began to skip about, chanting over and over—“Rupa is a king, Sona is a chamar—Rupa is a king, Sona is a chamar!”

When they reached home, Dhania was standing at the doorway, waiting for them. She spoke irritably, “Why are you so late today, Gobar? No one gives up their life for work, you know.”

Then, turning to her husband with some heat, she said, “You came back from your earnings, and instead of coming home, you went straight to the fields. Were the fields going to run away?”

There was a well at the doorstep. Hori and Gobar poured a pitcher of water each over their heads, bathed Rupa, and then went to eat. There were barley rotis, but they were as white and smooth as wheat. There was arhar dal, with raw mangoes in it. Rupa sat down to eat from her father’s plate. Sona looked at her with eyes full of envy, as if to say, “Ah, the pampered one!”

Dhania asked, “What did the master say?”

Hori, pouring out a lota of water, replied, “It was about collecting the revenue, what else! We think the big men must be very happy, but to tell you the truth, they are even more troubled than we are. We worry only about our own bellies, but they are beset by a thousand anxieties.”

Hori could not recall everything Rai Sahib had said. Only the gist of the whole conversation stuck in his memory.

Gobar said with sarcasm, “Then why don’t they give us their land? We’re ready to give them our fields, our oxen, our ploughs, our spades. Will they exchange? It’s all trickery, pure arrogance. Whoever suffers doesn’t keep dozens of motorcars, doesn’t live in palaces, doesn’t eat sweets and puris, and doesn’t indulge in music and dance. They enjoy the pleasures of rule, and yet claim to be miserable!”

Hori snapped, “Who’s going to argue with you now, brother? Is anyone going to give up their inheritance just like that? What do we get from farming anyway? We don’t even earn an anna a day. Even a servant earning ten rupees a month eats and dresses better than us, but we can’t leave the land. If we give up farming, what will we do? Where can we get a job? And then, we have to maintain our dignity too. The respect that comes with farming doesn’t come with service. It’s the same with zamindars. They too are plagued by a hundred troubles—supplying rations to the officials, paying their respects, keeping the clerks happy. If they don’t pay the revenue on time, they’ll be thrown in jail, their property will be seized. No one throws us in jail. We just get a few scoldings and curses, that’s all.”

Gobar protested, “That’s just talk. We have to struggle for every grain, we don’t have proper clothes to wear, sweat runs from our heads to our heels, and still we can’t make ends meet. But what about them? They sit comfortably on their cushions and thrones, have hundreds of servants, rule over thousands of people. Maybe they don’t have ready cash, but they enjoy every pleasure. What else does a man do with wealth?”

“You think we and they are equals?”

“God made everyone equal.”

“That’s not so, son. The great and the small are made so by God Himself. Wealth comes only through great penance. They are enjoying the fruits of the deeds they did in their past lives. We did nothing, so what is there for us to enjoy?”

“That’s just a way to console yourself. God makes everyone equal. Here, whoever has the stick crushes the poor and becomes a big man.”

“That’s your illusion. Even now, the master spends four hours a day in prayer to God—”

These same thoughts were stirring in Hori’s heart, but it was necessary to quell the boy’s rebellious spirit. He replied, “If we don’t go to salute, then where will we live? When God has made us slaves, what power do we have? It’s only because of these salutations that we’ve managed to put up a hut at the gate, and no one said a word. Dhure put up a post at his door, and the officials fined him two rupees. We dug so much earth from the pond, but the official said nothing; if someone else digs, they must pay a bribe. I go to salute for my own needs—there’s no joy in it, nor do I have a craving for it. You stand for hours before the master even hears of you. Sometimes he comes out, sometimes sends word that he has no time.”

Gobar retorted, “There must be some pleasure in agreeing with the big men, otherwise why would people stand there for hours just to say ‘Yes, sir’?”

“When it comes upon your head, then you’ll know, son. Say what you like now. I used to think the same way, but now I’ve learned that our necks are pressed beneath others’ feet; pride gets you nowhere.”

Having vented his anger on his father, Gobar calmed down and walked on quietly.

Sona saw Rupa sitting in their father’s lap and felt a pang of jealousy. Scolding, she said, “Why don’t you get down and walk on your own? Are your legs broken?”

Rupa, clinging to her father’s neck, replied boldly, “I won’t get down, go away. Kaka, Didi teases me every day, saying, ‘You’re Rupa, I’m Sona.’ Give me a different name.”

Hori, glancing at Sona with mock severity, said, “Why do you tease her, Soniya? Sona may be nice to look at, but it’s Rupa that’s useful. If there were no Rupa, where would the money come from, tell me?”

Sona defended herself, “If there were no Sona, how would there be a Mohanmala, where would the nose rings come from, how would the necklaces be made?”

Gobar too joined in the playful argument. He said to Rupa, “You should say that Sona is yellow like a dry leaf, but Rupa is bright, like the sun.”

Sona replied, “At weddings, people wear yellow saris, no one wears white.”

Rupa was defeated by this argument. Neither Gobar nor Hori could counter it. She looked at Hori with troubled eyes.

A new idea struck Hori. He said, “Sona is for the rich. For us poor folk, Rupa is enough. Just as the king eats rice, and the chamar eats wheat—not because wheat is less, but because the rich eat rice, and we eat wheat.”

Sona had no answer to this clever reasoning. Defeated, she said, “You’re all ganging up on me. Otherwise, I’d have made Rupiya cry.”

Rupa wagged her finger and chanted, “Hey Ram, Sona chamar—Hey Ram, Sona chamar.”

--- Page 18 ---
The joy of this victory was so great for her that she could not remain in her father's lap. She leapt to the ground and began to skip about, chanting over and over—“Rupa the king, Sona the chamar! Rupa the king, Sona the chamar!”

When they reached home, Dhaniya was standing at the doorway, waiting for them. She spoke sharply, “Why are you so late today, Gobar? No one gives up their life for work.”

Then, turning her irritation toward her husband, she said, “You came back from earning wages, and went straight to the fields. Were the fields going to run away?”

There was a well at the doorway. Hori and Gobar poured a pitcher of water each over their heads, bathed Rupa, and went in to eat. The bread was made of barley, but it was as white and soft as wheat. There was arhar dal, with pieces of raw mango in it. Rupa sat down to eat from her father’s plate. Sona looked at her with eyes full of envy, as if to say, “Ah, the darling of the house.”

Dhaniya asked, “What did the malik say?”

Hori, pouring out a lota of water, replied, “It was about collecting the revenue, what else! We think big men must be very happy, but to tell the truth, they are more troubled than we are. We worry about our own bellies, but they are surrounded by a thousand worries.”

Hori could not recall all that the Raisahab had said. Only the gist of the conversation stuck in his memory.

Gobar said sarcastically, “Then why don’t they just give us their land? We’re ready to give them our fields, our oxen, our ploughs, our tools. Will they trade? It’s all trickery, pure arrogance. Whoever suffers doesn’t keep dozens of motorcars, doesn’t live in palaces, doesn’t eat sweets and delicacies, and doesn’t indulge in music and dance. They enjoy the pleasures of rule, and yet claim to be miserable!”

Hori replied irritably, “Who’s going to argue with you now, brother? No one gives up their inheritance, just as they won’t. What do we get from farming anyway? We don’t even earn a daily wage of an anna. Even a servant who earns ten rupees a month eats and dresses better than us, but still, we can’t leave the fields. If we give up farming, what will we do? Where are the jobs? And then, we must maintain our dignity too. The dignity that comes with farming isn’t there in service. The zamindars are the same. Their lives are plagued by hundreds of troubles—supplying the officials, paying respects, keeping the clerks happy. If they don’t pay the revenue on time, they’re thrown in jail, their property is seized. No one throws us in jail. We just get a few scoldings and threats, that’s all.”

Gobar protested, “That’s just talk. We are starving for every grain, don’t have a decent rag to wear, sweat from our heads to our heels, and still can’t make ends meet. What about them? They sit comfortably on cushions and bolsters, have hundreds of servants, rule over thousands. Maybe they don’t save money, but they enjoy every pleasure. What else does a man do with wealth?”

“You think we and they are equals?”

“God made everyone equal.”

“That’s not so, son. The high and the low are made by God’s own hand. Wealth comes only through great penance. They are enjoying the fruits of their deeds from past lives. We didn’t accumulate anything, so what can we enjoy?”

“That’s just a way to console yourself. God makes everyone equal. Here, whoever holds the stick crushes the poor and becomes a big man.”

“That’s your delusion. Even now, the malik spends four hours a day in devotion to God.”

--- Page 19 ---
“On whose strength do all these hymns, feasts, and acts of charity take place?”

“On their own strength.”

“No, on the strength of the kisans and the toil of the mazdoors. How can this wealth, earned through sin, ever be digested? That’s why they must give in charity, that’s why they sing the Lord’s praises. Let them try worshipping God while living hungry and naked, then we’ll see. If someone gave us enough to eat twice a day, we too would spend all eight watches chanting God’s name. But make us weed the sugarcane fields for a day, and all that devotion would be forgotten.”

Hori, defeated, said, “Who can argue with you, brother? You even find fault with God’s own leela.”

In the afternoon, when Gobar picked up his spade to leave, Hori said, “Wait a moment, son, I’ll come with you. But first, take out a little bhusa and set it aside. I’ve told Bhola I’ll give him some. Poor fellow is in a bad way these days.”

Gobar looked at him with eyes full of defiance. “We don’t have any bhusa to sell.”

“I’m not selling it, brother, just giving it. He’s in trouble; we have to help him.”

“He never gave us even a single cow.”

“He was going to, but we didn’t take it.”

Dhaniya interjected, swaying her head, “A cow! He was going to give us a cow? He’d give you a cow? He never even sent a handful of milk to line our eyes, and he’d give a cow?”

Hori swore, “No, by my youth I swear, he was giving his own best cow. He’s in a tight spot, couldn’t keep up with the fodder. Now he wants to sell a cow to buy some bhusa. I thought, how could I take a man’s cow when he’s in distress? I’ll just give him a little bhusa. If I get some money together, I’ll buy the cow. I’ll pay it off bit by bit. It’s worth eighty rupees, but such a cow that a man would just stand and gaze at her.”

Gobar retorted, “This is exactly the kind of righteousness that’s ruined you. It’s simple—he has a cow worth eighty rupees, let him take twenty rupees’ worth of bhusa from us and give us the cow. Sixty rupees will remain, we’ll pay that off slowly.”

Hori smiled mysteriously, “I’ve thought of such a plan that the cow will come to us for almost nothing. Bhola has a marriage to arrange, that’s all. I’ll give a few maunds of bhusa just to show goodwill.”

Gobar sneered, “So now you’re going to arrange everyone’s marriage?”

Dhaniya shot him a sharp look, “As if that’s the only business left for us. We’re not giving bhusa to anyone. We haven’t eaten anyone’s salt here.”

Hori defended himself, “If, through my efforts, someone’s home can be settled, what harm is there in that?”

Gobar picked up his chilam and went off to get a light. He had no patience for such complications.

Dhaniya shook her head, “Whoever arranges his marriage won’t be satisfied with just an eighty-rupee cow. He’ll count every last paisa.”

Hori tried to reassure her, “I know that, but look at his decency too. Whenever he meets me, he only praises you—says what a Lakshmi you are, how capable you are.”

A gentle pride glimmered on Dhaniya’s face. With the air of “the heart delights, the head nods,” she said, “I’m not hungry for his praise. Let him keep his praise to himself.”

Gobar, with an affectionate smile, said, “I told him, brother, she won’t let a fly sit on her nose, she talks in abuses. But he keeps saying she’s not a woman, she’s Lakshmi herself.”

--- Page 20 ---
It was that his wife was a very sharp-tongued woman. The poor fellow was always running about in fear of her. He used to say, “The day I see your wife’s face in the morning, something or the other surely comes my way.”  
I said, “Maybe you get something, but here we see her every day, and never once do we get any money.”  
“If your fate is rotten, what can I do about it?”  
“There you go, bad-mouthing your wife—she wouldn’t even give alms to a beggar, would chase them off with a broom, and she was so greedy she’d even borrow salt from other houses.”  
“What’s the use of speaking ill of the dead? She’d burn with jealousy at the sight of me.”  
“Bhola was a real sufferer, that he managed to live with her. Anyone else would have eaten poison and died. Bhola must be ten years older than me, but he greets me with ‘Ram-Ram’ first.”  
“So what did he say? That when he saw your wife’s face in the morning, what would happen?”  
“On those days, God would send something from somewhere.”  
“The daughters-in-law are just as greedy. This time, all of them ate two rupees’ worth of melons on credit. Once they get something on credit, they never worry about paying it back.”  
“Oh! Why is Bhola crying?”  

Govind came and said, “Bhola Dada has arrived. There’s a maund or two of chaff—give it to him, then go look for a match for his engagement.”  

Dhaniya explained, “The man is sitting at the door, and you haven’t even put out a cot for him, and on top of that, you’re grumbling. Learn a little decency. Take a pitcher, fill it with water, set it out so he can wash his hands and face, offer him something to drink. It’s only in times of trouble that a man has to stretch out his hand before others.”  

Hori said, “There’s no need for drinks and such, he’s not some special guest.”  

Dhaniya retorted, “And what else is a guest? Does he come to your door every day? He’s come from so far, in this heat—he must be thirsty. Rupa, see if there’s any tobacco left in the tin—though with Govind around, there’s likely none left. Run and buy a penny’s worth of tobacco from the Sahuwain’s shop.”  

Never before had Bhola been treated with such hospitality. Govind set out a cot, Sona brought sweetened water, Rupa filled the pipe with tobacco. Dhaniya stood behind the door, eager to hear her own praise with her own ears.  

Bhola took the chillum in his hand and said, “When a good housewife comes into a home, consider that Lakshmi herself has arrived. She alone knows how to respect and serve both young and old.”  

Dhaniya’s heart trembled with joy. Her soul, wounded by worry, despair, and want, felt a gentle, soothing touch in these words.  

When Hori picked up Bhola’s basket and went inside to fetch the chaff, Dhaniya followed close behind. Hori said, “I wonder where he got such a big basket from. Must have borrowed it from some grain-roaster. It won’t fill with less than a maund. Even if I give two baskets, it’ll come to two maunds.”  

Dhaniya, beaming, said with a reproachful look, “Either don’t invite anyone, or if you do, feed them well. He hasn’t come to collect flowers and leaves that you send him off with a handful. If you’re going to give, then give three baskets. Why didn’t the good man bring his sons? How far can he carry it alone? He’ll collapse.”  

“I can’t give three baskets.”  

“So will you send him off with just one? Tell Govind to fill his own basket and go along with him.”
