Hori said, “There’s no need for all this fuss. He’s not some special guest.”  
Dhaniya retorted, “And what else is a guest? He doesn’t come to your door every day, does he? He’s come all this way in the heat and sun—he must be thirsty. Rupa, see if there’s any tobacco left in the tin—though with Gobar around, how could any be left? Run and buy a penny’s worth of tobacco from Sahuwain’s shop.”  

Never before had Bhola been treated with such hospitality. Gobar set out the cot, Sona brought sweetened water, Rupa fetched tobacco. Dhaniya stood behind the door, hidden, eager to hear her own praise with her own ears.  
Bhola took the chillum in his hand and said, “If a good housewife comes into a home, consider Lakshmi herself has arrived. Only she knows how to honor and serve young and old alike.”  
A tremor of joy ran through Dhaniya’s heart. Her soul, wounded by worry, despair, and want, felt in these words a gentle, soothing touch.  

When Hori picked up Bhola’s basket and went inside to fetch the chaff, Dhaniya followed him in. Hori said, “I wonder where he got such a big basket from. Must have borrowed it from some grain-roaster. It won’t fill with less than a maund. Even if I give him two baskets, it’ll be two maunds.”  
Dhaniya was beaming. She looked at him reproachfully and said, “Either don’t invite anyone, or if you do, feed them properly. They haven’t come to collect flowers and leaves from you, that you’d send them away with a handful. If you’re giving, then give three baskets. Why didn’t the good man bring his sons? How far can he carry it alone? He’ll be exhausted.”  
“I can’t give three baskets.”  
“So you’ll try to get away with just one? Tell Gobar to fill his own basket and take it to him.”

--- Page 23 ---
“Gobar is going to weed the sugarcane field.”

“The sugarcane won’t wither if it isn’t weeded for a day.”

“It was his job to take someone along. God has given him two sons.”

“They must not be at home. Perhaps they’ve gone to the market with the milk.”

“What a fine arrangement—give away your own goods, and then deliver them to their house as well. Load it, carry it, and even provide someone to help.”

“All right, then. Let no one go. I’ll deliver it myself. There’s no shame in serving our elders.”

“And if I give them three baskets, what will our own oxen eat?”

“All this should have been considered before inviting them. If not, then you and Gobar should both go together.”

“Charity should be given as charity, not by giving away your whole house!”

“If the zamindar’s peon were to come now, you’d carry the straw on your own head and deliver it—yourself, your son, your daughter, everyone. And there, you might even have to chop a maund or two of firewood.”

“The zamindar is different.”

“Yes, because he gets work done by force of the stick, doesn’t he?”

“Don’t we plough his fields?”

“We plough his fields, but don’t we pay rent as well?”

“All right, enough, stop nagging. We’ll both go. I shouldn’t have asked them to give the straw. Either she won’t walk at all, or if she does, she’ll start running.”

The three baskets were filled with straw. Gobar was sulking. He had no faith in his father’s ways. He thought, wherever his father went, something or the other from home was always lost. Dhania was pleased. As for Hori, he was tossed between duty and self-interest.

Hori and Gobar together carried one basket outside. Bhola immediately twisted his angoccha into a bundle, placed it on his head, and said, “I’ll drop this off and be right back. I’ll take another basket.”

Hori said, “Not just one, there are two more filled and ready. You won’t need to come back. Gobar and I will each take a basket and go with you.”

Bhola was stunned. Hori seemed to him not just a brother, but even closer. He felt a satisfaction within himself, as if his whole life had suddenly turned green and lush.

The three of them set off with the straw, and on the way, conversation began.

Bhola asked, “Dussehra is coming up. There’ll be a grand celebration at the landlords’ house, won’t there?”

“Yes, the tents and canopies are already up. This time, I’ll also be working in the play. Rai Sahib has said I’ll have to play the gardener of King Janak.”

“The masters are very pleased with you.”

“It’s their kindness.”

After a moment, Bhola asked again, “Have you managed to arrange some money for the auspicious rituals? Once you become the gardener, you won’t be let off easily.”

Hori wiped the sweat from his face and said, “That’s the very worry that’s killing me, Dada. All the grain was weighed right at the threshing floor. The zamindar took his share, the mahajan took his. For me, only five seers of grain remain. This straw, I carried away and hid at night. Otherwise, not even a wisp would have been left.”

--- Page 24 ---
The zamindar is only one, but there are three moneylenders: the sahuain, Mangru, and Pandit Datadeen, each separate. I haven’t even managed to pay off the interest to any of them. Even with the zamindar, half the money remains unpaid. I borrowed more from the sahuain, and somehow managed to get by. I’ve tried every way to economize, brother, but nothing works. It seems I was born only to shed my blood and fill the coffers of the mighty. I’ve already paid double the principal in interest, yet the principal itself still weighs on my head as before. People say, “Spend with restraint during festivals, pilgrimages, and rituals.” But no one shows the way. Raisahab squandered thirty thousand rupees on his son’s wedding—no one says a word to him. Mangru spent five thousand on his father’s funeral rites—no one questions him. Yet everyone’s honor is supposed to be the same.

“We are people too, aren’t we?”

“Who says you and I are people? What humanity do we possess? A real person is one who has wealth, authority, knowledge. We are like oxen, born only to be yoked. And on top of that, we cannot even bear to see each other prosper. There is no unity among us. If one farmer would not encroach on another’s field, how could anyone commit injustice? Love has vanished from this world.”

For the old, there is no topic more entertaining than reminiscing about past joys, lamenting present sorrows, and dreading the ruin of the future. The two friends continued to pour out their woes. Bhola spoke of his sons’ misdeeds, Hori wept over his brothers, and then, setting their burdens down by a well, they sat to drink water. Gobar asked the baniya for a lota and began to draw water.

Bhola asked with sympathy, “You must have been deeply hurt at the time of the separation. You raised your brothers like your own sons.”

Hori replied, his voice choked with emotion, “Don’t ask, Dada. I felt as if I should drown myself somewhere. Everything happened before my very eyes. Those for whom I sacrificed my youth in the dust became my accusers. And what was the root of the quarrel? Only that my wife wouldn’t go to work in the fields. Tell me, doesn’t someone need to look after the house? Who will manage the give-and-take, the storing and keeping, the care and order? And it’s not as if she just sits at home—sweeping, cooking, cleaning, tending to the children—is that a small task? Could Sobha’s wife manage the house, or did Heera’s wife have that skill? Since the separation, both households cook only one meal a day. Before, we used to feel hungry four times a day. Now, let them eat four times and see. Only I know what misery Gobar’s mother has endured in this role of mistress—she spent her days wearing her sisters-in-law’s tattered clothes, probably went to bed hungry herself, yet always looked after the daughters-in-law, even their snacks. She didn’t have so much as a thread of jewelry on her own body, but had two or four ornaments made for her sisters-in-law. If not gold, at least silver. The real bitterness was: why is she the mistress? It’s good that we separated. That burden is off my head.”

Bhola poured a lota of water and said, “It’s the same in every house, brother! It’s not just brothers—even with sons, there’s no harmony. And the reason is, I cannot keep my mouth shut when I see someone’s bad ways. You’ll gamble, smoke charas, take puffs of ganja—but whose house does it come from? If you want to spend, then earn, but none of them wants to work. They’ll spend with open hands. The eldest, Kamta, goes to market with goods, and half the money disappears. Ask him, and there’s no answer. The younger, Jangi, is always lost in his crowd. Evening comes, and he sits with the drum and cymbals. I don’t say singing and music are bad, but these are things for leisure. I’m not saying don’t work at all, but don’t spend all your time lost in that world. The burden falls on my head.”

--- Page 25 ---
I milk both the cow and the buffalo, then take the milk to the market. This is the snare of household life—a golden sickle, which you can neither swallow nor spit out. There is the girl, Jhunia—ill-fated, too. You had come for her engagement, remember? What a good match it was! The groom worked at a milk shop in Bombay. Then, during those riots between Hindus and Muslims, someone stabbed him in the stomach. The whole household was ruined. There was no way she could stay there. I brought her back, thinking I would arrange another match for her, but she refuses to agree. And both her sisters-in-law torment her day and night. The house is in a constant uproar, like the Mahabharata. Driven by misfortune, she came here, but even here she finds no peace.

Lost in these laments, the road passed by. Bhola’s hamlet was small, but lively. Mostly Ahirs lived there, and compared to other peasants, their condition was not so bad. Bhola was the headman of the village. At his doorway stood a large trough, around which ten or twelve cows and buffaloes were eating fodder. In the veranda lay a great wooden cot, so heavy that even ten men could not lift it. From one peg hung a dholak, from another a pair of manjira. On a shelf, a book wrapped in cloth was kept—perhaps the Ramayana. Both daughters-in-law sat in front, patting cow dung cakes, while Jhunia stood at the threshold. Her eyes were red, and the tip of her nose was flushed. It seemed she had just finished crying. In her full, healthy, well-shaped limbs, youth seemed to surge like a wave. Her face was broad and round, cheeks plump, eyes small and deep-set, forehead narrow, but the swell of her chest and the softness of her body drew the eye. The printed pink sari she wore made her look even more radiant.

As soon as Bhola appeared, she rushed to take the basket off his head. Bhola helped Gobar and Hori with their baskets, then said to Jhunia, “First, fill a chilam, then make a little sugarcane juice. If there’s no water, bring the gagra, I’ll draw it myself. You know Hori Mahto, don’t you?”

Then he turned to Hori and said, “A house cannot stand without a mistress, brother! There’s an old saying—‘A short bullock cannot plough, nor can daughters-in-law manage a home.’ What farming will stunted oxen do, and what home will daughters-in-law keep? Since her mother died, it’s as if the house’s blessing has vanished. The daughters-in-law knead the dough, but what do they know of running a household? Oh, they know well enough how to wag their tongues. The boys must be loafing at some stall. All of them are lazy, shirkers. While I live, I chase after them. When I die, they’ll sit with their hands on their heads and weep. The girl is the same. Even if she does a little work, she grumbles. I bear it, but will a husband bear it?”

Jhunia came quickly, a filled chilam in one hand, a pot of juice in the other. Then, taking a rope and a pitcher, she went to fetch water. Gobar, reaching out shyly to take the pitcher from her, said, “Let me, I’ll fetch it.”

Jhunia did not give up the pitcher. Going to the well’s edge, she smiled and said, “You are our guest. You’ll say, ‘Not even a pot of water was offered to me.’”

“How am I a guest? I’m your neighbor, after all.”

“If a neighbor doesn’t show his face even once a year, he’s a guest all the same.”

“If I came every day, there’d be no respect left.”

Jhunia laughed, glancing at him from the corners of her eyes. “That’s the respect I’m giving you. Come once a month, I’ll give you cool water. Come every fifteen days, you’ll get a chilam. Come every seven days, I’ll let you sit on the bench. Come every day, you’ll get nothing.”

“At least you’ll let me see you?”

“For a glimpse, you’ll have to perform a puja.”

As she said this, it was as if some forgotten memory returned to her. Her face grew sad.

--- Page 26 ---
At first, there was a protector at the threshold of her womanhood—her husband sat as her guardian. She was carefree then.  
Now, there was no one to guard that threshold, and so she always kept it tightly shut. Sometimes, weary of the monotony of the household, she would open the door, but at the sight of anyone approaching, she would be frightened and hastily bolt both panels.

Gobur filled the pitcher and brought it out. Everyone drank the sweet sap, and after sharing a hookah of tobacco, they prepared to leave.  
Bhola said, “Come tomorrow and take the cow, Gobur. Right now, she is eating her fodder.”  
Gobur’s eyes remained fixed on the cow, and inwardly, he was growing more and more enchanted. He had never imagined the cow would be so beautiful and well-formed.  
Hori, restraining his greed, said, “I’ll send for her. What’s the hurry?”  
“You may not be in a hurry, but I am,” Bhola replied. “When you see her at your door, you’ll remember my words.”  
“I’m very concerned about her, Dada.”  
“Then send Gobur tomorrow.”  
Both men placed their baskets on their heads and moved on. They were so happy, it was as if they were returning from a wedding. Hori was elated at the fulfillment of his lifelong desire—and that too, without spending any money.  
Gobur had received something even more precious: a longing had awakened in his heart.  
Seizing the moment, he glanced back. Jhunia was standing at the doorway, restless and eager, like hope intoxicated.

Hori could not sleep all night. Lying on his bamboo cot beneath the neem tree, he gazed again and again at the stars. He would have to set up a manger for the cow. It would be better if her manger was separate from the oxen’s. For now, she would have to stay outside at night, but during the monsoon, he would have to find another place for her. People cast the evil eye when animals are kept outside. Sometimes, they perform such witchcraft that the cow’s milk dries up—she won’t even let you touch her udder, and she kicks. No, it’s not right to keep her outside. And who would allow me to set up a manger outside anyway? The estate manager would pout about the evil eye. It wouldn’t be proper to trouble the Rai Sahib with every little complaint. And who listens to me in front of the manager? I’ll tie her inside. The courtyard is small, but a little shed will do. This is just the beginning. She won’t give less than five seers of milk. Gobur alone will need a seer. Rupa always eyes the milk greedily. Now she can drink as much as she wants. Sometimes, I’ll take two or four seers to the landlords. I’ll have to keep the manager happy too. And Bhola’s money should be repaid. Why drag him into the farce of an engagement? A man who trusts me so much—would it be right to betray him? He gave me an eighty-rupee cow on trust alone; here, no one trusts you with a single paisa. What will I get from the harvest? Even if I give Bhola twenty-five rupees, he’ll feel reassured. I shouldn’t have told Dhaniya. If I had quietly brought the cow home, she would have been bewildered. She would have kept asking, “Whose cow is this? Where did you get her from?” I would have teased her a lot before telling, but how can I keep anything in? Even when a few extra coins come my way, I can’t hide them. And it’s just as well—she worries about the household. If she ever found out I kept money, she’d start making demands. Gobur is lazy. No, I would serve the cow as she deserves. There’s no such thing as laziness. I’d start cutting fodder from nightfall. If anyone woke me up, I’d get angry and run away from home.

--- Page 27 ---
When will the boys experience even a little happiness in life, if not in front of their own parents? And when life’s burdens fall upon their heads, what happiness will they find then? Didn’t I take charge of the house as soon as my father died? The whole village used to say that Hori would ruin the household, but the moment the weight fell on my shoulders, I changed so completely that people could only watch in amazement. Sobha and Heera separated; otherwise, the fate of this house would have been very different today. Three ploughs would have worked together. Now, each one works alone. It is all the play of time. What fault was it of Dhaniya’s? Poor woman, from the day she entered this house, she never sat in peace. The moment she stepped down from the palanquin, she took all the household work upon herself. She would twist the winnowing fan as easily as a betel leaf. She gave herself up for the sake of the family, and if she asked her sisters-in-law to help with the work, what wrong did she do? After all, she too deserved some rest, but if rest were written in her fate, she would have had it. Back then, she lived for her brothers-in-law; now, she lives for her own children. If she had not been so simple, so forbearing, so pure-hearted, Sobha and Heera, who now twirl their moustaches with pride, would be begging in the streets. How selfish a man becomes! The very people you fight for become your enemies.

Hori looked again towards the east. Perhaps dawn was breaking. Why was Gobar waking up? No, he had said he would leave while it was still dark. He thought of going and fixing the manger, but no, until the cow was at the door, it was not right to fix the manger. What if Bhola changed his mind, or for some other reason did not give the cow? Then the whole village would clap their hands in mockery—went to get a cow, did he! The way Patta fixed the manger so quickly, it was as if that was all that was needed. Bhola may be the master of his house, but once the sons have grown up, what say does the father have? If Kamta and Jangi put their foot down, would Bhola give me the cow of his own will? Never.

Suddenly, Gobar sat up with a start, rubbing his eyes, and said, “Oh! It’s already dawn. Did you fix the manger, Dada?”

Hori looked with pride at Gobar’s sturdy body and broad chest, thinking to himself how strong he would have been if he had enough milk to drink, and replied, “No, not yet. I thought, what if we don’t get the cow? It would be embarrassing for nothing.”

Gobar frowned and said, “Why wouldn’t we get it?”

“What if they have a change of heart?”

“Change of heart or even if they turn into dacoits, they’ll have to give us the cow.”

Gobar said no more. He slung his stick over his shoulder and set off. Hori watched him go, feeling his heart cool. Now there should be no delay in arranging the boy’s engagement. He’s in his seventeenth year, but how to proceed? There’s not even a glimpse of money. Ever since the three brothers separated, the house’s reputation has gone. The Mahto families come to see the boy, but when they see the state of the house, they leave with a sour face. A couple agreed, but then they demanded money. Two or three hundred rupees for the bride price, and just as much again for the wedding expenses—only then would the marriage happen. Where would so much money come from? The harvest is measured in the threshing floor, but there’s not enough even to eat. How can there be a wedding? And now Sona is of marriageable age. If the boy’s marriage is delayed, so be it. But if the girl’s marriage is delayed, the whole community will laugh. First, her engagement must be arranged; the rest can be seen later.

A man came and greeted him with “Ram-Ram,” and asked, “Mahato, do you have any bamboos in your shed?”

Hori looked and saw Damri Bansor standing there—short, dark, very stout, a broad face, thick moustaches, red eyes, a bamboo-cutting knife tucked into his waistband. Once or twice a year, he would come and cut a few bamboos to make winnowing fans, stools, baskets, and other things.

--- Page 28 ---
Hori was pleased. He now had some hope of warming his palm. He took the Chaudhary along, showed him all three of his bamboo groves, bargained with him, and secured an advance for fifty bamboos at the rate of twenty-five rupees per hundred. Then the two returned. Hori offered him a chillum, served him some refreshments, and then, with a mysterious air, said, “My bamboos never go for less than thirty rupees, but you are like family—how could I bargain with you? That boy of yours, the one who was engaged—has he returned from Pardes yet?”

The Chaudhary took a pull from the chillum, coughed, and replied, “Mahato, I nearly lost my life over that boy! The young bride was sitting at home, and he went off to Pardes to enjoy himself with another woman from the biradari. The daughter-in-law ran off with someone else as well. The Mahato caste is a sorry lot—never loyal to anyone. I tried to explain to him: eat what you want, wear what you want, but don’t bring shame to my name. But who listens? God gives a woman everything, but if He doesn’t give her beauty, she can’t be controlled. The groves must have been divided by now?”

Hori looked up at the sky, as if soaring in its vastness, and said, “Everything is divided, Chaudhary! The ones I raised like my own sons are now equal partners. But I have no intention of eating into my brother’s share. As soon as I get the money from you, I’ll divide it between my two brothers. Why should I deceive anyone in this short life? I could just say I sold them for twenty rupees a hundred, and who would know? It’s not as if you’d go and tell them. I’ve always considered you my own brother.”

However much we may misuse the word ‘brother’ in our dealings, the sanctity of its sentiment is never tainted by our own darkness.

Having made this indirect proposal, Hori looked at the Chaudhary’s face to see if he accepted. On the Chaudhary’s face appeared that same false humility one sees on stout beggars when they ask for alms.

The Chaudhary, settling himself comfortably, cracked his whip: “Mahato, we’ve had a brotherhood for years—how could there be any such thing between us? But the truth is, a man only sells his honesty for some temptation. Not twenty rupees, I’d say fifteen, but you can tell them you got twenty.”

Hori, embarrassed, said, “Chaudhary, you’re being unfair. Where do bamboos go for twenty rupees a hundred?”

“Not just these—better bamboos go for ten rupees a hundred. Yes, if you go ten kos further west. The price isn’t for the bamboo, it’s for being close to the town. People think, the time it would take to go there, I could earn two or four rupees here.”

The deal was struck. The Chaudhary took off his mirzai, placed it on the eaves, and began cutting bamboos.

The sugarcane was being irrigated. Heera’s wife was taking breakfast to the well. Seeing the Chaudhary cutting bamboos, she called out from behind her veil, “Who’s cutting bamboos? No bamboos will be cut here.”

The Chaudhary paused and said, “I’ve bought the bamboos—paid an advance at fifteen rupees a hundred. I’m not cutting them for free.”

Heera’s wife was the mistress of her house. It was her rebellion that had caused the brothers to separate. She had defeated Dhania and become a lioness. Sometimes Heera would beat her. Only recently he had beaten her so badly she couldn’t get up from her cot for days, but she would never relinquish her authority. Heera, in his anger, would strike her, but in truth, he danced to her tune, like a horse that sometimes kicks its master but still walks under his hand.

She set down the basket of breakfast from her head and said, “Our bamboos won’t go for fifteen rupees.”

--- Page 29 ---
Chaudhary considered it improper to discuss such matters with women. He said, “Go and send your husband. Whatever needs to be said, let him come and say it.”

Heera’s wife was named Punia. She had only borne two children, yet she was already worn down. She tried to mask the passage of time with adornments, but when there was barely enough to eat in the household, where could she find money for cosmetics? This deprivation and helplessness had dried up the waters of her nature, leaving her hard and parched, a soil where even a spade would bounce off.

Coming closer, she tried to grab Chaudhary’s hand and said, “Why should I send my husband? Whatever you have to say, say it to me. I’ve told you, my bamboos will not be cut.”

Chaudhary tried to free his hand, but Punia kept grabbing it again and again. For a minute, this tussle continued. At last, Chaudhary pushed her away forcefully. Punia stumbled and fell, but quickly regained herself, and, slipping off her wooden sandal, began to strike Chaudhary blindly on the head, face, and back. How dare he push her, a woman, in such a way? Such an insult! She kept beating him and weeping at the same time. Chaudhary, having already been caught out once for using force against a woman, had no remedy but to stand there and take the blows.

Hearing Punia’s cries, Hori came running. Seeing him, Punia began to wail even louder. Hori thought Chaudhary had beaten Punia. His blood boiled, and breaking through the bonds of kinship, he rushed forward to confront everything. He landed a strong kick on Chaudhary and said, “If you value your life, Chaudhary, leave this place at once, or your corpse will be carried out of here. Who do you think you are? How dare you raise your hand against my daughter-in-law!”

Chaudhary began swearing oaths to prove his innocence. Under the blows of the sandal, his guilty soul was silent. That kick he had received without deserving it, and his swollen cheeks were wet with tears. He hadn’t even touched the woman. Was he so uncouth as to lay hands on the women of the Mahto household?

Hori, disbelieving, said, “Don’t try to throw dust in my eyes, Chaudhary. If you hadn’t said or done anything, would my daughter-in-law be crying for nothing? If it’s the heat of money, then that will be paid. So what if we live separately? We are still of the same blood. If anyone so much as looks at her with a crooked eye, I’ll gouge out their eye.”

Punia, transformed into a fury, shouted at the top of her lungs, “Didn’t you push me down? Swear on your own son if you dare!”

Heera too heard that Chaudhary and Punia were fighting. Chaudhary had pushed Punia, and Punia had beaten him with her sandal. He left his plough right there and, picking up his staff, hurried to the scene. He was known in the village for his temper. Short in stature, with a sturdy build, his eyes bulged like cowries, and the veins in his neck stood out. But his anger was not at Chaudhary—it was at Punia. Why had she fought with Chaudhary? Why had she dragged their honor through the mud? What need had she to quarrel with outsiders? She should have come and told Heera everything. Whatever Heera thought best, he would have done. Why did she go to fight with him? If he had his way, he would have kept Punia behind the veil. For her to speak out so boldly to a man was unbearable to him. As unruly as he himself was, he wanted Punia to be just as quiet. When his elder brother had already made a deal for fifteen rupees, who was she to interfere?

As soon as he arrived, he grabbed Punia’s hand, dragged her aside, and began to kick her. “Wretch! Are you bent on disgracing us? You go around fighting with petty men—whose honor do you think you’re lowering, tell me?” (He landed another kick.) “We’re sitting there waiting for our meal—”

--- Page 30 ---
You’re sitting here, picking a fight. Such shamelessness? Have you lost all sense of shame? I’ll dig a pit and bury you alive.

Punni kept wailing and cursing as she went—May you be ruined, may you get cholera, may death come for you, may the Mother Goddess swallow you whole, may you catch influenza. May God make you a leper. May your hands and feet rot and fall off.

Heera stood listening to all her curses, but this last one struck him. Cholera, death—those he didn’t mind much. You fall ill, and you’re gone. But leprosy! That hideous death, and an even more hideous life. He flared up, and, grinding his teeth, lunged at Punni, grabbed her hair, and rubbed her head against the ground again, shouting—If my hands and feet rot and fall off, will you lick my wounds? Will you feed my children? Will you run this big household? You’ll just find another husband and stand aside.

Chaudhary felt pity for Punni’s plight. He began to reason with Heera, kindly—Heera Mahto, let it go now, enough is enough. So what if your wife hit me? I haven’t become any less for it. Blessed am I that God let me see this day.

Heera snapped at Chaudhary—You keep quiet, Chaudhary, or you’ll get caught up in my anger, and that won’t end well. Womenfolk always talk like this. Today she fought with you, tomorrow she’ll fight with someone else. You’re a good man, you laughed it off, but someone else might not be so forgiving. If he too raises his hand, then how much honor will be left, tell me.

This thought stoked his anger again. He lunged, but Hori ran up and grabbed him, pulling him back—Enough, it’s over. The whole world has seen what a brave man you are. What now, will you grind her to dust and swallow her?

Heera still respected his elder brother. He wouldn’t fight him directly. If he wanted, he could have freed his hand in a single jerk, but he couldn’t bring himself to be so disrespectful. Looking at Chaudhary, he said—Why are you still standing there? Go cut your bamboo, I’ve settled things. Fifteen rupees per hundred, as agreed.

A moment ago, Punni was weeping. Now she sprang up, beat her head, and cried—Go ahead, set the house on fire, what do I care? My luck’s so rotten, I ended up with a butcher like you. Go on, burn the house down.

She left the basket of food right there and started toward the house. Heera thundered—Where do you think you’re going? Come to the well, or I’ll drink your blood.

Punni’s feet stopped. She didn’t want to play out the second act of this drama. Quietly, she picked up the basket and, weeping, walked toward the well. Heera followed behind.

Hori said—Now don’t start beating her again. That only makes a woman shameless.

Dhaniya came to the door and called out—What are you doing, standing there watching the spectacle? Does anyone even listen to you, or do you just give lectures for nothing? The other day, this very daughter-in-law called you “beardless” from behind her veil, have you forgotten? If a daughter-in-law starts fighting with strange men, shouldn’t she be scolded?

Hori came to the door, teasing—And what if I start beating you the same way?

“Haven’t you ever beaten me, that you’re itching to do it again?”

“If I’d beaten you so mercilessly, you’d have run away from home! Punni is made of sterner stuff.”

“Oh! So you’re the tender-hearted one, are you? The marks from your beatings are still there. Heera beats, but he also caresses. You only learned how to beat, never how to show affection. I’m the only one who could have married you.”

“All right, enough, don’t praise yourself so much. You were the one who used to sulk and run off to your father’s house. When for months—”

--- Page 31 ---
He would only come when you flattered him!  
He only came to appease me when he needed something, Lala. Not out of any affection for me.  
That’s why I praise you above all others.  

In the dawn of married life, desire rises with its rosy intoxication and bathes the entire sky of the heart in the golden rays of its sweetness. Then comes the scorching heat of midday, clouds gather moment by moment, and the earth begins to tremble. The golden veil of longing is lifted, and reality stands before us in its naked form. After that arrives the restful evening, cool and calm, when, like weary travelers, we recount the day’s journey—listening and telling, with detachment, as if we have climbed to some lofty peak where the clamor of the world below cannot reach us.

With a twinkle in her eyes, Dhaniya said, “Come, come, great man of praise! Let something go wrong, and you’re quick to jump down my throat.”

Hori replied with gentle reproach, “Now, Dhaniya, this injustice of yours I cannot bear. Ask Bhola what I said to him about you.”

Dhaniya changed the subject. “Look, is Gobar bringing the cow or coming back empty-handed?”

Chaudhary arrived, drenched in sweat, and said, “Mahto, come count the bamboos. Tomorrow I’ll bring a cart and take them away.”

Hori saw no need to count the bamboos. Chaudhary was not that kind of man. And even if he cut one or two extra, what of it? Every day people cut bamboos on loan. During wedding seasons, people take away dozens to build mandaps.

Chaudhary took out seven and a half rupees and placed them in Hori’s hand. Hori counted and said, “And the rest? By my reckoning, there’s two and a half left.”

Chaudhary replied indifferently, “Didn’t we agree on fifteen rupees? Hira Mahto said fifteen right in front of you. Shall I call him?”

“The deal was for twenty, Chaudhary! But you win, say what you will. Two and a half are left, but if you give me even two, I’ll take it.”

But Chaudhary was no novice at such games. What did he have to fear now? Hori’s mouth was sealed shut. What could he say? He could only bow his head in defeat. He managed to say, “This isn’t right, Chaudhary. You won’t become a king by pocketing two rupees.”

Chaudhary retorted sharply, “And what about you? Trying to become a king by squeezing a few rupees from your brothers? You were ready to sell your honor for two and a half rupees, and now you preach to me! If I reveal everything, you’ll be ashamed.”

It was as if Hori had been struck by a hundred shoes. Chaudhary left the money on the ground and walked away, but Hori sat under the neem tree for a long time, lost in regret. Only now did he realize how greedy and selfish he was. If Chaudhary had given him the two and a half rupees, how proud he would have felt, congratulating himself on his cleverness for earning money while sitting idle. It is only after stumbling that we learn to walk with care.

Dhaniya had gone inside. When she came out and saw the money lying on the ground, she counted it and said, “Where’s the rest? Shouldn’t there be ten?”

Hori replied with a long face, “Hira gave them for fifteen. What could I do?”

--- Page 32 ---
"Heera, give it for five rupees."  
"I will not sell at that price."  
"There was a scuffle going on there. How could I speak up in the middle of it?"  

Hori swallowed his defeat in silence, as if a man had climbed a tree to steal firewood and, falling down, dusted himself off quietly so that no one would see. When you win, you may boast of your cunning; in victory, all is forgiven. But the shame of defeat is something to be swallowed alone.

Dhaniya began to scold her husband. Such chances came to her rarely. Hori was the cleverer of the two, but today the game was in Dhaniya’s hands. Waving her arms, she said, "Why not? When your brother demanded fifteen rupees, how could you object? Oh, Ram-Ram! Wouldn’t your dear brother’s heart have broken if you had? And when such a great calamity was happening, that your beloved sister-in-law’s neck was at the knife’s edge, how could you possibly speak up? Even if someone had robbed you of everything at that moment, you would not have noticed."

Hori listened in silence, not even grunting in reply. He felt irritation, anger, his blood boiled, his eyes burned, his teeth ground together, but he said nothing. Quietly, he picked up his hoe and set off to weed the sugarcane.

Dhaniya snatched the hoe from him and said, "Is it still morning, that you’re off to the fields? The sun is already overhead. Go and bathe. Food is ready."

Hori muttered, "I’m not hungry."

Dhaniya sprinkled salt on his wounds, "Of course, why would you be hungry? Your brother must have fed you plenty of sweets! May God grant everyone such dutiful sons."

Hori lost his temper. His anger was now straining at its leash. "You’re just asking for a beating today."

Dhaniya, feigning humility, replied, "What can I do? You spoil me so much, I’ve lost my senses."

"Will you let me stay in this house or not?"

"This is your house, you are the master. Who am I to throw you out?"

Today, Hori could not get the better of Dhaniya. His wits seemed to have deserted him. He had no shield against these barbed words. He quietly put down the hoe, picked up his towel, and went to bathe. He returned after half an hour, but Gobar had still not come home. How could he eat alone? The boy must be sleeping over there. That wild girl of Bhola’s, Jhunia, is there. He must be joking and laughing with her. Even yesterday, he was following her around. If he hasn’t brought the cow, why hasn’t he come back? What is he doing there?

Dhaniya said, "Why are you just standing there? Gobar will come back in the evening!"

Hori said nothing more, fearing Dhaniya might start again.

He ate his meal and lay down in the shade of the neem tree.

Rupa came to him crying, naked except for a loincloth, her tangled hair falling about her face. She flung herself onto Hori’s chest. Her elder sister, Sona, says, "When the cow comes, I will make the cow dung cakes." Rupa cannot tolerate this. Who is Sona to claim all the dung for herself? Is Rupa any less than her in anything? Sona cooks the bread, but does Rupa not wash the dishes? Sona fetches water, but does Rupa not carry the rope to the well? Sona goes off to iron clothes, but it is Rupa who coils up the rope. When both of them make dung cakes together, does Rupa not graze the goats? Then why should Sona alone have the right to the cow dung?

--- Page 33 ---
Hori, enchanted by her innocence, said, “No, you will be the one to pat the cow dung cakes. If Sona goes near the cow, chase her away.”

Rupa put her arms around her father’s neck and said, “I will also be the one to milk her.”

“Yes, yes, who else would milk her if not you?”

“She will be my cow.”

“Yes, she will be yours, through and through.”

Delighted by her victory, Rupa ran off to give the good news to the vanquished Sona. “The cow will be mine. I will milk her, I will pat her dung, and you will get nothing.”

Sona, a maiden in age, a young woman in form, and a child in intellect, seemed as if her youth pulled her forward while her childhood tugged her back. In some matters, she was so clever that she could teach graduate girls, and in others, so naïve that she lagged behind even infants. Her face was long and rough, yet cheerful, her chin drawn downward, a kind of contentment in her eyes. There was no oil in her hair, no kohl in her eyes, no ornament on her body, as if the burdens of household life had pressed her youth down and stunted it.

Tossing her head, she said, “Go on, you pat the dung. When you have milked the cow and set the pot aside, I’ll drink it all.”

“I will lock the pot of milk away.”

“I’ll break the lock and take the milk out.”

Saying this, she walked off toward the orchard. The mangoes had begun to ripen. Now and then, a gust of wind would bring one tumbling to the ground—shriveled and yellow from the hot winds, but the village children still hovered around the orchard, hoping to catch the fallen fruit. Rupa followed her sister. Whatever Sona did, Rupa was sure to do as well. Talks of Sona’s marriage were underway, but no one spoke of Rupa’s. That is why she herself insisted on her own marriage. She would describe in detail what her groom would be like, what he would bring, how he would keep her, what he would feed her, what he would clothe her in—so elaborately that no boy, upon hearing it, would ever agree to marry her.

Evening was falling. Hori was so weary that he could not go to weed the sugarcane. He tied the oxen to the trough, gave them fodder and oil-cake, and sat down to smoke a chilam. Even after selling everything at the threshing floor this harvest, he still owed about three hundred rupees, with another hundred in interest piling up. Five years ago, he had borrowed sixty rupees from Mangru Sah for an ox; he had already paid back sixty, but the original sixty remained as it was. He had borrowed thirty rupees from Pandit Datadin to plant potatoes. Thieves had dug up the potatoes, and in three years, that thirty had become a hundred. Dulari, the widow sahuain who ran the village shop for salt, oil, and tobacco, had lent him forty rupees at the time of the family partition, which he had to give to his brothers. That too had grown to nearly a hundred, as the interest was eight annas per rupee. He still owed twenty-five rupees in land revenue, and he had to arrange for the shagun money by Dussehra. The money from the sale of bamboo had come at just the right time. The shagun problem would be solved, but who knew? Here, if a single coin came into your hand, the whole village would know, and creditors would swoop in from all sides. These five rupees he would give for shagun, come what may, but two great burdens still weighed on his life: the marriages of Gobar and Sona. No matter how much he tried to economize, the expenses would not be less than three hundred rupees. Where would those three hundred come from? How he wished he could borrow not a single paisa from anyone, and pay back every last pie he owed! But no matter how much hardship he endured, he could not break free. The interest would keep mounting, and one day, his house and land would all be auctioned off, and his...

--- Page 34 ---
The children, left without support, would wander about begging. Whenever Hori finished his day’s labor and sat down to smoke his chilam, this worry would close in on him like a black wall from all sides, and he could see no way out. If there was any consolation, it was only that this calamity was not his alone. Nearly all the peasants were in the same plight. Most were even worse off. It had only been three years since Shobha and Heera separated from him, but both were already burdened with debts of four hundred rupees each. Jhingur farms two ploughs’ worth of land, yet owes a little more than a thousand. At Jiawan Mahto’s house, even beggars cannot find alms, but there is no certainty in anything. Who here has been spared?

Suddenly, Sona and Rupa came running and shouted together, “Bhaiya is bringing a cow! The cow is in front, and Bhaiya is right behind her!”

Rupa had seen Govar approaching first. She deserved the honor of bringing this news. Sona, however, could not bear to let her share in the glory. How could she tolerate Sona becoming her equal?

Rupa stepped forward and said, “I saw them first, that’s why I ran. Didi only saw them from behind.”

Sona could not accept this claim. She retorted, “How could you recognize Bhaiya? You said a cow was running this way. I was the one who said it was Bhaiya.”

The two of them ran off again toward the field to welcome the cow.

Dhaniya and Hori began making arrangements to tie up the cow. Hori said, “Come on, let’s quickly set up the manger.”

A youthful glow lit up Dhaniya’s face. “No, first let’s mix a little flour and jaggery in a plate for her. The poor thing must have walked in the sun. She must be thirsty. You go set up the manger, I’ll prepare the mixture.”

“There was a bell lying around somewhere. Find it, we’ll tie it around her neck.”

“Where’s Sona? Go to the sahuain’s shop and get a little black thread. Cows attract the evil eye easily.”

“Today, my heart’s deepest longing has been fulfilled.”

Dhaniya wanted to keep her joy in check. She feared that such a great fortune might bring some new misfortune with it, and this anxiety sent a tremor through her weary heart. Looking up at the sky, she said, “The true joy of the cow’s arrival will be if her calf is healthy too. It is all in God’s hands.”

It was as if she wanted to deceive even God, to show Him that the cow’s arrival had not brought her so much happiness that a jealous God might tip the scales and send some new calamity.

She was still mixing the flour when Govar arrived at the door with the cow, followed by a procession of children. Hori ran and threw his arms around the cow’s neck. Dhaniya dropped the flour and quickly tore a black border from an old sari and tied it around the cow’s neck.

Hori gazed at the cow with reverent, tear-filled eyes, as if the goddess herself had entered his home. Today, God had granted him this day, when his house was sanctified by the cow’s footsteps. Such good fortune! Who knows by whose merit it had come?

Dhaniya said anxiously, “What are you standing there for? Set up the manger in the courtyard.”

“There’s no space in the courtyard.”

“There’s plenty of space.”

“I’ll set it up outside.”

“Don’t act mad. You know the state of the village, yet you pretend ignorance.”

“Arre! Where will a cow fit in a courtyard barely a few feet wide, brother?”

“If you don’t know something, don’t meddle in it. You haven’t studied all the world’s knowledge alone.”

Hori truly was not himself. For him, the cow was not merely an object of devotion and reverence, but a living wealth. He wished to increase the beauty of his doorway and the honor of his home through her. He wanted people, seeing the cow tied at his gate, to ask—whose house is this? And for people to reply—Hori Mahto’s. Only then would the families of prospective brides be impressed by his prosperity. If the cow were tied in the inner courtyard, who would see her? Dhania, on the other hand, was wary. She wanted to keep the cow hidden behind seven veils. If the cow could have stayed in the room all day and night, perhaps she would never have let her out. Usually, Hori prevailed in every matter. He would stubbornly stick to his side, and Dhania would have to yield. But today, Hori could not have his way before Dhania. Dhania was ready to fight. Gobar, Sona, and Rupa—the whole household—were on Hori’s side, but Dhania alone defeated them all. Today, a strange self-assurance had arisen in her, and in Hori, a peculiar humility.

But how could the spectacle be stopped? The cow had not arrived in a palanquin. How could such a great event occur in the village without drawing a crowd? Whoever heard, left their work and came running to see. This was no ordinary desi cow. She had come from Bhola’s house for eighty rupees. What would Hori pay eighty rupees—he must have brought her for fifty or sixty. Even the arrival of a fifty or sixty rupee cow was an unprecedented event in the village’s history. Bulls had come for fifty rupees, even a hundred, but what farmer could afford to spend such a sum on a cow? Only the hearts of the cowherds could bear to count out handfuls of rupees. What is a cow, after all, but the very form of the goddess herself? Spectators and critics thronged, and Hori ran about, welcoming everyone. Never before had he been so humble, so cheerful.

Seventy-year-old Pandit Datadin came, leaning on his staff, and said with his toothless mouth, “Where are you, Hori? Let me see your cow too. I’ve heard she’s very beautiful.”

Hori ran to touch his feet and, inwardly savoring a proud delight, led Panditji to the courtyard with great respect. The Maharaj examined the cow with his old, experienced eyes—looked at her horns, her udder, her dewlap—and, filling his eyes hidden under thick white brows with the vigor of youth, said, “There’s no flaw, son. Hair, whorl, all perfect. If God wills, your fortunes will open. Such good signs—wonderful! Just don’t let her milk decrease. Each calf will be worth a hundred.”

Hori, diving in a sea of joy, replied, “It’s all your blessing, Dada.”

Datadin spat out a stream of tobacco and said, “Not my blessing, son, it’s God’s mercy. All is the Lord’s grace. Did you pay cash?”

Hori brushed the question aside. How could he miss such a chance to display his prosperity before his mahajan? When we put a new cap on our head, we start strutting; when we ride for even a moment, we feel we’re flying in the sky—so why shouldn’t his head be in the clouds, having gained such wealth? He said, “Bhola is not that kind of man, Maharaj! Paid cash, counted every paisa.”

Boasting thus before his mahajan, Hori had acted foolishly, but no trace of displeasure appeared on Datadin’s face. How much truth there was in this claim could not be hidden from those old eyes, in which experience had taken the place of light.

Pleased, he said, “No matter, son, no matter. God will see to all your welfare.”

Dhaniya immediately interjected—“Oh no, Maharaj, not that much! She’s grown old now. And where is there any night’s yield left here?”

Datadeen looked at her with eyes full of understanding, accepting her vigilance, as if to say, “This is the true dharma of a housewife; haggling is men’s work—let them haggle.” Then, in a voice thick with secrecy, he said, “Don’t tie her outside, I’m telling you now.”

Dhaniya looked at her husband with triumphant eyes, as if to say—So, will you believe me now?

She said to Datadeen—“No, Maharaj, why would we tie her outside? If God wills, three more could be tied in this very courtyard.”

The whole village came to see the cow. The only ones who didn’t come were Sobha and Heera, Hori’s own brothers. Hori’s heart still held a tender place for his brothers. If only they had come to see, and left pleased, his heart’s desire would have been fulfilled. Evening fell. Both brothers returned from the fields. They passed by this very doorway, but said nothing.

Hori said to Dhaniya, with a trace of fear, “Neither Sobha came, nor Heera. Perhaps they haven’t heard?”

Dhaniya replied, “And who here is going to call them?”

“You don’t understand, you’re always ready for a quarrel. When God has shown us this day, we should bow our heads and walk humbly. A man longs to hear his praise or blame from the mouths of his own kin, not from outsiders. No matter how bad one’s brothers may be, they are still brothers. Everyone fights for their share, but that doesn’t split the blood between them. We should call them both and show them. Otherwise, they’ll say, ‘He brought a cow and didn’t even tell us.’”

Dhaniya wrinkled her nose and said, “I’ve told you a hundred times, a thousand times, don’t praise your brothers in front of me. Just hearing their names sets my body aflame. The whole village has heard—do you think they haven’t? It’s not as if they live so far away. The whole village came to see, but their feet were dyed with henna—they didn’t come. How could they? They must be burning with jealousy that a cow has come to your house. Their hearts must be bursting.”

It was time for the evening lamps. Dhaniya went to check and found there was no kerosene in the bottle. She picked up the bottle and went off to get some oil. If there had been money, she would have sent Rupa. But since it had to be borrowed, she would have to say a few words, flatter a little, only then would she get oil on credit.

Hori called Rupa over, sat her lovingly in his lap, and said, “Go and see if Heera Kaka has come home. And check on Sobha Kaka too. Tell them, Dada has called you. If they don’t come, grab their hands and drag them here.”

Rupa pouted and said, “Chhoti Kaki scolds me.”

“What will you do at Kaki’s place? And Sobha’s wife loves you.”

“Sobha Kaka teases me... I won’t go.”

“What does he say, tell me?”

“He teases me.”

“He says, ‘I’ve caught a mouse for you. Take it, roast it, and eat it.’”

A tickle of laughter stirred inside Hori.

“You should tell him, ‘You eat it first, then I’ll eat it.’”

“Amma scolds me. She says, ‘Don’t go to their house.’”

“Are you your mother’s daughter or your father’s?”  

Roopa put her arms around his neck and said, “Mother’s,” and began to laugh.

“Then get down from my lap. Today I won’t let you eat from my plate.”

There was only one brass plate in the house, and Hori ate from it. Roopa would eat with Hori to share in the honor of eating from that plate. How could she give up such a privilege? She protested eagerly, “Alright, yours.”

“Then will you listen to me, or to Amma?”

“To you.”

“Then go and fetch Heera and Soma.”

“And what if Amma gets angry?”

“Who’s going to tell Amma?”

Roopa, delighted, ran off to Heera’s house. The web of the world ensnares only the big fish; the little ones either never get caught or slip away at once. For them, this deadly net is a plaything, not a terror. Hori no longer spoke to his brothers, but Roopa went freely between both houses. What quarrel could there be with children?

But Roopa had barely stepped out when Dhaniya, carrying oil, met her. She asked, “Where are you going at this hour? Come home.” Roopa could not resist the temptation to please her mother.

Dhaniya scolded, “Come home. You’re not to go calling anyone.”

Holding Roopa’s hand, she returned home and said to Hori, “I’ve told you a thousand times, don’t send my daughter to anyone’s house. If something happens, will I have to answer for it with you? If you love them so much, why don’t you go yourself? Haven’t you had enough yet?”

Hori was plastering the cattle trough. With mud on his hands, feigning ignorance, he replied, “Why are you angry, woman? It doesn’t look good to bark at the wind like a blind dog.”

Dhaniya had to pour oil into the lamp and did not wish to prolong the quarrel. Roopa, meanwhile, slipped away to join the other children.

It was well past the first watch of the night. The trough had been set. Fodder and oilcake had been placed in it. The cow sat forlorn, as if a bride newly arrived in her in-laws’ house—she would not even put her mouth to the trough. Hori and Gobar brought her half their own rotis, but she did not even sniff at them. But this was nothing new; animals too often grieve for their lost homes.

Hori sat outside on the cot, smoking his chilam, and his thoughts turned again to his brothers. No, on this auspicious occasion, he could not ignore them. His heart had grown generous with this new blessing. So what if he was separated from his brothers? He was not their enemy. If this cow had come three years earlier, all would have had equal claim to her. And when she gives milk tomorrow, would he not send milk or curds to his brothers’ homes? That would not be his dharma. Why should he harbor ill-will because they do? Each man’s deeds are his own.

He set the coconut down by the leg of the cot and walked toward Heera’s house. Sobha’s house was nearby as well. Both brothers lay on their cots at their doors. It was quite dark. Neither noticed Hori’s approach. They were talking together. Who can resist listening when he hears himself discussed?

She caught the bird and said, “If you’re afraid of your brothers, then go and fall at their feet. I’m not afraid of anyone. If our prosperity makes someone’s heart burst with envy, let it burst—I don’t care.”

Hori replied in a humble voice, “Speak softly, Maharani! If someone hears, they’ll say we’re quarreling at this hour of the night! You don’t know what I’ve heard with my own ears. There’s talk going around that when we separated, I hid away some money and deceived my brothers. Now that money is coming to light.”

“The whole village isn’t saying it—Heera alone is saying it. I’ll go right now and ask how much money your father left behind when he died. We ruined ourselves running after these bearded ones, wasted our whole lives, raised and cared for them, and now we’re the cheats! I’m telling you, if the cow is taken out of this house, there will be disaster. Yes, we kept the money, hid it, buried it in the middle of the field. I say it openly—I’ve hidden a potful of gold coins. Let Heera, Sobha, and the whole world do what they will. Why shouldn’t we keep the money? Didn’t we marry off two sons, didn’t we perform their gauna?”

Hori was flustered. Dhania snatched the rope from his hand, tied the cow to the post, and headed towards the door. Hori tried to stop her, but she was already outside. He sat down, holding his head in his hands. He didn’t want to put on a show by running after her outside. He knew Dhania’s anger well. When she lost her temper, she became a fury—no one could reason with her, no matter what. But Heera was just as hot-tempered. If he raised his hand, there would be catastrophe. No, Heera isn’t that foolish. How did I let this fire spread? He began to feel angry at himself. If he had kept the matter to himself, none of this would have happened.

Suddenly, Dhania’s harsh voice reached his ears. He also heard Heera’s thunderous shouts. Then Punni’s sharp, shrill words pierced him. Suddenly, he remembered Gobar. He rushed outside and looked at Gobar’s cot—Gobar wasn’t there. This was terrible. Gobar must have gone there too! Now nothing good could come of this. He was young and hot-blooded—who knew what he might do? But how could Hori go there? Heera would say, “You don’t speak yourself, but you send this witch to fight for you.” The commotion was growing louder by the minute. The whole village seemed to have woken up. It was as if a fire had broken out, and people were leaping from their beds to put it out.

For a long time, Hori sat there, restraining himself. Then he couldn’t bear it any longer. He grew angry at Dhania—why did she go and pick a fight? In one’s own home, who knows what people say? Until someone says something to your face, you should assume nothing has been said. Hori’s peasant nature shied away from conflict. Better to swallow a few bitter words than to have a quarrel. If things came to blows, there would be the police, the courts, endless pleading, dusting the floors of the courthouse, and the fields and crops would go to ruin. He had no control over Heera, but he could drag Dhania back by force if needed. At worst, she’d curse him and sulk for a day or two, but at least it wouldn’t come to the police. He went and stood at the far end of Heera’s courtyard, hidden behind the gate, like a general surveying the battlefield before entering. If his side was winning, there was no need to intervene; if they were losing, he’d jump in at once.

He saw that dozens of people had gathered there. Pandit Datadeen, Lala Pateshwari, both Thakurs—the village’s leading men—were all present. Dhania’s case was weakening. Her fierceness was turning public opinion against her. She was not skilled in strategy. In her anger, she was saying such bitter things that people’s sympathy was slipping away from her.

--- Page 40 ---
She was thundering—Why do you burn with envy when you see us? Why does your chest burst at the sight of us?  
We raised you, nurtured you, brought you up to manhood, and this is your reward? Had we not cared for you, you would be begging on the streets today. Not even the shade of a tree would have sheltered you.

To Hori, these words sounded harsher than necessary. It was his duty to raise his brothers. After all, their share of the property was in his hands. How could he not care for them? Would he have been able to show his face in the world otherwise?

Heera replied, “We know nothing of all that. In your house, we ate scraps like dogs and worked all day long. We never even knew what childhood or youth meant. Day after day, we sifted through dry cow dung. And even then, you wouldn’t give us bread without hurling ten curses first. Life became bitter, falling into the hands of a she-demon like you.”

Dhaniya grew even more furious, “Mind your tongue, or I’ll rip it out. The she-demon is your own wife. Who are you calling names, you headless fool, you crumb-eater, you ingrate!”

Datadeen intervened, “Why do you speak such harsh words, Dhaniya? A woman’s duty is to bear sorrow. He is just a simpleton; why do you stoop to his level?”

Lala Pateshwari, the patwari, supported him, “A word for a word, not abuse. You raised him in his childhood, but why do you forget that his property was in your hands?”

Dhaniya felt that everyone was conspiring to humiliate her. She prepared herself for a battle on all fronts, “Enough, Lala! Let it be. I know everyone here. I’ve lived in this village for twenty years. I know each and every vein of every person. I am the one abusing, and he is showering flowers, is that it?”

Dulari Sahuwain poured oil on the fire, “Truly, she is a foul-mouthed woman! She dares to answer men back. Only a man like Hori could live with her. Any other man would have left her in a day.”

Had Heera softened even a little at this moment, he would have won. But hearing these insults, he lost control. Emboldened by the support of others, he roared, “Get away from my door, or I’ll answer you with my shoes. I’ll grab you by the hair and drag you out. You witch! You’ve grown arrogant because of your son. Blood…”

The dice turned. Hori’s blood boiled, as if a spark had fallen into gunpowder. Stepping forward, he said, “Enough, Heera, be silent now. I can’t listen any longer. What can I say to this woman? When dust falls on my back, it is because of her. I don’t know why she cannot keep quiet.”

Now, from all sides, a shower of rebuke fell on Heera. Datadeen called him shameless, Pateshwari called him a thug, Jhinguri Singh branded him a devil, and Dulari Sahuwain called him an unworthy son. One reckless word had lightened Dhaniya’s burden; another harsh word had put Heera in a tight spot. Hori’s measured words finished whatever was left.

Heera regained his composure. The whole village had turned against him. Now, silence was his only safety. Even in his anger, he had enough sense left.

Dhaniya’s heart swelled with pain. She said to Hori, “Listen well. You would die for your brothers. These are your brothers—may no one ever have such a brother. He will beat me with his shoes. After all the food I’ve given him…”

Hori scolded her, “Then why do you keep prattling? Why don’t you go home?”

Dhaniya sat down on the ground and cried out in anguish, “Now I’ll take his shoes. Let me see his manhood. Where is Gobar? When will he be of use? You see, I am being beaten with shoes.”

--- Page 41 ---
By lamenting thus, she had roused her anger into action. She fanned the embers until they burst into flame. Hira, defeated, retreated. Punia was pulling him by the hand toward the house. Suddenly, like a lioness, Ghuniya sprang forward and shoved Hira so hard that he tumbled to the ground, and said, “Where do you think you’re going? Beat him with your shoes, beat him! Let me see your manliness.”

Hori ran over, grabbed her hand, and dragged her back toward the house.

:5:

Meanwhile, after eating, Gobar made his way to the Ahir quarter. Today, he had spoken at length with Jhunia. When he had set out with the cow, Jhunia had accompanied him halfway. How could Gobar have taken the cow alone? It was only natural for the animal to hesitate to go with a stranger. After walking some distance, Jhunia looked at Gobar with soulful eyes and said, “Why would you ever come here again?”

Until a day ago, Gobar had been a boy. All the young women in the village were either his sisters or his bhabhiya. There could be no mischief with sisters, and though the bhabhiya sometimes teased him, it was always innocent fun. In their eyes, his youth had only just begun to blossom. Until the fruit appeared, what use was it to throw stones at the flowers? With no encouragement from any quarter, his innocence clung to him like a second skin. But Jhunia’s yearning heart, made all the more restless by the bhabhiya’s jests and laughter, was drawn to that very innocence, and in the boy, too, at the slightest touch, youth awoke like a sleeping beast roused by the rustle of leaves.

With unguarded playfulness, Gobar replied, “If a beggar hopes for alms, he will stand at the giver’s door day and night.”

Jhunia retorted with a sly glance, “So, say it plainly—you’re a friend only for your own gain.”

The blood in Gobar’s veins surged. He said, “If a hungry man stretches out his hand, he should be forgiven.”

Jhunia waded into deeper waters. “But how will a beggar fill his belly unless he goes to ten doors? I don’t bother with such beggars. You find them in every street. And what does a beggar give in return? Blessings. But blessings fill no one’s stomach.”

Simple-minded Gobar could not grasp Jhunia’s meaning. Since she was a little girl, Jhunia had carried milk to customers’ houses. Even in her in-laws’ home, she had to deliver milk to customers. These days, she was the one who sold curd. She had encountered all sorts of people. Sometimes she earned a few rupees, sometimes a moment’s amusement, but this pleasure was like something borrowed—fleeting, without permanence, without surrender, without rights. She longed for a love for which she could live and die, to which she could surrender herself completely. She wanted not the fleeting glow of a firefly, but the steady light of a lamp. She was a householder’s daughter, whose womanhood had not been crushed by the flirtations of pleasure-seekers.

With a face alight with longing, Gobar said, “If a beggar can be fed his fill at one door, why should he wander from door to door?”

Jhunia looked at him with tenderness. How innocent he is, how little he understands.

--- Page 42 ---
"Where does a beggar ever get a full meal at one door? He will only get a pinchful. You will receive everything only when you give everything."

"What do I have, Jhuniya?"

"You have nothing? I believe that what you have for me, even the richest men do not possess. You need not beg from me—you can win me."

Gobur stared at her with astonished eyes.

Jhuniya continued, "And do you know what price you must pay? You will have to be mine, and remain so. If I ever see you begging before anyone else, I will drive you out of the house."

It was as if Gobur, groping in the dark, had found the very thing he sought. A strange, fearful joy thrilled through every fiber of his being. But how could this be? If he took Jhuniya in, how could he keep a kept woman in the house? There would be trouble with the biradari. The whole village would begin to caw and clamor. All would become his enemies. Amma would not even let her set foot in the house. But if she, a woman, was not afraid, why should he, a man, be afraid? At worst, people would cast him out. He would live apart. Who else in the village was like Jhuniya? How wisely she spoke! Did she not know he was unworthy of her? Yet she loved him, was willing to be his. And if the villagers cast them out, was there not another village in the world? And why should he leave the village at all? When Matadin took in a Chamarin, what could anyone do? Datadin could only gnash his teeth. Matadin at least saved his dharma. Even now, without bathing and worship, he does not put water in his mouth. He cooks his own meals both times a day, and now, he does not cook separately. Datadin and he eat together.

When Jhinguri Singh took in a Brahmani, what could anyone do? He is as respected today as ever, perhaps even more so. Once he wandered in search of work; now, with her money, he has become a mahajan. He always had the pride of a Thakur, and now the pride of a moneylender as well. But then a doubt crept in—what if Jhuniya was only teasing him? First, he must be sure of her.

He asked, "Do you say this from your heart, Jhuniya, or are you only tempting me? I am already yours, but will you truly be mine?"

"How do I know you are mine already?"

"If you wish, I can give you my life."

"Do you even understand what it means to give your life?"

"You can explain it to me."

"To give your life means to stay together, to be steadfast. Once you take my hand, you must hold it for life, no matter what the world says, even if you must leave parents, brothers, home, everything. I have seen many who promise their lives with words. Like bees, they take the nectar from the flower and fly away. Will you fly away like that too?"

In one hand, Gobur held the cow's tether. With the other, he took Jhuniya's hand. It was as if he had touched a live wire. His whole body trembled at the first touch of youth. How soft, how tender, how delicate her wrist!

Jhuniya did not withdraw her hand, as if the touch meant nothing to her. Then, after a moment, she spoke gravely, "Today you have taken my hand—remember this."

"I will remember, Jhuniya, and I will keep my promise till my dying breath."

Jhuniya smiled with disbelief. "That is what everyone says."

--- Page 43 ---
With sweet, smooth words. If there is deceit in your heart, then tell me. I will be on my guard. I do not give my heart to such people. With them, I only keep a bond of empty laughter and conversation. For years, I have been taking milk to the market. All sorts of babus, mahajans, Thakurs, lawyers, clerks, officers try to ensnare me with their charm. Some place a hand on their chest and say, “Jhunia, don’t torment me.” Some gaze at me with intoxicating, longing eyes, as if they are faint with love. Some show me money, some show me jewelry. All are ready to become my slaves, for a lifetime—even in the next life—but I know the nature of all of them. Every one of them is a bumblebee, who will take the nectar and fly away. I tempt them too. I look at them with sidelong glances, I smile. They try to make a fool of me, I make fools of them. If I were to die, not a tear would come to their eyes. If they were to die, I would say, “Good riddance, the wretch is gone.” When I give myself to someone, it will be for a lifetime—in happiness and sorrow, in wealth and hardship, I will stand by him. I am not a fickle woman, laughing and talking with everyone. I am not hungry for money, nor for jewelry or fine clothes. I only want the company of a good man, who considers me his own, and whom I can call my own.

There is a certain Panditji, who wears a grand tilak and marks. He takes half a seer of milk. One day, his wife had gone somewhere for a feast. How was I to know? As on other days, I went inside with the milk. I called out, “Bahuji, Bahuji,” but no one answered. Then I saw Panditji coming, closing the outer door behind him. I understood at once—his intentions were bad. I scolded him, “Why have you closed the door? Has Bahuji gone somewhere? Why is the house so silent?”

He said, “She has gone to a feast,” and took two steps closer to me.

I said, “If you want the milk, take it. Otherwise, I am leaving.” He said, “Today you will not be able to leave, Jhunia Rani! Every day you cut my heart and run away, but today you will not escape my hands.” I tell you the truth, Gobar, my hair stood on end.

Gobar, in a fury, said, “If I see that child, I will dig a pit and bury him. I will suck his blood. Just show him to me.”

“Listen, I am enough to break the face of such men. My heart began to pound. If he tried any mischief, what would I do? No one would hear if I screamed. Gathering my courage, I said, ‘Don’t be mistaken, Panditji! I am an Ahir’s daughter. I will pluck out every hair of your mustache. Is it written in your holy books that you should lock up other people’s wives and daughters in your house and dishonor them? Is this why you sit here with your tilak and marks, spinning your web? Just to test him, I said, “I will take fifty rupees.” I swear to you, Gobar, he immediately went to the inner room and brought out five ten-rupee notes and tried to put them in my hands. When I dropped the notes on the ground and turned to the door, he grabbed my hand. But I was ready. I smashed the pot on his face. From head to toe, he was drenched. He was hurt too. He sat down, holding his head, and began to wail. When I saw he could do nothing more, I gave him two kicks on his back and ran out, opening the door.

Gobar burst out laughing, “You did very well. He must have been bathed in milk. Even his tilak and marks—”

It must have been washed away. Why didn’t you shave off your moustache as well?

“The next day, I went to his house again. His wife had returned. He was lying in his sitting room, a bandage tied around his head. How could Govar tolerate the slight to his manhood? Boasting, he said, “What does being big matter! I have bones of steel. I do three hundred push-ups every day. If only I got milk and ghee, by now I’d be shining like gold.”

Saying this, he puffed out his chest.

I don’t know what it is with men—whenever they see a young, beautiful woman, they start staring, beating their chests. And those who call themselves great men, they’re the worst rakes of all. Govar objected, “You? When I look at you, I just want to keep you close to my heart.”

Jhunia gave him a light punch on the back. “You too have started flattering me like the others. I know what I am. But these people, all they want is a young woman to amuse themselves for a while. A man only looks for qualities in the one he has to spend his life with. I hear and see a lot. These days, the ways of the big houses are strange. In the same neighborhood where my in-laws live, there was a Kashmiri named Gapdu. He was a very wealthy man. They used five seers of milk a day in his house. He had three daughters, all about twenty or twenty-five years old. Each one more beautiful than the other. All three went to study at the big college. One even taught there, earning three hundred rupees a month. They all played the sitar, the harmonium, danced, sang, but none of them married. God knows whether they didn’t like any man, or no man liked them. Once, I asked the eldest, and she laughed and said, ‘We don’t nurture that disease.’ But secretly, they enjoyed themselves plenty. Whenever I saw them, two or four boys would be surrounding them. The eldest would wear a coat and trousers, ride a horse, and go out for rides with men. Their exploits were famous throughout the city. Gapdu Babu would walk around with his head bowed, as if he had blackened his face. He would scold and try to reason with his daughters, but all of them would openly say, ‘You have no right to interfere in our matters. We are queens of our own hearts; we will do as we please!’ Poor father, what could he say to his young, grown daughters? He couldn’t beat or tie them up, nor could he restrain them with scolding. But then, who can question the ways of the rich? Whatever they do is considered right. They aren’t even afraid of the law or the panchayat. I just don’t understand how someone can change every day. Has man fallen below even cows and goats? But, brother, the mind becomes what you make of it. I have seen such people too, who—”

--- Page 45 ---
Sometimes, just to change the taste, one craves for halwa and puri. And then I see those women who feel feverish at the mere sight of home-cooked roti and dal. There are some poor souls, too, who are content with their simple roti and dal, caring nothing for halwa and puri. Look at my two sisters-in-law. My brothers are not blind or crippled, they are men among men, yet the sisters-in-law are not pleased with them. They want someone who will buy them gold earrings, bring them fine saris, treat them to sweets every day. I too am not immune to the charms of earrings and sweets, but if you tell me to sell my honor for them, may God protect me from that. To spend a lifetime eating and wearing whatever you have, with just one man—that is my song. More often than not, it is the men who spoil the women. When a man casts his eyes here and there, the woman too will glance about. If the man chases after other women, the woman will surely chase after other men. A man’s unfaithfulness is just as bitter to a woman as a woman’s is to a man. Remember that. I told my husband plainly: if you stray, I will do as I please. If you think you can do as you wish and keep your wife in check by threats and beatings, that will not do. If you act openly, she will act in secret. You cannot burn her and hope to live in peace.

For Gobar, these were words from a new world. He listened, absorbed, as if spellbound. Sometimes, he would stop in his tracks, then, realizing it, would walk on again. At first, Jhunia had enchanted him with her beauty. Today, she had captivated him with her wisdom, her experience, and the story of her fidelity. If only he could find such a woman—beautiful, virtuous, wise—what fortune! Then why should he fear the panchayat or the biradari?

When Jhunia saw that her words had taken deep root, she pressed her hand to her chest, bit her tongue between her teeth, and said, “Oh, look, we’ve reached your village. You’re quite sly—you didn’t even tell me to turn back.”

With that, she turned to leave.

Gobar pleaded, “Why don’t you come to my house for a moment? Let my mother see you too.”

Jhunia, averting her eyes in shyness, replied, “I won’t go to your house like this. What amazes me is how I came this far at all! Well, tell me, when will you come again? There will be good company at my door all night. Come, I’ll meet you in the backyard.”

“And if you don’t come?”

“Then go back.”

“Then I won’t come again.”

“You’ll have to come, I’m telling you.”

“Will you promise to meet me?”

“I won’t promise.”

“Then I won’t come.”

“As you please.”

Jhunia showed him her thumb and walked away. In this very first meeting, both had staked their claim on each other. Jhunia knew he would come—how could he not? Gobar knew she would meet him—how could she not?

As Gobar walked on alone, driving the cow ahead, it felt as though he had fallen from heaven.
