The desolate and scorching evening of Jeth was growing cool and cheerful in the streets and lanes of Semri, thanks to the sprinkling of water. Around the pavilion, pots of flowers and plants had been arranged, and electric fans were whirring. Rai Sahib had installed his own electricity generator in his factory. His guards, dressed in uniforms with blue turbans, strutted about, impressing the public with their authority. The servants, clad in spotless kurtas and saffron turbans, were busy welcoming and honoring the guests and village elders.

At that moment, a motorcar stopped before the Singh Dwar, and three distinguished gentlemen alighted. The one in a khaddar kurta and sandals was Pandit Omkarnath, the illustrious editor of the daily newspaper 'Bijli', worn thin by his concern for the nation. The second gentleman, dressed in a coat and trousers, was a lawyer by training, but since his practice did not flourish, he worked as an agent for an insurance company and earned far more by arranging loans for zamindars from mahajans and banks than he ever did from the law. His name was Shyamvihari Tankha. The third, attired in a silk achkan and tight pajama, was Mr. V. Mehta, a professor of philosophy at the University. These three were Rai Sahib’s former classmates, invited to the auspicious celebration. Today, all the tenants of the estate would arrive to offer their shagun money. At night, there would be a Dhanush-Yajna and a grand feast for the guests.

Hori had given five rupees as shagun, and now, dressed in a pink mirzai, a pink turban, a knee-length kathni, holding a small hoe in his hand, and with powder dusted on his face, he had become Raja Janak’s gardener. He was so puffed up with pride, it seemed as if the entire celebration was taking place solely because of his efforts.

Rai Sahib welcomed the guests. He was a tall man with a sturdy build, a radiant face, a high forehead, and a fair complexion, upon which the silken shawl of Sharavati shone splendidly.

Pandit Omkarnath asked, “Which play do you intend to stage tonight? That is the only thing that interests me.”

Rai Sahib, seating the three gentlemen on chairs before his mansion, replied, “First, there will be the Dhanush-Yajna, and afterwards, a farce. I couldn’t find a suitable play. Some were so long they wouldn’t end even in five hours, and so complex that perhaps not a soul here would understand them. In the end, I wrote a farce myself, which will be completed in two hours.”

Omkarnath greatly doubted Rai Sahib’s creative abilities. He believed that true talent shines only in poverty, like a lamp that reveals its light in darkness. With a look of disregard, which he made no effort to conceal, Pandit Omkarnath turned his face away.

Mr. Tankha did not wish to get involved in such pointless discussions. Yet he wanted to show Rai Sahib that he, too, had a right to speak on the subject. He said, “Any play can be good if the actors are good. Even the best play can be ruined in the hands of poor actors. Until educated actresses appear on our stage, our dramatic art cannot be redeemed. This time, you have created quite a stir in the Council with your questions. I can say with confidence that no member’s record is as impressive as yours.”

--- Page 47 ---
They claim to be well-wishers of the peasants, eager to grant them all manner of concessions, wishing to strip the zamindars of their rights—indeed, they even go so far as to call them the curse of society. Yet, they themselves are zamindars, zamindars just like thousands of others. If you truly believe that the peasants deserve concessions, then begin with yourself. Give the tenants their leases without demanding nazrana, abolish begar, renounce the practice of increasing rent, relinquish the grazing lands. I have no sympathy for those who speak like communists but live the life of the wealthy—lives just as luxurious, just as self-indulgent.

Raisahab was taken aback. Wrinkles appeared on the lawyer’s brow, and it was as if a shadow passed over the editor’s face. He himself was a devotee of collectivism, but was reluctant to set his own house on fire. Tankha came to Raisahab’s defense—“I believe that if all zamindars treated their tenants as well as Raisahab does, there would be no question at all.”

Mehta struck the second blow—“I admit, your conduct towards your tenants is exemplary. But the question is, is there not self-interest in it? Could it not be that food cooks best over a gentle flame? One who kills with sugar may be far more successful than one who kills with poison. I know only this: we are either communists or we are not. If we are, let us act accordingly; if not, let us stop this talk. I am opposed to a life of pretense. If you believe eating meat is good, then eat it openly; if you believe it is wrong, then abstain. That I can understand. But to believe it is good and eat it in secret—I cannot understand that. I call it cowardice, and cunning as well, which are, in truth, one and the same.”

Raisahab was a man skilled in the ways of public debate. He was practiced in bearing insult and injury with patience and generosity. Somewhat perplexed, he replied, “Your view is absolutely correct, Mehtaji! You know how much I respect your frankness, but you forget that, as with any journey, the journey of thought too has its stages, and you cannot leap from one stage to the next. The history of human life is proof of this. I was raised in an environment where the king is God and the zamindar is God’s minister. My late father showed such mercy to his tenants that, in times of flood or drought, he would sometimes forgive half the rent, sometimes all of it. He would take grain from his own granary to feed the tenants. He sold the family’s ornaments to help with the marriage of their daughters. But only so long as the people called him their ruler and their avatar of righteousness, worshipped him as their deity. Caring for the people was his eternal duty, but when it came to rights, he would not part with a single cowrie. I was raised in that very atmosphere, and I am proud that, whatever my actions, in my thoughts I have gone beyond him, and have come to believe that unless these concessions are granted to the peasants as rights, and not merely as acts of goodwill, their condition will never improve. If goodwill can renounce self-interest, it is an exception. Even as I act with goodwill, I cannot renounce self-interest, and I wish that our pride be compelled by law and policy to surrender its self-interest. You may call this cowardice; I call it helplessness. I accept that no one has the right to grow fat on another’s labor. To live off others is a matter of deep shame. Work is the duty of every living being. A society in which a few enjoy and the many are crushed and consumed can never be happy. Capital and education—which I consider merely another form of capital—the sooner their fortress falls, the better. For those who cannot even secure bread for their bellies, their masters and employers...

--- Page 48 ---
Ten thousand, five thousand rebukes—this is both laughable and shameful. I know well how much luxury, how much vice, how much servility, and how much shamelessness this system has filled us zamindars with. Yet, it is not for these reasons that I oppose this system. My point is that even from the perspective of self-interest, it cannot be justified. To maintain this grandeur, we must murder our souls to such an extent that not even a trace of self-respect remains in us. We are compelled to exploit our tenants. If we do not present costly gifts to the officials, we are considered uncouth; if we do not maintain our pomp, we are called misers. At the slightest hint of progress, we tremble and rush to the authorities, pleading for protection. We no longer trust ourselves, nor do we possess any vigor. Our condition is like that of children raised on milk fed by a spoon—plump on the outside, but weak, spiritless, and dependent within.

Mehta clapped his hands and said, “Hear, hear! If only your mind possessed half the wisdom your tongue does. The pity is that, despite understanding everything, you do not put your thoughts into practice.”

Omkarnath replied, “A single grain cannot break the oven, Mr. Mehta! We must move with the times, and also make the times move with us. Cooperation is not needed only for evil deeds; it is just as necessary for good ones. Why do you alone pocket eight hundred rupees a month, when your millions of brothers survive on only eight?”

Raisahab looked at the editor with outward regret but inner satisfaction, and said, “Please do not make personal criticisms, Editor Sahib! Here, we are discussing the structure of society.”

Mr. Mehta, in the same calm tone, replied, “No, no, I do not consider it wrong. Society is made up of individuals, and without considering the individual, we cannot discuss any system. I draw such a salary precisely because I do not believe in this system.”

The editor was surprised. “So, you support the present system?”

“I support the principle that there will always be great and small in the world, and that there should always be. To try to abolish this would lead to the ruin of humanity.”

The nature of the contest changed. Raisahab stepped aside. The editor entered the arena. “You, in the twentieth century, believe in distinctions of high and low?”

“Yes, I do, and most emphatically so. The doctrine you support is not new either. From the moment human beings developed a sense of possession, that doctrine was born. Buddha, Plato, and Christ—all were champions of equality in society. Greek, Roman, and Syrian civilizations all experimented with it, but because it is unnatural, it could never become permanent.”

“Your words astonish me.”

“Astonishment is another name for ignorance.”

“I am grateful to you. If only you would begin a series of articles on this subject—”

“I am not such a fool. Secure me a good sum, and I shall, by all means.”

“You have chosen a principle by which you can openly loot the public treasury.”

“The only difference between you and me is that I live by what I believe. You people believe one thing and do another. You wish to equalize wealth by any means, but can you equalize character, beauty, talent, or strength? The distinction between great and small does not arise from wealth alone. I have seen many—”

Godan: 43

--- Page 49 ---
I have seen people bow their knees, and surely you have seen it too. Even the greatest of kings have rubbed their noses at the threshold of beauty. Is this not social inequality? You will cite the example of Russia. But what is it there, except that the owner of the mill has assumed the role of a state official? Intelligence ruled then, it rules now, and it shall always rule.

The betel leaves arrived on a tray. Offering betel and cardamom to the guests, Rai Sahib said, “If intelligence is free from self-interest, we have no objection to accepting its supremacy. This is the very ideal of socialism.

“We bow our heads before sadhus and saints because they possess the strength of renunciation. In the same way, we wish to grant intelligence authority, respect, and leadership—but never property. The authority and respect of intelligence die with the individual, but his property, left behind, becomes even more powerful for those who inherit it. No society can function without intelligence. We only wish to break the sting of this scorpion.”

Another motorcar arrived, and out stepped Mr. Khanna, manager of a bank and managing director of a sugar mill. Two ladies accompanied him. Rai Sahib helped the ladies alight. The one dressed in a khadi sari, with a grave and thoughtful demeanor, was Mr. Khanna’s wife, Kamini Khanna. The other, wearing high-heeled shoes and with laughter ever blooming on her face, was Miss Malti. She had studied medicine in England and now practiced as a doctor. She was a frequent visitor to the mansions of the taluqdars. She was the very embodiment of the new age: her body delicate, but brimming with energy; no trace of hesitation or shyness; adept at makeup, dazzlingly quick-witted, well-versed in the psychology of men, believing pleasure and amusement to be the essence of life, and skilled in the arts of charm and persuasion. Where there should be soul, there was display; where there should be heart, there were gestures and affectations; her emotions were held under strict control, as if desire and longing had been erased.

Shaking hands with Mr. Mehta, she said, “Truly, you look like a philosopher. In your new work, you have thoroughly dismantled the idealists. More than once, as I read, I felt like quarreling with you. Why is it that philosophers are always so devoid of feeling?”

Mehta was embarrassed. He was unmarried and sought refuge from the women of the new age. Among men, he was lively and talkative, but the moment a woman appeared, his tongue was tied, as if a lock had been placed on his mind. He could not even manage the simplest of courtesies with women.

Mr. Khanna asked, “What special feature is there in a philosopher’s appearance, Devi-ji?”

Malti looked at Mehta with a touch of pity and said, “If Mr. Mehta doesn’t mind, shall I tell you?”

Khanna was among Miss Malti’s admirers. Wherever Miss Malti went, Khanna was sure to follow, hovering around her like a bee. He always wished to speak with her as much as possible, to draw her gaze to himself as much as he could.

Khanna winked and said, “A philosopher never takes offense. That is their very trait.”

“Then listen,” said Malti, “Philosophers are always dead-hearted. Whenever you see them, they are lost in their own thoughts. They look in your direction, but they do not see you. You may talk to them as much as you like, but they hear nothing, as if they are floating in the void.”

Godan: 44

--- Page 50 ---
Everyone burst into laughter. Mr. Mehta seemed to sink into the ground.

“My philosophy professor at Oxford was named Husband.”

Khanna interrupted, “That’s quite an unusual name.”

“Yes, and he was a bachelor…”

“Mr. Mehta is a bachelor too…”

Now Mehta found his opportunity. He said, “Are you afflicted by the same malady?”

“I have vowed to marry only a philosopher, and this class is terrified at the very mention of marriage. Mr. Husband, for instance, would hide in his house at the sight of a woman. Several of his students were girls. If any of them ever went to his office to ask a question, he would become as anxious as if a lion had entered! We used to tease him a lot, but he was a simple-hearted soul. He earned several thousand a year, but I always saw him in the same suit. He had a widowed sister who managed the entire household. Mr. Husband never cared about food. Fearing visitors, he would lock his door and immerse himself in reading and writing. When it was time to eat, his sister would quietly enter through the inner door and close his book for him—only then would he realize it was mealtime. Even at night, the dinner hour was fixed. His sister would turn off the lamp in his room. One day, when she tried to close his book, he clutched it with both hands, and a tug-of-war began between brother and sister. In the end, she dragged his wheeled chair into the dining room.”

Raisahab said, “But Mehta sahib is so cheerful and sociable—why would he join this commotion?”

“Then he cannot be a true philosopher. When our own worries give us headaches, how can anyone be happy carrying the burdens of the world on their head?”

Meanwhile, Editorji was telling Mrs. Khanna about the hardships of financial struggle. “Just imagine, Madam, the life of an editor is a long lamentation, which people, instead of sympathizing with, cover their ears to avoid. The poor man can neither help himself nor others. The public expects him to be at the forefront of every movement, to go to jail, to get beaten, to have his household goods auctioned off—they consider this his duty. But no one pays attention to his difficulties. He must be everything—master of every art and every science—but he is not granted even the right to live. You don’t write anything these days. Why do you deprive me of the little fortune I might have in serving you?”

Mrs. Khanna had a fondness for writing poetry. Because of this, Editorji would occasionally visit her, but lately, she had not been able to write anything, being busy with household duties. The truth was that it was Editorji who had encouraged her and helped her achieve some success, though her genuine talent was rather limited.

“What should I write? Nothing comes to mind. Have you ever asked Miss Matli to write something?”

Editorji replied with indifference, “Her time is valuable, but when one makes life itself the goal, what will they write?”

Kamini said with a touch of jealous humor, “If you write something, the publicity will be doubled. In Lucknow, there isn’t a single person who doesn’t read the newspaper.”

--- Page 51 ---
“If wealth had been the ideal of my life, I would not be in this condition today. I, too, know how to earn money. If I wished, I could make lakhs even now, but I have never valued wealth. The service of literature is the purpose of my life, and always will be.”

“At least have my name entered among your subscribers.”

“I will not list your name among the subscribers, but among the patrons.”

“Reserve the title of patron for queens and princesses, whom you can flatter a little and turn your journal into a profitable venture.”

“You are my queen. Before you, I see no worth in any queen or princess. She who has compassion and wisdom is my queen. I abhor flattery.”

Kamini teased, “But you are flattering me, Editor Sahib!”

The editor, growing serious, replied in a tone of reverence, “This is not flattery, Devi-ji, but the true outpouring of my heart.”

Raisahab called out, “Editor Sahib, please come here for a moment. Miss Malti wishes to speak with you.”

All the editor’s pride vanished. He stood before them, the very image of humility and respect. Malti looked at him kindly and said, “I was just saying that in this world, I am most afraid of editors. You people can ruin anyone in a moment if you wish. The Chief Secretary Sahib himself once said to me, ‘If I could only put this wretched Omkarnath in jail, I would consider myself fortunate.’”

Omkarnath’s large moustaches bristled. Pride shone in his eyes. Though he was a man of very calm nature, a challenge would always rouse his manhood. With a voice full of resolve, he replied, “I am grateful for such kindness. It is enough that my name is mentioned in that assembly, no matter how. Please tell the Secretary Sahib that Omkarnath is not one of those men who are frightened by such threats. His pen will rest only when his life’s journey ends. He has taken it upon himself to uproot injustice and tyranny from their very foundations.”

Miss Malti pressed further, “But I do not understand your policy. When you can win the cooperation of officials with a little courtesy, why do you keep your distance from them? If you would temper the fire and venom in your criticisms, I promise you I could secure considerable support for you from the government. You have already seen the public: you appealed to them, flattered them, told them of your hardships, but nothing came of it. Now try the officials for once. If within three months you are not riding in a motorcar and receiving invitations to government banquets, you may blame me as much as you like. Then these very aristocrats and nationalists who pay you no heed now will be circling your door.”

Omkarnath replied with pride, “That is precisely what I cannot do, Devi-ji. I have always kept my principles high and pure, and as long as I live, I shall defend them. Worshippers of wealth are found in every street; I am among the worshippers of principle.”

“I call that arrogance.”

“As you wish.”

“You do not care for wealth?”

Godan: 46

--- Page 52 ---
“Not by murdering principles.”

“Then why do you carry advertisements for foreign goods in your newspaper? I haven’t seen so many foreign advertisements in any other paper. You pretend to be an idealist, a man of principle, but you send the country’s wealth abroad for your own profit without the slightest regret? You cannot justify this policy by any argument.”

Omkarnath truly had no answer. Seeing him at a loss for words, Rai Sahib came to his defense—“Then what do you want? If we are attacked from every side, how can the paper survive?”

Miss Malati had never learned to show mercy.

“If the paper cannot survive, then close it down. You have no right to promote foreign goods just to keep your paper running. If you are so helpless, then stop pretending to be principled. I am infuriated by so-called idealistic papers. Sometimes I feel like striking a match and burning them all. A person who cannot maintain harmony between word and deed is no idealist, whatever else he may be.”

Mehta brightened. Only a short while ago, he himself had expressed this very thought. Now he realized that this lady possessed not only beauty but also the power of thought—she was no mere butterfly. His hesitation vanished.

“This is exactly what I was saying just now. The lack of harmony between thought and action is nothing but cunning, hypocrisy.”

Miss Malati replied with a pleased smile, “Then on this subject, you and I are one, and I too can claim to be a philosopher.”

Khanna’s tongue was itching to speak. He said, “Every inch of you is steeped in philosophy.”

Malati reined him in—“Oh, so you too have a hand in philosophy? I always thought you drowned your philosophy in the Ganges long ago. Otherwise, you wouldn’t be a director of so many banks and companies.”

Rai Sahib steadied Khanna—“So you think philosophers should always be paupers?”

“Yes, indeed. If a philosopher cannot conquer desire, what kind of philosopher is he?”

“By that standard, perhaps even Mr. Mehta would not be considered a philosopher.”

Mehta, as if rolling up his sleeves, said, “I have never made such a claim, Rai Sahib! I only know that the tools a blacksmith uses are not the same as those a goldsmith uses. Do you want a babool or a palm tree to flourish in the same conditions as a rose? For me, wealth is only the name for those conveniences that allow me to make my life meaningful. Wealth is not something for me to accumulate and thrive upon; it is merely a means. I have no desire for wealth itself. Just provide me with the means by which I can make use of my life.”

Omkarnath was a collectivist. How could he accept this primacy of the individual?

“In that case, every laborer could say he needs a thousand rupees a month for the facilities to do his work.”

“If you think your work cannot proceed without that laborer, you will have to provide him those facilities. If another laborer can do the same work for less, then there is no need to flatter the first one.”

--- Page 53 ---
“If laborers held power, then for them, women and liquor would become as essential comforts as they are for philosophers.”

“Then believe me, I would not envy them,” Mehta replied.

“If a woman is so necessary to give meaning to your life, why don’t you get married?” Malati asked.

Mehta answered without hesitation, “Precisely because I believe that free enjoyment is not an obstacle to the development of the soul. Marriage locks the soul and life inside a cage.”

Khanna supported him, “Bondage and restraint are old theories. The new theory is free enjoyment.”

Malati seized the opportunity, “Then Mrs. Khanna should be ready for divorce now.”

“If only the divorce bill would pass.”

“Perhaps you’ll be the first to make use of it?”

Kamini shot Malati a venomous glance and pursed her lips, as if to say—Khanna is yours, I care not.

Malati turned to Mehta, “What are your views on this, Mr. Mehta?”

Mehta grew serious. Whenever he expressed his opinion on a question, it was as if he poured his entire soul into it.

“I consider marriage a social contract, and neither man nor woman has the right to break it. Before the contract, you are free; once it is made, your hands are tied.”

“So you are opposed to divorce, then?”

“Absolutely.”

“And the principle of free enjoyment?”

“That is for those who do not wish to marry.”

“Everyone wants the full development of their soul, so who should marry, and why?”

“Precisely because everyone desires freedom, but very few can free themselves from greed.”

“Which do you consider superior—married life or unmarried life?”

“From society’s perspective, married life; from the individual’s perspective, unmarried life.”

The performance of the Dhanush-Yajna was near. The program was Dhanush-Yajna from ten to one, and farce from one to three. Preparations for the meal had begun. Separate arrangements were made for the guests to stay in the bungalow. Two rooms were reserved for the Khanna family. Many other guests had also arrived. Everyone went to their rooms, changed clothes, and gathered in the dining hall. Here, there was no distinction of untouchability. People of all castes and varnas sat together to eat. Only Editor Omkarnath ate fruit alone in his room, and Kamini Khanna, suffering from a headache, refused to eat. There were no fewer than twenty-five guests in the dining hall. There was liquor and meat as well. For this celebration, Raisahab would have a special quality of liquor brought in. It was brought under the name of medicine, but it was pure liquor. Various kinds of meat were cooked—kofta, kebab, and pulao. Chicken, hens, goat, deer, partridge, peacock—whoever liked whatever, could have it.

As the meal began, Miss Malati asked, “Where has the Editor gone? Send someone to fetch him.”

--- Page 54 ---
Rai Sahib, have them brought here.

Rai Sahib replied, “He is a Vaishnav. Why would you call him here and ruin the poor man’s religion? He is a man of strict principles.”

“Oh, if nothing else, at least it will be a spectacle.”

Suddenly, seeing a gentleman, she called out, “You are here too, Mirza Khurshid. This task is entrusted to you. Your abilities will be put to the test.”

Mirza Khurshid was a fair-complexioned man, with light brown mustaches, blue eyes, a stout build, and a clean-shaven head. He wore a gleaming achkan and tight-fitting pajama, topped with a hat. He was a Sufi Muslim, had performed the Hajj twice, yet drank copiously. He would say, “When I never obey even a single command of God, why should I lay down my life for religion?” He was a great humorist, a carefree soul. In earlier days, he had been in the indigo trade. He made lakhs, but misfortune struck when he became entangled with a memsahib. There was a lawsuit; he narrowly escaped jail. He was ordered to leave the country within twenty-four hours. He left everything where it was and fled with only fifty thousand rupees. He had agents in Bombay. He thought he would settle accounts with them and live off whatever remained, but the agents tricked him out of even those fifty thousand. Disheartened, he went to Lucknow. On the train, he met a Mahatma, who, showing him a vision of paradise, relieved him of his watch, rings, and all his money. When the poor fellow reached Lucknow, he had nothing but the clothes on his back.

He had known Rai Sahib from before. With some help from him and a few other friends, he opened a shoe shop. Now it was the most popular shoe shop in Lucknow, with daily sales of four or five hundred rupees. In a short time, the public had come to trust him so much that he defeated a prominent Muslim taluqdaar and made it to the council. Seated in his place, he said, “No, I don’t corrupt anyone’s faith. You should do this yourself. The real fun would be if you could make him drink wine. That would be a true test of your beauty’s magic.”

Voices rose from all sides, “Yes, yes, Miss Malti, show your skill today!”

Malti challenged Mirza, “Will you give me a reward?”

“A pouch of a hundred rupees.”

“Ha! A hundred rupees? You want me to ruin a faith worth a lakh for a mere hundred?”

“All right, name your own fee.”

“One thousand, not a paisa less.”

“Very well, agreed.”

“No, deposit it in Mehtaji’s hands.”

Mirzaji immediately took a hundred-rupee note from his pocket, and standing up, displaying it, said, “Brothers! This is a matter of all our honor. If Miss Malti’s request is not fulfilled, we shall have nowhere to show our faces. If I had the money, I would sacrifice a lakh for each of Miss Malti’s charms. An old poet once sacrificed the provinces of Samarkand and Bukhara for a single mole on his beloved’s face. Today is the test of all your gallantry and love of beauty. Whatever you have, I swear by the true warrior, do not step back. Friends! The money will be spent, but the name will remain forever. Such a spectacle is cheap even at millions. See how the queen of Lucknow’s beauties casts the spell of her charm on an ascetic!”

As soon as his speech ended, Mirzaji began searching everyone’s pockets. First, Mr.—

--- Page 55 ---
Khanna was searched. Five rupees were found in his pocket.

Mirza pulled a wry face and said, “Well, Khanna Sahab, well! Such a grand name, but so little to show for it. Director of so many companies, earning lakhs, and only five rupees in your pocket. God save us! Where is Mehta? Please, go and collect at least a hundred rupees from Mrs. Khanna.”

Khanna, embarrassed, replied, “Oh, she won’t have a single paisa. Who could have guessed you’d start searching pockets here?”

“Never mind, you keep quiet. Let us try our luck.”

“Alright, I’ll go and ask her.”

“No, no, you can’t leave. Mr. Mehta, you’re a philosopher, a master of psychology. See that you don’t embarrass yourself.”

Mehta would become intoxicated after drinking, and in that state, his philosophy would vanish and his wit would come alive. He dashed off to Mrs. Khanna, and in just five minutes, returned, his face fallen.

Mirza asked, “What, empty-handed?”

Raisahab laughed, “Even the mice in a qazi’s house are clever.”

Mirza said, “Khanna, you are truly fortunate, by God!”

Mehta burst out laughing and pulled out five notes of a hundred rupees each from his pocket. Mirza leapt up and embraced him.

Voices rose from all around, “Amazing! I admit it, you’re a master. Why wouldn’t you be—you’re a philosopher, after all!”

Mirza touched the notes to his eyes and said, “Mehta, from today I am your disciple. Tell me, what magic did you work?”

Mehta, puffing up and staring with red eyes, replied, “Oh, nothing at all. It was no great feat. I went and asked, ‘May I come in?’ She said, ‘It’s you, Mehta-ji, please come.’ I went in and said, ‘People are playing bridge in there. That ring is worth no less than a thousand. You’ve seen it yourself. Well, here’s the thing: if you have the money, give five hundred and take something worth a thousand. Such an opportunity won’t come again. If Miss Malti doesn’t give the money now, she’ll miss her chance. Who will give it later? Perhaps that’s why she took off her ring—who keeps five hundred rupees at home? She smiled, immediately took out five notes from her purse, and said, ‘I never leave home without something. Who knows when it might be needed?’”

Khanna, still embarrassed, said, “When this is the state of our professors, only God can save the university.”

Khurshed rubbed salt in the wound, “Oh, is it such a large sum that your heart is sinking? God forbid I lie, but this is your daily income! Just imagine, if you fall ill for a day, it’ll all go to Miss Malti anyway. The cure for your heartache lies with Miss Malti alone.”

Malti kicked back, “Look here, Mirza-ji, it’s not good to make mischief in the stable.”

Mirza wagged his tail, “I beg your pardon, Devi-ji!”

Mr. Tankha was searched. With difficulty, ten rupees were found. Only an eight-anna coin was found in Mehta’s pocket. Several gentlemen contributed one or two rupees each. When the total was added up, there was still a shortfall of three hundred.

--- Page 56 ---
This shortfall was generously made up by Rai Sahib.

The editor had eaten dried fruits and fresh fruit, and was just stretching his back a little when Rai Sahib came and said, “Miss Malti is asking for you.”

Pleased, he replied, “Miss Malti is asking for me? What good fortune!” And together with Rai Sahib, he entered the hall.

Meanwhile, the servants had cleared the tables. Malti stepped forward to welcome them.

The editor showed humility. “Please, be seated, don’t stand on ceremony. I am not such a great man.”

Malti replied in a voice full of reverence, “You may think it’s mere formality, but I believe I am honoring myself. You may not consider yourself to be someone important—and indeed, such airs do not suit you—but everyone gathered here is well acquainted with your service to the nation and to literature. The important work you have done in this field—perhaps people do not recognize its value yet, but that time is not far off. In fact, I would say that time has already come, when streets in every city will bear your name, clubs will be founded in your honor, and your portraits will hang in the town halls. Whatever little awakening there is at present is the blessing of your great efforts. You will be glad to know that there are now followers of yours in the country, eager to lend a hand in your rural reform movement. These gentlemen have a great desire that this work be organized, and that a Rural Reform Association be established, with you as its president.”

For Omkarnath, this was the first time in his life that he was accorded such honor among the elite. True, he sometimes spoke at public meetings and was secretary or vice-president of several societies, but the educated classes had so far ignored him. He never managed to fit in among them, and so he would complain of their inactivity and selfishness at public meetings, and would lambast each one of them in his newspaper. He had a sharp tongue, a harsh manner, and in the name of frankness, he often lapsed into recklessness, so people considered him an empty drum. And now, in that very society, he was being honored so highly! Where were now the editors of ‘Swaraj’, ‘Swadhin Bharat’, and ‘Hunter’? Let them come and see, and cool their burning hearts. Surely today, the gods were smiling upon him. Good deeds never go unrewarded—so say the sages. He felt himself rise in his own estimation. Filled with gratitude, he said, “Devi-ji, you are dragging me through thorns. Whatever service I have rendered to the people, I did as my duty. I do not consider this honor mine, but rather the honor of the cause to which I have dedicated my life. But my humble request is that the position of president be given to someone influential. I do not believe in titles. I am a servant and wish only to serve.”

Miss Malti could not accept this in any way. Pandit-ji must become president. She could see no one else in the city so influential. The one whose pen is magical, whose tongue is magical, whose very personality is magical—how could he say he was not influential? The days are gone when wealth and influence went hand in hand. Now is the age when talent and influence go together. The editor must accept this position. Miss Malti would be the secretary. Already a thousand rupees had been raised for the association, and the whole city and province still lay ahead. To raise four or five lakhs would be no great matter.

Omkarnath began to feel a kind of intoxication. The flutter in his heart took the form of a grave responsibility. He said, “But let us understand this, Miss—”

--- Page 57 ---
Malati, this is a task of great responsibility, and you will have to devote a lot of your time to it. On my part, I assure you that you will always find me present in the assembly hall before anyone else.

Mirza-ji interjected, “Even your greatest enemy could not claim that you have ever shirked your duty.”

Miss Malati noticed that the wine was beginning to take effect, so she became even more solemn and said, “If we did not understand the greatness of this work, neither would this assembly have been established, nor would you be its president. We could have made some wealthy landlord or taluqdar the president, amassed plenty of money, and used the guise of service to fulfill our own interests. But that is not our aim. Our sole purpose is to serve the people, and the greatest means to that end is your newspaper. We have resolved that it will be promoted in every town and village, and that its subscription should reach twenty thousand as soon as possible. All the municipalities and district board chairmen of the province are our friends. Several chairmen are present here right now. If each of them takes even five hundred copies, you can be certain of twenty-five thousand subscriptions. Then, Rai Sahib and Mirza Sahib have suggested that a proposal be put before the council: that a copy of ‘Bijli’ be ordered for every village at government expense, or that some annual grant be accepted. We are fully confident that this proposal will pass.”

Omkar Nath, swaying slightly as if intoxicated, said, “We will have to take a deputation to the Governor.”

Mirza Khurshid replied, “Certainly—certainly.”

“We must tell him what a shame and disgrace it is for any civilized government that, even though ‘Bijli’ is the only paper for rural upliftment, its very existence is not acknowledged.”

Mirza Khurshid said, “Absolutely—absolutely.”

“I do not take pride. The time for pride has not yet come, but I do claim that for the cause of rural organization, ‘Bijli’ has made more effort—”

Mr. Mehta corrected him, “No, sir, call it penance.”

“I thank Mr. Mehta. Yes, it should indeed be called penance, a most arduous penance. The penance that ‘Bijli’ has performed is unprecedented not only in the history of this province, but in the history of this nation.”

Mirza Khurshid said, “Indeed—indeed.”

Miss Malati turned another page—“Our association has also decided that for the seat now vacant in the council, you should be put forward as a candidate. You need only give your consent. We will take care of everything else. You need not worry about expenses, propaganda, or running about.”

The light in Omkar Nath’s eyes doubled. With proud humility, he said, “I am your servant. Whatever work you wish of me, I am ready to do.”

“That is exactly what we expect from you. We have grown weary of bowing before false gods and have gained nothing. Now, in you, we have found a true guide, a true guru, and on this auspicious day, we must, with one mind and one heart, renounce our egos and our pride. From today, among us, there is no Brahmin, no Shudra, no Hindu, no Muslim, no high, no low. We are all children of the same mother, playing in the same lap, eating from the same plate, brothers all. Those who believe in discrimination, who worship separation and fanaticism, have no place in our assembly.”

--- Page 58 ---
In an assembly presided over by the venerable Omkarnath, a man of such vast-heartedness, there could be no question of high or low, of restrictions on food and drink, or of caste distinctions. Those gentlemen who did not believe in unity and nationalism were kindly requested to take their leave.

Raisahab raised a doubt: “In my opinion, unity does not mean that everyone must abandon their dietary principles. If I do not drink alcohol, must I then leave this assembly?”

Malati replied in a merciless tone, “Indeed, you must. You cannot remain in this association while harboring any kind of discrimination.”

Mehtaji tapped the water pot and said, “I suspect that even our esteemed president does not truly believe in unity regarding food and drink.”

Omkarnath’s face turned pale. What was this scoundrel doing, playing the shehnai at such an inopportune moment? The wretch might start digging up old graves, and then this entire good fortune would vanish like a dream into nothingness.

Miss Malati looked at his face with an inquisitive gaze and said firmly, “Your suspicion is baseless, Mr. Mehta! Do you think that such a worshipper of national unity, such a broad-minded man, such a sensitive poet, would accept this meaningless and shameful distinction? To doubt him thus is to insult his sense of nationality.”

Omkarnath’s countenance lit up; a glow of happiness and satisfaction shone forth.

Malati continued in the same tone, “And even more, it would be an insult to his manhood. What gentleman, upon being offered a cup of wine by a lady’s hand, would refuse? That would be an affront to womankind—the very womankind whose arrow-like glances every man longs to have pierce his heart, whose charms have driven even mighty kings to ruin. Bring forth the bottle and glasses, and let the round begin! On this great occasion, any doubt, any objection, is nothing less than treason to the nation. First, let us drink a toast to the health of our president.”

The ice, wine, and soda were already prepared. Malati handed Omkarnath a glass filled with the red poison, and looked at him with such an enchanting gaze that all his convictions, all his sense of caste superiority, melted away. His mind said, “All codes of conduct are subject to circumstance. Today you are poor, and when you see a motorcar raising dust, you are so angered you wish to smash it with stones—but do you not secretly long for a car yourself? Circumstance is fate, nothing else. My ancestors never drank, nor did they ever get the chance. Their livelihood was in scriptures and ledgers. Where would they have gotten wine, and even if they had, where would they have drunk it? Besides, they never boarded a train, never drank yesterday’s water, considered reading English a sin. How times have changed! If you cannot keep pace with the times, the times will leave you behind. Even if poison is offered by such delicate hands, it must be accepted with bowed head. The good fortune for which great kings yearn stands before me today—can I spurn it?”

He took the glass, bowed his head in gratitude, and drained it in a single breath. Then he looked at the others with proud eyes, as if to say, “Now do you believe me? Did you think I was just a timid pandit? Now none of you can dare call me arrogant or a hypocrite.”

The hall erupted in such a commotion that it was impossible to describe, as if a flock of nightingales had burst from a box. “Bravo, Deviji! What can one say! Wonderful, Miss Malati, wonderful! You have broken it, broken the salt law! Broken the fortress of dharma, shattered the vessel of tradition!”

--- Page 59 ---
As soon as the liquor passed Omkarnath’s throat, his wit grew voluble. Smiling, he said, “I have entrusted the treasure of my faith into Miss Malti’s delicate hands, and I am certain she will guard it as it deserves. For this blessing at her lotus feet, I could sacrifice a thousand such faiths.”

The hall rang with laughter.

The editor’s face bloomed with pleasure, his eyes lowered. Filling another glass, he declared, “This is a toast to Miss Malti’s health. Drink, all of you, and bless her.”

Once again, everyone emptied their glasses.

At that moment, Mirza Khushend brought a garland and placed it around the editor’s neck. He said, “Gentlemen, your humble servant has just composed a qasida in praise of our esteemed President. With your permission, may I recite it?”

Voices called from all sides, “Yes, yes, do recite it!”

Omkarnath was accustomed to drinking bhang, and his mind was used to its effects. The intoxication of bhang would come slowly, like a dream, and settle over his mind like a cloud. His consciousness remained intact. He knew that at such times his speech was most eloquent, his imagination most powerful. But the intoxication of liquor pounced on him like a lion and seized him. He meant to say one thing, but something else came out of his mouth. Soon, even this awareness vanished. He no longer knew what he was saying or doing. This was not the romantic strangeness of a dream, but the delirium of wakefulness, where the tangible dissolves into the intangible.

Somehow, the idea arose in his mind that reciting a qasida was a grave impropriety. Striking the table with his hand, he shouted, “No, absolutely not! No qasida will be recited here, none at all! I am the President. My order stands. I can dissolve this assembly right now. I can dissolve it! I can throw everyone out. No one can do anything to me. I am the President. There is no other President.”

Mirza folded his hands and said, “Huzoor, this qasida is in your praise.”

The editor looked at him with red, yet lifeless eyes. “Why did you praise me? Tell me, why did you praise me? I am no one’s servant. I am not anyone’s father’s servant, nor do I eat at anyone’s expense. I am the editor myself. I will praise everyone in it. Devi-ji, I will not praise you. I am not a great man. I am everyone’s slave. I am the dust of your feet. Malti Devi is my Lakshmi, my Saraswati, my Radha...”

Saying this, he bent towards Malti’s feet and collapsed face-first onto the floor. Mirza Khushend rushed to support him, moved the chairs aside, and laid him down on the ground. Then, bringing his mouth close to Omkarnath’s ear, he said, “Ram-naam satya hai. Shall we carry out your funeral procession?”

Raisahab remarked, “Just watch, he’ll take his revenge in his paper. He’ll lambaste each and every one of us, so thoroughly you’ll remember it. He’s a real scoundrel, shows no mercy to anyone. When it comes to writing, there’s no one like him. How such a donkey of a man writes so well is a mystery.”

Several men lifted the editor and carried him to his room. Meanwhile, the Dhanush-Yajna was underway in the pavilion. Several times, people had come to call them. Some officials had also arrived at the pavilion. People were preparing to go when suddenly an Afghan appeared. Fair-skinned, with large moustaches, tall stature, broad chest, and eyes brimming with fearlessness...

--- Page 60 ---
Wild-eyed, wearing a loose, low-hanging kurta, salwar on his legs, a vest embroidered with zari work, a turban and kulah on his head, a leather bag slung over his shoulder, a rifle resting on his hip and a sword strapped at his waist—who knows from where he appeared and stood before them, roaring, “Beware! No one is to leave from here. My companion has been attacked by dacoits. The authorities here have robbed my man, and you will have to return his goods. Every single coin must be repaid. Where is your leader? Call him here.”

Raisahab stepped forward, his voice brimming with anger. “What robbery? What dacoity? This is the work of your own people. No one here robs anyone. Speak plainly—what is the matter?”

The Afghan’s eyes bulged as he struck the butt of his rifle against the ground and declared, “You ask what robbery, what dacoity? You rob, your men rob. I am the owner of the kothi here. I have fifty men in my kothi. My man was bringing money for safekeeping—one thousand rupees. You have stolen it, and now you ask what dacoity? I’ll show you what dacoity is. Twenty-five of my men are on their way. We will loot your whole village. No one here can do a thing, not a thing.”

Seeing the Afghan’s fierce demeanor, Khanna quietly tried to slip away. The leader shouted, “Where are you going? No one leaves! If anyone tries, we’ll kill everyone. We’ll open fire. You can do nothing to us. We are not afraid of your police. The police run at the sight of us. We have our own consul—we can write to him and go to the Viceroy. We will not let anyone leave until our thousand rupees are returned. If you do not give our money, we will not leave anyone alive. You people rob others’ wealth and drink wine here with your beloveds.”

Miss Malti, trying to slip out unnoticed, was suddenly blocked as he sprang before her like a hawk. “You make these scoundrels return my money, or I will take you away with me and celebrate in my kothi. I have fallen in love with your beauty. Either give me my thousand rupees right now, or you must come with me. I will not let you go. I am your lover now. My heart and soul are bursting. I have twenty-five men here. In this district, five hundred of my men are at work. I am the Khan of my tribe. My tribe has ten thousand soldiers. I can fight the Amir of Kabul. The British Government pays me twenty thousand a year in tribute. If you do not return my money, I will loot the village and carry off your beloved. I take pleasure in bloodshed. I will make rivers of blood flow.”

Terror descended upon the gathering. Miss Malti forgot her usual cheerfulness. Khanna’s legs were trembling. Poor man, he lived in a single-story bungalow for fear of injury—climbing stairs was no less than mounting the gallows for him. Even in the heat, he slept indoors out of fear. Raisahab, proud of his Thakur heritage, would have considered it laughable to be afraid of a Pathan in his own village—but what could he do against that rifle? If he protested even a little and the man fired, what then? These people are reckless, and their aim is deadly. If he hadn’t had a rifle in his hand, Raisahab would have been ready to lock horns with him. The trouble was, the wretch wouldn’t let anyone leave. Otherwise, in no time, the whole village would have gathered and beaten his entire gang to a pulp.

At last, Raisahab steeled his heart and, risking his life, spoke, “We have already told you—we are not thieves or dacoits. I am a member of the local council, and this lady is a renowned doctor from Lucknow. All the people here are respectable and honorable. We have no knowledge at all—”

--- Page 61 ---
Who has robbed the people? Go and file a report at the police station.

Khan stamped his foot on the ground, shifted his stance, and, taking the gun off his shoulder, roared, “Don’t prattle on! I crush a council member under my feet like this!” (He grinds his heel into the earth.) “My hand is strong, my heart is strong. I fear no one but Khudatala. If you do not give me my money, then I—” (pointing at Raisaheb) “—will kill you right now.”

Seeing the barrel of the gun pointed at him, Raisaheb ducked down to the level of the table. What a strange predicament he was trapped in! The devil kept repeating, “You have robbed me of my money.” He would listen to nothing, understand nothing, and let no one enter or leave. The servants, the constables, everyone was absorbed in watching the bow-sacrifice. Zamindari servants are lazy and indolent as it is; unless called ten times, they won’t even speak, and now they were engaged in what they considered an auspicious task. The bow-sacrifice was not just a spectacle for them, but a divine play. If even one person had come this way, the constables would have gotten wind of it, and in a moment, all of Khan’s bravado would have vanished; every hair in his beard would have been plucked out. What a furious man! He is a butcher at heart—neither afraid of death nor delighted by life.

Mirza Sahib looked on with astonished eyes—What can I say? My mind is not working. Today, of all days, I left my pistol at home, or else I would have taught him a lesson.

Khanna, his face on the verge of tears, said, “Give him some money and somehow get rid of this calamity.”

Raisaheb looked at Malti—“Deviji, what is your advice now?”

Malti’s face was flushed with anger. She said, “What will happen? I am being so humiliated, and you all just sit and watch. With twenty men present, a single uncouth Pathan is disgracing me so, and not a drop of blood stirs in any of you. Is your life so precious? Why doesn’t someone go outside and raise an alarm? Why don’t you all rush him and snatch the gun from his hands? So he’ll fire the gun? Let him fire! At most, one or two lives will be lost. Let them be lost.”

But what Deviji found so easy—to die—others did not. If anyone dared to go outside and the Pathan, in his rage, fired off a few rounds, there would be a massacre here. At most, the police would sentence him to hang. Even that is uncertain—he is the leader of a powerful clan. The government would think twice before hanging him. Pressure would come from above. Who cares for justice in the face of politics? If counter-cases were filed against us and punitive police posted here, it would not be surprising. How merrily we were joking and laughing just moments ago! We would still be enjoying the drama. This devil has come and created a new calamity, and it seems he will not be satisfied without spilling some blood.

Khanna scolded Malti, “Deviji, you are rebuking us as if saving one’s own life is a sin. The love of life is found in every living creature, and if it is found in us, there is no shame in that. It pains me to see that you consider our lives so cheap. It’s only a matter of a thousand rupees. If you have a spare thousand, why don’t you give it to him and send him away? You are subjecting yourself to this humiliation—how is it our fault?”

Raisaheb, his temper rising, said, “If he lays a hand on Deviji, even if my corpse is left writhing here, I will fight him. After all, he is a man like any other.”

Mirza Sahib shook his head doubtfully and said, “Raisaheb, you still do not understand the temperament of these people.”

--- Page 62 ---
They are not aware. If he starts this game, he won’t leave anyone alive. His aim is unerring.

Poor Mr. Tankha had come to solve the problem of the upcoming elections. He was dreaming of going home after pocketing ten or twenty thousand rupees. Here, his very life was at stake. He said, “The simplest solution is the one Khanna-ji just suggested. It’s only a matter of a thousand rupees, and the money is available, so why are you all hesitating so much?”

Miss Malti looked at Tankha with eyes full of contempt.

“I never thought you people were such cowards.”

“I never thought you loved money so much—especially when it comes for free.”

“If you can bear to see me insulted, then surely you can bear to see the women of your own household insulted as well.”

“And you, for the sake of money, would not hesitate to sacrifice the men of your own house?”

Khan had been listening to their bickering with growing irritation. Suddenly, he thundered, “I will not agree now. I have been standing here for so long, and you people will not give the money.” (Taking a whistle from his pocket) “I give you one more moment. If you do not give the money, I will blow this whistle, and my twenty-five men will be here. That’s it.”

Then, filling his eyes with the fire of passion, he looked at Miss Malti.

“Will you come with me, beloved? I will be devoted to you. I will lay my life at your feet. So many men are your admirers, but none is a true lover. I will show you what true love is. At your signal, I could plunge a dagger into my own chest.”

Mirza pleaded, almost whimpering, “Devi-ji, for God’s sake, give this wretch the money.”

Khanna folded his hands in supplication, “Have mercy on us, Miss Malti!”

Raisahab straightened and declared, “Absolutely not. Whatever is to happen today, let it happen. Either we will die ourselves, or we will teach these tyrants a lesson they will never forget.”

Tankha scolded Raisahab, “It’s no bravery to walk into a lion’s den. I call it foolishness.”

But Miss Malti’s feelings were quite different. The desire-lit eyes of Khan had reassured her, and now she was beginning to enjoy the recklessness of the situation. Her heart, after spending some time among these men, was tempted to savor the wildness of his barbaric love. She had already experienced the weakness and lifelessness of genteel love. Today, her heart raced for the frenzied passion of these rugged, uncouth Pathans, just as after enjoying music, one might rush to watch a battle of intoxicated elephants.

She walked up to Khan Sahib and said, without a trace of fear, “You will not get the money.”

Khan reached out his hand, “Then I will carry you away.”

“You cannot take me away from amidst so many people.”

“I can take you away from amidst them.”

“You will have to pay with your life.”

“You will have to pay with your life.”

He grabbed Malti’s hand and pulled her. At that very moment, Hori stepped into the room. He was dressed as King Janak’s gardener, and his performance had the villagers rolling with laughter.

--- Page 63 ---
He wondered, why hasn’t the master come yet? He too should come and see how skilled these villagers are at this work. His friends should see it as well. How could he call the master? He was searching for an opportunity, and as soon as he found a moment, he slipped away and came here. But upon seeing the scene before him, he stood stunned. Everyone was silent, trembling, their anxious eyes fixed on Khan, who was pulling Malti towards himself. Hori’s simple mind quickly grasped the situation. At that very moment, Raisahib called out—“Hori, run and fetch the policemen, quickly!”

Hori had just turned to go when Khan leveled his gun at him and barked, “Where are you going, you swine? I’ll shoot you!”

Hori was a rustic man. The sight of a red turban would make his heart leap into his throat, but he would not hesitate to take up a stick and charge at a rampaging bull. He was no coward; he knew both how to kill and how to die. But before the tricks of the police, he was powerless—who wants to be dragged about in chains, where would he get the money for bribes, upon whom would he leave his wife and children? But when the master calls out, then what is there to fear? Then he could leap even into the jaws of death.

He lunged forward, grabbed Khan by the waist, and tripped him so hard that Khan fell flat on his back, cursing in Pashto. Hori climbed onto his chest and, grabbing his beard, gave it a mighty yank. The beard came away in his hand. Khan immediately tore off his cap and, with a forceful shove, stood up. Ah! It was Mr. Mehta. The very same.

The people surrounded Mehta from all sides. Some patted him on the back, but on Mr. Mehta’s face there was neither a smile nor pride. He stood silently, as if nothing had happened.

Malti said with feigned anger, “Where did you learn this trickery? My heart is still pounding.”

Mehta replied with a smile, “I was merely testing the bravery of these good men. If I have been impertinent, please forgive me.”

When this performance ended, the Dhanur-Yajna in the theater had also concluded, and preparations for a social farce were underway. But these gentlemen had little interest in that. Only Mr. Mehta went to watch and stayed from beginning to end. He was thoroughly enjoying himself. From time to time he clapped and, urging the actors with “Say it again, say it again,” encouraged them further. In this farce, Raisahib had caricatured a litigious rural zamindar. It was called a farce, but it was filled with pathos. The protagonist’s constant references to legal statutes, filing a lawsuit against his wife simply because she was a little late in preparing his meal, the antics of the lawyers, the cunning and tricks of the village witnesses—how they were quick to agree to testify, but when it came time to appear in court, they would demand all sorts of favors and make fools of everyone—these scenes had the audience rolling with laughter. The finest scene was when the lawyer was coaching the witnesses on their statements. The witnesses kept making mistakes, the lawyer grew frustrated, then the protagonist explained things to them in rustic dialect, and finally, in court, the witnesses changed their stories entirely. It was so vivid and true to life that Mr. Mehta jumped up and, when the play ended, embraced the protagonist and announced a medal for each of the actors. A feeling of reverence for Raisahib welled up in his heart. Raisahib was backstage, directing the play.

--- Page 64 ---
Mehta ran up and embraced him, exclaiming with delight, “I had no idea your gaze was so keen.”

The next day, after breakfast, there was a plan for a hunt. The idea was to prepare a meal right there on the bank of some river in the forest, to frolic in the water, and return home by evening. They would savor the pleasures of rural life. The guests who had pressing matters took their leave; only those remained who were close to Rai Sahib. Mrs. Khanna had a headache and could not go, and the editor, smoldering with resentment toward this group, was preoccupied with the idea of launching a series of articles to expose them.

“All of them are seasoned ruffians,” he thought. “They squander ill-gotten wealth and swagger about. What do they know of the world’s troubles? Who dies in their neighborhood—what do they care? Their only concern is their own indulgence and luxury. This Mehta, who struts about as a philosopher, is obsessed with making life ‘complete.’ If you earn a thousand rupees a month, you have the luxury to make your life whole or perfect. But what about the man who is crushed by worries—how to marry off his sons, how to summon a vaidya for his ailing wife, or how to pay next month’s rent? How is he to make his life complete? Like stray bulls, you trample others’ fields and imagine that all is well in the world. Your eyes will only open when the revolution comes and you are told, ‘Come, boy, take up the plough and till the fields.’ Then we’ll see how complete your life is.

“And that Malti—she’s tasted water from seventy-two streams and still parades as ‘Miss.’ She won’t marry, claiming that marriage is bondage, and in bondage life loses its fullness. Just keep all attachments at bay, and your life is complete. What could be easier? Can’t get along with your parents? Defy them. Don’t marry—marriage is bondage. Children are fetters of affection. But then, why do you pay taxes? The law is also a bondage—why not break that? Why do you skirt around it? You know well that the slightest disobedience to the law will land you in chains. No, you only break those bonds that hinder your pleasures. You beat the rope, imagining it a snake, and strut about as heroes. But when you see a real snake, you flee at the first hiss. This is your ‘complete’ life.”

At eight o’clock, the hunting party set out. Khanna had never hunted before—he trembled at the sound of a gun—but how could he stay behind when Miss Malti was going? Mr. Tankha had not yet found an opportunity to discuss the election; perhaps he would get the chance there. Rai Sahib had not visited this part of his estate in a long time; he wished to see how things stood. Sometimes, visiting the estate helps maintain relationships with the people and keeps up one’s authority. The clerks and peons also remain alert. Mirza Khurshid was eager for new experiences, especially those that required a bit of courage. How could Miss Malti stay alone? She needed a crowd of admirers. Only Mr. Mehta was going with genuine enthusiasm for the hunt. Rai Sahib wished to take along all the provisions, cooks, porters, and servants, but Mr. Mehta objected.

Khanna said, “But after all, are we to eat there or starve?”

Mehta replied, “Why shouldn’t we eat? But today, we shall do all our work ourselves. We should see whether we can survive without servants or not. Miss Malti will cook, and we shall eat. In the villages, you can always find pots and leaf plates, and there’s never any shortage of firewood.”

--- Page 65 ---
We shall go hunting, no matter what.

Malati complained, “Forgive me. Last night, you gripped my wrist so tightly that it still hurts.”

“We will do the work,” someone replied, “you just keep giving us directions.”

Mirza Khurshed said cheerfully, “Oh, you all just enjoy the spectacle, I’ll take care of all the arrangements. What’s the big deal? Searching for pots and pans in the jungle is foolishness. Hunt a deer, roast it, eat it, and then take a nap in the shade of a tree.”

This proposal was accepted. Two cars set off. One was driven by Miss Malati herself, the other by Rai Sahib. After twenty or twenty-five miles, the hilly region began. High mountain ranges ran on both sides. The road grew more winding. After climbing for a while, suddenly there was a descent, and the car rolled downward. From afar, the riverbed could be seen, weak and motionless like a sick man. The cars were stopped under the shade of a large banyan tree on the bank, and everyone got out. It was decided that they would split into pairs, go hunting, and return here by noon.

Miss Malati was ready to go with Mehta. Khanna was left sulking. The plan he had come with seemed to have been punctured. If he had known Malati would betray him, he would have gone home. But though Rai Sahib’s company was not as delightful, it was not unpleasant either. There were many matters of business to discuss with him. Khurshed and Tanka paired off together—their team was already formed. The three groups set off in different directions.

After walking a short distance along the stony path with Mehta, Malati said, “You just keep going. Let me catch my breath.”

Mehta smiled, “We haven’t even walked a mile yet. Are you already tired?”

“Not tired, but why not rest for a bit?”

“Until we find some game, we have no right to rest.”

“I didn’t come here to hunt.”

Feigning ignorance, Mehta asked, “Oh? I didn’t know that. Then what did you come for?”

“How can I tell you?”

A herd of deer appeared, grazing. Both of them hid behind a rock, took aim, and fired. The shot missed. The herd fled.

Malati asked, “Now what?”

“Nothing. Let’s go, we’ll find more game.”

They walked on in silence for a while. Then Malati paused and said, “This heat is unbearable. Come, let’s sit under this tree.”

“Not yet. If you want to sit, go ahead. I won’t sit.”

“You are so cruel, truly.”

“Until we find some game, I cannot sit.”

“Then you’ll kill me for sure. Tell me, why did you torment me so much last night? I was very angry with you. Do you remember what you said to me? ‘Will you come with us, darling?’ I never knew you could be so mischievous. Tell me honestly, would you have taken me with you then?”

Mehta gave no reply, as if he hadn’t heard.

They walked on a little further. The blazing summer sun, the rocky path—Malati, exhausted, sat down.

--- Page 66 ---
She left.

Mehta remained standing and said, “Very well, you take some rest. I’ll come back here.”

“You’ll leave me alone?”

“I know you can take care of yourself.”

“How do you know?”

“That is the very quality of the women of this new age. They do not seek a man’s protection; they wish to walk shoulder to shoulder with him.”

Malti, blushing, said, “You are a pure philosopher, Mehta, truly.”

On a tree ahead, a peacock was perched. Mehta took aim and fired his gun; the peacock flew away.

Malti, delighted, said, “That’s very good. My curse worked.”

Mehta slung the gun over his shoulder and said, “You have not cursed me, but yourself. Had I gotten the prey, I would have given you ten minutes’ grace. Now you must leave at once.”

Malti stood up, took Mehta’s hand, and said, “Perhaps philosophers have no hearts. It’s good you never married. You would have killed that poor creature, but I will not let you off so easily. You cannot leave me behind.”

Mehta shook her hand off with a jerk and walked ahead.

Malti’s eyes filled with tears as she said, “I’m telling you, don’t go. Otherwise, I’ll dash my head against this rock.”

Mehta quickened his pace. Malti watched him go. When he had gone about twenty steps, she rose in irritation and ran after him. There was no joy in resting alone.

Catching up, she said, “I did not take you for such a brute.”

“The skin of the deer I kill, I shall present to you.”

“Let the skin go to hell. I won’t speak to you anymore.”

“If we catch nothing and the others get good game, I’ll be terribly embarrassed.”

A wide ravine yawned before them, its rocks in the middle like jagged teeth. The current was so swift that the waves leapt and danced. The sun had reached its zenith, and its thirsty rays played upon the water.

Malti said cheerfully, “Now we’ll have to turn back.”

“Why? We’ll cross to the other side. That’s where the game is.”

“The current is so strong! I won’t go.”

“Very well! You sit here, I’ll go.”

“Yes, you go. I have no quarrel with my own life.”

Mehta stepped into the water and, steadying his feet, began to cross. The farther he went, the deeper it became, until the water reached his chest.

Malti grew anxious. Her mind was restless with worry, a kind of agitation she had never felt before. She called out loudly, “The water is deep! Stop, I’m coming too.”

“No, no, you’ll slip. The current is strong.”

“No matter, I’m coming. Don’t go any farther, I warn you.”

Malti lifted her sari and stepped into the stream. But this time, she was determined.

--- Page 67 ---
He came.

Mehta grew anxious. Motioning with both hands for her to go back, he said, “Don’t come here, Malati! The water is up to your neck here.”

Malati took another step forward and said, “Let it be. If it is your wish that I should die, then I will die right here, by your side.”

Malati was waist-deep in the water. The current was so strong that it seemed her footing would slip at any moment. Mehta turned back and caught hold of Malati with one hand.

With intoxicated eyes filled with anger, Malati said, “I have never seen a heartless man like you. You are completely made of stone. Well, torment me today as much as you wish; one day, I too will understand.”

Malati’s feet seemed to falter. Clutching the rifle, she clung to him.

Mehta reassured her, “You cannot stand here. Let me carry you on my shoulders.”

Malati frowned and said, “Is it really so necessary to cross to the other side?”

Mehta gave no reply. He slung the rifle from his temple to his shoulder and, lifting Malati with both hands, seated her on his shoulders.

Trying to hide her delight, Malati said, “What if someone sees us? It would look so improper.”

After two steps, she said in a plaintive voice, “Tell me, if I were to drown here, would you feel any sorrow or not? I think you wouldn’t feel any sorrow at all.”

Mehta replied in a wounded tone, “Do you think I am not human?”

“That’s exactly what I think. Why should I hide it?”

“Is that the truth, Malati?”

“What do you think?”

“I? Someday I’ll tell you.”

The water had reached Mehta’s neck. With the next step, it might rise to his head. Malati’s heart began to pound. She said, “Mehta, for God’s sake, don’t go any further, or I’ll jump into the water.”

In that moment of crisis, Malati remembered God, whom she used to mock. She knew there was no God sitting somewhere who would come to save them, but where else could the mind find the support and strength it needed?

The water began to recede. Malati, delighted, said, “Now you can put me down.”

“No, no, stay seated quietly. There might be a pit ahead.”

“You must think, how selfish this woman is.”

“Then pay me for my labor.”

A tickle ran through Malati’s heart.

“What payment will you take?”

“Only this: when you find yourself in such a situation in life, call me.”

They reached the shore. Malati wrung the water from her sari on the sand, emptied her shoes, washed her face and hands, but those words, with their mysterious meaning, kept dancing before her eyes.

Savoring the joy of this experience, she said, “I will remember this day.”

--- Page 68 ---
Mehta asked, “Were you very frightened?”

“At first I was afraid,” Malti replied, “but then I became certain that you could protect us both.”

Mehta looked at Malti with pride—his face glowed with the flush of exertion and a certain radiance.

“How happy it makes me to hear you say that, Malti—can you understand?”

“When did you ever reassure me?” she retorted. “On the contrary, you drag me through forests and wilds, and now, on the way back, we’ll have to cross this stream again. You’ve put us in such danger. If I had to stay with you, I wouldn’t last a single day.”

Mehta smiled. He understood well the meaning behind her words.

“So you think me such a brute? And what if I say that I love you—will you marry me?”

“Who would marry someone so wooden and hard? You’d burn me to ashes, day and night.” And she looked at him with sweet eyes, as if to say—You know very well what I mean. You’re not such a fool.

Mehta, as if suddenly alert, said, “You speak the truth, Malti! I could never keep a woman happy. No woman could pretend to love me. I would reach into the depths of her soul. And then I would lose interest.”

Malti shivered. There was so much truth in those words.

She asked, “Tell me, what kind of love would satisfy you?”

“Only this—that what is in the heart should be on the lips. For me, beauty, gestures, coquettishness, all those airs and graces—they matter only as much as they should. I want the nourishment that satisfies the soul. I have no need for intoxicating or stimulating things.”

Malti pursed her lips and drew a breath. “No one will be able to deal with you. You’re a sly one. Well then, tell me—what do you think of me?”

Mehta smiled mischievously. “You can do anything—you are intelligent, clever, talented, kind, lively, proud, capable of sacrifice—but you cannot love.”

Malti looked at him sharply. “You’re a liar, an utter liar. This claim of yours, that you reach the heart of a woman, seems empty to me.”

They walked along the edge of the stream. It was past midnight, but now Malti had no desire to think, nor to return. In today’s conversation, she felt a joy that was entirely new to her. She had, with a single smile, a single day, a single sweet phrase, made fools of many scholars and leaders. She could not attribute this feeling to God or fate. Today, from this hard, solid, cold-as-stone man, sparks were flying, and his very hardness drew her in more and more.

A gunshot rang out. A reddish bird was flying over the stream. Wounded, it flew a little farther, then fell into the current and began to drift with the waves.

“And now?”

“I’ll go fetch it. Where can it go?”

Saying this, he ran into the sand, shouting, and began to swim toward the bird, but he could not reach it.

Even in death, the bird seemed to fly on.

--- Page 69 ---
Suddenly, they saw a young woman emerge from a hut on the bank. Seeing the bird struggling in the water, she lifted her sari up to her thighs and waded in. In a moment, she caught the bird and, showing it to Mehta, called out, “Come out of the water, Babuji, here is your bird.” Mehta was enchanted by the girl’s agility and courage. He immediately struck out for the shore and, within two minutes, was standing beside her.

The girl’s skin was dark—deeply so. Her clothes were extremely dirty and shabby; as for ornaments, she wore only two thick bangles on each wrist. Her hair was tangled and unkempt, falling in separate locks. There was not a single feature of her face that could be called beautiful or graceful, yet the pure, unspoiled air of this place had lent a certain charm to her darkness, and, nurtured in the lap of nature, her limbs had grown so shapely, sturdy, and free that no better image of youth could be found. Her robust health seemed to fill Mehta’s heart with strength and vigor.

Thanking her, Mehta said, “You arrived at just the right moment. Otherwise, who knows how far I would have had to swim.”

The girl replied cheerfully, “I saw you swimming over, so I ran. You must have come here to hunt?”

“Yes, we came to hunt, but it’s already noon, and this is the only bird we’ve found.”

“If you want to hunt a leopard, I can show you its trail. It comes here to drink water every night. Sometimes, it even comes in the afternoon.”

Then, lowering her head a little shyly, she added, “But you’ll have to give us the skin. Come to my doorstep. There’s shade under the pipal tree. How long will you stand here in the sun? Your clothes are wet, too.”

Mehta, glancing at her sari clinging to her body, said, “Your clothes are wet as well.”

She replied carelessly, “Oh, what of it? We’re people of the jungle. We stand in the sun and water all day. You city folk couldn’t manage that.”

How sensible this girl is, and yet so utterly rustic.

“What will you do with the skin?”

“Our grandfather sells it in the bazaar. That’s our livelihood.”

“But if we spend the afternoon here, what will you feed us?”

The girl, blushing, said, “What is there in our house fit for you to eat? If you’ll eat maize rotis, there are some at home. I’ll cook a curry with the bird. You just tell me how to prepare it. There’s a little milk, too. Once, a leopard attacked our cow. She drove it away with her horns, and since then, the leopard is afraid of her.”

“But I’m not alone. There’s a woman with me.”

“Is she your wife?”

“No, not my wife—just an acquaintance.”

“Then I’ll run and call her. You go and sit in the shade.”

“No, no, I’ll call her myself.”

“You must be tired. Why would city people come to the jungle? We’re wild folk. She must be standing right by the shore.”

Before Mehta could say anything, she was gone like the wind. Mehta climbed up and sat in the shade of the pipal tree. This free, untrammeled life stirred a longing in his heart. The mountain range before him stood like a symbol of spiritual truth.

--- Page 70 ---
Vast and boundless, as if expanding knowledge itself, as if the soul were beholding that knowledge, that light, that unfathomable mystery in its manifest, immense form. On a distant, towering peak stood a small temple, high above like the intellect itself, yet lost and solitary—as if a bird, having flown so far, wished to rest there but could find no place to settle.

Mehta was absorbed in these thoughts when the young woman arrived, bringing Miss Malti with her. One, blooming in the sunlight like a wildflower; the other, wilted and lifeless like a potted plant deprived of the sun.

Malti said to Vedili, “The shade of the pipal tree must feel very pleasant, doesn’t it? And here I am, dying of hunger.”

The young woman brought two large pitchers and said, “You sit here for now. I’ll run and fetch water, then light the stove. If you eat what I cook, I’ll have the rotis ready in a moment. Otherwise, you can make them yourself. There’s no wheat flour in my house, and there isn’t a shop anywhere nearby where I could get some.”

Malti was growing irritated with Mehta. She said, “Why have you dragged us all the way out here?”

Mehta teased her, “For once, enjoy a taste of this life. See how delicious these maize rotis are.”

“I simply can’t eat maize rotis,” Malti replied. “And even if I somehow manage to swallow them, I won’t be able to digest them. I’m really regretting coming with you. You made me run the whole way, and now you’ve dumped me here.”

Mehta had taken off his outer clothes and sat wearing only a blue loincloth. When he saw the young woman carrying the pitchers away, he took them from her and went to the well to fetch water. Even in his deep study of philosophy, he had maintained his health, and as he walked with a pitcher in each hand, his muscular, broad chest and well-shaped thighs resembled the sculpted limbs of a Greek statue, bearing witness to his manliness. The young woman watched him draw water with eyes full of affection. He was no longer an object of pity, but of reverence.

The well was very deep, some sixty feet, and the pitchers were heavy. Despite Mehta’s habit of exercising, he grew weary drawing up the first pitcher. The young woman ran over, snatched the rope from his hands, and said, “You won’t be able to manage this. Go sit on the cot; I’ll draw the water.”

Mehta could not bear this affront to his manhood. He took the rope back from her and, mustering his strength, drew up the second pitcher in a single effort. Carrying both pitchers, he stood at the doorway of the hut. The young woman quickly lit the fire, singed the feathers off the partridge, cut it into pieces with a knife, set the meat to cook over the fire, and began boiling milk in a pan on the other side of the stove.

Meanwhile, Malti, her brows furrowed, lay on the cot, watching the scene with a sullen heart, as if preparations were being made for her operation.
