Standing at the doorway, Mehta watched the young woman’s domestic skill with eyes full of affection and said, “Give me something to do as well. What should I do?”

The young woman replied with a gentle chiding, “You don’t have to do anything. Go sit with Bai; the poor thing is very hungry. The milk is almost hot—give it to her.”

She took flour from a jar and began to knead it. Mehta watched the graceful movements of her limbs. The young woman, too, would glance at him from the corner of her eye now and then, then return to her work.

--- Page 71 ---
Malati called out, “Why are you standing over there? I have a terrible headache. Half my head feels as if it’s about to split open.”

Mehta came over and said, “It seems you’ve got a touch of sunstroke.”

“How was I to know you were bringing me here to kill me?”

“You haven’t brought any medicine with you, have you?”

“Was I coming to see a patient that I should carry medicines with me? I have a box of medicines, but it’s in Semri. Uff! My head is splitting.”

Mehta sat down on the ground beside her and began to gently massage her head.

Malati closed her eyes.

A young woman, her hands dusted with flour, hair disheveled, eyes reddened and moist from smoke, her whole body drenched in sweat so that her full bosom was clearly visible, came and stood nearby. Seeing Malati lying there with her eyes closed, she asked, “What’s happened to the memsahib?”

Mehta replied, “She has a severe headache.”

“Is it in the whole head or just one side?”

“She says it’s on one side.”

“Right or left?”

“Left.”

“I’ll run and fetch a medicine. As soon as it’s applied, the pain will go away.”

“Where will you go in this blazing sun?”

The young woman didn’t even hear him. Swiftly, she disappeared among the hills. About half an hour later, Mehta saw her climbing a high hill. From a distance, she looked just like a little doll. He thought to himself—what a spirit of service and practical knowledge this wild girl has. In this scorching heat and blazing sun, she’s scaling the sky itself.

Malati opened her eyes and asked, “Where’s that dark girl gone? She’s black as ebony, like a chunk of coal. Send her to tell Rai Sahib to send the car here. In this heat, I’ll suffocate.”

“She’s gone to fetch some medicine. She says it cures half-headaches very quickly.”

“Their medicines work only for them, not for me. You’ve become quite smitten with this girl, haven’t you? How frivolous you are! Like attracts like.”

Mehta never hesitated to speak the bitter truth.

“There are some qualities in her that, if you possessed them, you would truly be a goddess.”

“Let her keep her virtues; I have no desire to become a goddess.”

“If you wish, I’ll go and fetch the car, though I can’t say if it can even get here.”

“Why don’t you send that dark girl?”

“She’s gone to get medicine, and then she’ll cook the meal.”

“So today you’re her guest? Perhaps you’re planning to stay the night as well. Night is the best time for hunting, after all.”

Annoyed by this accusation, Mehta said, “The love and respect I feel for this young woman is such that if I were to look at her with desire, may I go blind. Even among my closest friends—”

--- Page 72 ---
would not have gone up that high hill in this scorching sun and fierce wind, not even for herself. And we are only guests here for a short while—she knows this. Yet she would run with the same eagerness for any poor woman. I can only write essays and give speeches about universal brotherhood and love, but she can actually live that love and self-sacrifice. To do is far harder than to say. You know this as well.

Malti said mockingly, “Enough, enough—she’s a goddess, I admit it. She has a full bosom, heavy hips—what more does one need to be a goddess?”

Mehta was stung. He immediately got up, put on his clothes, which had dried, picked up the gun, and prepared to leave. Malti flared up, “You cannot go, leaving me here alone.”

“Then who will go?”

“She will—your goddess.”

Mehta stood bewildered. Today, for the first time in his life, he realized how easily a woman can conquer a man.

She came running, panting—the same dark-skinned young woman, holding a bunch of herbs in her hand. Coming near and seeing Mehta ready to leave, she said, “I have found the herb. I will grind it and apply it now, but where are you going? The meat must be cooked by now, I’ll bake the rotis. Eat a couple. Bai can drink the milk. Once it cools, you can go.”

Without hesitation, she began unbuttoning Mehta’s achkan. Mehta restrained himself with difficulty; he felt like kissing the feet of this village woman.

Malti said, “Leave your medicine. Our car is parked by the riverbank, under the banyan tree. There will be others there. Tell them to bring the car here. Run along.”

The young woman looked at Mehta with pleading eyes. She had brought the herb with such effort, and now it was being disregarded! If they did not trust this village woman’s medicine, so be it, but would it have hurt to let her apply a little, just to please her?

She placed the herb on the ground and asked, “By then the stove will have gone cold, Bai-ji. Shall I bake the rotis and keep them ready? Babu-ji can eat, you can drink the milk, and both of you can rest. Meanwhile, I’ll fetch the car.”

She went into the hut, rekindled the dead fire. She saw the meat had boiled, some of it had even burned. She quickly baked the rotis, cooled the hot milk, and brought it to Malti in a bowl. Malti made a face at the ugliness of the bowl, but could not resist the milk. Mehta sat at the hut’s doorway, eating meat and rotis from a plate. The young woman stood fanning them.

Malti said to her, “Let him eat. He’s not running away anywhere. You go and bring the car.”

The young woman looked at Malti with questioning eyes—what did she want? What was her intention? She did not see on Malti’s face the humility, gratitude, or pleading of the sick. Instead, there was a glimmer of pride and arrogance. The village woman was shrewd in reading emotions. She said, “I am not anyone’s servant, Bai-ji! You are great, the mistress of your house. I do not come to you for anything. I will not go to fetch the car.”

Malti scolded, “So, you have decided to be insolent! Tell me, whose land do you live on?”

“This is Rai Sahib’s land.”

“Then I’ll have Rai Sahib whip you with his hunters!”

“If it pleases you to have me beaten, then do so, Bai-ji. No queen or empress—”

--- Page 73 ---
A regiment will have to be sent.”

Mehta had just swallowed a few morsels when he heard Malti’s words. The food stuck in his throat. He quickly washed his hands and said, “She will not go. I am going.”

Malti also stood up. “She will have to go.”

Mehta replied in English, “By insulting her, Malti, you are not increasing your own honor.”

Malti retorted sharply, “Men only like such maidservants, whether they have any other virtue or not, who run about at their bidding with a cheerful heart and consider themselves fortunate that this man has asked them to do something. They are goddesses, embodiments of power, of greatness. I thought at least you were not lacking in manliness, but inside, in your very nature, you are just as barbaric as the rest.”

Mehta was a scholar of psychology. He understood the mysteries of Malti’s mind. Never before had he seen such a unique example of jealousy. In this woman, who was so gentle by nature, so generous, so cheerful, such a fierce flame of envy!

He said, “Say what you will, I will not let her go. I cannot reward her services and kindnesses in this way and become base in my own eyes.”

There was such firmness in Mehta’s voice that Malti quietly stood up, ready to leave. She said, burning with anger, “Fine, then I will go. You can worship at her feet and follow behind.”

Malti walked two or three steps ahead, and Mehta said to the young woman, “Now give me leave, sister. Your affection, your selfless service, I will always remember.”

The young woman, her eyes brimming with tears, folded her hands and bowed to him, then went inside the hut.

* * *

The second group consisted of Raisahib and Khanna. Raisahib was dressed in his usual silk kurta and silk shawl, but Khanna had worn a hunting suit, which perhaps had been tailored especially for today, for where would Khanna, always busy hunting down tenants, find time to hunt animals? Khanna was short, slender, and handsome, with wheat-colored skin, large eyes, pockmarks on his face, and a great skill in conversation.

After walking for some time, Khanna brought up the topic of Mr. Mehta, who had been preying on his mind like a demon since yesterday.

He said, “That Mehta is a strange fellow. He seems a bit artificial to me.”

Raisahib respected Mehta and considered him a sincere and honest man, but he had business dealings with Khanna and was by nature peace-loving, so he did not contradict him. He said, “I consider him merely a source of entertainment. I never argue with him, and even if I wanted to, where would I find such learning? Someone who has never set foot in the field of life, if he starts propounding new theories about life, I can only laugh. He draws a thousand rupees a month with ease. No wife, no children, no worries, no obstacles—if he doesn’t indulge in philosophy, who will? Living free of all conflicts, he dreams of perfecting life. What’s the point of arguing with such a man?”

“I’ve heard his character isn’t good.”

“How can one’s character remain good in such carefreeness? Live in society, fulfill its duties and boundaries, then you’ll see.”

“Who knows what Malti sees in him that she’s so infatuated?”

--- Page 74 ---
“I think she’s only trying to make you jealous.”

“How could she make me jealous? Poor thing! I consider her nothing more than a plaything.”

“Don’t say that, Mr. Khanna. You’re quite taken with Miss Malti.”

“If that’s so, I could say the same about you.”

“I truly see her as a plaything. But you—you’ve made her into an idol.”

Khanna burst out laughing, though there was nothing particularly funny.

“If a single offering of water could bring a boon, what harm is there in that?”

Now Rai Saheb laughed loudly too, though there was no real reason.

“Then you haven’t really understood that goddess. The more you worship her, the further she’ll run from you. The more you run from her, the more she’ll chase after you.”

“In that case, she should be running after you.”

“After me! I am entirely outside that circle of pleasure-seekers, Mr. Khanna, I swear. Whatever intelligence and strength I possess, it is all spent in managing this estate. Every member of my household is absorbed in their own pursuits—some in worship, some in sensual pleasure. One is lost in this, another in that. And it is my duty to feed all these pythons. Many of my fellow landlords indulge in luxury, I know this well. But they burn down their own houses just to watch the spectacle. The burden of debt grows heavier on their heads, decrees are issued every day. They borrow but never repay, and are infamous everywhere. I would rather die than live such a life. I don’t know what samskara keeps a spark of life burning in my soul, binding me to my country and society. When the Satyagraha movement broke out, all my brothers were lost in drink and pleasure. I could not restrain myself. I went to jail, bore the burden of fines worth lakhs of rupees, and am still paying off the damages. I have no regrets—none at all. In fact, I am proud of it. I do not consider a man truly human who does not strive and sacrifice for the good of his country and society. Do you think I enjoy sucking the blood of helpless peasants just to satisfy the desires of my family? But what can I do? The very system in which I was raised and have lived—I despise it, yet I cannot break free from its hold. I am caught in its spinning wheel day and night, trying somehow to preserve my honor and not murder my soul. Such a man, Mr. Khanna, could never chase after Miss Malti—or any Miss, for that matter. And if he did, it would mean his utter ruin. A little amusement, perhaps—that’s another matter.”

Mr. Khanna too was a man of courage, one to step forward in battle. He had been to jail twice. He knew no fear. He didn’t wear khadi, and drank French wine. When the occasion demanded, he could endure great hardship. In jail, he didn’t touch a drop of liquor, and though he was entitled to A-class privileges, he ate the coarse bread of C-class prisoners. He could have enjoyed every comfort, but even a chariot going to war cannot run without oil. A little pleasure was essential to his life. He said, “You could become a renunciant, but I could not. I believe that he who has not tasted pleasure cannot enter the struggle with full enthusiasm. He who cannot love a woman—I cannot trust his love for his country.”

Rai Saheb smiled. “Now you’re taunting me.”

“It’s not a taunt, it’s a matter of principle.”

“Perhaps it is.”

“Look within your own heart, and you’ll see.”

--- Page 75 ---
“I have seen it from within, and I assure you, whatever other vices may exist there, there is no lust for sensual pleasure.”

“Then I feel pity for you. The only reason you are so unhappy, so despondent, and so anxious, is your restraint. As for me, I will play out this drama, even if it ends in tragedy. She mocks me, pretends she doesn’t care for me, but I am not one to lose heart. I still haven’t understood her temperament. I cannot tell where my aim will strike true.”

“But perhaps you will never find that key. Mehta might outplay you.”

A stag was grazing with several does, its antlers large, its coat jet black. Raisahab took aim. Khanna stopped him—“Why kill, my friend? The poor creature is just grazing, let it be.” The sun grew harsh. “Come, let’s sit somewhere. There are some things I wish to discuss with you.”

Raisahab fired his gun, but the stag escaped. He said, “Even when I had a target, my shot missed.”

“At least you were spared one killing.”

“Do you grow sugarcane in your area?”

“With great effort.”

“Then why not join our sugar mill? Shares are selling rapidly. You need not buy many, just a thousand shares.”

“Good heavens, where would I get so much money?”

“A zamindar of such repute, and short of money! It’s only fifty thousand rupees. And even then, you only need to pay twenty-five percent right now.”

“No, brother, I have absolutely no money at the moment.”

“Take as much as you need from me. The bank is yours. And, by the way, you probably haven’t insured your life yet. Take a good policy from my company. You can easily pay a hundred or two hundred rupees a month. And you’ll receive a lump sum—forty or fifty thousand. There’s no better arrangement for your sons. Look at our rules. We operate entirely on the principle of cooperation. Except for office and staff expenses, not a single paisa of profit goes into anyone’s pocket. You’ll be surprised that the company runs on such a policy! And on my advice, start a little speculation as well. All these hundreds of millionaires today—every one of them made their fortune through speculation. Cotton, sugar, wheat, rubber—speculate on any commodity. In minutes, fortunes are made and lost. The work is a bit tricky. Many people get burned, but only those who are inexperienced. For experienced, educated, and farsighted people like you, there is no better way to profit. The fluctuations of the market are not random events. There is a science to it. Once you observe it closely, there’s no way you’ll be deceived.”

Raisahab distrusted companies; he had suffered bitter experiences once or twice. But he had seen Mr. Khanna’s rise with his own eyes and had become a devotee of his capability. Just ten years ago, the man who was a clerk in a bank was now, through sheer diligence, effort, and talent, revered in the city. His advice could not be disregarded. If Khanna became his guide in this matter, he could achieve much success. Why let such an opportunity slip by? He continued to ask all sorts of questions.

--- Page 76 ---
Suddenly, a villager appeared, carrying a large basket filled with roots, leaves, and some fruits.

Khanna asked, “Hey, what are you selling?”

The villager was startled, afraid he might be pressed into forced labor. He replied, “Nothing, sahib! Just some grass and leaves.”

“What will you do with them?”

“I’ll sell them, sahib! They are medicinal herbs.”

“What kinds of herbs? Tell me.”

The villager opened up his little pharmacy and showed its contents. They were ordinary things, the kind that forest folk dig up and bring to the city, selling them to quacks for a few annas—like makoy, kanghi, sahdeiya, kukraunde, datura seeds, madar flowers, karje, ghumchi, and so on. He showed each item and, in memorized words, described its virtues: “This is makoy, sarkar! For fever, indigestion, spleen trouble, palpitations, colic, cough—one dose brings relief. These are datura seeds, malik! For rheumatism, sciatica…”

Khanna asked the price. The villager said eight annas. Khanna tossed him a rupee and ordered him to deliver the basket to the camp. The poor man, who had not expected even the price, received double. He went away showering blessings.

Raisahab asked, “What will you do with these grasses and leaves?”

Khanna smiled and said, “I’ll turn them into gold coins. I am an alchemist, you see. Perhaps you didn’t know.”

“Then, my friend, teach me that mantra too.”

“Yes, yes, with pleasure. Become my disciple. First, bring me one and a quarter seers of laddus as an offering, then I’ll tell you. The thing is, I come across all sorts of people. Some are so devoted to herbs that if they learn this is a remedy given by a fakir, they’ll flatter you, beg you, and if you give it to them, they’ll be indebted to you for life. If, for one rupee, you can drape the shawl of favor over ten or twenty petitioners, what’s the harm? With a little kindness, great things can be accomplished.”

Raisahab asked with curiosity, “But how do you remember the properties of all these herbs?”

Khanna burst out laughing. “Oh, Raisahab! You say the most delightful things. Just ascribe whatever property you wish to any herb—it all depends on your own cleverness. Health is eight annas of faith in a rupee. All these high officials you see, these scholars with long tails, these aristocrats—they are all superstitious. I know a professor of botany who doesn’t even recognize kukraunde. Our Swamiji mocks such scholars mercilessly. You’ve probably never met him. Next time you come, I’ll introduce you. Since he’s been staying in my garden, there’s been a constant stream of visitors, day and night. Maya hasn’t touched him at all. He drinks only one bowl of milk a day. I’ve never seen such a learned soul. Who knows how many years he spent in penance in the Himalayas. He is a true siddha. The moment he sees you, he’ll tell you your past and future. His face is so radiant that your heart blooms at the sight of him. What’s astonishing is that, though he’s such a great mahatma, he calls renunciation and asceticism, temples and monasteries, sects and creeds—all of it hypocrisy, mere sham. ‘Break the chains of tradition and become human,’ he says. ‘Forget about becoming a god. By becoming a god, you cease to be human!’

--- Page 77 ---
Raisahab felt a pang of doubt. He, too, had that faith in holy men which is common among those in power. He yearned for the peace that comes to the suffering soul through self-reflection. Whenever he was disheartened by financial troubles, he would think of turning away from the world, sitting in solitude, and contemplating salvation. Like ordinary men, he saw the bonds of the world as obstacles on the path to self-realization, and to be free from them was his own ideal as well. But without renunciation and sacrifice, what other way is there to break these bonds?

"But if he calls renunciation a sham, then why did he himself renounce the world?"

"When did he renounce the world, sahab? He says a man should work till the very end. Freedom of thought is the essence of his teachings."

"I do not understand any of this. What does freedom of thought mean?"

"I do not understand it either. When you come next, you should speak with him. He calls love the truth of life, and explains it so beautifully that the heart is enchanted."

"Have you introduced Miss Malti to him?"

"You must be joking. What would Malti have to do with him..."

The sentence was not finished when, startled by a rustling sound in the bushes ahead, he instinctively moved behind Raisahab for protection. From the thicket, a leopard emerged and walked slowly forward.

Raisahab raised his gun and was about to take aim when Khanna said, "What are you doing? Why provoke it needlessly? What if it turns back?"

"If it turns back, it will fall right here."

"Then at least let me climb up that mound. I am not so fond of hunting."

"Then why did you come hunting?"

"Foolishness, what else."

Raisahab lowered his gun.

"That was a fine prey. Such chances are rare."

"I cannot stay here any longer. This place is dangerous."

"Let me at least shoot one animal. It is shameful to return empty-handed."

"Please, just see me safely to the car. Then hunt leopard or cheetah as you wish."

"You are very timid, Mr. Khanna, truly."

"To risk one's life for nothing is not bravery."

"Very well, you may return if you wish."

"Alone?"

"The path is quite clear."

"No, sir. You must come with me."

Raisahab tried to reason with him, but Khanna would not listen. His face had turned pale with fear. At that moment, if even a squirrel had come out of the bushes, he would have screamed and collapsed. He was trembling all over, drenched in sweat. Raisahab, helpless, had to turn back with him.

When the two had gone a good distance, Khanna finally regained his composure.

--- Page 78 ---
He said, “I do not fear danger, but to put one’s finger in the jaws of danger is sheer folly.”

“Oh, go on! You saw a mere leopard and your soul almost left your body.”

“I consider hunting a relic of those times when man was still an animal. Since then, civilization has advanced far ahead.”

“I shall expose you before Miss Malti.”

“I do not consider it a shame to be an advocate of non-violence.”

“So, that was your non-violence? Bravo!”

Khanna replied with pride, “Yes, that was my non-violence. You take pride in the names of Buddha and Shankar, yet you kill animals. It is you who should feel ashamed, not I.”

For a while, both walked on in silence. Then Khanna spoke, “So, when will you come? I wish you would fill out the policy form today itself, and also the shares in the sugar company. I have both forms with me.”

Raisahab replied in a worried tone, “Let me think it over a bit.”

“There is nothing to think about.”

* * *

The third pair consisted of Mirza Khurshid and Mr. Tankha. For Mirza Khurshid, the past and the future were like blank sheets of paper. He lived in the present. He neither regretted the past nor worried about the future. Whatever came before him, he threw himself into it with all his heart and soul. Among friends, he was the very embodiment of wit and humor. In the council, there was no member more enthusiastic than he. Once he took up a question, he would make ministers weep. He knew not how to show partiality or make concessions. In between, he would also crack jokes. For him, there was only today; who knew about tomorrow? His temper was such that he would come forward with a thump of his fist. Before humility, he would bow low, but if anyone showed off their status, he would roll up his sleeves and go after them. He remembered neither what he owed others nor what others owed him. He had a fondness for poetry and for wine. Woman, to him, was merely an object of amusement. Long ago, his heart had gone bankrupt.

Mr. Tankha was a man of schemes and stratagems—an expert in striking deals, settling matters, throwing in obstacles, extracting oil from sand, squeezing throats, and slipping away with a flick of his tail. He could sail a boat on sand, make grass grow on stone. Arranging loans for zamindars from mahajans, launching new companies, fielding candidates at election time—this was his profession. Especially during elections, his fortunes would shine. He would field some seasoned candidate, work for him with heart and soul, and pocket ten or twenty thousand in the process. When the Congress was in ascendance, he supported Congress candidates. When the Communal Party gained strength, he began working for the Hindu Sabha. Yet, for every such reversal, he had such convincing arguments that no one could point a finger at him. He was on friendly terms with all the city’s elite, all the officials, all the wealthy. Even if people did not approve of his policies in their hearts, his manners were so gentle that no one could say a word to his face.

Mirza Khurshid wiped the sweat from his brow with a handkerchief and said, “Today is not a day fit for hunting. Today, there should have been a mushaira.”

The lawyer agreed, “Yes, right there in the garden. It would have been delightful.”

After a while, Mr. Tankha brought up the matter at hand.

“This time, the elections will bring forth great spectacles. Even for you, it will not be easy.”

--- Page 79 ---
Mirza spoke with a tone of detachment, “This time, I will not stand for election.”

Tankha asked, “Why not?”

“Who wants to indulge in pointless chatter for nothing? What’s the use? I have lost all faith in this democracy. A trifling matter, and months of endless debate. Yes, it’s a fine masquerade for throwing dust in the people’s eyes. It would be far better to have a single governor, whether he be Hindustani or English, it makes no difference. An engine can pull a train for thousands of miles with ease, but even ten thousand men together cannot pull it as swiftly. I have grown weary of this whole spectacle after watching the council. If I had my way, I would set fire to the council itself. What we call democracy is, in practice, nothing but the rule of big merchants and zamindars, nothing else. In elections, only the one with money wins. With the power of money, all conveniences are arranged for him. Great pandits, great maulvis, great writers and orators, who can sway the public with their tongues and pens in any direction they please, all bow their heads at the feet of the golden idol. I have made up my mind, I will not go near elections again. My propaganda now will be against democracy.”

Mirza Sahib then cited verses from the Quran to prove how lofty were the ideals of kings in ancient times. Today, we cannot even look in that direction; our eyes would be dazzled. The king did not have the right to spend even a single coin from the treasury for his personal needs. He would make a living by copying books, sewing clothes, or teaching children. Mirza recited a long list of such ideal monarchs. Where is that king who nourished his subjects, and where are today’s ministers, who must receive five, six, seven, eight thousand rupees a month? Is this loot or democracy?

A herd of deer appeared, grazing nearby. The thrill of the hunt lit up Mirza’s face. He readied his gun and took aim. A dark deer fell. “Got him!” he cried out, and with this wild exclamation, Mirza too ran forward, leaping and clapping like a child.

Nearby, a man was chopping wood on a tree. He quickly climbed down and ran with Mirza. The deer had been shot in the neck; its legs trembled, and its eyes had turned glassy.

The woodcutter looked at the deer with compassionate eyes and said, “It’s a fine buck, must weigh at least a maund. If you wish, shall I carry it for you?”

Mirza said nothing. He gazed at the deer’s lifeless, pain-filled eyes. Just a minute ago, it was alive. If even a leaf rustled, it would prick up its ears and bound away. It had been eating the grass grown by God, with its friends and young ones, but now it lay motionless. Skin it, cut it to pieces, make mince of it—it will not know. The charm, the joy that was in its playful life, is that present in this lifeless corpse? What a beautiful form it had, what lovely eyes, what a delightful image! Its leaps would send waves of joy through the heart. With its bounds, our own spirits would leap. Its vitality spread life, like a flower spreads fragrance—but now! To look upon it is to feel disgust.

The woodcutter asked, “Where shall I take it, sahib? Give me a few coins as well.”

Mirza seemed to start from his reverie. “All right, take it. Where will you go?”

“Wherever you wish, sahib!”

“No, wherever you wish, take it there. I give it to you.”

--- Page 80 ---
The woodcutter looked at Mirza with curiosity. He could not believe his ears.

“Oh no, sahib, you have hunted this animal—how can I take it?”

“No, no, I say it gladly. Take it with you. How far is your home from here?”

“About half a kos, sahib.”

“Then I will come along with you. I want to see how your children rejoice.”

“In that case, I cannot take it, sarkar! You have come from so far, hunted in this blazing sun—how can I carry it away?”

“Take it, take it, don’t delay. I know now, you are a good man.”

The woodcutter, still fearful, kept glancing anxiously at Mirzaji’s face, worried he might get angry, but finally lifted the deer. Suddenly, he set the deer down and stood up, saying, “I understand now, sahib. You have not performed its halal.”

Mirzaji laughed and said, “Enough, enough, you have understood well. Now pick it up and let’s go home.”

Mirzaji was not so bound by religious scruples. He had not offered namaz in ten years. Once every two months, he would fast for a day—completely without food or water. But the satisfaction the woodcutter felt, believing that the deer was now forbidden for Mirzaji and his companions, was something Mirzaji did not wish to spoil.

The woodcutter, his heart now light, hoisted the deer onto his shoulders and set off for home. Sanda and the others stood indifferently under the tree. Why trouble themselves to go into the sun to fetch the deer? They had some inkling of what was happening, but when they saw the woodcutter walking away in the opposite direction, Sanda asked Mirza, “Where are you going, Hazrat? Have you lost your way?”

Mirza smiled guiltily and replied, “I have given the game to this poor man. I am just accompanying him to his home. Why don’t you come along?”

Tankha looked at Mirza with curiosity and said, “You are at home wherever you are, I cannot say otherwise. I myself do not know.”

“Why did you give him the game?”

“Because the joy he will feel on receiving it is greater than any I or anyone in his house would feel.”

Tankha interjected, “Well, Rai Sahib and Mehta will want to discuss something about the election. You are not standing, so it is your wish, but what is the hesitation? Those people are standing, and we must gather votes. I only ask that you do not support any other candidate. The votes of the landlords are all with them, but your influence over the public is such that their attention does not stray. They are ready to spend ten or twenty thousand rupees just to show their strength. No, let me finish. To reach this point, please stay with us. I will draft a manifesto on your behalf and you can collect a thousand rupees in cash.”

Mirza Sahib looked at him with disdain. “I will send it.”

Mr. Tankha did not take offense in the least. Everyone else also refused politely.

--- Page 81 ---
“Send as many curses upon me as you wish, but by cursing money, you are only harming yourself.”

“I consider such money to be haram.”

“You are not so strictly bound by the Shariat.”

“One does not need to be bound by Shara to consider loot as haram.”

“So, in this matter, can you not change your decision?”

“No, I cannot.”

“Very well, let it be. But you have no objection to becoming a director of an insurance company, do you? You won’t even have to buy a single share. You only need to lend your name.”

“No, I do not accept that either. I have been a director of many companies, a managing agent of several, a chairman of others. Wealth once kissed my feet. I know how much comfort and luxury can be amassed with money, but I also know how much it makes a man selfish, pleasure-seeking, cunning, and shameless.”

The lawyer no longer had the courage to make another proposal. His faith in Mirza’s intelligence and influence had greatly diminished. For him, wealth was everything, and with a man who spurned Lakshmi, he could have no association.

The woodcutter was hurrying along with the deer slung over his shoulder. Mirza too quickened his pace, but the portly Tankha lagged behind.

He called out, “Listen, Mirzaji, you’re walking too fast!”

Mirzaji, without stopping, replied, “That poor fellow is carrying such a heavy load and walking so swiftly. Can we not at least keep pace with him, carrying only our own bodies?”

The woodcutter had set the deer down on a stump and was catching his breath.

Mirza Sahib came up and asked, “Tired, are you?”

The woodcutter replied shyly, “It’s very heavy, sarkar.”

“Come, let me carry it for a while.”

The woodcutter laughed. Mirza was much taller and stouter than him, yet this thin, wiry man laughed at his offer. Mirzaji felt as if he had been whipped.

“Why did you laugh? Do you think I cannot lift it?”

The woodcutter, as if seeking forgiveness, said, “Sarkar, you are great men. Carrying loads is the work of laborers like us.”

“But I am twice your size.”

“What of that, malik?”

Mirzaji’s manhood could not bear this slight. He stepped forward, hoisted the deer onto his neck, and set off. But he had barely gone fifty steps when his neck began to ache, his legs trembled, and his eyes swam with dizziness. He steeled his heart and managed another twenty paces. Where had that wretch gone? It was as if this corpse had been filled with lead. If only I could put this on Mr. Tankha’s neck, that would be something. He walks around puffed up like a water-skin—let him taste this too. But how to put it down? Both of them will say to themselves, “He tried to show off his manliness, but after fifty steps he squealed.”

The woodcutter teased, “Well, malik, how does it feel? Very light, isn’t it?”

--- Page 82 ---
Mirzaji now found the load somewhat lighter. He said, “I will at least carry it as far as you have brought it.”

“Your neck will ache for days, malik!”

“What do you think, I am puffed up for nothing?”

“No, malik, now I don’t think so. But please don’t trouble yourself. There’s a rock ahead—set it down there.”

“I can still carry it a little farther.”

“But it doesn’t seem right that I should walk empty-handed while you bear the load.”

Mirza Sahib set the deer down on the rock. The lawyer sahib also arrived.

Mirza baited him, “Now you too must carry it a little way, janab.”

In the lawyer sahib’s eyes, Mirzaji now held no importance. He said, “Forgive me. I make no claims to wrestling prowess.”

“It’s not very heavy, truly.”

“Oh, let it be.”

“If you carry it a hundred steps, I promise I’ll accept whatever proposal you put before me.”

“I don’t fall for such tricks.”

“I’m not tricking you, by God. However you command, I’ll stand. When you order, I’ll sit. Whatever company you name, I’ll become its director, member, accountant, canvasser—anything you say. Just carry it a hundred steps. I only get along with friends who can do anything when the time comes.”

Tankha’s heart began to itch. Mirza was known to keep his word; there was no doubt about that. How heavy could a deer really be? After all, Mirza had carried it this far, and he didn’t seem overly tired. If he refused, a golden opportunity would slip away. After all, it wasn’t a mountain. At most, it would weigh four or five seers. At worst, his neck would ache for a few days. When there’s money in your pocket, even a little illness is a pleasure.

“It’s just a matter of a hundred steps.”

“Yes, a hundred steps. I’ll count as you go.”

“See that you don’t cheat.”

“I curse anyone who cheats.”

Tankha retied his shoelaces, took off his coat and handed it to the woodcutter, rolled up his trousers, wiped his face with a handkerchief, and looked at the deer as if he were about to put his head in the mortar. Then he tried to lift the deer onto his neck. After two or three attempts, he managed to get the carcass onto his shoulders, but his neck wouldn’t straighten. His back bent, he began to pant, and was about to drop the carcass to the ground when Mirza supported him and urged him forward.

Tankha took a step as if he were stepping into a swamp. Mirza encouraged him, “Bravo! My lion, well done!”

Tankha took another step. It felt as if his neck would break.

“You’ve conquered the field. Long life to you, my friend!”

Tankha took two more steps. His eyes were bulging.

--- Page 83 ---
"Just one more try, my friend! The wager of a hundred steps was too much. Let it be only fifty."

Vakil Sahib was in a wretched state. Like a lifeless deer in the jaws of a lion, he was being crushed, his very heart’s blood being sucked out. All his strength had deserted him. Only greed, like an iron beam, was holding up the roof. The wager ranged from one to twenty-five thousand. But in the end, even that beam gave way. Greed’s back was broken. Darkness swam before his eyes. His head spun, and he collapsed on the stony ground, the prey still slung over his shoulders.

Mirza immediately helped him up, fanning him with his handkerchief and patting his back.

"You gave it your all, my friend, but luck is not on your side."

Tankha, panting, drew a long breath and said, "You nearly killed me today. That thing must weigh two maunds at least!"

Mirza laughed. "But, brother, I carried it just as far, didn’t I?"

Vakil Sahib began to plead, "I only wanted to fulfill your request. You wanted a spectacle, and you’ve had it. Now you must keep your promise."

"When did you keep your end of the bargain?"

"I tried with all my might."

"That doesn’t count."

Meanwhile, the woodcutter had hoisted the deer again and was running off with it. He wanted to show them that if they had managed to carry it ten steps with much groaning and effort, they should not think themselves victorious. Even in weakness, he would outdo them in this field. Yes, they could blacken as much paper as they liked and concoct as many false cases as they wished.

They came to a stream, with just a trickle of water. Across the stream, on a small mound, was a hamlet of five or six houses, and several children were playing under a tamarind tree. As soon as they saw the woodcutter, they ran to greet him, clamoring with questions—"Who killed it, Bapu? How did you kill it? Where did you shoot it? Why did this one get shot and not the others?"

The woodcutter, grunting in reply, reached the tamarind tree, set down the deer, and ran to fetch cots from a nearby hut for the two gentlemen. His four children took charge of the prey and tried to chase away the other boys.

The youngest child declared, "This is ours."

His elder sister, about fourteen or fifteen, glanced at the guests and scolded her little brother, "Be quiet, or the police will take you away."

Mirza teased the boy, "It’s not yours, it’s ours."

The boy sat astride the deer to assert his claim. "Bapu brought it."

His sister prompted, "Say, brother, it’s yours."

Their mother was nearby, gathering leaves for the goat. Seeing two strangers, she quickly pulled her veil a little lower and felt ashamed—her sari was so dirty, so torn, so threadbare. How could she appear before guests in such a state? Yet she could not avoid it; she had to offer water and hospitality.

It was still a little before noon, but Mirza Sahib decided to spend the midday in this village. He gathered the villagers, sent for liquor, had the game cooked, brought ghee and flour from the nearby bazaar, and hosted a feast for the entire village. Young and old, men and women, all enjoyed the banquet.

--- Page 84 ---
They drank heavily and sang merrily until evening. Mirzaji became a child among children, a drinker among drinkers, an old man among the old, and a youth among the young. In such a short time, he had become so intimately acquainted with the entire village that it seemed as if he were a native himself. The boys clung to him. One would snatch his tasseled cap and put it on his own head, another would strut about with his rifle perched on his shoulder, another would unfasten his wristwatch and tie it on his own wrist. Mirza himself drank copious amounts of country liquor and, swaying, sang with the wild men.

When they departed at sunset, the entire village—men and women—came a long way to see them off. Some even wept. Perhaps it was the first time in the lives of these poor villagers that a hunter had hosted them. Surely, he must be a king—who else could possess such generosity? When would they ever behold him again?

After walking some distance, Mirza turned back and said, “How happy they were! If only such days came often in my life. Today was truly a blessed day.”

Tankha replied coldly, “It may have been blessed for you, but for me it was nothing but ill-fated. Nothing came of it. After wandering all day through forests and hills, we return empty-handed.”

Mirza said mercilessly, “I have no sympathy for you.”

When the two men reached the banyan tree, both groups had already returned. Mehta sat with his face downcast. Malti sat apart, looking despondent—a new thing. Raisahib and Khanna had both gone hungry, and no one uttered a word. Vakil Sahib was upset because Mirza had betrayed him. Only Mirza Sahib was cheerful, and his cheerfulness was otherworldly.

:8:

Ever since the cow arrived in Hori’s house, the household’s prosperity seemed transformed. Ghuniya’s pride swelled beyond measure. At every moment, there was talk of the cow.

The fodder had run out. A little green cane from the sugarcane field had been given to her. They had to chop up whatever they could and feed it to the animals. Their eyes remained fixed on the sky, praying for rain to fall and grass to grow. Half of Ashadh had passed, and still there was no rain.

Suddenly, one day, clouds gathered and a heavy Ashadh downpour fell. The peasants took up their ploughs to sow the kharif crop, when the steward of Raisahib sent word: until all arrears were paid, no one would be allowed to plough their fields. It was as if a thunderbolt had struck the farmers. Never before had there been such severity—what kind of order was this? No one could just abandon the village and run away. If the fields were not ploughed, where would the money come from? It could only be raised from the fields themselves. All of them went together to plead with the steward. His name was Pandit Gosairam. He was not a bad man, but he was bound by the master’s orders. How could he defy them? Only the other day, Raisahib had spoken so kindly and piously, and today, such cruelty upon the tenants. Hori was ready to go to the master himself, but then thought, once the order had been given to the steward, why would the master go back on it? Why should he take the lead and make himself the villain? When no one else spoke up, why should he leap into the fire? Whatever befell everyone, he too would bear.

There was turmoil among the peasants. All of them rushed to the village mahajans for money. In the village...

--- Page 85 ---
Mangru Sah was riding high these days. This year, he had made a good profit from hemp. He hadn’t earned any less from wheat and linseed either. Pandit Datadin and Dulari Sahuain also did some moneylending. But the greatest moneylender in the village was Jhinguri Singh. He was an agent for a prominent city moneylender. Under him worked several men who roamed the neighboring villages, dealing in loans and collections. Besides him, there were many smaller moneylenders who lent money at a rate of two annas per rupee, often without any written record.

The villagers were so fond of lending and borrowing that whoever managed to save ten or twenty rupees would immediately set himself up as a moneylender. Once, even Hori had dabbled in moneylending. The effect of that was such that people still believed Hori had hidden money stashed away somewhere. But where had that wealth gone? It hadn’t come out during the division of property, Hori hadn’t gone on any pilgrimage, nor performed any great fasts or feasts—so where had it all disappeared? Even though he was often beaten down by fate, he always seemed to bounce back.

Some people made offerings to one deity, others to another. Some agreed to pay one anna per rupee in interest, others two annas. Hori’s self-respect had not been entirely extinguished. How could he show his face to those to whom he still owed money? Apart from Jhinguri Singh, he could think of no one else. Jhinguri Singh always insisted on a formal contract, took a separate fee for the transaction, another for his commission, and yet another for the stamp paper. On top of that, he would deduct a year’s interest in advance before handing over the money. If you signed a note for twenty-five rupees, you’d be lucky to receive seventeen in hand. But what else could be done in such dire times? It was only because of Rai Sahib’s tyranny that he was forced to beg at anyone’s door.

Jhinguri Singh was sitting, chewing on a neem twig. He was a short, stout, bald, and dark man, with a long nose and large, bushy mustache—altogether clownish in appearance, and indeed, he was a great joker. He had made this village his in-laws’ home, addressing the men as brothers-in-law or fathers-in-law, and the women as sisters-in-law. On the road, boys would tease him—“Panditji, shall we touch your feet?” And Jhinguri Singh would quickly bless them: “May your eyes go blind, may your knee break, may you have fits, may your house catch fire,” and so on. The boys never tired of these blessings, but when it came to moneylending, he was ruthless. He would not forgive a single paisa of interest and would not leave your doorstep without collecting his due.

Hori greeted him respectfully and told him of his misfortune.

Jhinguri Singh smiled and said, “So, what happened to all that old money?”

“If I had any old money left, Thakur, would I not have freed myself from the moneylenders? Who likes paying interest forever?”

“Better to pay endless interest than to dig up your hidden hoard. That’s always been your people’s way.”

“What hidden hoard, Babu Sahib? We barely have enough to eat. My son is grown, but there’s no prospect for his marriage. My eldest daughter is of marriageable age now. If I had money, would I keep it buried for nothing?”

Ever since Jhinguri Singh had seen the cow at Hori’s door, he had set his sights on it. The cow’s build and form told him it could give no less than five seers of milk. He had already decided in his heart that he must find a way to get that cow from Hori, by hook or by crook. Now, the opportunity had come.

He said, “All right, brother, you have nothing left, I believe you now. Take as much money as you need. But for your own good, let me advise you—if you have any jewelry or ornaments, pawn them and take the money. If you sign a stamped contract, the interest will increase and you’ll get entangled in trouble.”

Hori swore that there wasn’t even a scrap of thread in the house that could be called jewelry. The bangles on Dhaniya’s wrists were only gilt.

Jhinguri Singh, putting on a look of sympathy, said, “Then do one thing—this new cow you have…”

--- Page 86 ---
“Have you brought it? Sell it to us directly. Spare yourself the trouble of interest, stamps, and all those disputes. Whatever price four men agree upon, take that from us. We know you brought her home out of your own desire and do not wish to sell her, but this crisis must be overcome.”

At first, Hori laughed at this proposal; he did not even wish to consider it seriously. But Thakur explained the consequences, painted such a dreadful picture of the moneylender’s tricks that the idea took root in Hori’s mind as well. Thakur was right, after all—once you have the money in hand, you can always buy another cow. If you sign a note for thirty rupees, you’ll barely get twenty-five, and if you can’t repay it for three or four years, it will swell to a hundred. Past experience had taught him that debt is a guest who, once arrived, never leaves.

He said, “Let me go home and consult everyone, then I’ll tell you.”

“There’s no need for consultation,” Thakur replied. “Just tell them that borrowing money is nothing but ruin.”

“I understand, Thakur. I’ll come back with an answer soon.”

But as soon as he brought up the proposal at home, an uproar broke out. Dhania protested less, but both daughters raised a storm. “We will not give up our cow! Bring the money from wherever you wish!” Sona even declared, “It would be better to sell me instead. You’d get about as much for me as for the cow.”

Hori was thrown into confusion. The two girls truly loved the cow. Rupa would wrap her arms around its neck and would not take a bite of food until she had fed her first. The cow would lick her hand with such affection, gaze at her with such loving eyes! How beautiful her calf would be! They had already named it—Matru. She would sleep with it by her side. The two sisters had fought many times over this cow. Sona claimed, “She loves me more,” while Rupa insisted, “No, she loves me.” The matter had never been settled, and both claims stood.

But after much persuasion, Hori managed to convince Dhania. To borrow a cow from a friend and sell it was a shameful thing, but in adversity, even a man’s dharma falters—what was so extraordinary about this? If it were not so, why would people fear misfortune so much? Gobar, too, did not object much; these days, he was lost in his own thoughts. It was decided that when both girls had fallen asleep at night, the cow would be taken to Jhinguri Singh.

Somehow, the day passed. Evening came. By eight o’clock, both girls had eaten and gone to sleep. Gobar, unable to bear the sorrowful scene, had slipped away somewhere. How could he watch the cow being taken away? How could he hold back his tears? Hori, too, maintained a hard exterior, but his heart was restless. There was not a soul in the world he would not have begged for twenty-five rupees at that moment, even if he had to repay fifty. He went and stood before the cow, and it seemed to him that tears welled in her large, dark eyes. She seemed to say, “Have you grown weary of me in just four days? You promised never to sell me as long as you lived. Was that your vow? I never asked anything of you. Whatever little you gave me, I ate contentedly, without complaint.”

Dhania said, “The girls are asleep. Why don’t you take her now? If you must see it, then see it through.”

Hori muttered to himself, “I cannot lift my hand, Dhania! I cannot bear to look at her face.” He began to count out the money. “If Bhagwan wills it, we will pay it all back. What are three or four villages’ worth of debt?”

--- Page 87 ---
Dhaniya looked at him with pride-filled affection—“And why not? After so much sacrifice, at last a cow has entered our home. Sell her too, then. Here, take the money. Just as all other debts will be repaid, so will this one!”

Inside, the air was stifling. There was no breeze. Not a single leaf stirred. Clouds had gathered, but there was no sign of rain. Hori tied the cow outside. Dhaniya protested, “Where are you taking her?” But Hori did not hear; he said, “I’ll tie her outside in the fresh air. She’ll be comfortable. She is a living being too.” After tying the cow, he went to see his middle brother, Shobha. For the past several months, Shobha had been suffering from asthma. There was no way to get medicine, no arrangements for food and drink, yet he had to work himself to the bone; so his condition grew worse day by day. Shobha was a patient man, one who fled from quarrels and disputes. He kept to himself, minding his own business. Hori cared for him, and Shobha too respected Hori. The two began talking about money matters. The new order from the Raisahab had become the center of much discussion.

It was around eleven when Hori returned, and as he was about to go inside, he sensed that someone was standing near the cow. He called out, “Who’s there?”

Heera replied, “It’s me, Dada. I came to get a light for my chilam from your hearth.”

That Heera had come to take a light from his hearth—this small thing made Hori feel the closeness of his brother’s affection. There were other hearths in the village; he could have gotten a light anywhere. But Heera came to his hearth, as if it were his own. The whole village came to this hearth for fire; it was the most prosperous hearth in the village. But Heera’s coming was different, especially after the quarrel that day. Heera harbored no deceit in his heart. He was quick-tempered, but pure of heart.

Hori asked in a tone full of affection, “Do you have tobacco, or shall I bring some?”

“No, I have tobacco, Dada.”

“Shobha is in a bad way today.”

“He won’t take any medicine, so what can be done? According to him, all the vaids, doctors, hakims are fools. Whatever wisdom God had, He gave it all to him and his wife.”

Hori said anxiously, “That’s his one fault. He never counts anyone his equal, and everyone gets irritable when they’re sick. Don’t you remember, when you had pleurisy, you used to throw the medicine away? I would hold both your hands, and then your bhabhi would put the medicine in your mouth. And you would curse her a thousand times for it.”

“Yes, Dada, how could I forget? If you hadn’t done so much for me, how would I have been able to keep fighting with you?”

Hori felt as if Heera’s voice had grown heavy. His own throat tightened.

“Son, quarrels are the religion of life. Those who are your own don’t become strangers because of them. When there are four people in a house, there are bound to be quarrels. Who will quarrel with the one who has no one?”

The two smoked the chilam together. Then Heera went home, and Hori went inside to eat.

Dhaniya said angrily, “Did you see your precious son’s doings? It’s so late, and he still hasn’t come home from his friend’s place. I know everything. I’ve found out all about it. That widow girl of Bhola’s, Jhuniya—he’s entangled with her.”

Hori too had heard whispers of this, but he hadn’t believed it. Poor Gobar, what would he know of such things!

Godan: 82

--- Page 88 ---
He said, “Who told you?”

Dhaniya flared up. “Maybe you don’t know, but everyone else is talking about it. That Bhagga—he’s been around the block. She’s got him dancing on her fingers, and he thinks she’s ready to die for him. You better talk some sense into him. If anything untoward happens, you’ll be ruined.”

Hori’s heart was light with mischief. He joked, “Jhunia isn’t bad to look at or listen to. Why not get engaged to her? Where else would you find such a cheap wife?”

Dhaniya felt this jest like an arrow. “If Jhunia sets foot in this house, I’ll burn my face, that wretched woman! If Gobar is so fond of her, let him take her and live wherever he wants.”

“And what if Gobar brings her here?”

“Then who will marry these two girls? Who in the community will even look at you? No one will even stand at your door.”

“He doesn’t care about that.”

“I won’t let that Lala off so easily. I’ve raised him with my own blood and sweat, and now Jhunia will come and rule the house? I’ll set fire to that wretch’s mouth!”

Suddenly, Gobar came running in, his voice trembling with fear. “Dada, what’s happened to Sundariya? Did a black snake bite her? She’s lying there, writhing!”

Hori had already gone to the kitchen. He left his plate and rushed outside. “What ill omen are you speaking of? I just saw her—she was lying down.”

All three hurried out. By the light of a lamp, they saw Sundariya foaming at the mouth. Her eyes were glazed, her belly swollen, and all four legs splayed out. Dhaniya began to beat her head in despair. Hori ran to Pandit Datadeen—the village’s only expert in animal medicine. Panditji was about to go to bed, but he came running. In no time, the whole village had gathered.

Someone had fed the cow something. The signs were clear—she had been poisoned. But who in the village could have done such a thing? Nothing like this had ever happened here before. Had someone from outside come into the village? Hori had no enmity with anyone that would make him suspect them. He had quarreled with Heera, but that was just a brotherly spat. In fact, Heera was the most aggrieved of all, swearing that if he found the murderer, he would drink his blood. For all his temper, he could never stoop to such a vile act.

The crowd lingered until midnight. Everyone shared Hori’s grief and cursed the killer. If the culprit had been found at that moment, he would not have survived. With things like this happening, who would dare leave their cattle outside? Until now, all the animals had been left outside, even at night, without a care. But now a new calamity had struck. What a cow she was—one could watch her for hours, worthy of worship! She gave no less than five seers of milk. Each of her calves would have fetched a hundred rupees. It hadn’t been long since she’d calved, and now the calf was gone.

When everyone had gone back to their homes, Dhaniya began to berate Hori. “People try to warn you, but you always do as you please. When you were taking the cow out of the yard, I tried to stop you, but you wouldn’t listen. Times are hard for us; who knows what might happen? But no, you were worried she’d get too hot. Well, now she’s cold enough—and so is your heart. The Thakur wanted her—if you’d given her, one burden would have been lifted, and we’d have been done with it. But now look at the slap we’ve received! When a cow has calves, her mind goes first. Why did she keep bringing her calves into the house all these days? She got to know everyone so quickly, it was as if—”

--- Page 89 ---
The children used to play with her horns. She wouldn’t even move her head. Whatever you put in the trough, she would lick and clean it spotless. She was Lakshmi herself—why would she stay in the house of the unfortunate? Even Sona and Roopa, awakened by the commotion, had started crying bitterly. Most of the burden of caring for her had fallen on those two. She had become their companion. When both finished eating, each would feed her a piece of bread with their own hands. How eagerly she would stick out her tongue and eat! And until she received a morsel from their hands, she would stand there, watching expectantly. Their luck had turned.

Gobur and the two girls, after crying themselves out, had fallen asleep. Hori also lay down. When Dhaniya came to place a water pot by his pillow, Hori said quietly, “You can’t keep a secret in your belly. If you hear anything, you’ll go beating the drum all over the village.”

Dhaniya protested, “Why would I? What have I ever blabbed that you tarnish my name for nothing?”

“All right, do you suspect anyone?”

“I don’t suspect anyone. It must have been some outsider.”

“You won’t tell anyone, will you?”

“If I don’t tell, how will the villagers make me new ornaments?”

“If you tell anyone, I’ll kill you.”

“You won’t be happy after killing me. You won’t find another wife now. As long as I’m here, I keep your house together. The day I die, you’ll weep with your hand on your head. Right now, you see only faults in me, but then tears will flow from your eyes.”

“I suspect Heera.”

“Lies, absolute lies. Heera isn’t that low. He just has a foul mouth, that’s all.”

“I saw it with my own eyes. I swear on your head.”

“You saw it yourself? When?”

“When I came back after seeing Sobha, he was standing near Sundariya’s trough. I asked, ‘Who’s there?’ He said, ‘It’s me, Heera. I came to get a coal for my fire.’ He chatted with me for a while, gave me his chilam to smoke. He went that way, I came inside, and right then Gobur started shouting. It seems I tied up the cow and went to Sobha’s house, and he came here and fed her something. Maybe he came back again to see if she had died.”

Dhaniya let out a long sigh. “So this is what brothers are like—who don’t hesitate to cut their own brother’s throat. Oh! Is Heera’s heart really so black? And I raised that bearded wretch with my own hands.”

“All right, go to sleep now, but don’t mention this to anyone, not even by mistake.”

“Who, me? If I don’t send Lala to the police station first thing in the morning, I’m not my father’s daughter. Is this what a brother does? He’s an enemy, a true enemy, and there’s no sin in killing an enemy—letting him go would be the sin.”

Hori threatened, “I’m warning you, Dhaniya, disaster will strike.”

Dhaniya, in a frenzy, replied, “Not disaster—retribution! I won’t rest until Lala is sent to the big house. Three years he’ll grind grain, three years! When he gets out, he’ll have to perform the rites for murder. He’ll have to give a feast. Don’t think otherwise, Lala, and I’ll have you testify, with your hand on your son’s head.”

She went inside and bolted the door, while Hori lay outside, cursing himself.

--- Page 90 ---
He himself could not keep a secret in his own heart; how then could Dhaniya keep it in hers? Now this witch would not relent. Once she set her mind to something, she would listen to no one. Today, he had made the greatest mistake of his life.

All around, a deep and silent darkness prevailed. Now and then, the bells around the necks of the two bullocks would ring out. Ten paces away lay the dead cow, and Hori tossed and turned in bitter remorse. Nowhere in the darkness could he see even a single ray of light.

:9:

At dawn, a great commotion broke out in Hori’s house. Hori was beating Dhaniya. Dhaniya was hurling abuses at him. Both daughters clung to their father’s feet, screaming, while Gobar tried to protect his mother. Again and again, he would grab Hori’s hand and push him back, but whenever a curse escaped Dhaniya’s lips, Hori would wrench his hand free and land a few more blows and kicks. It was as if his old anger had summoned some hidden, stored-up strength. The whole village was astir. People came running, pretending to mediate but eager to witness the spectacle. Shobha arrived, leaning on his staff. Datadeen shouted, “What’s this, Hori? Have you gone mad? Who raises a hand against the Lakshmi of his house like this? You never had this disease before. Has Heera’s infection caught you too?”

Hori, folding his hands, said, “Maharaj, do not speak now. I will not rest until I break her stubbornness today. The more I try to reason with her, the more defiant she becomes.”

Dhaniya, her eyes brimming with angry tears, cried out, “Maharaj, bear witness! I will not drink water today until I have sent him and his murderous brother to jail. His brother fed poison to the cow and killed her. Now, when I am going to the police to file a report, this murderer beats me. I have ruined my life for him, and this is my reward.”

Hori, grinding his teeth and glaring, said, “You’re saying it again? Did you see Heera feed poison to the cow?”

“Swear that you did not see Heera standing by the cow’s trough.”

“Yes, I did not see him, I swear.”

“Swear on your son’s head.”

Hori, his hand trembling, placed it on Gobar’s head and said in a shaking voice, “I swear on my son that I did not see Heera by the trough.”

Dhaniya spat on the ground and said, “Shame on your lies. You yourself told me Heera was standing there like a thief, and now you lie to protect your brother. Shame! If even a hair on my son’s head is harmed, I’ll set fire to the house. I’ll burn everything we own. O God, how shamelessly a man can deny his own words!”

Hori stamped his foot and said, “Dhaniya, don’t make me angrier, or it will be worse for you.”

“You’re already beating me, beat me more! Go on, if you are your father’s son, you’ll only drink water today after killing me. The sinner has thrashed me to a pulp, yet he’s not satisfied. He thinks himself a hero for beating me. In front of his brothers, he turns into a kitten, the wretch, the murderer.”

Then she began to weep, saying, “What have I not endured since coming to this house? How I have pinched and scraped, how I have longed for every scrap of cloth, how I have fed the whole family and gone to bed with only water myself. And now—”

--- Page 91 ---
Alas! God sits and watches this injustice, yet does not rush to protect the innocent. He had once hurried down from Vaikuntha to save the elephant king. Why does He now slumber in indifference?

Suspicion now slowly crept towards Dhaniya. No one doubted any longer that Heera had poisoned the cow. People had come to believe that Hori had sworn a false oath. The fear of the consequences of this false oath and the calamity it would bring had filled Hori with dread. On top of that, when Datadeen scolded him harshly, Hori was utterly defeated. He slipped out quietly. Truth had prevailed.

Datadeen asked Shobha, “Do you know anything, Shobha? What happened?”

Shobha, lying on the ground, replied, “Maharaj, I haven’t left the house in eight days. Hori Dada sometimes brings me something to eat, and that’s how I get by. He came to see me last night too. Who did what, I know nothing. Yes, yesterday evening Heera came to my house to borrow a khurpi. He said he needed to dig up a root. Since then, I haven’t seen him.”

Emboldened by this, Dhaniya spoke up, “Pandit Dada, it’s his doing. He borrowed the khurpi from Shobha’s house, dug up some root, and fed it to the cow. He’s been harboring a grudge ever since the quarrel that night.”

Datadeen said, “If this is proven, it will be murder. Whether the police do anything or not, Dharma will not let him go unpunished. Rupa, go and call Heera. Tell him Pandit Dada is calling. If he hasn’t committed murder, let him take up the Ganga-jali and swear an oath on the chabutra.”

Dhaniya said, “Maharaj, what faith can we have in his oath? He’ll swear falsely without a second thought. When this one, who pretends to be so pious, could swear a false oath, what trust can we place in Heera?”

Now Gobar spoke, “Let him swear a false oath. Let the line end here. Let the old ones live on. What use is it for the young to live?”

In a moment, Rupa returned and said, “Kaka isn’t at home, Pandit Dada! Kaki says he’s gone somewhere.”

Datadeen stroked his long beard and said, “Did you not ask where he went? Maybe he’s hiding in the house. Sona, go see if he’s inside.”

Dhaniya interjected, “Don’t send her, Dada! Heera has murder on his mind, who knows what he might do?”

Datadeen himself took up a stick and went to investigate, returning with the news that Heera had indeed gone somewhere. Puniya said he had taken his lota, rope, and stick. She had asked where he was going, but he did not answer. He had five rupees kept in a niche; those were gone too. It seemed he had taken the money with him.

Dhaniya said coldly, “He must have smeared his face with black and run away somewhere.”

Shobha said, “Where will he run to? Maybe he’s gone to bathe in the Ganga.”

Dhaniya doubted, “If he was going to the Ganga, why would he take money? And there’s no festival these days.”

No one could resolve this doubt. The belief hardened.

That day, no food was cooked in Hori’s house. No one fed or watered the oxen. The whole village was abuzz with the news. Groups of two or four gathered in various places, all discussing the same matter. Heera had surely run away somewhere. He must have seen that his secret was out, and now he would have to go to jail and face hard labor. So, he ran away. Puniya was weeping in a corner, saying nothing to anyone, who knows what she had lost.

--- Page 92 ---
What little remained was completed at dusk when the police inspector of the district arrived. Nand’s watchman had reported the incident, as was his duty. And how could the sahib of the police station ever neglect his own duty? Now, the villagers too must fulfill their obligations by welcoming and serving him. Dattadin, Jhinguri Singh, Nokhe Ram, their four favorites, Mangru the moneylender, and Tala Pateshwari—all gathered and stood before the inspector with folded hands.

Hori was summoned. It was the first time in his life that he stood before a police inspector. He was so terrified, it felt as if he was about to be hanged. When he had beaten Dhaniya, every limb of his had trembled with rage; now, before the inspector, he shrank within himself like a tortoise. The inspector looked at him with a scrutinizing gaze and seemed to see straight into his heart. He was not learned in bookish psychology, but he was a master of practical understanding. He knew men’s veins well. He was convinced: today, fortune is on my side. And Hori’s face declared plainly that a single threat would suffice for him.

The inspector asked, “Who do you suspect?”

Hori touched the ground and replied with folded hands, “I suspect no one, sahib. The cow died a natural death. She had bloated.”

Dhaniya had come and stood behind him. She immediately spoke up, “The cow was killed by your own brother, Hira. Sahib, these are not such simple matters that you will believe whatever is said. You have come here to investigate.”

The inspector asked, “Who is this woman?”

Several men, eager for the honor of speaking to the inspector, tried to answer at once, each hoping to be the first to speak. They all said together, “She is Hori’s wife, sahib!”

“Then call her here. I will record her statement first. Where is Hira?”

The notables replied in unison, “He left somewhere early this morning, sahib!”

“I will search his house.”

A search. Hori’s breath quickened. His brother Hira’s house must not be searched, and now he had no relation with Dhaniya. Let her go wherever she pleases. If she is bent on ruining his honor, how can she stay in his house? When she wanders from street to street, then she will understand.

The village notables began to devise various schemes to avert this dire crisis.

Dattadin shook his bald head and said, “It’s all just a show. Ask them, what is there in Hira’s house?”

Pateshwari Lal was very tall, but not a fool for all his height. Stretching his long, dark neck, he said, “Why else has he come here? And now that he’s here, will he leave without getting something?”

Jhinguri Singh nudged Hori and whispered in his ear, “Give whatever you have to give. Don’t try to hide.”

Now the inspector spoke with a bit more authority, “I will search Hira’s house.”

The color drained from Hori’s face, as if all the blood in his body had turned to ash. If disgrace comes to his brother’s house, it is the same as if it comes to his own. Hira may be estranged, but after all, he is still his brother. Yet, at this moment, he was powerless. If he had even a few rupees, he would have placed them at the inspector’s feet and said, “Sahib, my honor is at stake.” But he did not have a single coin, not even enough to buy poison. As soon as he approached Dhaniya…

--- Page 93 ---
He stood there, silent, his head bowed like a man condemned to death, feeling the keen sting of humiliation, for why would that witch ever give in?

Datadeen roused Hori, “Standing here like this won’t do, Hori. Find some way to arrange the money.”

Hori replied in a pleading tone, “What can I say now, Maharaj? I already carry the burden of old debts on my head—how can I ask for more? But please, save me from this calamity. If I live, I will repay every last coin. Even if I die, Gobar is still there.”

The village leaders began to confer among themselves. What offering should be made to the daroga? Datadeen proposed fifty rupees. According to Jhinguri Singh, the matter would not be settled for less than a hundred. Nokhe Ram also favored a hundred. For Hori, there was no difference between fifty and a hundred; if only this crisis of the search could be averted from his head! No matter how much he had to sacrifice in worship. Whether you burn a corpse with a mound of wood or with ten, what does it matter to the dead?

But Pateshwari could not bear this injustice. There had been no robbery or murder—only a search was being conducted. Twenty rupees would be more than enough for that.

The leaders rebuked him, “Then go and talk to the daroga yourself. We won’t go near him. Who wants to face his threats?”

Hori placed his head at Pateshwari’s feet, “Brother, save me. As long as I live, I will serve you faithfully.”

The daroga, with all the force of his broad chest and even broader belly, declared, “Where is Heera’s house? I will search his house.”

Pateshwari stepped forward and whispered in the daroga’s ear, “What will you gain by searching, huzoor? His brother is here, ready to serve you.”

The two men stepped aside to speak privately.

“What sort of man is he?”

“Very poor, huzoor. He doesn’t even have enough to eat.”

“Truly?”

“Yes, huzoor, I swear it.”

“What! So he can’t even manage fifty rupees?”

“Where is that possible, huzoor? If he can get ten, consider it a miracle. Fifty is impossible, not even in fifty lifetimes—and that too only if a mahajan stands as guarantor.”

The daroga pondered for a minute, then said, “Then what’s the use of harassing him? I don’t trouble those who are already dying.”

Pateshwari saw the opportunity slipping away and said, “No, huzoor, don’t do that, or where will we go then? What other means do we have?”

“You are the patwari of the area, aren’t you? What are you saying?”

“When such occasions arise, thanks to your grace, we too get something. Otherwise, who cares for the patwari?”

“All right, go and arrange for thirty rupees. Twenty for me, ten for you.”

“There are four headmen, please consider that.”

“Fine, split it half and half, but hurry up. I am getting late.”

--- Page 94 ---
Pateshwari spoke to Jhinguri. Jhinguri beckoned Hori over, took him into his house, counted out thirty rupees and handed them to him, pressing the weight of obligation upon him as he said, “Have the papers drawn up today itself. I am giving you this money only because I see your honest face, because I trust your decency.”

Hori took the money, tied it into the corner of his cloth, and, his face beaming with relief, set off towards the daroga. Suddenly, Dhaniya darted forward, snatched the cloth from his hand with a jerk. The knot was not tight. At the tug, it came undone and all the rupees scattered onto the ground. Hissing like a cobra, she said, “Where are you taking this money? Tell me! If you care for your own good, return every last rupee, I warn you. The people of this house toil day and night, starve for every morsel, don’t even have rags to wear, and here you are, taking a handful of rupees to save your so-called honor! Is your honor so precious? Even a house where rats run riot is supposed to have honor? At most, the daroga will search the house. Let him search wherever he wants! First, we lost a hundred-rupee cow, and now this extra burden. Bravo, your honor!”

Hori swallowed his anger and stood silent. The whole crowd seemed to tremble. The leaders bowed their heads. The daroga’s face darkened; never in his life had he been so rebuked.

Hori stood stunned. For the first time in his life, Dhaniya had thrown him down in the middle of the arena, left him staring at the sky. How could he ever raise his head again?

But the daroga was not one to accept defeat so easily. Irritated, he said, “It seems to me that this she-devil’s aunt herself poisoned the cow to frame Hira.”

Dhaniya, waving her arms, retorted, “Yes, I did it! It was my own cow, I killed it! After all, I didn’t kill someone else’s animal, did I? If that’s what your investigation concludes, then write it down! Put handcuffs on me! I have seen your justice and the reach of your wisdom. Cutting the throats of the poor is one thing, but separating milk from water is another.”

Hori, eyes blazing, lunged towards Dhaniya, but Gobar stepped in front of him and said fiercely, “Enough, Dada, that’s enough! Step back now, or I swear you’ll never see my face again. I won’t raise my hand against you—I’m not such a wretch. I’ll hang myself right here instead.”

Hori stepped back, and Dhaniya, emboldened, said, “You move aside, Gobar, let me see what he’ll do to me! The daroga is sitting right here. Let’s see if he dares. His honor is at stake if the house is searched, but dragging his wife through the mud before the whole village doesn’t harm his honor. This is the dharma of heroes! If you’re such a hero, go fight a real man. Don’t call yourself brave by beating the woman you dragged here. You think you feed and clothe me? From today, look after your own house. Watch, I’ll live in this very village, grinding grain on your chest, and I’ll eat and wear better than before. See for yourself if you wish.”

Hori was defeated. Only now did he realize how powerless, how helpless a man is before a woman.

The leaders had gathered up the scattered rupees and were signaling to the daroga to leave. Dhaniya delivered one final blow, “Whoever these rupees belong to, take them and return them. We don’t want to borrow from anyone. And if we must pay, we’ll take it from the one we owe. I won’t give a single coin, even if I have to go all the way to the magistrate’s court. We asked for twenty-five rupees to pay off our debt—no one gave us a paisa. But today, a handful of rupees is produced in a flash! I know everything. There was going to be a division here, everyone’s mouths would be sweetened. These are the murderers who call themselves village leaders, sucking the blood of the poor. Interest, usury, extra charges, gifts, bribes—whatever the means, loot the poor. And then they talk of self-rule! You won’t get self-rule by going to jail. Self-rule will come through righteousness, through justice.”
