--- Page 95 ---
The leaders’ faces were blackened with shame. The face of the daroga looked as if it had been swept with a broom.

To save their honor, they headed towards Hira’s house.

On the way, the daroga admitted, “That woman is truly fearless.”

Pateshwari said, “Not fearless, huzoor, but sharp-tongued. Such a woman should be shot.”

“She’s made things difficult for you all. You used to get four rupees each, at least.”

“Huzoor, even you lost fifteen rupees.”

“How can my money be lost? If he doesn’t pay, the village headmen will. And not just fifteen, but a full fifty rupees. You people arrange it quickly.”

Pateshwari Lal laughed and said, “Huzoor, you have quite a sense of humor.”

Datadeen added, “That’s how great men are. Where do we get to see such fortunate ones?”

The daroga said in a harsh tone, “Save your flattery for later. Right now, get me fifty rupees, in cash. And understand this—if you hesitate, I’ll search all four of your houses. It’s very possible that you’ve trapped Hira and Hori just to extort a hundred or so rupees from them.”

The leaders still thought the daroga was joking.

Jhinguri Singh winked and said, “Come on, Patwari Sahib, bring out the fifty rupees!”

Nokhe Ram supported him, “It’s Patwari Sahib’s jurisdiction. He should definitely oblige you.”

Pandit Nokhe Ram’s chaupal came into view. The daroga sat down on a charpoy and said, “What have you all decided? Will you pay up, or shall I conduct a search?”

Datadeen objected, “But huzoor…”

“I don’t want to hear any buts.”

Jhinguri Singh gathered courage, “Sarkar, this is outright—”

“I give you fifteen minutes. If I don’t get the full fifty rupees by then, I’ll search all four of your houses. And you know Gandasingh—no one dares cross him.”

Pateshwari Lal said loudly, “You have the authority, go ahead and search. This is a fine joke—someone else does the deed, and someone else gets caught.”

“I’ve been a police officer for twenty-five years, do you know that?”

“But such tyranny has never happened before.”

“You haven’t seen real tyranny yet. If you wish, I can show you. I can send each of you to jail for five years. That’s child’s play for me. I could send the whole village to kala pani for dacoity. Don’t be under any illusion.”

The four gentlemen went inside the chaupal to confer.

What happened next, no one knew, but the daroga looked pleased, while the four gentlemen’s faces were clouded with humiliation.

When the daroga rode off on his horse, the four leaders ran after him. Once the horse was out of sight, they turned back, as if returning from the cremation ground after performing the last rites of a loved one.

Suddenly, Datadeen spoke, “If my curse doesn’t strike, I’ll never show my face again.”

Nokhe Ram agreed, “I’ve never seen such money bring any good.”

Pateshwari predicted, “Ill-gotten gains always go to waste.”

Godan: 90

--- Page 96 ---
Today, Jhiguri found himself doubting the justice of God. Who knows what sort of Lord He is, that He witnesses such darkness and yet does not punish the sinners.

At this moment, these gentlemen were a sight worth painting.

Heera was nowhere to be found, and the days kept passing by. Hori did all he could to search for him, but in the end, exhausted, he had to give up. He also had to think of the fields and the crops. What could a single man do alone? And now, more than his own land, Hori was worried about Puniya’s fields. Puniya, now left alone, had become even more formidable. Hori spent his days trying to appease her. When Heera was there, he kept Puniya in check. But with his departure, there was no one left to restrain her. Hori’s partnership was with Heera; Puniya was a woman, helpless—what conflict could he have with her? And Puniya knew Hori’s nature well, and she punished him thoroughly for his goodness. Fortunately, the estate clerk did not press Puniya hard for the overdue rent; he was satisfied with a small offering. Otherwise, Hori would have been ready to take another loan to pay off not just his own arrears, but hers as well.

In the month of Sawan, it was time to plant the paddy. There was such a rush that no laborers could be found, and Hori could not transplant rice in his own fields. But how could Puniya’s fields be left untilled? Hori worked late into the night, planting her paddy. Now, he alone was her protector. If Puniya suffered any hardship, the world would laugh at him. The result was that Hori’s own kharif crop yielded very little grain, while Puniya’s granary was so full of rice there was no room left to store it.

Since that day, there had been a constant rift between Hori and Dhaniya. Hori had stopped speaking even to Gobar. Mother and son had joined together to boycott him. He had become a stranger in his own home. He was suffering the fate of those who try to ride two boats at once. Even in the village, he was no longer respected as before. With her courage, Dhaniya had not only won the leadership of the women, but also the men. For months, the incident was the talk of the surrounding villages. It even took on a supernatural hue—“Her name is Dhaniya, you see. She is favored by Bhawani. The moment the police put handcuffs on her man, Dhaniya invoked Bhawani. The goddess descended upon her. Then she gained such strength that she broke her husband’s handcuffs with a single jerk, grabbed the inspector by the moustache and tore it off, and then sat atop his chest. Only after much pleading did she let him go.” For some days, people came just to catch a glimpse of Dhaniya. That story had now grown old, but Dhaniya’s respect in the village had greatly increased. She possessed a marvelous courage, and if the time came, she could even humble a man.

But gradually, a change was coming over Dhaniya. Even when she saw Hori working in Puniya’s fields, she said nothing. And it was not because she had grown indifferent to Hori, but because she too now felt pity for Puniya. Heera’s flight from home was enough to satisfy her sense of retribution.

In the midst of all this, Hori was struck by fever. The seasonal ague was spreading, and Hori fell prey to it. After many years, a fever came that settled all his old scores. For a month, Hori lay bedridden. This illness crushed not only Hori, but also conquered Dhaniya. When a husband is dying, what enmity can remain? In such a state, one cannot hold a grudge even against enemies.

--- Page 97 ---
A husband is a husband. No matter how bad he may be, twenty-five years of life have been spent with him—if there was happiness, it was with him; if there was sorrow, it was with him. Now, whether he is good or bad, he is mine. Daadhijar struck me in front of everyone, stripped me of my dignity before the whole village, but since then he has been so ashamed that he cannot look me in the eye. When he comes to eat, he keeps his head down, eats in silence, and leaves, always afraid that I might say something.

When Hori recovered, there was reconciliation between husband and wife.

One day, Dhaniya said, “How did you get so angry? No matter how angry I get with you, I would never raise my hand.”

Hori, embarrassed, replied, “Let’s not talk about that, Dhaniya. Some evil spirit had possessed me. Only I know how much pain it has caused me.”

“And what if, in that rage, I had drowned myself?”

“Would I have just sat there and wept? My corpse would have gone to the pyre with yours.”

“All right, be quiet. Don’t speak such nonsense.”

“The cow is gone, gone for good, but she has left a calamity on my head. The worry for Puniya is killing me.”

“That’s why they say, may God never make one the head of the house. No one laughs at the younger ones. All the good and bad falls upon the elders.”

It was the month of Magh. The cold was biting. A dense darkness had settled. First, the chill of winter nights, and then the rain of Magh. A deathly silence prevailed. One could not even see the darkness itself. After supper, Hori lay on the bund of Puniya’s pea field in his little hut. He wished to forget the cold and fall asleep, but the tattered blanket, the torn mirzai, and the damp straw, chilled by the gusts of wind—facing so many enemies, how could sleep dare to come? Even tobacco was not to be had tonight, to while away the time. He had brought a cow-dung cake to smolder, but even that had gone out in the cold. He tucked his cracked, chapped feet into his belly, pressed his hands between his thighs, and buried his face in the blanket, trying to warm himself with his own breath. It had been five years since this mirzai was made. Dhaniya had practically forced him to have it stitched, back when they had bought cloth from the Kabuli, which had led to so much trouble and so many curses. And the blanket was older than he was. As a child, he had slept in it with his father; in his youth, Gobar had spent his winters in it; and now, in old age, this same blanket was his companion—but now the teeth that once chewed food only ached.

There had never been a day in his life when, after paying the rent and the mahajan, anything was left over, and now, on top of it all, this new entanglement had arisen. If he did nothing, the world would laugh; if he did something, there was always the suspicion of what people might say. Everyone believed he was robbing Puniya, that he took all her produce into his own house. Far from being thanked, he was being slandered. And on the other hand, Bhola had reminded him several times to arrange a match somewhere, that things could not go on like this. Sobha had told him more than once that Puniya’s intentions towards him were not good. Even if they were not, he would still have to take care of Puniya’s household, whether with a smile or with tears.

Dhaniya’s heart, too, had not yet cleared. There was still regret in her mind. I should not have struck her in front of everyone. To beat the one with whom twenty-five years have been spent, and that too before the whole village—such baseness was mine. But Dhaniya, too, had not spared my honor...

--- Page 98 ---
I left no stone unturned. She avoids me as if we never even knew each other. If she has something to say, she tells it through Sona or Rupa. I see her sari is torn, but even yesterday, when she spoke to me, it was only about Sona’s sari—she didn’t mention her own. Sona’s sari could last another month or two with a few patches. But hers is nothing but a rag, covered in so many patches. And it’s not as if I’m the one trying to coax her. If I had spoken a few kind words to her, would it have made me any less of a man? She would have put on a show, said a few sharp things, but would it have hurt me? Yet, I remained a fool, silent and stubborn. Thank goodness this illness softened her; otherwise, who knows how long she would have kept her face turned away.

And today, the words exchanged between us were like food for the starving. She spoke from her heart, and Hori was overwhelmed. He felt like laying his head at her feet and saying—if I have wronged you, then here, I bow my head, strike me as much as you wish, curse me as much as you want.

Suddenly, he heard the tinkling of bangles in front of the hut. He listened intently. Yes, someone was there—perhaps the patwari’s daughter, or the pandit’s wife. Maybe she had come to pick peas. Who knows why these people are so greedy? They dress better than anyone in the village, eat better than anyone, have thousands of rupees buried in their homes, lend money, collect dues, take bribes, charge commissions, always finding some way to grind down the poor, and yet their hearts are so corrupt! As the father, so the children. And they never come themselves, always send their women. If I got up now and caught her hand, what would be left of their honor? The so-called low-castes are called low, but the minds of the high-castes are even lower. But how can a man catch a woman’s hand? Sometimes you have to swallow the bitter pill. Go ahead, take as much as you want. Pretend I’m not here. If the big people won’t protect their own honor, the small people must protect theirs.

But no, it’s Dhaniya. She’s calling me.

Dhaniya called out—Are you asleep or awake?

Hori quickly got up and stepped outside the hut. Today, it felt as if the goddess herself was pleased and had come to grant him a boon. Yet, in this drizzle and cold, so late at night, her coming was also cause for concern. Surely, something must have happened.

He said, “Who can sleep in this cold? How did you come here in this weather? Is everything all right?”

“Yes, everything is fine.”

“Why didn’t you send Govind to call me?”

Dhaniya gave no answer. Entering the hut, she sat down on the straw and said, “Govind has blackened his face. Why ask about him? What I feared has come to pass.”

“What happened? Did he get into a fight with someone?”

“How should I know what he’s done? Go and ask that woman yourself.”

“Which woman? What are you saying? Have you lost your mind?”

“Why wouldn’t I lose my mind? What has happened is enough to make my heart burst.”

A long ray of light entered Hori’s mind.

“Why don’t you say it plainly? Which woman are you talking about?”

“That Sunia, who else?”

“So Sunia has come here?”

“Where else would she go? Who else would take her in?”

--- Page 99 ---
“Isn’t Gobar at home?”

“No trace of Gobar. Who knows where he’s run off to? And she’s five months pregnant.”

Hori understood everything. He had been suspicious, seeing Gobar go to the Ahir quarter again and again, but he had never thought him so cunning. Young men are always a little mischievous; there’s nothing new in that. But that the cotton-fluff he had seen floating in the blue sky, at which he had only smiled, would one day spread across the whole sky and darken his path so completely—no god could have foreseen that. Gobar, such a scoundrel! That simple rustic, whom he still thought a child. But Hori was not worried about the feast, nor about the panchayat, nor even about how Jhunia would stay in the house. His worry was for Gobar. The boy was shy, inexperienced, proud—what if he did something foolish?

Anxiously, he asked, “Did Jhunia say nothing? Where has Gobar gone? He must have told her before leaving?”

Dhaniya snapped, “Your brains have turned to grass. His beloved is sitting right here—where would he run off to? He must be hiding somewhere nearby. It’s not as if he’s a child who’ll get lost. I’m worried about this wretched Jhunia—what am I to do with her? I won’t let her stay in my house for even a moment. Ever since he went to bring the cow, the two of them have been bickering. If she weren’t pregnant, the matter wouldn’t have come out. But now that she is, Jhunia’s started to panic. She kept saying, ‘Let’s run away somewhere.’ Gobar kept putting her off. Where could he go, taking a woman along? He couldn’t think of anything. Finally, when she insisted, ‘Take me away from here, or I’ll kill myself,’ he said, ‘Come, stay in my house—no one will say a word, I’ll convince Amma.’ And this fool went with him. For a while, he walked ahead, then who knows where he slipped away. She stood there, calling for him. When night fell and he didn’t return, she ran back here. I told her, ‘As you’ve sown, so shall you reap.’ The witch has ruined my boy. She’s been sitting here crying ever since. She won’t get up. Says, ‘How can I show my face at home?’ God, better to be barren than have such children. By morning, the whole village will be in an uproar. Sometimes I feel like eating poison. I’m telling you, I won’t keep her in my house. If you want to keep Gobar, keep him on your own head. There’s no place here for such shameless ones. And if you say a word in their favor, then either you’ll stay or I will.”

Hori said, “If you couldn’t handle her, you shouldn’t have let her into the house.”

“I tried everything, but she wouldn’t budge. She’s sitting there, stubborn as a mule.”

“All right, come, let’s see how she doesn’t get up. I’ll drag her out myself.”

The bearded Bhola had seen everything, but sat there in silence. “Are even fathers so shameless?”

“How would he know what was brewing between them?”

“Why wouldn’t he? Gobar was always hanging around—were his eyes blind? He should have wondered why he kept coming here.”

“Come, I’ll ask Jhunia myself.”

The two of them left the hut and walked toward the village. Hori said, “It must be past midnight.”

“Thieves don’t come to villages like this. They go to the houses of the rich,” said Dhaniya.

“Yes, of course. But what a deep sleep everyone’s in! If a thief came, he could carry off the whole village.”

“Thieves don’t come to villages like this. They go to the houses of the rich.”

Dhaniya stopped, grabbed Hori’s hand, and said, “Look, don’t make a scene, or the whole—”

--- Page 100 ---
The village will awaken, and the news will spread.

Hori said in a harsh tone, “I don’t care about any of that. I’ll grab her by the hand, drag her out, and leave her outside the village. The truth will come out one day—why not today? Why did she come to my house? Let her go wherever Gobar is. If she committed a sin with him, did she ask our permission?”

Dhaniya caught his hand again and spoke softly, “If you grab her hand, she’ll scream.”

“Let her scream.”

“But think—where will she go in the dead of night, in this darkness and silence?”

“She can go to her own people. What business does she have in our house?”

“Yes, but it’s not right to throw her out so late at night. She’s with child—if she gets frightened, there will be more trouble. In such a state, what else can we do?”

“What do I care if she lives or dies? She can go wherever she wants. Why should I blacken my own face? I’ll throw Gobar out too.”

Dhaniya said with deep concern, “The shame that was to come has already come. Now she can never be freed, not while she lives. Gobar has sunk the boat.”

“Not Gobar—she did. He was just a boy. He fell into her clutches.”

“Whoever did it, the boat has sunk now.”

They reached the doorway. Suddenly, Dhaniya put her arms around Hori’s neck and said, “Look, I swear on myself—don’t raise your hand against her. She’s already weeping. If fate hadn’t turned against her, would this day have come?”

Hori’s eyes filled with tears. Even in that darkness, Dhaniya’s motherly affection seemed to light up his careworn face like a lamp. In both their hearts, a memory of youthful days stirred awake. Even now, Hori saw in her the same tender-hearted girl who had entered his life twenty-five years ago. In that embrace was a boundless love that could absorb every stain, every obstacle, every ancient tradition rooted in the soil.

They came to the door and peered inside through the crack. On the shelf, a small oil lamp burned, and in its dim light, Jhunia sat with her head on her knees, facing the door, searching in the darkness for that happiness which, a moment ago, had shown its enchanting face and then vanished. Hounded by calamity, wounded by taunts, and battered by life’s blows, she wandered in search of shelter like a bird seeking shade, and she had found a home, where she felt safe and content. But today, that home, with all its comfort and joy, had disappeared like Aladdin’s palace, and the future stood before her like a monstrous demon, ready to swallow her whole.

Suddenly, as the door opened and she saw Hori enter, she rose trembling with fear, fell at his feet, and weeping, cried, “Dada, I have no one but you now. Beat me, cut me down if you must, but please don’t drive me away from your door.”

Hori bent down, stroked her back with a loving hand, and said gently, “Don’t be afraid, child, don’t be afraid. This is your home, your door, and we are your people. Stay here in peace. As you are Bhola’s daughter, so you are my daughter too. As long as I live, don’t worry about anything. While I am here, no one will dare look at you with a crooked eye. Whatever feast or ceremony is needed, we will provide it all. You need not worry.”

--- Page 101 ---
Jhunia, comforted, clung even tighter to Hori’s feet and said, “Dada, now you are my father, and Amma, you are my mother. I am an orphan. Give me shelter, or else my uncle and brothers will devour me alive.”

Dhaniya could no longer restrain the surge of compassion within her. She said, “Come, sit inside the house. I will deal with your uncle and brothers. They do not rule the world. At most, they will take back their jewelry. Throw it at them, take it off and give it to them.”

Only a short while ago, in a fit of anger, Dhaniya had called Jhunia all manner of names—wanton, defiler, shameless. She had been about to sweep her out of the house. But now, when Jhunia heard these words, full of affection, forgiveness, and reassurance, she let go of Hori’s feet and clung to Dhaniya’s instead. And that same virtuous woman, who had never looked at any man but Hori, now embraced this sinner, wiped away her tears, and soothed her tormented heart with gentle words, like a bird sheltering her chick beneath her wings.

Hori signaled to Dhaniya to give Jhunia something to eat and drink, and then asked Jhunia, “Tell me, child, do you know where Gobar has gone?”

Hori could not hide his anxiety.

“When you saw him today, did he seem upset?”

“He was laughing and talking as usual. Only God knows what was in his heart.”

“What does your heart say—do you think he’s still in the village, or has he gone somewhere else?”

“I have a suspicion he’s gone away somewhere.”

“That’s what my heart says too. What foolishness! We were not his enemies. Once a thing is done, whether good or bad, it must be borne. By running away like this, he has brought calamity upon us.”

Dhaniya, taking Jhunia by the hand and leading her inside, said, “Coward! If you take someone’s hand, you must stand by them, not run away in disgrace. If he comes back now, I won’t let him set foot in this house.”

Hori lay down right there in the straw. Where had Gobar gone? This question circled in his heart like a restless bird.

Such an extraordinary event caused the commotion in the village that was only to be expected, and it continued for months. Jhunia’s two brothers, carrying sticks, roamed about searching for Gobar. Bhola swore that he would never again look at Jhunia’s face, nor set foot in this village. The talks of marriage that Hori had begun with them were now broken off. Now they would take the price of their cow, and in cash, and if there was any delay, they would file a claim against Hori and have his house and land auctioned off. The villagers excommunicated Hori; no one would share a hookah with him, nor drink water from his house. There was even talk of cutting off his water supply, but everyone had seen Dhaniya’s fierce form, so no one dared go that far.

Dhaniya announced to all, loud enough for everyone to hear, “If anyone stops her from drawing water, I’ll spill their blood along with my own.” This challenge made everyone’s courage melt away. The most miserable of all was Jhunia, for whom all this trouble had arisen, and the fact that there was no news of Gobar only deepened her sorrow.

--- Page 102 ---
She has become even more wretched. All day long, she hides her face and stays inside the house. If she steps outside, a rain of sharp words pours down from all sides, making it almost impossible to survive. She spends her days doing the household chores, and whenever she finds a moment, she weeps. She is constantly trembling with fear that Dhania might say something to her. She cannot cook alone, for no one will eat food prepared by her hand, but she has taken all the other work upon herself. Wherever four men or women gather in the village, this slander becomes the subject of their talk.

One day, Dhania was returning from the market when she met Pandit Datadeen on the way. Dhania lowered her head and wished to slip past him unnoticed, but how could Panditji let such an opportunity to tease her slip by? He could not resist and said, “So, Dhania, have you received any news from Gobar? Such a worthless son, he has ruined the honor of the entire household.”

These very thoughts often troubled Dhania’s own mind. She replied with a heavy heart, “When bad days come, Panditji, a man loses his sense. What else can I say?”

Datadeen said, “You should not have kept that wretched girl in your house. If a fly falls into the milk, one throws the fly out and drinks the milk. Think how much disgrace and ridicule you are facing. If that wanton woman had not stayed in your house, nothing would have happened. Young men make such mistakes, but until you feed the biradari and give a feast to the Brahmins, how will you be absolved? If you had not kept her in your house, nothing would have happened. Hori is mad, but how did you get deceived?”

Datadeen’s own son, Matadeen, was entangled with a Chamarin. The whole village knew it, but he wore his tilak, recited scriptures, narrated the Bhagavat, and performed religious rites. His reputation had not suffered in the least. He bathed and prayed daily, thus atoning for his sins. Dhania knew that all this calamity had come only because she had given shelter to Jhunia. Who knows why she felt pity for her? Otherwise, she would have turned Jhunia out that very night—then why would there have been so much ridicule? But she was also afraid: where would Jhunia have gone, except to the river or the well? By sacrificing a life—no, two lives—how could she have preserved her own honor? And then, the child in Jhunia’s womb was a piece of Dhania’s own heart. How could she take its life out of fear of mockery? Moreover, Jhunia’s humility and helplessness always disarmed her. Dhania would return home seething with anger, but as soon as Jhunia brought her a pot of water and began to massage her feet, her anger would melt away. The poor girl was already crushed by her shame and sorrow—why should she be crushed further? What is the use of striking the dead?

She said in a sharp voice, “Honor and reputation are not so dear to me, Panditji, that I would kill a living soul for their sake. She may not be wedded, but it was my son who held her hand. With what face could I have turned her out? The same deeds are done by the great ones, yet no one says a word to them, no one calls them disgraced. But when the poor do the same, their honor is ruined, their noses are cut off. The great value their honor above others’ lives; we do not hold our honor so dear.”

Datadeen was not one to give up easily. He was the Narad of this village. From here to there, from there to here—that was his business. He did not steal himself, for that was dangerous, but he was always present when it came time to divide the spoils. He never let dust settle on his back. He had never paid the zamindar a single paisa of rent; if attachment proceedings came, he would threaten to throw himself into the well. Nokheram could do nothing to him. Yet he lent money to the tenants at interest. If any woman needed jewelry made, Datadeen was ready to serve. He took great pleasure in arranging marriages—he earned both fame and fees from it. In times of illness, he provided medicines, and if needed, exorcisms too—whatever the patient desired.

--- Page 103 ---
Among children, he was a child; among elders, an elder. He was a friend to thieves as well as to the wealthy sah. No one in the village truly trusted him, yet there was something so compelling in his words that, even after being deceived time and again, people would still seek his refuge.

Nodding his head and stroking his beard, he said, “You are right, Dhaniya! This is indeed the dharma of the virtuous, but one must also abide by the customs of the world.”

In the same way, one day Lala Pateshwari teased Hori. In the village, he was renowned as a pious soul. On every full moon, he listened to the story of Satyanarayan, yet, being the patwari, he would have the fields ploughed as begar, have the irrigation done as begar, and pit tenants against each other to extract more money. The entire village trembled before him. By lending ten or five rupees to the poor, he had amassed a fortune of several thousand. He would collect the produce from the tenants and offer it as gifts to the officials of the court and the police. This had earned him great influence throughout the region. The only one who had not fallen into his clutches was Daroga Gandasingh, who had recently arrived in the area. He, too, was a philanthropist. During times of fever, he would distribute government quinine and earn praise; if anyone was ill, he would be sure to visit and inquire after their health. He would settle minor disputes among the villagers himself. During weddings, he would lend his palanquin, carpets, and items for the mehfil, rescuing people in their hour of need. He never missed an opportunity, but he always served those whose bread he ate.

He said, “What illness have you taken upon yourself, Hori?”

Hori turned back and asked, “What did you say, Lala? I didn’t hear.”

Pateshwari, stepping up beside him, said, “I was saying, has your mind gone to grass along with Dhaniya’s? Why don’t you send Jhunia back to her father’s house? You’re making a laughingstock of yourself for nothing. Who knows whose child she’s brought, and you’ve taken her in. You still have two daughters waiting to be married—think, how will you ever manage?”

Hori was weary of hearing such criticisms and well-wishings. Disheartened, he replied, “I understand all this, Lala. But you tell me, what should I do? If I turn Jhunia out, will her father take her in? If he agrees, I’ll take her to his house today. If you can persuade him, I’ll be indebted to you for life. But over there, both his sons are ready to kill her. How can I send her away? First, she found a worthless man who betrayed her after taking her hand. If I too throw her out, in such a state she won’t even be able to work for her living. If she drowns herself somewhere, who will bear the blame? As for marrying off my daughters, that is in God’s hands. When the time comes, some way or another will be found. In our biradari, no girl has ever remained unmarried. Out of fear of the community, I cannot commit murder.”

Hori was a man of gentle nature. He always walked with his head bowed and bore his sorrows in silence. Except for Heera, no one in the village wished him ill, but how could society tolerate such a great transgression? And look at his obstinacy—he refuses to understand even when explained to. Both husband and wife seemed to be challenging society, as if to say, “Let’s see what you can do to us.” And society, too, would show them that those who break its code cannot sleep in peace.

That very night, a meeting of the village elders was called to deliberate on this matter.

Datadeen said, “It is not my habit to speak ill of anyone. What sins are not committed in this world—what is it to me? But that widow Dhaniya is ready to pick a fight with me. Now that she has a few coins in her hand by depriving her brothers of their share, what else can she think of but the path of vice? Low caste—where…”

--- Page 104 ---
They ate their fill and walked with a swagger. That is why the scriptures say—it is better to kick the low-born.  
 
Pateshwari, taking a puff from his coconut hookah, said, “This is what’s wrong with them—show them a few coins and their eyes change. Today, Hori put on such airs that I was left speechless. Who knows what he thinks of himself! Now think, what will be the consequence of this injustice in the village? Seeing Jhunia, will not other widows feel emboldened? Today it happened in Bhola’s house. Tomorrow it will happen in yours or mine. Society runs on the strength of fear. If society’s restraint is lost, then see what calamities befall the world.”

Jhinguri Singh was husband to two women. The first wife had died, leaving behind five sons and daughters. At that time, he was about forty-five, but he married again, and when no child was born of that marriage, he took a third wife. Now he was fifty, and his young wives sat at home. All sorts of rumors circulated about both of them, but for fear of the Thakur sahib, no one dared say a word, nor was there an occasion to say it. Under the shield of a husband, everything is permissible. Trouble is for those who have no protection. Thakur sahib ruled his women with a harsh hand and prided himself that no one had even seen the faces behind their veils. But what shelter is there for a drop of water? What did he know of that?

He said, “Such a woman’s head should be cut off. By keeping this outcaste woman in his house, Hori has sown poison in society. To let such a man remain in the village is to corrupt the whole village. The Rai Sahib must be informed of this at once. It should be made clear: if this injustice is allowed in the village, no one’s honor will remain safe.”

Pandit Nokheram, the clerk, was a most respectable Brahmin. His grandfather had been a royal priest to some raja, but had given up everything at the feet of God and become an ascetic. His father too had spent his life in devotion to Ram. Nokheram had inherited the same piety. He would sit for worship at dawn and keep writing the name of Ram till ten o’clock, but as soon as he rose from before the deity, his mind, distorted by these obstacles, would corrupt his thoughts, words, and deeds. In this matter, he felt his authority insulted, and shame rose in his puffed-up cheeks—why should I ask the Rai Sahib? I can do as I please. If I wish, I’ll have them thrown out of the village. I’ll file for eviction at once.

Pateshwari said, “But hasn’t the rent already been paid?”

Jhinguri Singh agreed, “Yes, when it comes to rent, I hold my tongue.”

Nokheram said proudly, “But it hasn’t been paid yet. What does it matter if he says he’s paid the rent?”

It was unanimously decided that on Holi, the penalty would be collected accordingly. Only one thing remained: to gather the villagers and go through the motions of seeking their approval. Perhaps this would delay things by ten or fifteen days, but that very night, Dubey’s wife gave birth to a son, and the very next day, the village panchayat convened. Hori and Dhania were summoned to hear the verdict on their fate. The crowd in the chaupal was so thick there was not even space to set one’s feet. The panchayat decided that on Holi, a fine of one hundred rupees in cash and thirty maunds of grain would be imposed.

Dhania, standing in the midst of the assembly, spoke in a choked voice, “Pandit, you will not find happiness by tormenting the poor, remember that. We will be ruined, who knows if we’ll even remain in this village, but my curse will surely fall upon you. Such a heavy penalty is being imposed on me just because I gave shelter to my daughter-in-law…”

--- Page 105 ---
Did you keep her in the house? Why didn’t you throw her out and make her a beggar on the street? Is this justice, eh?

Pateshwari said, “She is not your daughter-in-law, she is a harjai—a faithless woman.”

Hori scolded Dhaniya, “Why do you speak, Dhaniya? The Panchayat is the seat of God. Whatever justice they deliver, we must accept with bowed heads. If it is God’s will that we must leave the village, then what power do we have? Panchayat, whatever we possess is still in the threshing yard. Not a single grain has come into the house. Take as much as you want, take it all if you wish. God is our master; if there is still a shortfall, take our two bullocks as well.”

Dhaniya, her teeth clenched, retorted, “I will not give a single grain of food, nor a single coin of fine. Whoever has the strength, come and take it from me. What a fine joke! You must have thought, ‘Let’s seize all their property in the name of a fine, then take a bribe and hand it over to someone else. Sell the orchards and gardens and enjoy the spoils.’ As long as I live, this will not happen, and your greed will remain in your heart. We will not stay in this biradari. Even if we remain, will we ever be free? Now we eat the bread of our own sweat, and so we shall, wherever we go.”

Hori folded his hands before her and said, “Dhaniya, I beg you, be silent. We are all servants of the biradari; we cannot step outside it. Whatever fine they impose, accept it with bowed head. Better to hang oneself than to live as an outcast. If we die today, is it not the biradari that will see us to our graves? Only the biradari can save us, and if they save us, we are saved. Panchayat, may I never see the face of my grown son if I possess anything besides the grain in the threshing yard. I will not betray the biradari. If the Panchayat has any mercy for my children, let them care for them a little; otherwise, I must obey their command.”

Dhaniya, furious, stormed away, and Hori spent half the night carrying grain from the threshing yard and piling it in Jhinguri Singh’s chaupal. There were twenty maunds of barley, five maunds of wheat, as much peas, a little gram and oilseed as well. One man, bearing the burden of two households. All this was achieved by Dhaniya’s hard work. Jhunia managed all the work inside, while Dhaniya, with her daughters, toiled in the fields. Both had hoped that the wheat and oilseed would pay one installment of the rent, and perhaps a little interest as well. The barley would serve for food. Somehow, they would scrape by for five or six months, and then it would be time for juar, maize, samba, and paddy. All those hopes were now dust. The grain was gone, and a debt of a hundred rupees now weighed on their heads. Now there was no certainty of food. And what would become of Gobar, only God knew. No news, no word. If the heart was so weak, why do such a thing? But who can avert fate? The terror of the biradari was such that he carried the grain on his own head, as if digging his own grave with his own hands. What zamindar, what moneylender, what government ever had such power? The worry of what the children would eat tomorrow gnawed at his soul, but the fear of the biradari sat on his head like a demon, goading him on. He could not even imagine a life apart from the biradari. Marriage, tonsure, birth, death—everything was in the hands of the biradari. The biradari had sunk its roots deep into his life, its veins ran through every fiber of his being. To leave the biradari would be to unravel his life, to tear it to shreds.

When only a maund and a half or two of barley remained in the threshing yard, Dhaniya ran over, caught his hand, and said, “Enough, leave it now. You have done enough to save the biradari’s honor. Will you leave nothing for the children, or will you throw it all into the biradari’s fire? I give up. It was my fate to be saddled with a fool like you.”

--- Page 106 ---
Hori, freeing his hand, began to fill the basket with grain and said, “That will not do, Dhaniya. To keep back even a single grain from the eyes of the panchayat would be a sin for me. I will take everything there and pile it up. If compassion arises in the hearts of the panchayat, they may give something for my children; if not, God is the master.”

Dhaniya, burning with anger, retorted, “These are not panchayat elders, they are demons, true demons! They all want to seize our land and property and enrich themselves. The fine is just an excuse. I keep trying to make you understand, but you refuse to open your eyes. You expect mercy from these fiends, you think they’ll set aside a few maunds for you? Keep dreaming!”

When Hori would not listen and began to lift the basket onto his head, Dhaniya seized it with both hands, using all her strength, and declared, “I will not let you take this, even if you take my life. We earned this through sweat and toil, watering the fields through the night, keeping vigil, just so the panchayat can twirl their moustaches and feast while our children starve for every grain? You did not do everything alone. I too have suffered with our daughters. Put the basket down, or today our bond will be broken forever. I warn you.”

Hori fell into thought. There was truth in Dhaniya’s words. What right did he have to snatch away his children’s earnings to pay a penalty? He was the head of the household so that he could provide for all, not so that he could rob them of their livelihood to save face before the community. The basket slipped from his hands. Softly, he said, “You are right, Dhaniya. I have no claim on the share of others. Take what is left. I will speak to the panchayat.”

Dhaniya, placing the basket of grain inside the house, began to sing the sohar—songs of childbirth—at the top of her lungs with her two daughters, so that the whole village could hear. This was the first time that, on such an auspicious occasion, not a single woman from the community was present. From her confinement, Jhunia had sent word that there was no need to sing the sohar, but Dhaniya would not listen. If the community cared nothing for her, she too would care nothing for them.

At that very moment, Hori was mortgaging his house to Jhinguri Singh for eighty rupees. He could find no other way to pay the fine. Twenty rupees had come from selling oilseeds, wheat, and peas. For the rest, he had to mortgage the house. Nokhe Ram had wanted the bullocks to be sold, but Pateshwari and Datadin opposed it. If the bullocks were sold, how would Hori farm? The community could recover their money from his property, but not to the extent that he would be forced to flee the village. Thus, the bullocks were spared.

Hori returned home around eleven at night, having written the mortgage deed. Dhaniya asked, “What kept you there so late?”

Hori, venting his anger at the weaver on his own beard, replied, “What else was I doing? Paying for that boy’s misdeeds. The wretch started the fire and ran away, and now I have to put it out. I had to mortgage the house for eighty rupees. What else could I do? Now the hookah is open to me again. The community has forgiven my offense.”

Dhaniya bit her lip and said, “And if the hookah had not been opened to you, what would have been lost? If we didn’t smoke anyone’s hookah for four or five months, would we have become any less? I ask you, why are you so simple? You act so wise before me, but outside, why do you become tongue-tied? The only thing left from our ancestors was this house, and today you have mortgaged even that. In this way, tomorrow…”

--- Page 107 ---
Three or four bighas of land—write that off too, and then go beg from door to door. Tell me, did you not have a tongue in your mouth to ask those panchayat elders, what makes you such great men of virtue, that you go around imposing fines on others? Even to look at your faces is a sin.

Hori scolded her, “Be quiet! Don’t talk so insolently. You haven’t yet been caught in the snare of the biradari, or you wouldn’t dare utter a word.”

Dhaniya grew more agitated. “What sin have we committed, that we should fear the biradari? Have we stolen from anyone, cut someone’s crop? To take a woman is not a sin. Yes, to take her and then abandon her, that is a sin. It is not good for a man to be too meek. The result of such meekness is that even dogs come to lick your face. Today, over there, they must be singing your praises—how well you have upheld the honor of the biradari! I must have been cursed to be saddled with a man like you. Never once have I known a day’s peace.”

“Did I go to fall at your father’s feet? He was the one who tied you to my neck.”

“A stone must have fallen on his wits—what can I say to him? Who knows what he saw in you to become so besotted? It’s not as if you were so very handsome.”

The quarrel drifted into banter. Eighty rupees were gone, but at least they had a child worth a hundred thousand. No one could take him away. If Gobar returned home, Dhaniya would be content even in her separate hut.

Hori asked, “Whose child is it?”

Dhaniya replied with a happy face, “It’s Gobar’s, truly.”

“Is he healthy and strong?”

“Yes, he is well.”

That night, as Gobar walked with Jhunia, he trembled as if he had been disgraced. The moment the villagers saw Jhunia, there would be an uproar, wailing from all sides, Dhaniya would hurl curses—these thoughts made his steps falter. He did not fear Hori; he would roar once, then fall silent. It was Dhaniya he feared—she might swallow poison, set the house on fire. No, he could not bring Jhunia home with him now.

But what if Dhaniya refused to let Jhunia enter, chased her with a broom? Where would the poor girl go then? She could not return to her own home. What if she jumped into a well or hanged herself? What then? He sighed deeply. Whose shelter could he seek?

But Amma was not so cruel as to run after her to beat her. In anger, she might hurl a few abuses, but when Jhunia clung to her feet and wept, surely she would feel pity. Until then, he would hide somewhere. When the commotion had died down, he would quietly return one day and persuade Amma. If, in the meantime, he found some work and came home with a few rupees, Dhaniya would have nothing more to say.

Jhunia said, “My heart is pounding. How could I have known you would bring this curse upon me? Who knows what ill-starred moment I first saw you. If you hadn’t come to buy the cow, none of this would have happened. You go ahead and say whatever needs to be said. I’ll come after you.”

Gobar replied, “No, no, you go first and say you were coming home from the market after selling your wares. It got late, and now you can’t go back. By then, I’ll be there.”

Jhunia said anxiously, “Your Amma is very hot-tempered. I am truly afraid…”

--- Page 108 ---
What will I do if they start beating me?

Gobur tried to reassure her. “Amma isn’t like that. She’s never even slapped us, not even once—why would she beat you? Whatever she has to say, she’ll say to me. She won’t even speak to you.”

The village was now close. Gobur stopped and said, “Now you go on ahead.”

Jhunia pleaded, “Don’t be long.”

“No, no, I’ll be there in a moment. You go on.”

“I don’t know why, but my heart feels uneasy. I’m angry with you.”

“Why are you so afraid? I’m coming right behind you.”

“It would have been better if we’d just run away somewhere else.”

“When we have our own home, why should we run? You’re worrying for nothing.”

“You’ll come quickly, won’t you?”

“Yes, yes, I’m coming right away.”

“You’re not deceiving me, are you? Sending me home and then slipping away yourself?”

“I’m not that low, Jhunia. When I’ve taken your hand, I’ll stand by you till my last breath.”

Jhunia started towards the house. Gobur stood for a moment, uncertain. Then suddenly, the thought of the shame and reproach hovering over his head took on a terrifying shape before him. What if Amma really does rush to beat her? His feet seemed rooted to the ground. Only a small mango grove separated him from his home. He could see Jhunia’s dark shadow slowly moving away. All his senses were heightened. He thought he heard Amma cursing Jhunia. His mind felt as if an axe was about to fall on his head. It was as though all the blood in his body had dried up. 

A moment later, he saw Dhaniya leaving the house, heading somewhere. She must be going to Dada. Perhaps Dada had finished eating and gone to guard the peas. Gobur set off towards the pea field, trampling through the barley and wheat fields as if something was chasing him. There was Dada’s hut. He stopped and crept quietly to sit behind it.

His guess was right. He had barely arrived when he heard Dhaniya’s voice. Oh! It’s all over! Amma is so harsh. She feels not a shred of pity for an orphaned girl. And if I go and confront her, telling her she has no right to speak to Jhunia, all my bravado will vanish. And now Dada is angry too. Even the clay pot is sharper today, all because I tried to be respectful. That’s what I get. Now Dada is heading back as well. If they beat Jhunia, I won’t be able to bear it. O God! Now I can only rely on you. I never knew I’d be caught in such a calamity. Jhunia must think me so cunning, cowardly, and base, but how can they beat her? How can they throw her out of the house? Don’t I have a share in this home? If anyone raises a hand against Jhunia, there will be a Mahabharata today. Parents are only parents as long as they protect their children. If there’s no affection left, what kind of parents are they?

As soon as Hori left the hut, Gobur too crept quietly behind him, but seeing the light at the doorway, his feet stopped. He could not step into that line of light. He stood pressed against the wall in the darkness. His courage failed him. Alas! These people are raging at poor, innocent Jhunia, and he can do nothing. The spark he had tossed in play would now burn down the whole threshing floor—he had never imagined that. And now he didn’t have the courage to come forward and say, “Yes, I threw the spark.” The same pegs with which he had—

--- Page 109 ---
He had steadied his heart, but now, in this earthquake, all those supports were crumbling, and his little hut had collapsed. He turned back. How could he face Jhunia now?

He walked a hundred steps, but it was as if a soldier were fleeing the battlefield. All the words of love and marriage he had spoken to Jhunia came flooding back to him. He remembered the sweet memories of their secret meetings, when, in his wild sighs and intoxicating glances, it was as if he laid his very soul at her feet. Jhunia, like a lonely bird, had been living a solitary life in her tiny nest. There was no insistent demand of a male, no exuberant joy, no sweet chirping of fledglings—but neither was there the snare and deceit of the hunter. Who knows if Gobar had brought her any happiness by entering her secluded nest, but he had certainly brought her misfortune. He steadied himself. The fleeing soldier, hearing the rallying cry of a comrade, turned back.

When he reached the door, he saw it was shut. Slivers of light escaped through the cracks in the door. He peered in through one of them. Dhaniya was consoling Jhunia: “Daughter, come and sit inside the house. I will deal with your uncles and brothers. As long as we live, you need not worry about anything. While we are here, no one will dare cast a crooked glance at you.”

Gobar was overcome with emotion. If only he were worthy today, he would have adorned his father and mother with gold and said, “Now you need worry about nothing. Sit in comfort, eat as you please, and give as much in charity as your heart desires.” Now he had no doubts about Jhunia. The shelter he had wanted to give her, she had found. If Jhunia thought him a traitor, so be it. He would only return home when he could silence the whole village with the power of money, and when his father and mother would see him not as the shame of the family, but as its pride.

The deeper the wound to the heart, the deeper its response. This disgrace and stigma churned Gobar’s innermost being and brought forth a gem that had lain hidden until now. For the first time, he became aware of his responsibilities, and with that, a resolve was born. Until now, he had thought it his right to do as little as possible and eat as much as he could. Never had it occurred to him that he too had duties toward his family. Today, his parents’ noble forgiveness seemed to fill his heart with light. When Dhaniya and Jhunia went inside, he sat down in Hori’s cowshed and began to dream of the future.

He had heard that city laborers earned five or six annas a day. If he could earn six annas a day and live on just one, he could save five annas daily. That would be ten rupees a month, and a hundred and twenty-five in a year. If he returned home with a pouch of a hundred and twenty-five rupees, who would dare speak against him? Even Dattadin and Patesari would come and agree with him, and Jhunia would be swollen with pride. If he worked like this for two or four years, all the family’s debts would be wiped out. Right now, the whole family’s earnings didn’t add up to a hundred and twenty-five rupees a year. Now he alone would earn that much. People would say he worked as a laborer—let them say it. There was no sin in labor. And it wasn’t as if he would always earn just six annas. As he became more skilled, his wages would rise. Then he would tell his father, “Now you sit at home and worship Bhagwan. What is there in this farming except toil and hardship?” The very first thing he would do was buy a milch cow, one that gave four or five seers of milk, and tell his father, “You serve the holy cow, and you will be blessed in this world and the next.”

And why not? Couldn’t he live comfortably on one anna a day? What need had he for a house? He would sleep in someone’s verandah. There were hundreds of temples—what need for a home? For one anna, he could buy two and a half pounds of flour. For one anna, two and a half pounds of flour. For one anna, two and a half pounds of flour. For one anna, two and a half pounds of flour...

--- Page 111 ---
The man seized her hair and began to drag her. The young woman rolled onto the ground.

The man, defeated, said, “I tell you again, get up and come with me.”

With the same firmness, the woman replied, “I will not go to your house for seven lifetimes. Cut off my braid if you wish.”

“I’ll cut your throat!”

“Then you’ll hang for it.”

The man let go of her hair and sat down, holding his head in his hands. His manhood had reached its limit. Beyond this, he had no power left.

After a moment, he stood up again and, admitting defeat, asked, “What is it that you want, after all?”

The young woman also sat up and said calmly, “I want you to leave me alone.”

“At least say something—what happened?”

“Should I let anyone insult my father and brother?”

“Who insulted your father and brother?”

“Go ask in your own house.”

“I’ll ask only if you come with me.”

“What will you ask? Do you have any courage? Go, hide your face in Amma’s sari and sleep. She may be your mother, but she is nothing to me. You listen to her abuse if you wish—why should I? I eat one roti, but I work for four. Why should I bear anyone’s tyranny? I don’t even care for a brass ring of yours!”

The passersby were enjoying the drama of this quarrel, but there was no hope of it ending soon. Their journey was being delayed. One by one, people began to slip away. Gobar was disturbed by the man’s cruelty. He could not say anything in front of the crowd. When the field cleared, he spoke:

“Brother, it’s not right to interfere between a man and his wife, but such heartlessness is not good.”

The man glared at him and asked, “Who are you?”

Gobar replied without fear, “I am no one, but anyone would feel bad seeing such injustice.”

The man shook his head and said, “Seems like you haven’t got a wife yet, that’s why you feel so much pain.”

“When I do have a wife, I won’t drag her by the hair.”

“All right, then go your way. She’s my woman, I’ll beat her or kill her—who are you to speak? Go on, don’t stand here.”

Gobar’s hot blood boiled even more. Why should he leave? The road belonged to the government, not to anyone’s father. He could stand there as long as he wished. No one had the right to drive him away.

The man bit his lip and said, “So you won’t go? Shall I come at you?”

Gobar tied his gamchha around his waist, readying himself for a fight, and said, “Come or don’t come. I’ll leave only when I wish.”

“Looks like you’ll leave with your arms and legs broken.”

“Who knows whose arms and legs will break.”

“So you won’t go?”

“No.”

The man clenched his fist and lunged at Gobar. At that very moment, the young woman grabbed his dhoti, pulled him towards her, and said to Gobar, “Why are you set on fighting, brother? Go on your way.”

--- Page 112 ---
Why don’t you go? Is there some spectacle here? This is a quarrel between us. Sometimes he beats me, sometimes I scold him. What concern is it of yours?

Receiving this rebuke, Govar turned to leave. In his heart, he thought—this woman is only fit to be beaten.

As Govar walked ahead, the young woman scolded her husband—Why do you start fighting with everyone? What wrong did he say that hurt you so? If you do wrong, the world will speak ill of you. But he seems to be from a good family, and of our own community too. Why not consider him for your sister?

Her husband replied, doubtfully—Do you think he’s still unmarried?

“Why not just ask him?”

The man ran ten steps ahead, called out, and motioned with his hand for Govar to stop. Govar thought, perhaps he’s possessed again, that’s why he’s calling out. He won’t be satisfied until he beats me. In one’s own village, even a dog becomes a lion. But let him come.

But there was no challenge of battle on his face, rather an invitation of friendship. He asked Govar his village, name, and caste. Govar answered straightforwardly. The man’s name was Kodai.

Kodai smiled and said—We were on the verge of a fight, but it was averted. When you walked away, I thought—you spoke rightly. I was wrong to get angry with you. Do you have some land at home?

Govar replied that he had five ancestral bighas of land and farmed with one plough.

“Forgive me, brother, for what I said in anger! In rage, a man becomes blind. A woman is Lakshmi in virtue and wisdom, but sometimes, who knows what spirit possesses her. Now, you tell me, what control do I have over my mother? She gave me birth, raised me, cared for me. When there’s any matter, I speak only to my wife. I have authority over her. Tell me yourself, am I saying anything wrong? Yes, I shouldn’t have dragged her by the hair, but a woman won’t stay in line without some scolding. She wants me to separate from my mother. Tell me, how can I separate, and from whom? From my own mother? The one who gave me birth? That I cannot do. My wife may stay or leave.”

Govar, too, had to change his mind. “To honor one’s mother is everyone’s duty, brother! Who can ever repay a mother?”

Kodai invited him to his home. There was no way Govar could reach Lucknow today. Walking two or three miles, it would be evening. He would have to stay somewhere for the night.

Govar joked, “Has your wife agreed?”

“If she doesn’t, what can she do?”

“She scolded me so sharply, I was ashamed.”

“She herself is regretting it. Come, help me reason with mother. I can’t say anything to her. But she should also think—why does she abuse her daughter-in-law’s father and brother? After all, they’re our own kin. In a few days, my sister will be engaged. If her mother-in-law abuses us, will we be able to bear it? The fault isn’t all my wife’s. My mother is also to blame. If she always takes her own daughter’s side, naturally we’ll feel hurt. The good thing is, my wife, even if she leaves the house in anger, never answers abuse with abuse.”

Govar needed a place to stay for the night anyway, so he went along with Kodai. The two returned to the same spot where the young woman was sitting. She had now become the housewife, her demeanor changed. She had drawn her veil modestly.

--- Page 113 ---
...and she began to feel shy.

Kodai smiled and said, "He wouldn’t even come inside. He said, after being scolded like that, how could he go to their house?" The young woman, peeking from behind her veil, looked at Gobar and said, "You got scared from just a little scolding? When your wife comes, where will you run then?"

The village was close by. In truth, it was not even a proper village, but a hamlet—purwa—of ten or twelve houses, half with tiled roofs, half thatched with straw. When Kodai reached home, he brought out a cot, spread a rug over it, asked for sherbet to be made, and filled his chilam. In a moment, the same young woman arrived with a lota of sherbet, sprinkled a little water on Gobar as if to ask forgiveness. He was now to become her brother-in-law. Why shouldn’t the teasing begin already?

Gobar rose before dawn and took leave of Kodai. Everyone knew by now that he was married, so no one brought up any talk of his wedding. His gentle and modest nature had won over the whole household. To Kodai’s mother, he spoke in such sweet words, with such respect for her maternal status, and gave such wise counsel that she blessed him with joy.

"You are elder, Mother, worthy of reverence. A son can never repay his mother’s debt, not in a hundred lifetimes, not in a million."

The old woman was moved to tears by this boundless devotion. After that, whatever Gobar said seemed to her only for her own good. Once a vaidya cures a patient, the patient will even drink poison from his hands gladly. Now, say, if the daughter-in-law left the house in anger today, whose disgrace would it be? Who knows the daughter-in-law? Who knows whose daughter she is, whose granddaughter? Perhaps her father was nothing but a grass-cutter...

The old woman said with certainty, "She is a grass-cutter’s daughter, son, a true grass-cutter. If you see her face in the morning, you won’t get water all day."

Gobar replied, "So what is there to laugh at such a person? The joke is on you, and your husband. Whoever asks, will only ask whose daughter-in-law she is. And she is still just a girl, innocent, wild. The daughter of low-caste parents—how could she turn out well? You’ll have to teach the old parrot to chant the name of Ram. Beating won’t teach her; only gentle affection will. Scold her, but don’t argue with her. Nothing of hers is harmed, but your own honor is lost."

When Gobar was about to leave, the old woman mixed sugar and sattu and gave it to him to eat. Several other men from the village were also heading to the city in search of work. The journey passed in conversation, and by nine o’clock they all reached the bazaar at Aminabad. Gobar was astonished—where had so many people come from? People were jostling and pressing against each other.

That day, there could not have been fewer than four or five hundred laborers in the bazaar—masons, carpenters, blacksmiths, porters, cot-weavers, basket-carriers, and stonecutters, all had gathered. Seeing this crowd, Gobar felt disheartened. How could so many laborers find work? And he didn’t even have any tools. How would anyone know what work he could do? Why would anyone hire him? Without tools, who would even ask his name?

Gradually, one by one, the laborers began to find work. Some, disappointed, turned back for home...

--- Page 114 ---
They were returning. Most of those left behind were the old and the useless, who had no one to inquire after them, and among them was Gobar as well. But for today, at least, he had something to eat. There was no sorrow.

Suddenly, Mirza Khushend came among the laborers and called out in a loud voice, “Whoever wants to work for six annas a day, come with me. Everyone will get six annas. Work will end at five o’clock.”

Except for a handful of carpenters and masons, all of them were ready to go with him. A vast army of four hundred ragged souls assembled. At the front was Mirza, carrying a thick staff on his shoulder. Behind him stretched a long line of hungry men, like a flock of sheep.

An old man asked Mirza, “What work is it, sahib?”

When Mirza told them the work, everyone was even more astonished. It was only to play kabaddi. What sort of man was this, who was offering six annas a day just to play kabaddi? Was he mad? Wealth often drives a man insane. Too much learning can also make a man lose his mind. Some began to suspect—could this be a joke? What if he took them home and then said there was no work at all? What could anyone do to him? Whether he made them play kabaddi, or blind man’s bluff, or gilli-danda, as long as he paid the wages in advance, what did it matter? Who could trust such an eccentric man?

Gobar said timidly, “Sahib, I have nothing to eat. If I could get the money, I’d buy something to eat.”

Mirza immediately placed six annas in his hand and called out, “Everyone will be paid their wages in advance as we go. Don’t worry about that.”

Mirza sahib had bought a small piece of land outside the city. When the laborers arrived, they saw a large compound, fenced in, and inside it only a small thatched hut, with three or four chairs and a table. A few books lay on the table. The hut, surrounded by vines and creepers, looked very beautiful. On one side of the compound were saplings of mango, lemon, and guava; on the other, some flowers. Most of the land lay fallow. Mirza lined everyone up and distributed the wages right there. Now no one doubted his madness.

Gobar had already received his money. Mirza called him over and assigned him the task of watering the saplings. He would not get to play kabaddi. He felt a pang of resentment. He would have enjoyed tossing those old men around, but it didn’t matter. He had played enough kabaddi in his life. He had received his full wages.

Today, after ages, these decrepit old men had the good fortune to play kabaddi. Most of them couldn’t even remember if they had ever played before. All day they toiled in the city, returning home late at night, eating whatever dry morsel they could find, and collapsing into bed. At dawn, the same grind began again. Life had become dull, joyless, a mere routine. But today, given this chance, even the old became young again. The half-dead elders, with their walking sticks, toothless mouths, and empty bellies, dhotis or tahmads pulled up over their thighs, slapped their thighs and leapt about as if youth had flooded into their old bones. Teams were quickly formed, two captains chosen. Players were selected, and by noon the game began. The cool winter sunlight was the perfect season for such games.

Meanwhile, at the gate of the compound, Mirza sahib was handing out tickets to the spectators. He was always possessed by some whim or another—taking money from the rich and distributing it among the poor. This old men’s kabaddi match had been advertised for days. Large posters had been pasted up. Notices had been distributed. This game would be unique, unprecedented. Those who wished to see how sturdy India’s old men still were should come and feast their eyes. Whoever missed this spectacle would regret it. Such was the Godan.

--- Page 115 ---
Such an opportunity would not come again. Tickets ranged from ten rupees down to two annas. By three o’clock, the entire courtyard was filled. There was a constant stream of motorcars and horse-drawn carriages. The crowd could not have been less than two thousand. For the elite, there were arrangements of chairs and benches; for the common people, a clean patch of earth.

Miss Malti, Mehta, Khanna, Tankha, and Rai Sahib were all present.

As the game began, Mirza said to Mehta, “Come, Doctor Sahib, let’s have a bout between you and me.”

Miss Malti interjected, “A philosopher’s match can only be with another philosopher.”

Mirza twirled his moustache and said, “So you think I’m not a philosopher? I may not have a tail, but I am a philosopher. You may test me, Mehta-ji!”

Malti asked, “Very well, tell me—are you an idealist or a materialist?”

“I am both.”

“How so?”

“Very easily. I become whatever the occasion demands.”

“So you have no conviction of your own?”

“About a matter that has never been settled, and never will be, how can I be expected to settle it? Others strain their eyes and devour books to reach a conclusion, but I arrived there just like that. Can you tell me, has any philosopher ever done anything besides splitting hairs?”

Dr. Mehta, unbuttoning his achkan, said, “Then let us have our bout. Whether anyone else agrees or not, I consider you a philosopher.”

Mirza turned to Khanna, “Shall I find a partner for you as well?”

Malti chimed in, “Yes, yes, do take him—pair him with Mr. Tankha.”

Khanna, embarrassed, replied, “No, please excuse me.”

Mirza asked Rai Sahib, “Shall I bring a partner for you?”

Rai Sahib replied, “My partner is Omkarnath, but he does not seem to be here today.”

Mirza and Mehta, both bare-chested, wearing only loincloths, entered the field—one on this side, the other on that. The game began.

The crowd laughed at the old wrestlers, clapped, jeered, cheered, and placed bets. “Look at this old fellow! See how proudly he strides, as if he’ll defeat everyone and return victorious. Ah, from the other side, his elder brother appears. See how both are changing their moves? There’s still plenty of life in these old bones. The amount of ghee these men have eaten, we can’t even get that much water nowadays. People say India is becoming prosperous—perhaps it is. But we see that even the youth today would struggle to match the stamina of these old men. Look, the old man from that side has grabbed him! The poor fellow is struggling to break free, but now he can’t escape, my boy. Three have piled onto one.” In this way, people expressed their excitement. All their attention was fixed on the field. They were absorbed in the players’ attacks and counterattacks, their leaps and tackles, their victories and defeats. Sometimes laughter erupted from all sides; sometimes, seeing some injustice or foul play, people would raise a clamor—“Let go, let go!” Some, in their excitement, would rush toward the boundary. But the few gentlemen seated in the shamiana with high-priced tickets derived little enjoyment from the game. They were engaged in matters of greater importance.

--- Page 116 ---
Khanna emptied his glass of ginger ale, lit a cigar, and said to Rai Sahib, “I’ve already told you, the bank will not agree to any lower interest rate, and even this concession I am making only because you are like family.”

Rai Sahib curled a smile into his mustache and replied, “So, according to your policy, it is family who should be slaughtered with the bluntest knife?”

“What are you saying?”

“I’m speaking the truth. From Suryapratap Singh, you took only seven percent, but from me, you’re demanding nine percent—and on top of that, you expect gratitude. Why wouldn’t you?”

“I would take the same interest from you, if you agreed to the same terms. We have mortgaged his property, and it’s unlikely he’ll ever get it back.”

“I’d rather part with some of my property than pay nine percent. It’s better to separate the surplus property. Sell my bungalow on Jackson Road. Take your commission.”

“It won’t be easy to sell that bungalow. You know how far it is from the main town. But all right, I’ll see what I can do. What price do you estimate for it?”

Rai Sahib named one hundred and twenty-five thousand. “There are fifteen bighas of land with it as well.” Khanna was taken aback. He said, “You’re dreaming of prices from fifteen years ago, Rai Sahib! You should know that property values have fallen by fifty percent lately.”

Rai Sahib, offended, replied, “No, fifteen years ago its price was one and a half lakh.”

“I’ll look for a buyer, but my commission from you will be five percent.”

“With others, perhaps it would be ten percent. Why, what will you do with so much money?”

“Give me whatever you think fit. So, you agree now. Haven’t you bought shares in the sugar mill yet? Only a few shares are left now. You’ll be left wringing your hands. And you haven’t taken out an insurance policy either. You have this bad habit of procrastinating. If you delay so much even in matters of your own benefit, what good can others expect from you people? That’s why they say, an estate dulls a man’s wits. If it were up to me, I would confiscate all the taluqdars’ estates.”

Meanwhile, Mr. Tankha was casting his net for Malti. Malti had made it clear that she did not wish to get entangled in the mess of elections, but Tankha was not one to give up so easily. He came and leaned his elbows on the table and said, “Please reconsider the matter. I tell you, you may never get such an opportunity again. Rani Sahiba Chanda doesn’t stand a chance against you, not even by a fraction. My only wish is that people enter the council who have some experience in life and have served the public. A woman who has known nothing but luxury, who has always treated the public as mere fuel for her car, whose most valuable services are the parties she throws for governors and secretaries—there is no place for such a person in this council. The new council will have considerable powers in the hands of the representatives, and I do not want those powers to fall into the wrong hands.”

To extricate herself, Malti said, “But, sir, where do I have ten or twenty thousand to spend on an election? Rani Sahiba can easily spend two or four lakhs. Even I get a thousand or fifteen hundred rupees from her each year; that income will also slip away.”

“First, tell me, do you want to go or not?”

“I do want to go, but only if I get a free pass.”

--- Page 117 ---
“Then it’s my responsibility—you will get a free pass.”

“No, forgive me. I do not wish to bear the humiliation of defeat. When Rani Sahiba opens her bags of rupees and each vote is bought with a gold coin, perhaps even you will cast your vote for her.”

“In your opinion, elections can be won merely with money?”

“No, a person is something too. But what public service have I done except for that one time I went to jail? And to tell the truth, even then I went for my own reasons, just as Raisahab and Khanna did. The foundation of this new civilization is wealth. Knowledge, service, lineage, and caste—all are insignificant before money. Sometimes, history brings such moments when wealth must bow before a movement, but consider that an exception. I speak only for myself. If a poor woman comes to the clinic, I do not even speak to her for hours. But if a lady arrives in a car, I go to the door to welcome her and serve her as if she were a goddess incarnate. There is no comparison between me and Rani Sahiba. The kind of councils that are being formed—Rani Sahiba is far more suited for them.”

Out in the field, Mehta’s team was weakening. More than half the players had been eliminated. Mehta had never played kabaddi in his life. Mirza was a master of this art. Mehta’s holidays were spent practicing drama. He could astonish even the best with his transformations, while for Mirza, all his interest lay in the arena—whether of wrestlers or of fairies.

Malti’s attention was also drawn there. She got up and said to Raisahab, “Mehta’s party is being badly beaten.”

Raisahab and Khanna were discussing insurance. Raisahab seemed bored with the topic. Malti, as if, had freed him from a constraint. He stood up and said, “Yes, they are being beaten. Mirza is a seasoned player.”

“What possessed Mehta to do this? He’s making a fool of himself for nothing.”

“What’s foolish about it? It’s just a bit of fun.”

“Whoever steps out for Mehta’s side is immediately out.”

After a moment, she asked, “Is there no half-time in this game?”

Khanna was struck by mischief. He said, “You were the one who wanted to challenge Mirza. Thought this was philosophy too, did you?”

“I’m asking, is there no half-time in this game?”

Khanna teased her again, “The game is almost over now. The real fun will be when Mirza grabs Mehta and rubs him into the ground, and Mehta Sahib cries out in defeat.”

“I am not asking you. I am asking Raisahab.”

Raisahab replied, “Half-time in this game? Only one person comes forward at a time.”

“Well, another of Mehta’s men is out.”

Khanna said, “Just keep watching. In this way, everyone will be out, and in the end, Mehta Sahib will be out too.”

Malti was irritated. “You didn’t have the courage to step out yourself?”

“I don’t play the games of rustics. Tennis is for me.”

“And even in tennis, I have beaten you in hundreds of games.”

--- Page 118 ---
“I never claimed I could beat you.”

“If you do claim it, I am ready.”

Malti, pretending to scold him, returned to her seat. No one sympathized with Mehta. No one suggested that the game should end now. Mehta was a strange, naive man—why didn’t he cheat a little? Here he was, displaying his sense of justice. Soon he would lose and return, and applause would ring out from all sides. Now perhaps twenty people were on his side, and how happy the others were!

As the end drew near, people grew more impatient and pressed closer to the playing area. The rope enclosure that had been set up was broken down. The volunteers tried to restrain the crowd, but in the frenzy of excitement, they were powerless. The tide rose to its final crest, and Mehta was left alone—now he would have to play the part of the mute. Everything depended on him now. If he could escape and return to his side, his team would be saved. If not, he would have to return bearing all the shame and humiliation of defeat. Every opponent he touched and brought back to his side would be considered “dead,” and an equal number from his team would be revived. All eyes were fixed on Mehta. He began to move. The crowd surged in from all sides, encircling the playing field. The tension was at its peak. Mehta advanced toward his enemies with remarkable calm. Every movement of his was reflected in the crowd—someone craned their neck, another leaned forward. The atmosphere was electric; excitement had reached its boiling point. Mehta plunged into the enemy ranks. The group retreated before him. Their formation was so tight that Mehta could not touch or grab anyone. Many had hoped Mehta would at least revive five or ten of his teammates, but their hopes were fading.

Suddenly, Mirza leaped forward and seized Mehta around the waist. Mehta struggled to free himself, dragging Mirza toward his side. The crowd went wild. Now it was impossible to tell who was a player and who a spectator—everyone was jumbled together. Mirza and Mehta were wrestling. Several of Mirza’s old comrades rushed at Mehta and clung to him. Mehta lay quietly on the ground. If he could somehow drag himself forward just two more feet, dozens of his teammates would be revived, but he could not move an inch. Mirza sat on his neck. Mehta’s face was flushed, his eyes bloodshot, sweat streaming down, while Mirza pressed the weight of his heavy body onto Mehta’s back.

Malti stepped forward and said in an agitated voice, “Mirza Khushend, this isn’t fair. The match is drawn.”

Khurshed, pressing Mehta’s neck, said, “I won’t let go until he says ‘cheen.’ Why won’t you say it?”

Malti moved closer. “You can’t force him to say ‘cheen’ like this.”

Mirza pressed down harder and said, “Of course I can. Tell him to say ‘cheen,’ and I’ll get up at once.”

Mehta tried once more to rise, but Mirza pressed his neck down.

Malti tried to pull Mirza’s hand away and said, “This isn’t a game, it’s a hunger strike—an enmity if you like.”

“You won’t let go?”
