--- Page 120 ---
What is a woman, in my eyes? In this world, whatever is beautiful, I call its embodiment a woman. I expect from her that even if I were to kill her, not a trace of vengeance would arise in her heart. Even if I were to love another woman before her very eyes, not a flicker of jealousy would awaken in her. If I were to find such a woman, I would fall at her feet and offer myself to her completely.

Mirza shook his head and said, “Such a woman, you will scarcely find in this world.”

Mehta struck his hand and said, “Not just one, there are thousands—otherwise, the world would be desolate.”

“Give me just one example.”

“Take Mrs. Khanna, for instance.”

“But Khanna—?”

“Khanna is unfortunate, that having found a diamond, he mistakes it for a shard of glass. Think, how much sacrifice there is in her, and with it, how much love. In Khanna’s heart, so enamored of beauty, perhaps there is not even a grain of space for her, but if some calamity were to befall Khanna today, she would lay down her life for him. If Khanna were to become blind or a leper, her loyalty would not waver in the least. Khanna cannot appreciate her now, but you will see, one day this very Khanna will wash her feet and drink that water. I do not want a wife with whom I can debate Einstein’s theories, or who will proofread my writings. I want a woman who will make my life pure and radiant, with her love and her sacrifice.”

Khurshed stroked his beard, as if recalling some forgotten thought, and said, “Your idea is very true, Mr. Mehta! If such a woman could be found anywhere, I too would marry. But I have no hope of finding one.”

Mehta laughed and said, “You keep searching, and so will I. Perhaps fate will smile on us someday.”

“But Miss Malti is not going to let you go. Shall I write it down?”

“With such women, I can only amuse myself, not marry. Marriage is surrender of the self.”

“If marriage is surrender, then what is love?”

“Love becomes marriage only when it takes the form of surrender; before that, it is mere indulgence.”

Mehta dressed and took his leave. Evening had fallen. Mirza went out and saw that Gobar was still watering the trees. Mirza said happily, “Go on, your work is done for today. Will you come again tomorrow?”

Gobar replied with a pleading look, “I am looking for a job, sahib!”

“If you want a job, we’ll hire you.”

“How much will I get, huzoor?”

“As much as you ask.”

“What should I ask? Whatever you wish to give, sahib.”

“We’ll give you fifteen rupees, but you’ll have to work hard.”

Gobar was not afraid of hard work. If he got paid, he was ready to work day and night. Fifteen rupees! What more could he wish for? He would even give his life for that.

He said, “If I could get a room, I’d stay right here.”

“Yes, yes, I’ll arrange a place. Just sleep in a corner of this hut.”

It was as if Gobar had found paradise.

--- Page 121 ---
At that very moment, it was as if an earthquake had struck. Mirza Sahib was lying on the ground, and Mehta was running toward the pavilion, while thousands of people, like madmen, were tossing their caps, turbans, and sticks into the air. No one could comprehend how such a sudden transformation had occurred.

Mirza lifted Mehta in his arms and carried him to the shamiana. On every face were the words—“Doctor Sahib has won the day!” And everyone was astonished at how the lost game had suddenly been turned around. All were singing the praises of Mehta’s courage and perseverance.

Oranges had already been ordered for the laborers. Each was given an orange and sent on his way. In the shamiana, arrangements had been made for tea and refreshments for the guests. Mehta and Mirza sat facing each other at the same table. Malti sat beside Mehta.

Mehta said, “Today I have had a new experience. A woman’s sympathy can turn defeat into victory.”

Mirza looked at Malti. “Ah, so that was it! No wonder I was surprised at how you suddenly surged ahead.”

Malti blushed deeply. “You are a most unkind man, Mirzaji. I have learned that today.”

“The fault was his. Why didn’t he say ‘chee’?”

“I would never have said ‘chee’, even if you had taken my life.”

For a while, the friends chatted and joked. Then there were speeches of thanks and congratulations, and the guests took their leave. Malti had a visit to make and departed as well. Only Mehta and Mirza remained. They still needed to bathe—their bodies were covered in dirt. How could they put on clean clothes? Gobar fetched water, and the two friends began to wash.

Mirza asked, “So, when is the wedding?”

Mehta, surprised, asked, “Whose?”

“Yours.”

“My wedding! With whom?”

“Come now! You act as if this is something to hide.”

“No, truly, I have no idea. Is my wedding being arranged?”

“And what do you think, Miss Malti will remain your companion forever?”

Mehta replied gravely, “You are quite mistaken, Mirzaji. Miss Malti is beautiful, cheerful, intelligent, broad-minded—she has so many virtues. But the quality I seek in my life partner, I do not find in her, nor do I think I ever could. In my mind, a woman is the very image of fidelity and sacrifice, who, through her silence, her selflessness, by utterly effacing herself, becomes a part of her husband’s soul. The body may belong to the man, but the soul is the woman’s. You may ask, why should not the man efface himself? Why expect this only from the woman? The man does not possess that strength. If he were to efface himself, he would become a void—he would retreat into some cave and dream of merging with the Supreme Soul. He is a creature of fire, and in his pride, believing himself a vessel of knowledge, he imagines he can merge directly with God. Woman, like the earth, is patient, full of peace, enduring. When a man acquires the qualities of a woman, he becomes a saint. When a woman acquires the qualities of a man, she becomes a wanton. A man is drawn to a woman who is wholly woman. Malti has not yet attracted me. How can I tell you, in what words—

What is a woman, in my eyes? In this world, whatever is beautiful, I call its embodiment a woman. I expect from her that even if I were to kill her, not a trace of vengeance would arise in her heart. Even if I were to love another woman before her very eyes, not a flicker of jealousy would awaken in her. If I were to find such a woman, I would fall at her feet and offer myself to her completely.

Mirza shook his head and said, “Such a woman, you will hardly find in this world.”

Mehta struck his hand and replied, “Not one, but thousands—otherwise, the world would be desolate.”

“Give me just one example of such a woman.”

“Take Mrs. Khanna, for instance.”

“But Khanna?”

I do not want a wife with whom I can debate Einstein’s theories, or who will proofread my writings. I want a woman who, with her love and sacrifice, will make my life pure and radiant.”

Khurshed, stroking his beard as if recalling some forgotten thought, said, “Your idea is very true, Mr. Mehta! If such a woman could be found anywhere, I too would marry, but I have no hope of finding one.”

Mehta laughed and said, “You keep searching, and so will I. Perhaps one day fate will smile upon us.”

“But Miss Malti is not going to let you go. Shall I write it down?”

“Women like her, I can only amuse myself with, not marry. Marriage is surrender of the self.”

“If marriage is surrender, then what is love?”

“When love takes the form of surrender, only then is it marriage. Before that, it is mere indulgence.”

Mehta dressed and took his leave. Evening had fallen. Mirza went out to see, and found that Gobar was still watering the trees. Mirza, pleased, said, “Go now, your work is done. Will you come again tomorrow?”

Gobar, with a pleading look, said, “I am looking for a job, sahib!”

“If you want work, we will hire you.”

“How much will I get, huzoor?”

“As much as you ask.”

“What should I ask? Whatever you wish to give, sahib.”

“We will give you fifteen rupees, but we will make you work hard.”

Gobar was not afraid of hard work. If he got money, he was ready to work day and night. Fifteen rupees! What more could he wish for? He would even give his life for that.

He said, “If I could get a room, I would stay right here.”

“Yes, yes, I will arrange a place for you. In this very hut, you can sleep in one corner.”

It was as if Gobar had found heaven.

Hori’s entire crop had been claimed by the landlord’s dues. Somehow they scraped through the month of Vaishakh, but by the time Jeth arrived, not a single grain of food remained in the house. Five mouths to feed, and not a morsel to be found. If both meals could not be had, at least one should be. If not a full stomach, at least half. Who can go hungry day after day? But from whom could he borrow? He had to avoid the small and big mahajans of the village. And even if he wanted to work for wages, whose work would he do? In Jeth, there was enough work in his own fields. The sugarcane needed watering, but how could one labor on an empty stomach?

Evening had fallen. The youngest child was crying. If the mother had nothing to eat, how could she produce milk? Sona understood their plight, but what could little Rupa comprehend? She kept wailing for bread. All day she had distracted herself with raw mangoes, but now she needed something solid. Hori had gone to Dulari Sahua’in to ask for grain on credit, but she had closed her shop and gone to the market. Mangru Sah not only refused him, but scolded him as well—“Here you come asking for credit, and for three years you haven’t paid a paisa of interest, yet you keep asking for more. Now you’ll pay in the afterlife! When a man’s intentions turn bad, this is what happens. Even God cannot bear such injustice. When the clerk scolded you, you somehow managed to cough up the money. As if it was only my money you had to pay. And your wife—her temperament never matches mine.”

Dejected and on the verge of tears, Hori sat down when Punia came to borrow fire. She went to the kitchen door and saw it was shrouded in darkness. She asked, “Aren’t you making bread tonight, bhabhi? It’s already late.”

Since Gobar had broken the marriage, Punia and Dhaniya had started speaking again. She had even begun to acknowledge Hori’s kindness. Now she cursed Hira—“Murderer, killed a cow and ran away. He’s disgraced, how can he return home? And even if he does, I won’t let him set foot inside. He felt no shame in killing a cow. It would have been better if the police had taken him away and made him grind grain in jail.”

Dhaniya could not make any excuses. She replied, “How can I make bread when there isn’t a grain in the house? Your Mahto fed the whole biradari, but whether our children live or die, now the biradari doesn’t even look our way.”

Punia’s harvest had been good, and she admitted that it was thanks to Hori’s hard work. She had never prospered so much with Hira.

She said, “Why didn’t you ask for grain from my house? Isn’t that Mahto’s earning too? When good times come, you can quarrel, but in sorrow, it’s only by weeping together that we get by. Am I so blind that I can’t recognize a good heart? If Mahto hadn’t supported me, where would I have found shelter today?”

She turned back at once, taking Sona with her. In a moment, she returned with two baskets filled with grain and set them down in the courtyard. There was no less than two maunds of barley. Before Dhaniya could say anything, she left again and came back with a large basket brimming with arhar dal, saying, “Come, I’ll light the fire for you.”

Dhaniya saw that on top of the barley, in a small basket, there were four or five seers of flour as well. For the first time in her life, she felt defeated. With eyes brimming with love and gratitude, she said, “You’ve brought everything—did you leave anything at home? What if you run out?”

The baby was lying in the cradle in the courtyard, crying. Punia picked him up and, cuddling him, said, “Thanks to your kindness, there’s still plenty left, bhabhi. Fifteen maunds of barley, ten of wheat. Five maunds of peas—I have nothing to hide from you. Both our households will manage. In two or three months, there’ll be maize again.”

--- Page 124 ---
She will go. Beyond this, God is the master.

Jhuniya came and touched her younger mother-in-law’s feet with the end of her sari. Puniya blessed her. Sona went to light the fire, and Rupa picked up the water pitcher. The halted cart began to move again. The whirlpool, the foam, the noise, the rush of water that had been caused by the obstruction in the stream—all these, with the obstruction now gone, flowed on in a calm, sweet, even, unhurried current.

Puniya said, “What was the hurry to give the Mahato the dike?”

Dhaniya replied, “How else could we show our faces in the biradari?”

“Bhabhi, may I say something, if you don’t mind?”

“Say it, why would I mind?”

“I’m afraid if I say it, you might get upset.”

“I promise, I won’t say a word. Go on.”

“You shouldn’t have kept Jhuniya in the house.”

“What else could I do? She would have drowned herself.”

“You could have sent her to my house. Then no one would have said a word.”

“That’s easy for you to say now. If I’d sent her that day, you’d have chased her away with a broom.”

“With all this expense, Gobar’s wedding could have been arranged.”

“Who can escape fate, silly girl? Even now, we’re not free of trouble—Bhola is now demanding the price of his cow. Back then, he gave the cow on the condition that we arrange his engagement somewhere. Now he says he doesn’t want to get engaged, just give him his money back. Both his sons roam around with sticks. Who do we have who can stand up to them? This accursed cow has ruined everything.”

After some more talk, Puniya left with the fire. Hori had been watching everything. Coming inside, he said, “Puniya has a pure heart.”

“Heera had a pure heart too, didn’t he?” Dhaniya replied.

Dhaniya had accepted the grain, but in her heart she felt ashamed and humiliated. Such are the turns of fate, that today she had to suffer this indignity.

“You never yield to anyone, that’s your flaw,” Hori said.

“Why should I yield? Is my husband not risking his life for their household? And it’s not as if I’ve taken charity. I’ll repay every single grain.”

Yet, understanding her jethani’s feelings, Puniya continued to repay Hori’s kindness. Whenever their grain ran out, she would bring over a maund or two. But when the monsoon came and there was no rain, the problem became dire. The month of Sawan arrived, and the cranes began to fly. Even the wells dried up, and the sugarcane withered in the heat. A little water could be fetched from the river, but every day there were fights over it. Even the river finally gave up.

Thefts began to occur everywhere, dacoities broke out. The whole province was in an uproar. By some good fortune, rain finally came in Bhadon, and the farmers breathed a sigh of relief. What joy there was that day! The thirsty earth seemed insatiable, and the parched farmers leapt about as if gold coins, not water, were pouring from the sky. Gather as much as you can! Where the cranes had gathered in the fields, now ploughs began to move. Groups of boys ran from pond to pond, inspecting the water levels. “Oh! The pond is half full!” And from there, they’d dash to the next pond.

But no matter how much it rained now, the sugarcane was lost. Each stalk was only a hand’s length tall.

--- Page 125 ---
Maize, millet, and kodon won’t pay the rent, nor will they fill the mahajan’s belly. Yes, at least there’s fodder for the cows and the people have survived.

When the month of Magh had passed and Bhola still hadn’t received his money, one day he stormed into Hori’s house, agitated, and said, “Is this how you keep your word? Was it not from your own mouth that you promised to pay me after crushing the sugarcane? Now the cane has been crushed. Hand over my money.”

When Hori, after recounting his misfortunes and pleading in every possible way, failed to move Bhola, and Bhola refused to leave the threshold, Hori, exasperated, said, “Mahato, at this moment I have no money, nor can I get a loan from anywhere. Where am I to bring it from? There’s not a grain to spare. If you don’t believe me, come inside and see for yourself. Take whatever you find.”

Bhola replied coldly, “Why should I search your house? Nor do I care whether you have the money or not. You promised to pay me after crushing the cane. The cane is crushed. Now give me my money.”

“Then what do you want me to do?”

“What should I say?”

“I leave it to you.”

“I’ll take your two oxen.”

Hori looked at him with astonished eyes, as if he couldn’t believe his ears. Then, bewildered, he lowered his head. Did Bhola intend to reduce him to a beggar? If both oxen were taken, it would be as if both his hands were cut off.

In a pleading voice, he said, “If you take both oxen, I’ll be ruined. If that’s what your conscience tells you, then take them.”

“I don’t care what happens to you. I want my money.”

“And what if I say I’ve already paid you?”

Bhola was stunned. He couldn’t believe his ears. That Hori could commit such a great dishonesty seemed impossible.

He said angrily, “If you swear on the Ganga water in your hand that you’ve paid me, I’ll accept it.”

“I wish I could say it, I’m desperate enough, but I won’t.”

“You can’t say it.”

“No, brother, I can’t. I was only joking.”

For a moment, he stood in hesitation. Then he said, “Why are you holding such a grudge against me, Bhola bhai? What heaven have I gained because Jhunia came into my house? My son was lost, I had to pay two hundred rupees in fines. I’ve been ruined. And now you too are digging up my roots. God knows, I had no idea what the boy was up to. I thought he must be going to listen to music. I only found out the day Jhunia came home at midnight. If I hadn’t kept her then, just think—where would she have gone? Whose would she have become?”

Jhunia, standing hidden by the doorway, was listening to all this. She no longer saw her father as a father, but as an enemy. She was afraid Hori might give up the oxen. She went to Rupa and said, “Go quickly and call Amma. Tell her it’s urgent—don’t delay.”

Dhaniya had gone to the fields to throw out dung. When she heard her daughter-in-law’s message, she came and said, “Why did you call me, bahu?”

--- Page 126 ---
“I was so frightened.”

“You’ve seen Kaka, haven’t you?”

“Yes, I saw him, sitting at the door like a butcher. I didn’t even speak to him.”

“Our two bullocks—they’re demanding them from Dada.”

Dhaniya’s insides twisted with anxiety.

“They’re demanding both bullocks?”

“Yes, they say, either give us our money or we’ll untie the bullocks and take them away.”

“What did your Dada say?”

“He said, if your conscience allows, then take them.”

“Let them take them, but if they ever come to this door to beg, then spit in my name. If his heart finds warmth from our blood, then let it be so.”

Still burning with anger, she came out to Hori. “The Mahto is demanding both bullocks—then why don’t you give them? Let their bellies be filled; our fate is in God’s hands. They won’t cut off our hands! Until now, we’ve worked our own fields, now we’ll work for others. If it’s God’s will, we’ll have bullocks again, and if we keep working as laborers, what’s so bad about that? At least we won’t have the burden of taxes and rent. I never knew they were our enemies—if I had, why would I have brought that cow into our home and courted disaster? The day that wretched cow’s tether entered our house, everything was ruined.”

Bhola, who until now had kept his weapon hidden, saw his chance. He was sure these people had nothing left but the bullocks. To save them, they would do anything. Like a skilled marksman, he steadied his mind and spoke, “If you want us to be disgraced while you sit in peace, that will not happen. You weep over your two hundred rupees, but here, honor worth a lakh has been lost. Your best course is to do as you did with Jhunia—just as you took her in, now drive her out. Then we will neither demand the bullocks nor the price of the cow. She has shamed us, and I want to see her begging for scraps. She sits here like a queen, while we walk with blackened faces, mourning her name—I cannot bear it. She is my daughter; I have cradled her in my arms, and God is my witness, I never thought of her as less than my sons. But today, seeing her beg and pick grains from the dust will cool my heart. If, after all this disgrace, I have hardened my heart so much, imagine what a wound I must have suffered! This wretched girl has drowned the name of seven generations, and you keep her in your house—if that is not grinding salt into my wounds, then what is?”

Dhaniya drew a line in stone with her words. “Then listen to me, Mahto. What you want will never happen. Not in a hundred lifetimes. Jhunia is with us as long as we live. You only want to take the bullocks—take them. If that restores your lost honor, then do it. If it saves your ancestors’ reputation, then save it. Jhunia has done wrong, yes. The day she set foot in my house, I raised a broom to drive her out, but when tears streamed from her eyes, I felt pity for her. You are old now, Mahto, but even today you’re obsessed with marriage alliances. And she is still a child.”

Bhola looked at Hori with pleading eyes. “Do you hear her words, Hori? Now it’s not my fault. I will not leave without the bullocks.”

Hori said firmly, “Take them.”

--- Page 127 ---
“Then don’t come crying that someone has untied my oxen.”

“I won’t cry.”

Bhola was already untying the oxen’s ropes when Jhuniya, dressed in a sari with bright borders, her child in her arms, came out and spoke in a trembling voice, “Kaka, see, I am leaving this house. As you wish, I will beg for food to feed myself and my child, and when even begging fails, I will drown myself somewhere.”

Bhola, irritated, said, “Get away from my sight. God forbid I should ever have to see your face again. You bring ruin upon families, you bring shame. For you, dying is the only proper thing left.”

Jhuniya did not even glance at him. There was in her a kind of anger that seeks to consume itself, not others—a fury without violence, only surrender. If the earth had opened up at that moment and swallowed her, she would have considered herself blessed. She took a step forward.

But she had not gone even two steps when Dhaniya ran after her, caught her, and with a love filled with fierce protectiveness said, “Where are you going, daughter-in-law? Come inside. This is your home, in our lifetime and after we are gone as well. Let the one who bears enmity towards his own child be the one to drown. This good man feels no shame saying such things. He tries to lord it over me, the wretch. Go on, drink the blood of the oxen…”

Jhuniya, weeping, said, “Amma, when my own father rejects me, let me drown. Because of my misfortune, you have only suffered. Since I came, your house has fallen into ruin. The love with which you have kept me, not even a mother could have given. If God gives me birth again, let it be from your womb—this is my only wish.”

Dhaniya, pulling her closer, said, “He is not your father, he is your enemy, a murderer. If he were a mother, he would have felt the pain. Let the engagement come, and if the wife doesn’t beat him with her slippers, then say I’m wrong.”

Jhuniya followed her mother-in-law back into the house. Meanwhile, Bhola untied both oxen from their posts and drove them home, as if he had gone to a feast and been served shoes instead of sweets. “Now go and farm, now play your flute. Everyone wants to insult me, taking out old grudges. Otherwise, what decent man would keep such a girl in his house? Truly, they have all become shameless. That’s why the boy never got married. And look at this widow Jhuniya’s insolence, standing before me! Any other girl would never have shown her face. They have lost all shame. All of them are wicked and foolish. They think Jhuniya is theirs now. They don’t realize, the one who couldn’t stay in her father’s house will never stay in anyone’s house. Times are bad, otherwise I would have dragged that witch Dhaniya by her hair through the marketplace. How many curses she has hurled at me!”

Then he looked at the two oxen—how fine they were, what a pair! He could sell them for a hundred rupees wherever he wished. His eighty rupees would be recovered.

He had not even left the village when he saw Datadeen, Pateshwari, Shobha, and a crowd of ten or twenty men running after him. Bhola’s blood ran cold. Now there would be a criminal case, the oxen would be taken, and he’d get a beating too. He stopped, bracing himself. If he was to die, he would die fighting.

Datadeen came near and said, “What disaster have you brought upon yourself, Bhola? You untied his oxen and brought them away, and he said nothing, so you became arrogant. Everyone was busy with their own work, no one even knew. If Hori had given the slightest signal, not a single hair on your head would have remained. If you want to do the right thing, take the oxen back. There’s not a shred of decency in you.”

Pateshwari said, “This is the result of his simplicity. If you have a complaint, go to the civil court…”

--- Page 128 ---
File a claim, get a decree. What right do you have to take away the oxen? If I file a criminal case against you right now, you’ll end up in chains.

Bhola pressed his lips together and said, “But Lala sahib, I didn’t take the oxen by force. Hori himself gave them to me.”

Pateshwari said to Bhola, “Return the oxen, Bhola! No farmer gives away his oxen willingly. He will have to yoke them to his plough.”

Bhola stood in front of the oxen. “Get me my money back, what use do I have for these oxen?”

“We’re taking the oxen; if you want your money, file a claim. Otherwise, you’ll be beaten and thrown aside. Did you pay him cash? You saddled the poor man with a cursed cow, and now you’re taking away his oxen too.”

Still, Bhola did not move from in front of the oxen. He stood silent, resolute, as if he would only leave if he died. How could he argue with the patwari?

Datadeen took a step forward, straightening his bent back, and shouted, “Why are you all just standing there watching? Beat him and drive him away! He’ll take oxen from our village?”

Vanshi was a strong young man. He gave Bhola a hard shove. Bhola couldn’t keep his balance and fell. As he tried to get up, Vanshi struck him again with his fist.

Hori came running. Bhola took ten steps toward him and asked, “Tell me honestly, Hori Mahto, did I take the oxen by force?”

Datadeen interpreted, “He says Hori gave him the oxen of his own free will. He’s trying to make fools of us.”

Hori, embarrassed, said, “He told me, either send Jhunia away from your house or give me my money, or else I’ll take your oxen. I said, I will neither send my daughter-in-law away nor do I have any money. If your dharma tells you to, then take the oxen. That’s all—I left it to his conscience, and he took the oxen.”

Pateshwari, lowering his face, said, “If you left it to his dharma, then where’s the force? His dharma told him to take them. Go, brother, the oxen are yours.”

Datadeen agreed, “Yes, when it’s a matter of dharma, what can anyone say?”  
All of them looked at Hori with eyes full of contempt, defeated, and turned back. Victorious, Bhola lifted his head with pride and led the oxen away.

: 15 :

Outwardly, Malti is a butterfly, but within, she is a honeybee. Her life is not all laughter. Who can live on sweetness alone? And even if one could, such a life would not be a happy one. She laughs, perhaps because she is paid for it, or because she has made her sense of self so large in her own eyes that whatever she does, she does for herself. No, she chirps and jokes because it lightens the burden of her duties. Her father was one of those strange creatures who, with nothing but their tongues, could turn the fortunes of millions. Arranging the sale of great zamindars’ and noblemen’s estates, securing them loans, or settling their affairs with the help of officials—this was his profession. In other words, he was a broker. People of this class are often highly talented. Whatever work promises some gain—

--- Page 129 ---
Let us hope he will manage somehow, and carry it through in some way or another. Arrange the marriage of a prince with a princess and pocket ten or twenty thousand in the process. These same brokers, when they deal in small matters, are called touts and we despise them. But when they handle great affairs, the same touts go hunting with kings and drink tea at the tables of governors. Mr. Kaul was one of those fortunate men. He had three daughters, and only daughters. His ambition was to send all three to England and have them reach the pinnacle of education. Like many other great men, he too believed that education in England transforms a person into something else altogether. Perhaps the climate there has some power to sharpen the mind. But this wish of his was fulfilled only by a third.

Malti was still in England when he suffered a stroke and became incapacitated. Now, with great difficulty, he could sit or stand only with the help of two people. His tongue had become completely paralyzed, and when the tongue is silenced, so is one’s income. Whatever there was, had been earned by his tongue. He had never been in the habit of saving anything. His income was irregular, and so were his expenses; thus, for several years now, he had been in dire straits. The entire responsibility fell upon Malti. With Malti’s four or five hundred rupees, how could there be any luxury or grandeur? Still, it was enough that the two younger girls could continue their education, and life could be lived respectably. Malti ran about from morning till late at night. She wished her father would live with restraint, but he was so addicted to wine and kebabs that he could not give them up. If he got nothing from anywhere else, he would write a promissory note to a mahajan at his own bungalow and borrow a thousand or two. The mahajan was an old friend of his, who had earned lakhs in business thanks to him, and out of courtesy, never said a word. He was owed twenty-five thousand now, and could have had everything seized whenever he wished, but he kept up the pretense of friendship. The shamelessness that comes to those who live off others had come to Kaul as well. Let there be as many demands as there might, he did not care. Malti was always irritated by his extravagance, but her mother, who was a veritable goddess and even in this age considered service to her husband the chief purpose of a woman’s life, kept consoling her, so that there was no domestic strife.

Evening had fallen. There was still warmth in the air. A haze hung over the sky. Malti and her two sisters were sitting on the grass in front of the bungalow. The grass had withered for want of water, and the earth beneath was exposed.

Malti asked, “Does the gardener not water at all?”

The middle sister, Saroj, replied, “He just lies around sleeping, the pig! Whenever you tell him, he comes up with twenty excuses.”

Saroj was studying for her B.A., thin, tall, pale, dry, and sharp-tongued. She liked nothing about anyone. She was always finding faults. The doctors had advised that she should not exert herself and should live in the hills, but the family’s situation was such that she could not be sent there.

The youngest, Varda, disliked Saroj for the very reason that the whole household revolved around her. She wished that if an illness could be so enjoyable, why had it not come to her? She was fair, proud, healthy, with lively eyes, and a spark of intelligence on her face. Except for Saroj, she felt sympathy for the whole world. It was her nature to contradict whatever Saroj said. She said, “Grandfather keeps sending him to the bazaar all day, where does he get any free time? He doesn’t even get a break to die, how can he just lie around and sleep?”

Saroj scolded, “When does Grandfather ever send him to the bazaar, you liar?”

Godan: 122

--- Page 130 ---
“They send it every day, every single day. Why, I just sent it today. If you wish, I can call and have them asked.”

“You’ll have them questioned, shall I call?”

Malti grew uneasy. If the two of them started arguing, it would become impossible to sit here. Changing the subject, she said, “Alright, let it be. Wasn’t there a lecture by Dr. Mehta at your place today, Saroj?”

Saroj wrinkled her nose and replied, “Yes, there was, but no one liked it. He began to declare that the sphere of women in this world is entirely separate from that of men. For women to enter the domain of men is the disgrace of this age. All the girls started clapping and whistling. Poor man, he sat down, embarrassed. He seems a rather strange sort of person. He even went so far as to say that love is nothing but a poet’s fancy, that there is no sign of it in real life. Lady Hookoo made quite a mockery of him.”

Malti remarked sharply, “Lady Hookoo? She dares to speak on such a subject? You should have listened to Dr. Sahib’s speech from beginning to end. What must he have thought of the girls in his heart?”

“Who had the patience to listen to the whole speech? It was as if he were rubbing salt into wounds.”

“Then why invite him at all? After all, he has no enmity with women. He only propagates what he believes to be true. He is not one to say what pleases women just to make them happy. And besides, who can say whether the path women wish to tread is truly the right one? It is quite possible that, in the future, we may have to change our own beliefs.”

She spoke of the ideals of women’s lives in France, Germany, and Italy, and said, “Soon, there will be a lecture by Mehta under the auspices of the Women’s League.”

Saroj grew curious.  
“But you yourself say that the rights of women and men should be equal.”

“I still say so, but we must also listen to what the other side has to say. Perhaps we are the ones in error.”

This League was a new institution in the city, established through Malti’s efforts. All the educated women of the city were members. Dr. Mehta’s first lecture had stirred up quite a commotion among the women, and the League had resolved to give him a fitting, combative reply. The responsibility was placed on Malti. For several days, Malti searched for arguments and evidence to support her side. Many other ladies were also writing their speeches. That evening, when Mehta arrived at the League’s hall, it seemed as if the hall would burst at the seams. He felt a sense of pride—such enthusiasm to hear his speech! And that enthusiasm was not only on their faces and in their eyes. Today, all the ladies were adorned in gold and silk, as if they had come to a wedding procession. They had employed the power of splendor to defeat Mehta, and who can say that brilliance is not a part of power? For today, Malti had brought out a new fashion sari, had new-cut jumpers made, and was lavishly adorned with makeup and flowers, as if it were her own wedding. Never before had the Women’s League seen such celebration. Dr. Mehta was alone, yet the hearts of the ladies trembled. A single spark of truth can reduce a mountain of falsehood to ashes.

In the very last row sat Mirza, Khanna, and the Editor. Rai Sahib arrived after the speech had begun and stood at the back.

Mirza said, “Come, take a seat. How long will you keep standing?”

Rai Sahib replied, “No, brother, I’ll suffocate in here.”

“Then I’ll stand. You take my seat.”

Godan: 123

--- Page 131 ---
Raisahab pressed their shoulders—“No formality, please, remain seated. If I get tired, I’ll make you get up and I’ll sit down myself. Well then, Miss Malti has become the president of the assembly. Khanna Sahab, please arrange for some prize.”

Khanna put on a woeful face and said, “Now all hopes rest on Mr. Mehta. I have fallen behind.”

Mr. Mehta began his speech:

“Ladies! When I address you in this manner, does anything about it trouble you? You accept this honor as your right. But have you ever heard a woman address men as ‘divine’ beings? If you call a man a god, he’ll think you’re mocking him. You possess compassion, reverence, and self-sacrifice to offer as gifts. What does a man have to give? He is not a giver, but a taker. He uses violence, wages battles, and stirs up strife to claim his rights.”

Applause resounded. Raisahab remarked, “What a clever way he’s found to please the ladies!”

The editor of ‘Bijli’ was displeased—“Nothing new. I’ve expressed this sentiment many times before.”

Mehta continued, “That is why, when I see our progressive-minded ladies, dissatisfied with the life of compassion, reverence, and sacrifice, rushing toward a life of struggle and violence, thinking this is the paradise of happiness, I cannot congratulate them.”

Mrs. Khanna looked at Malti with proud eyes. Malti lowered her head.

Khushend said, “Now what do you say? Mehta is a bold man. He speaks the truth, and right to your face.”

The editor of ‘Bijli’ wrinkled his nose, “Those days are gone when ladies were taken in by such flattery. You keep usurping their rights and keep saying, ‘You are goddesses, you are Lakshmi, you are mothers.’”

Mehta pressed on, “It pains me just as much to see a woman adopting the ways and deeds of men as it does a man adopting the ways and deeds of women. I am sure you do not consider such men worthy of your trust and affection, and I assure you, such a woman too cannot become worthy of a man’s love and reverence.”

A glow of heartfelt joy appeared on Khanna’s face.

Raisahab teased, “You are very pleased, Khanna ji?”

Khanna replied, “If I meet Malti now, I’ll ask her—what do you say now?”

Mehta continued, “In the evolution of living beings, I consider the position of women superior to that of men. Just as I consider love, sacrifice, and reverence superior to violence, conflict, and strife. If our ladies wish to leave the divine temple of creation and nurture for the demonic realm of violence and conflict, it will not bring welfare to society. I am firm on this point. In his pride, man has given more importance to his own glory. By seizing his brother’s rights and shedding his blood, he believes he has achieved a great victory. The children whom women have conceived and nurtured with their own blood, he sends to become prey to bombs, machine guns, and thousands of tanks, and considers himself a conqueror. And when our own mothers, anointing his forehead with saffron and girding him with the armor of their blessings, send him into the field of violence, it is no wonder that man has come to see destruction as the path to the world’s welfare, and his tendency toward violence has grown day by day. Today we see this demonism raging, trampling the whole world, crushing living beings, burning green fields, and turning blooming settlements into desolate wastelands. Ladies, I ask you, by supporting this demonic spectacle, by stepping into this battlefield, will you bring welfare to the world? I ask you—”

--- Page 132 ---
I beg you, let the destroyers do their work; you must continue to uphold your own dharma.

Khanna said, “Malti cannot even lift her head.”

Raisahab supported these thoughts, “Mehta speaks only the truth.”

The editor of ‘Bijli’ objected, “But nothing substantial has been said. Opponents of the women’s movement always take refuge in such absurdities. I do not accept that the world has progressed through sacrifice and love. The world has advanced through manliness, valor, intellect, and brilliance.”

Khursheed interjected, “Well, will you let us listen, or will you keep singing your own tune?”

Mehta’s speech continued, “Ladies, I am not among those who claim that men and women possess equal powers, equal inclinations, and that there is no difference between them. I cannot imagine a greater falsehood. This is a falsehood that seeks to cover up the accumulated experience of ages, just as a patch of cloud covers the sun. I warn you not to fall into this trap. Woman is as superior to man as light is to darkness. For mankind, forgiveness, sacrifice, and non-violence are the highest ideals of life. Woman has already attained this ideal. Man, by taking refuge in religion, spirituality, and the teachings of rishis, has been striving for centuries to reach that goal, but has not succeeded. I say, all his spirituality and yoga on one side, and the sacrifice of women on the other.”

Applause rang out. The hall trembled. Raisahab, moved, said, “Mehta speaks what is in their hearts.”

Omkarnath commented, “But all these are old, stale ideas.”

“Even an old idea, when spoken with inner strength, becomes new.”

“One who squanders a thousand rupees every month in luxury cannot possess such inner strength. These are merely ways to please women and men of old-fashioned views.”

Khanna looked at Malti, “Why is she swelling with pride? She ought to be ashamed.”

Khursheed goaded Khanna, “Now you must give a speech too, Khanna, or else Mehta will sweep you away. He has already won half the field.”

Khanna, embarrassed, replied, “Don’t count me in; I have trapped and released so many birds.”

Raisahab winked at Khursheed and said, “These days you are seen coming and going to the women’s society. Tell the truth, how much did you donate?”

Khanna was flustered, “I do not donate to such societies that, under the guise of art, spread immorality.”

Mehta’s speech continued—

“Men claim that all the philosophers, scientists, and inventors have been men. All the great saints have been men. All the warriors, all the masters of politics, the great navigators, the great everything—men. But what have these great assemblies achieved together? What have the saints and founders of religions done, except to flood the world with rivers of blood and kindle the fires of discord? What legacy have the warriors left, except the beheading of brothers? The only traces of politicians now are the ruins of vanished empires, and what problem have inventors solved except making man a slave to machines? In this culture created by men, where is peace? Where is cooperation?”

--- Page 133 ---
Omkarnath rose to leave—“Listening to such grandiose words from the mouths of these pleasure-seekers burns me to ashes.”

Khushend caught his hand and made him sit—“You too, Editorji, remain such a stickler! Ah, this is the world; whoever wishes, says whatever comes to mind. Some people listen and clap. Come, let the matter end here. Countless such orators will come and go, and the world will keep moving at its own pace. What is there to be upset about?”

“I cannot bear to hear falsehood,” Omkarnath replied.

Raisahab egged him on—“Who would not burn upon hearing words of chastity from the lips of a wanton?”

Omkarnath sat down again. Mehta’s speech continued—

“I ask you, if a swan, upon seeing a hawk preying on birds, abandons the blissful peace of Mansarovar to hunt birds itself, would that befit the swan? And if it becomes a hunter, would you congratulate it? The swan does not have such a sharp beak, nor such fierce talons, nor such keen eyes, nor such swift wings, nor such a thirst for blood. To acquire those weapons would take it centuries, and even then, whether it could become a hawk is doubtful. But whether it becomes a hawk or not, it would cease to be a swan—the swan that feeds on pearls.”

Khushend interjected—“These are arguments worthy of poets. The female hawk hunts just as the male does.”

Omkarnath was pleased—“And yet you pretend to be a philosopher, on the strength of this logic alone.”

Khanna vented his heart—“He is the tail of a philosopher. A true philosopher is one who…”

Omkarnath finished the thought—“Who never strays, even a hair’s breadth, from the truth.”

Khanna did not care for this solution—“I know nothing of truth and such. To me, a philosopher is one who is truly a philosopher.”

Khushend applauded—“You have given such a true definition of a philosopher. Wah, subhanallah! A philosopher is one who is a philosopher. Why not?”

Mehta continued—“I do not say that women do not need education. They do, and more than men. I do not say that women do not need power. They do, and more than men. But not that education and not that power with which men have turned the world into a field of violence. If you too acquire that same knowledge and that same power, the world will become a desert. Your knowledge and your rights do not lie in violence and destruction, but in creation and nurture. Do you think the salvation of humanity will come through votes, through wielding tongue and pen in offices and courts? For these artificial, unnatural, destructive rights, do you wish to abandon those rights which Nature herself has bestowed upon you?”

Saroj, until now, had sat restrained out of respect for her elder sister. She could no longer contain herself. She hissed—“We want the vote, equal to men!”

Several other young women called out—“Vote! Vote!”

Omkarnath stood and declared in a loud voice—“Let the opponents of womankind hang their heads in shame!”

Malti struck her hand on the table and said—“Silence! Whoever wishes to speak for or against will be given full opportunity.”

Mehta spoke—“The vote is the illusion of the new age, a mirage, a stain, a deception. In its snare—”

--- Page 134 ---
If you take this path, you will belong neither here nor there. Who says your sphere is limited, and that you have no scope for self-expression within it? We are all human beings first, and only after that, anything else. Our life is our home. It is there that our world is created, there that we are nurtured, there that all the affairs of life take place. If that sphere is limited, then what sphere is unlimited? Is it that struggle, where organized plunder prevails? You wish to leave the workshop where human beings and their destinies are shaped, and go to those factories where humans are crushed, where their blood is drained?

Mirza interrupted—It is the oppression of men that has given rise to this spirit of rebellion among women.

Mehta replied—Certainly, men have committed injustices, but this is not the answer. Eradicate injustice, but not by erasing yourself.

Malti said—Women seek rights so that they may use them well, and prevent men from misusing them.

Mehta answered—The greatest rights in this world are earned through service and sacrifice, and those rights are already yours. Compared to those rights, the right to vote is nothing. I regret that our sisters are adopting the ideals of the West, where women have lost their status, and fallen from being mistresses of the home to mere objects of pleasure. The Western woman seeks freedom only so that she may indulge in greater luxury. Our mothers never aspired to luxury. They have always managed the household solely by virtue of their right to serve. Take what is good from the West—there has always been exchange in culture—but blind imitation is a sign of mental weakness.

Today, the Western woman no longer wishes to remain the mistress of the home. The insatiable craving for enjoyment has made her unruly. She is sacrificing her modesty and dignity—her greatest virtues—for frivolity and pleasure. When I see the educated girls there displaying their beauty, their full, round arms, or their nakedness, I feel pity for them. Their desires have so overpowered them that they cannot even protect their own modesty. What greater downfall can there be for woman than this?

Raisahab clapped. The hall echoed with applause, like a string of firecrackers going off.

Mirza Sahib said to the Editor—You have no answer to this, do you?

The Editor replied with detachment—In the entire speech, this is the only thing he has said that is true.

“So you, too, have become a devotee of Mehta?”

“No, sir, our people are not devotees of anyone. I will find an answer to this—look for it in ‘Bijli’.”

“So that means you are not searching for the truth, only fighting for your own side.”

Raisahab interjected—And for this you take pride in your love of truth?

The Editor remained unmoved—A lawyer’s job is to look after his client’s interests, not to determine truth or falsehood.

“So, in other words, you are the advocate of women?”

“I am the advocate of all who are weak, helpless, and oppressed.”

“You are shameless, my friend.”

Mehta-ji was saying—And this is the conspiracy of men. To pull the ladies down from their lofty heights and make them their equals—this is the work of those men who are cowards, who lack the sense of responsibility required for married life.

--- Page 135 ---
There is no capacity for restraint, those who, like unbridled bulls intoxicated by the waves of sensual pleasure, wish to thrust their mouths into the lush fields of others and satiate their vile desires. In the West, their conspiracy succeeded, and the goddesses became butterflies. I am ashamed to say that even in this land of sacrifice and penance, India, the same wind has begun to blow. Especially among our educated sisters, this enchantment is spreading rapidly. They are abandoning the ideal of the housewife and adopting the colors of butterflies.

Saroj, growing agitated, said, “We do not seek advice from men. If they are free to decide for themselves, then women too are free in matters concerning themselves. Young women no longer wish to make marriage a profession. They will marry only on the basis of love.”

There was loud applause, especially from the front rows, where the women sat.

Mehta replied, “What you call love is an illusion, a distorted form of inflamed desire, just as renunciation is merely a sanctified form of begging. If there is little of that love in married life, there is none at all in unrestrained indulgence. True joy, true peace, lies only in the vow of service. That is the source of rights, the fountain of strength. Service is the cement that can bind a couple in lifelong affection and companionship, impervious to even the greatest blows. Where service is absent, there is divorce, abandonment, distrust. And upon you, as the helmswoman of the boat of a man’s life, the responsibility is greater. If you wish, you can steer the boat safely through storms and tempests, but if you are careless, the boat will sink, and you will sink with it.”

The speech ended. The topic was contentious, and several women asked for permission to respond, but it was already late. So Malti thanked Mehta and dissolved the gathering. However, it was announced that next Sunday, several ladies would present their views on the same subject.

Raisahab congratulated Mehta, “You have spoken the words of my heart, Mr. Mehta! I agree with every word you said.”

Malti laughed, “Of course you would congratulate him—thieves are always cousins to each other. But why is all this preaching always heaped upon poor women? Why is the burden of ideals, propriety, and sacrifice always thrust upon their heads?”

Mehta replied, “Because they understand these things.”

Khanna, looking at Malti with his large eyes as if trying to read her thoughts, said, “Doctor Sahib’s ideas seem to me to be a hundred years behind the times.”

Malti asked sharply, “Which ideas?”

“These—service, duty, and so on.”

“So you think these ideas are a hundred years old? Then please share your fresh thoughts. Do you have a new recipe for how a couple can live happily together?”

Khanna was embarrassed. He had spoken only to please Malti, but she had flared up. He said, “Mehta Sahib must know that recipe.”

“Doctor Sahib has already given his, and you consider it a hundred years old. So you should present a new one. Don’t you know there are many things in the world that can never become old? Such problems have always arisen in society and always will.”

Mrs. Khanna had gone out to the veranda. Mehta went over to her and, bowing respectfully, asked—

--- Page 136 ---
What is your opinion about the speech?

Mrs. Khanna lowered her eyes and said, “It was good, very good. But you are still unmarried, that is why women seem divine, noble, the helmswomen. Just get married, then I’ll ask you what women are. And you will have to marry, because you have already called men who shirk marriage cowards.”

Mehta laughed, “That is precisely what I am preparing the ground for.”

“A match with Miss Malti would be a good one.”

“On the condition that I may sit at your feet for a while and learn the duties of a woman from you.”

“That’s just like selfish men! Have you learned the duties of a man?”

“I am just wondering, from whom should I learn?”

“Mr. Khanna could teach you very well.”

Mehta burst out laughing, “No, I will learn the duties of a man from you alone.”

“Very well, learn from me. The first thing is to forget that woman is superior and that all responsibility rests on her. The man is superior, and the entire burden of the household is his. He alone can foster service, self-restraint, and duty in a woman. If he lacks these qualities, then the woman will lack them too. The rebellion among women today is because men have become devoid of these virtues.”

Mirza Sahib came and lifted Mehta up in his arms, saying, “Congratulations!”

Mehta looked at him questioningly, “Did you like my speech?”

“The speech was as it was, but it was certainly successful. You have captured the fairy in a glass case. Count yourself lucky—she who has never looked at anyone before is now reciting your creed.”

Mrs. Khanna said softly, “Let’s see when the intoxication wears off.”

Mehta said with detachment, “Who would like a bookworm like me, Deviji? I am a thorough idealist.”

Mrs. Khanna saw her husband heading towards the car and went that way. Mirza too stepped outside. Mehta picked up his cane from the stage and was about to leave when Malti came up, took his hand, and said with pleading eyes, “You cannot leave just yet. Come, let me introduce you to Papa, and have dinner at our place tonight.”

Mehta put his hand to his ear and said, “No, forgive me. Saroj will eat me alive there. I am very uneasy around these girls.”

“No, no, I take full responsibility—she won’t even open her mouth.”

“All right, you go ahead, I’ll come in a little while.”

“No, that won’t do. My car has already left with Saroj. You must come with me to drop me off.”

Both of them sat in Mehta’s car. The car started.

After a moment, Mehta asked, “I have heard that Khanna Sahib beats his wife. Since then, I have come to detest the sight of him. A man who is so cruel—I cannot even consider him a man. And yet he pretends to be a great champion of women. Have you never tried to make him understand?”

Malti, agitated, replied, “You always forget that it takes two hands to clap.”

“I cannot even imagine any reason that would justify a man beating his wife.”

--- Page 137 ---
"Even if a woman is utterly insolent?"

"Yes, no matter how much."

"Then you are a new kind of man."

"If a man is ill-tempered, in your opinion, should he be lashed with whips, then?"

"A woman can be forgiving in ways a man cannot. You yourself admitted this today."

"And is this the reward for a woman’s forbearance? I believe, by indulging Khanna, you only embolden him further. With the respect he shows you, the devotion he has for you, you could easily set him straight. But by defending him, you make yourself complicit in his wrongdoing."

Malti, growing agitated, said, "You have needlessly brought up this topic now. I do not wish to speak ill of anyone, but you have not yet understood Govindi Devi. Seeing her innocent, serene demeanor, you have taken her for a saint. I do not wish to place her on such a high pedestal. The lengths she has gone to defame me, the blows she has dealt me—if I were to recount them, you would be astonished, and then you would have to admit that such a woman deserves just this treatment."

"But there must be some reason for her enmity towards you?"

"Ask her the reason. How should I know what lies in another’s heart?"

"Even without asking, one can guess. And it is this: if any man dared to come between me and my wife, I would shoot him dead. And if I could not, I would shoot myself. In the same way, if I were to bring another woman between myself and my wife, my wife too has every right to do as she pleases. On this matter, I can make no compromise. It is an unscientific instinct inherited from our wild ancestors, and nowadays some call it uncivilized and antisocial behavior, but I have not yet conquered that instinct, nor do I wish to. In this matter, I care nothing for the law. In my house, my law prevails."

Malti asked sharply, "But how have you assumed that I wish, in your words, to come between Khanna and Govindi? By making such an assumption, you insult me. I do not consider Khanna worth even the dust on my shoes."

Mehta replied with disbelief, "You do not say that from your heart, Miss Malti! Do you take the whole world for fools? If everyone sees what is plain, and if Mrs. Khanna sees it too, I cannot blame her."

Malti retorted, "The world delights in slandering others. That is its nature. How can I change its nature? But this is a baseless stain. Yes, I am not so heartless as to turn Khanna away if he comes to me. My very work requires me to welcome and honor all. If someone chooses to interpret this otherwise, then that... that..."

Malti’s voice choked, and she turned away, wiping her tears with a handkerchief. After a moment, she said, "You too, along with the others, have... have... hurt me. I did not expect this from you."

--- Page 138 ---
Perhaps then she felt a pang of regret at her own weakness. She spoke out, her voice fierce—"You have no right to cast aspersions on me. If you are one of those men who cannot see a man and woman together without raising a finger, then by all means, do so. I do not care in the least. If a woman were to come to you again and again on some pretext, to consider you her deity, to seek your counsel in every matter, to lay her eyes at your feet, to be ready to leap into fire at your command—then I can say with certainty, you would not ignore her. If you could reject her, then you are not human. Bring forth as many arguments and proofs as you like against this, but I will not believe them. I say, forget ignoring, forget rejecting—before long, you would be washing that woman’s feet and drinking the water, and she would become the queen of your heart. I beg you, with folded hands, never mention Khanna’s name before me again."

Mehta, as if warming his hands over this fire, replied, "The only condition is that I do not see Khanna with you."

"I cannot murder humanity. If he comes, I will not drive him away."

"Tell him to behave decently with his wife."

"I do not consider it proper to interfere in anyone’s private affairs. Nor do I have the right."

"Then you cannot silence anyone’s tongue."

Malti’s bungalow had arrived. The car stopped. Malti got down and walked away without even shaking hands. She even forgot that she had invited Mehta for dinner. She wanted to go somewhere alone and weep her heart out. Govindi had wounded her before, but today’s blow was deeper, broader, and more piercing than any before.

Raisahab received news that an incident had occurred in the estate and that the panchayat of Hori’s village had already collected the fine. He immediately summoned Nokhe Ram and demanded an explanation—why had he not been informed? There was no place in his court for such a thankless, treacherous man.

Nokhe Ram, after enduring so much abuse, finally retorted, a little heated, "I was not alone. There were other panchayat members too. What could I have done by myself?"

Raisahab cast a spear-like, piercing glance at his belly—"Don’t argue! You should have said right then and there: until the zamindar is informed, I will not allow the panchayat to collect the fine. What right do the panchayat members have to interfere between me and my tenants? Apart from this land and water tax, what other income is there in the estate? The collection has gone to the government. The arrears have been claimed by the tenants. Then where am I to go? What am I to eat—your head? Where am I to get the lakhs of rupees needed each year? It is a shame that after two generations of service, I have to explain this to you today. How much was collected from Hori?"

Nokhe Ram, flustered, replied, "Eighty rupees."

"In cash?"

"How would he have cash, huzoor? He gave some grain, and for the rest, he signed over his house."

Raisahab, setting aside his own interest, took Hori’s side—"So, you and Bagula Bhagat and the other panchayat members together have ruined one of my most important tenants. I ask you, what right did you have to collect a fine from my tenant in my estate without informing me? For this alone—"

--- Page 139 ---
If I wished, I could have that cruel patwari and that cunning pandit sent to jail for seven years each. Do you think you are the lords of this land? Let me make it clear: by this evening, the full amount of the fine must reach me, or there will be consequences. I will have each one of you grinding at the millstone before I let you go. Now go, and yes, send Hori and his son to me.

Nokheram muttered under his breath, “His son has run away from the village. The very night this incident happened, he fled.”

Raisahab said angrily, “Don’t lie. You know well that lies set my blood aflame. Never in my life have I heard of a young man bringing his beloved to her home, only to run away himself. If he had wanted to run, why would he have brought the girl? Surely, you people are up to some mischief in this as well. Even if you drowned yourselves in the Ganga to prove your innocence, I would not believe you. To protect your precious social codes, you must have threatened him. What else could the poor boy do but run?”

Nokheram could not protest. Whatever the master says is right. He could not even suggest that Raisahab himself come and investigate the truth. The anger of great men demands complete submission; they cannot bear to hear a single word against themselves.

When the panchayat heard Raisahab’s verdict, their intoxication vanished. The grain was still lying untouched, but the money had long since disappeared. Hori’s house had been mortgaged, but who cared for such a house in the village? Just as a Hindu wife is the mistress of the home with her husband, but if he abandons her, she is left with nothing, so too this house was worth a fortune to Hori, but in truth, it was worthless. And Raisahab would not be satisfied without the money. Surely Hori must have gone to him and wept. Pateshwari Lal was the most afraid; he might even lose his job. The four gentlemen sat pondering this grave problem, but no one’s mind could find a solution. Each blamed the other, and soon a fierce quarrel broke out.

Pateshwari shook his long, suspicious neck and said, “I warned you that we should keep silent about Hori. In the matter of the cow, everyone had to pay a fine. In this matter, even the fine won’t save us; we’ll lose our jobs. But you people only cared about the money. Now cough up twenty rupees each. There’s still time. If Raisahab files a report, you’ll all be in chains.”

Datadeen, displaying his Brahmin pride, said, “I don’t have even twenty paise, let alone twenty rupees. I fed the Brahmins, performed the homa—did you think that cost nothing? Does Raisahab dare send me to jail? I am a Brahmin—I could destroy his entire household. He has yet to encounter a true Brahmin.”

Jhinguri Singh echoed similar sentiments. He was not Raisahab’s servant. He had not beaten or pressured Hori. If Hori wished to atone, he merely gave him the opportunity. No one could accuse him of wrongdoing. But Nokheram’s neck would not slip so easily from the noose. Here he sat, ruling at his leisure. His salary was barely ten rupees, but his income was over a thousand a year, with hundreds under his command, four peons at his beck and call. All the work was done through forced labor. Even the thanedar offered him a chair—where else could he enjoy such comfort? And Pateshwari had become a mahajan thanks to his job. Where could he go? For two or three days, they remained sunk in worry, pondering how to escape this calamity. At last, a way occurred to them. Sometimes...

--- Page 140 ---
At the courthouse, they had access to the daily newspaper ‘Bijli’. If an anonymous letter were to be sent to its editor detailing how Rai Sahib extorted fines from his tenants, it would put Bachcha in a difficult position. Nokhe Ram agreed as well. Together, they composed a letter and sent it by registered post.

Editor Omkar Nath was always on the lookout for such letters. As soon as he received it, he immediately informed Rai Sahib. He wrote that he had received a piece of news which he wished not to believe, but the correspondent had provided such evidence that it could not be easily dismissed. Was it true, he asked, that Rai Sahib had collected a fine of eighty rupees from a tenant because his son had brought a widow into his house? The editor’s duty compelled him to investigate the matter and, in the public interest, publish it. Whatever Rai Sahib wished to say in his defense, the editor would also publish. The editor sincerely hoped the news was false, but if there was any truth to it, he would be obliged to bring it to light. Friendship could not sway him from the path of duty.

When Rai Sahib received this notice, he was beside himself with anger. At first, he was so incensed that he wanted to go and give Omkar Nath fifty lashes and tell him, “Wherever you print your letters, print this news as well!” But considering the consequences, he calmed himself and immediately set out to meet him. If he delayed and Omkar Nath published the report, his entire reputation would be tarnished.

Omkar Nath had just returned from his walk and was sitting, troubled, thinking about the editorial for today’s paper, but his mind wandered like a restless bird. The night before, his wife had said things to him that still pricked him like thorns. He could bear being called poor, unfortunate, or even a fool, but to be told he lacked manliness was intolerable. And what right did his wife have to say such a thing? She was expected to silence anyone who made such an accusation. True, he did not publish such news or write such comments that would bring calamity upon his head. He treaded carefully, step by step. In these days of draconian laws, what else could he do? But why did he not put his hand into the snake’s den? Only so that his family would not have to suffer. And this was the reward for his forbearance? It was unjust. He had no money—how could he buy a Banarasi sari? The wives of doctors, seths, and Professor Bhatia wore Banarasi saris—so what? Why did his wife not take pride in her khadi sari and shame those women with her simplicity? He himself made it a point to wear coarse clothes when meeting important people, and if anyone criticized him, he was ready to give a fitting reply. Why did his wife lack the same self-respect? Why did she become unsettled seeing others’ finery? She should understand that she was the wife of a patriot. What wealth does a patriot have except his devotion? As he pondered making this very subject the theme of today’s editorial, his thoughts turned to Rai Sahib’s affair. He wondered how Rai Sahib would respond to the notice. If he succeeded in defending himself, there would be no issue. But if he thought that Omkar Nath would shirk his duty out of pressure, fear, or consideration, he was mistaken. What other reward did he get for all his penance and discipline except that, when the time came, he could expose these legal bandits? He knew well that Rai Sahib was a highly influential man—a member of the council, with considerable clout among the authorities. If he wished, he could bring false charges against him…

--- Page 141 ---
They can set their thugs upon me, have me beaten on the road, but Omkarnath is not daunted by such things. As long as there is breath in his body, he will continue to hold oppressors to account.

Suddenly, startled by the sound of a motorcar, he took up his pen at once and began his article. In just a moment, Rai Sahib stepped into his room.

Omkarnath neither welcomed him, nor inquired after his well-being, nor offered him a chair. He looked at him as if a defendant had appeared before his court, and in a voice tinged with cold formality, asked, “Did you receive my letter of resignation? I was not obliged to write that letter; my duty was to— Is there any truth in that report?”

Rai Sahib could not deny its truth. Though he had not yet received the fine money, and could easily have refused to pay, he wanted to see which path this gentleman would take.

Omkarnath, expressing regret, said, “Then I have no choice but to publish that report. It pains me to criticize a dear and trusted friend, but if a man cannot fulfill the obligations of his seat, he has no right to occupy it.”

Rai Sahib settled into the chair, stuffed his mouth with betel leaves, and said, “But this will not be good for you. Whatever happens to me will come later—your punishment will be immediate. If you care nothing for friendship, then I too am a man of the same mettle.”

Omkarnath, assuming the dignity of a martyr, replied, “That has never frightened me. The day I took up the burden of editorship, I renounced all personal attachments. To me, the most glorious death for an editor is to sacrifice himself in the defense of justice and truth.”

“Very well,” said Rai Sahib. “I accept your challenge. Until now, I have considered you a friend. But if you are determined to fight, then so be it. After all, why do I give your paper a fivefold subscription? Only so it remains beholden to me. When you lament your losses, I give you seventy-five rupees so you keep your mouth shut. Whenever you cry about deficits and appeal for help—which happens nearly every quarter—I always offer some assistance. Why? During Diwali, Dussehra, Holi, I send you gifts, and twenty-five times a year I invite you to my table. Why? You cannot take bribes and fulfill your duty at the same time.”

Omkarnath, agitated, said, “I have never taken a bribe.”

Rai Sahib rebuked him, “If this is not bribery, then what is? Tell me. Do you think everyone but you is a fool, making up your losses out of pure selflessness? Take out your ledger and show me how much you have received from my estate so far. I am sure the sum will run into thousands. If you shout ‘Swadeshi’ and then shamelessly print advertisements for foreign medicines and goods, should I not be ashamed to collect fines, penalties, and tithes from my tenants? Do not think you alone have taken up the cause of the people. I am bound to live and die with the peasants—no one can be a greater well-wisher of theirs than I. But how am I to make ends meet? How am I to host officials and—”

--- Page 142 ---
“How can I give? How can I fulfill the needs of hundreds of people in my family? Perhaps you know what the expenses of my household are. Do you think money grows in my house? If anything comes, it comes from the tenants’ homes. You may imagine that zamindars and taluqdars are enjoying all the pleasures of the world. But you do not know their true condition. If we tried to live as men of virtue, it would be impossible for us to survive. If we do not offer gifts to the officials, the jail would become our home. We are not scorpions, stinging people for no reason. Squeezing the poor is not a source of any great joy, but one must uphold the boundaries of custom. Just as you wish to take advantage of my wealth, so does everyone else see us as a golden goose. Come to my bungalow, and I will show you how, from morning till night, arrows are aimed at me. Someone comes from Kashmir with shawls and wraps, another is an agent for perfumes and tobacco, another for books and magazines, another for life insurance, yet another rides on my head with a gramophone—someone or other, always. The beggars are countless. Should I sit before them all and pour out my own miseries? These people come to my door to tell their woes, to make a fool of me and extort something. If I abandon all sense of decorum, there will be applause. If I do not offer gifts to the authorities, I will be branded a rebel. Then you will not defend me in your articles. I joined the Congress, and I am still paying the price for it. My name is written in the black book. Have you ever asked how much debt I am under? If all the mahajans were to file decrees, even this ring on my finger would be auctioned off. You may ask, why do I maintain this show? Tell me, having grown up for seven generations in this environment, how can I break free? Toiling in the fields is impossible for me. You have no land, no property, no entanglements of status, so you can afford to be fearless, but even you keep your tail tucked in. Do you have any idea how much bribery goes on in the courts, how many poor people are bled dry, how many goddesses are defiled? Do you have the courage to write about it? I will give you the material, with evidence.”

Omkarnath, softening a little, said, “Whenever the occasion has arisen, I have never stepped back.”

Raisahab too softened, “Yes, I admit that on one or two occasions you have shown courage, but your eyes have always been on your own benefit, not on the welfare of the people. Don’t glare at me, and don’t get red in the face. Whenever you have entered the field, the only result has been an increase in your honor, influence, and income. If you are playing the same game with me, I am ready to oblige you. I will not give you money, for that would be a bribe. But I can have some jewelry made for your wife. Is that acceptable? Now I will tell you the truth: the information you received was wrong, but I must also say that, like all my brothers, I too collect fines from my tenants, and every year five or ten thousand rupees come into my hands. And if you try to snatch this morsel from my mouth, you will only be at a loss. You wish to live happily in this world, and so do I. What is the use of pretending to justice and duty, only to bring both of us to ruin? Speak from your heart. I am not your enemy. How many times have we eaten together at the same table, in the same kitchen? I also know that you are in distress. Your condition may be even worse than mine. But if you have sworn to become a Harishchandra, then so be it. I take my leave.”

Raisahab rose from his chair. Omkarnath caught his hand and said conciliatorily, “No, no, you must sit down. I want to make my position clear. I am grateful for the way you have treated me, but here it is a matter of principle, and you know, principles are dearer than life itself.”

--- Page 143 ---
Raisahab, sitting on the chair, spoke in a somewhat gentle tone, “Very well, brother, write whatever you wish. I do not wish to break your principles. What more can happen—there will be some disgrace. But really, how long should I keep chasing after reputation? Is there a single taluqdar who does not oppress his tenants to some extent? If a dog is made to guard a bone, what will it eat? All I can promise is that henceforth you shall have no such complaint from me. If you have any faith in me, then forgive me this time. I would not plead like this with any other editor. I would have had him beaten in the marketplace. But since you are my friend, I must yield. This is the age of newspapers—even the government fears them; what am I in comparison? You can make or break whomever you wish. Well, let us end this quarrel. Tell me, how is the paper faring these days? Have you gained more subscribers?”

Omkar Nath replied with reluctance, “Somehow or other, things keep going, and in the present circumstances, I do not expect more than that. I did not come into this field with any desire for wealth or pleasure, so I have no complaints. I came to serve the people, and I do so to the best of my ability. The welfare of the nation is my only wish. The happiness or sorrow of one individual has no value.”

Raisahab, a little more warmly, said, “All that is well and good, brother, but to serve, one must also live. In the midst of financial worries, how can you serve with a focused mind? Are you not gaining any new subscribers at all?”

“The fact is, I do not wish to lower the ideals of my paper. If I were to start publishing the pictures and stories of cinema stars, perhaps my subscribers would increase. But that is not my policy. There are many other tricks by which money can be made through newspapers, but I consider them contemptible.”

“It is precisely for this reason that you are held in such high esteem today. I wish to make a proposal. I do not know whether you will accept it or not. On my behalf, issue free subscriptions to a hundred people—I will pay the donation.”

Omkar Nath bowed his head in gratitude and said, “I accept your gift with thanks. It is a pity how indifferent the public is towards newspapers! There is no shortage of funds for schools, colleges, and temples, but not a single donor has come forward to give for the promotion of newspapers, even though the goal of public education can be achieved through newspapers at a fraction of the cost. If, just as institutions support schools, journalists too could receive such assistance, why would these poor souls have to devote so much of their time and space to advertisements? I am greatly obliged to you.”

Raisahab took his leave. There was no trace of happiness on Omkar Nath’s face. Raisahab had imposed no conditions, set no restrictions, yet even after such a stern rebuke today, Omkar Nath could not refuse this donation. The circumstances were such that he could see no way out. The press workers’ salaries were three months in arrears. The paper supplier’s bill had crossed a thousand rupees. Was it not enough that he did not have to beg?

His wife, Gomti, came in and said in a tone of rebellion, “Is it not time for your meal yet, or is there some rule that you cannot get up from your seat until the clock strikes one? How long must someone keep watch over the stove?”

Omkar Nath looked at his wife with sorrowful eyes. Gomti’s rebellion melted away. She...

--- Page 144 ---
She understood hardships. Sometimes, when she saw the clothes and ornaments of other women, feelings of rebellion would stir in her heart, and she would let slip a few sharp words at her husband. But in truth, this anger was not directed at him, but at her own misfortune, though a little of its heat would inevitably reach Omkarnath. She would feel both resentful and sympathetic when she saw his ascetic way of life. She simply thought him a little eccentric. Seeing his gloomy face, she asked, “Why are you so downcast? Is something wrong with your stomach?”

Omkarnath was forced to smile. “Who’s downcast? Me? I am happier today than I was even on my wedding day. This morning, I made a sale of fifteen hundred rupees. Must have seen the face of some fortunate soul.”

Gomti could not believe it. She said, “Liar. Where would you get fifteen hundred rupees? If you said fifteen rupees, I might believe you.”

“No, no, I swear on your head, it’s fifteen hundred. Rai Sahib himself came. He promised to pay the customers’ subscriptions from his own pocket.”

Gomti’s face fell. “So, you’ve already got it?”

“No, but Rai Sahib is a man of his word.”

“I’ve never seen a taluqdar keep his word. My father was a servant to one. He wouldn’t get his wages for a whole year. So he left and worked for another. That one didn’t pay a single paisa for two years. Once, when my father protested, he was beaten and thrown out. There’s no trust in their promises.”

“I’ll send the bill today itself.”

“Send it if you like. They’ll just say, ‘Come tomorrow.’ Tomorrow they’ll go off to their estate, and won’t return for three months.”
