Omkarnath grew doubtful. She was right—if Rai Sahib went back on his word, what could he do? Still, he steeled his heart and said, “That can’t be. At the very least, I don’t believe Rai Sahib is so deceitful. I have nothing outstanding with him.”

Gomti, still skeptical, said, “That’s why I call you a fool. The moment someone shows you a little sympathy, you swell with hope. These are fat aristocrats. They can digest as many promises as they make. If they actually fulfilled every promise, they’d be reduced to begging. The Thakur Sahib in my village wouldn’t settle accounts with the baniyas for two or three years at a time. As for the servants, he’d pay them only for the sake of appearances. He’d make them work a whole year, and when they asked for their wages, he’d beat them and throw them out. More than once, his sons’ names were struck off the school rolls because of unpaid fees. In the end, he had to call them home. Once, he even asked for a railway ticket on credit. This Rai Sahib is cut from the same cloth. Come, eat your meal and grind the grain, as fate has written for you. Just remember, it’s better that these great men keep scolding you. If they give you a single paisa, they’ll squeeze four times as much from their tenants. As long as you write what you want about them, you’ll have to flatter them. If you ever want to write the truth, you’ll have to hold your tongue.”

Panditji was eating, but each mouthful seemed to stick in his throat. In the end, it became impossible to eat without unburdening his heart. He said, “If he doesn’t pay, I’ll make sure he remembers it. I have him by the hair. The villagers can’t give false news. If they told the truth, their lives would be forfeit—how could they give false news? I have a report against Rai Sahib. If I print it, his son won’t be able to show his face outside. I’m not asking for charity. He’s only come to this point after being cornered. At first, he tried threats. When he saw that wouldn’t work, he threw out this bait. I thought, if only he could be set right, injustice would vanish from the land…”

--- Page 145 ---
No, then why should I not accept this gift? Certainly, I have fallen from my ideals, but even so—if Rai Sahib has acted deceitfully, then I too shall resort to cunning. One does not have to persuade his soul much to rob the one who robs the poor.

:17:

Word spread through the village that Rai Sahib had summoned the panchayat and scolded them severely, and all the money they had collected was taken back from them. He was even about to send them to jail, but they pleaded and begged, even stooping to lick their own spit, only then did he let them go. Dhaniya’s heart was soothed; she went about the village, shaming the panchayat members—“Men may not hear the cries of the poor, but God does.” People had thought they would feast on sweets with the fines they had extorted. But God struck them such a blow that the sweets flew out of their mouths. Each one had to cough up double. “Now, let’s see you take my house,” she taunted.

But how can one farm without oxen? The rumor spread through the village. If a farmer’s oxen die in the month of Kartik, it is as if both his hands have been cut off. Hori’s hands had been cut off. In all the other fields, ploughs were turning the soil, seeds were being sown, and sometimes the strains of songs could be heard. Hori’s fields lay desolate, like the home of a helpless widow. Puniya had a cow, Shobha had a cow, but where did they have the time to help sow Hori’s fields, busy as they were with their own? Hori wandered here and there all day, sometimes sitting in one man’s field, sometimes helping another with his sowing. In this way, he managed to get a little grain. Dhaniya, Rupa, and Sona all worked in other people’s fields. As long as the sowing lasted, they had food to eat, and did not suffer much. There was certainly mental anguish, but at least they had enough to eat. Every night, there would be a little quarrel between husband and wife. So Kartik passed, and even finding work in the village became difficult. Now, all their hopes rested on the sugarcane standing in the fields.

It was night. The cold was bitter. There was nothing to eat in Hori’s house today. In the day, they had managed to get a little roasted gram, but now there was no way to light the stove, and Rupa, tormented by hunger, sat crying by the fire at the door. When there is not a single grain of food in the house, what can one ask for, what can one say?

When hunger became unbearable, she went to Puniya’s house on the pretext of asking for fire. Puniya was cooking millet bread and wild greens. The aroma made Rupa’s mouth water.

Puniya asked, “What, your fire hasn’t been lit yet, girl?”

Rupa replied humbly, “There was nothing in the house today, how could we light a fire?”

“Then why have you come asking for fire?”

“Dada wants to smoke tobacco.”

Puniya tossed her a burning cow-dung cake, but Rupa did not pick it up. Instead, she went closer and said, “Your bread smells so good, auntie! I really like millet bread.”

Puniya smiled and asked, “Will you eat some?”

“Amma will scold me.”

“Who’s going to tell your mother?”

Rupa ate her fill of bread and, with her mouth still full, ran home.

--- Page 146 ---
Hori sat, his spirit crushed, when Pandit Datadeen came and called out to him. Hori’s heart began to pound.  
Was some new calamity about to befall him? He went and touched Datadeen’s feet, and placed a stool for him before the cow-dung fire.

As Datadeen sat down, he spoke with a tone of kindness, “So your fields are lying fallow this year, Hori! You didn’t say a word to anyone in the village—otherwise, Bhola would never have dared to untie the oxen from your doorstep. He would have dropped dead right there. I swear to you on my sacred thread, Hori, I did not levy the fine against you. Dhaniya goes about slandering me for no reason. This is all the doing of Lala Pateshwari and Jhinguri Singh. I only sat in the panchayat because people asked me to. They were going to impose an even harsher penalty. I pleaded and got it reduced. But now, everyone is wringing their hands and weeping. They thought they ruled here, not knowing that the real king of the village is someone else. So, what arrangements are you making for sowing your fields?”

Hori replied in a voice choked with sorrow, “What can I say, Maharaj? The fields will remain fallow.”

“They’ll remain fallow? That would be a great misfortune!”

“If it is God’s will, what power do I have?”

“How can your fields lie fallow while I am here to see it? Tomorrow, I will have your sowing done. There’s still some moisture left in the soil. The crop will be a bit late by ten days, that’s all. We’ll share the harvest equally—half for you, half for me. Neither of us will lose out. As I sat today, the thought pained me that ploughed and prepared fields should go to waste.”

Hori fell into thought. All through the monsoon, he had manured and tilled these fields, and now, just for the sowing, he would have to give up half the crop. And yet, Datadeen acted as if he were doing him a great favor. But what choice did he have? Better this than letting the fields lie barren. Even if he got nothing else, at least he would be able to pay the rent. If he refused now, eviction would surely follow.

He accepted the proposal.

Datadeen, pleased, said, “Come, I’ll weigh out the seed right now, so there’s no trouble in the morning. You’ve eaten, haven’t you?”

Hori, embarrassed, confessed that no fire had been lit in his house that day.

With gentle reproach, Datadeen said, “What! No fire in your house, and you didn’t even tell me? Am I your enemy? This is what pains me about you. Good man, what is there to be ashamed of? We are all one. So what if you are a Shudra and I am a Brahmin? We are all of the same household. No one’s days are always the same. Who knows, tomorrow I might face hardship, and if I don’t tell you my troubles, whom will I tell? Well, what’s done is done. Come, I’ll weigh you out a couple of maunds of grain to eat along with the seed.”

Within half an hour, Hori returned with a basket of barley on his head, and the household mill began to turn. Dhaniya wept as she ground the barley, yet she did so with courage. What sin was she being punished for by God?

The next day, the sowing began. Hori’s entire family worked as if the fields were their own. After several days, the irrigation was done in the same way. Datadeen found laborers for free or for a pittance. Now, sometimes his son Matadeen also began to come to the house. He was a young man, fond of pleasure and sweet-spoken. Whatever Datadeen managed to snatch or grab, Matadeen would squander on hemp and intoxicants. He had formed a liaison with a chamar woman, which was why he had not yet married. She lived with him, but though the whole village knew this secret, no one dared speak of it. Such was our dharma.

News spread through the village that Rai Sahib had summoned the panchayat and scolded them thoroughly, and all the money they had collected was forced out of them. He was about to send them to jail, but they pleaded, folded their hands and feet, and even licked their own spit, only then did he let them go. Dhaniya’s heart was soothed; she roamed the village, shaming the panches—"Men may not hear the cries of the poor, but God does." "Now lick my house away," she taunted.

But how can one farm without oxen? Sowing had begun in the villages. If a farmer’s oxen die in the month of Kartik, it is as if both his hands are cut off. Hori’s hands had been cut off. In every other field, ploughs were turning the soil, seeds were being sown, and sometimes the strains of songs could be heard. Hori’s fields lay desolate, like the home of a helpless widow. Puniya had a pair of oxen, Shobha had a pair too, but who had time to sow Hori’s fields when their own sowing was not done? Hori wandered all day from place to place. Sometimes he would sit in someone’s field, sometimes help with their sowing. In this way, he got a little grain. Dhaniya, Roopa, and Sona too worked in other people’s fields. When hunger became unbearable, she went to Puniya’s house on the pretext of asking for fire. Puniya was cooking bajra rotis and bathua greens. The aroma made Roopa’s mouth water.

Puniya asked, “Hasn’t your fire been lit yet, girl?”

Roopa replied meekly, “There was nothing in the house today, how could we light the fire?”

“Then why have you come asking for fire?”

“Dada wants to smoke tobacco.”

Puniya smiled and asked, “Will you eat?”

“Amma will scold me.”

“Who will go and tell your amma?”

Roopa ate her fill of rotis and ran home with her mouth still full.

Hori replied in a voice choked with sorrow, “What can I say, Maharaj? The fields will remain fallow.”  

“They’ll remain fallow? That would be a great misfortune!”  

“If it is God’s will, what can I do?”

With gentle reproach, Datadeen said, “Oh! The fire didn’t burn in your house, and you didn’t even tell me! Am I your enemy? This is what pains me about you. My good man, what’s there to be ashamed of? We are all one. So what if you’re a Shudra and I’m a Brahmin? We belong to the same village, the same home. Not every day is the same for everyone. Who knows, tomorrow some calamity may befall me, and if I don’t share my sorrow with you, who will I turn to? Well, what’s done is done. Come, I’ll weigh out a maund or two of grain for you along with the seed.”  

Within half an hour, Hori returned with a basket of barley on his head, and the household mill began to turn. Dhaniya wept as she ground the barley, yet worked with courage. For what sin was God punishing her so?  

The next day, the sowing began. Hori’s entire family threw themselves into the work as if it were all their own. After several days, the irrigation was done in the same way. Datadeen found laborers for free. Now, sometimes his son Matadeen began to visit the house as well. A young man, full of wit and sweet talk. Whatever Datadeen managed to snatch or seize, Matadeen would squander on hemp and intoxicants. He had become involved with a chamar woman, which was why he had not yet married. She lived with him, but though the whole village knew this secret, no one dared speak of it. Such was the dharma of their world.

--- Page 149 ---
Food. As long as food remains pure, our faith cannot be tainted. The very rotis become a shield, protecting us from unrighteousness.

Now that the land was being farmed jointly, Matadeen found more opportunities to speak with Jhunia. He would come at such times when no one was home except Jhunia—sometimes on one pretext, sometimes on another. Jhunia was not beautiful, but she was young, and she was better than his Chamarin lover. She had spent some time in the city, knew how to dress, how to speak, and she was modest too, which is a woman’s greatest charm. Sometimes Matadeen would take her child in his arms and dote on him. Jhunia would be overjoyed.

One day he said to her, “What did you see in Gobar that you came with him, Jhoona?”

Jhunia replied shyly, “Fate pulled me, Maharaj, what else can I say?”

Matadeen spoke with a heavy heart, “He’s a faithless man. Leaving a Lakshmi like you, who knows where he’s wandering now? He’s a fickle sort, and that’s why I worry he may have gotten entangled somewhere else. Such men ought to be shot. A man’s dharma is to stand by the one whose hand he has taken. What is this, that you ruin one woman’s life and then go looking for another?”

The young woman began to weep. Matadeen glanced around, then took her hand and tried to console her. “Why do you care for him, Jhoona? He’s gone—let him go. What do you lack? Money, jewelry, clothes—whatever you want, take it from me.”

Jhunia gently pulled her hand away and stepped back. “It’s all your kindness, Maharaj! I belong nowhere now. Left my home, and here too I have no place. I found neither love nor God. I didn’t know the ways of the world. I got trapped by his sweet words.”

Matadeen began to speak ill of Gobar. “He’s nothing but a loafer, neither of home nor of the world. Always quarreling with his parents. If he gets any money, he’ll gamble it away at once. He lives for charas and ganja, roams around with the Sohos, teases women—this is all he does. The police inspector was about to file a case against him for mischief; we pleaded a lot, only then was he let off. He used to steal grain from other people’s fields and storehouses. Many times I myself caught him, but let him go for the sake of the village.”

Sona came out and said, “Bhabhi, Amma says take out the grain and spread it in the sun today, otherwise too much chaff will come out. It’s as if Panditji poured water in the granary.”

Matadeen tried to explain himself, “Seems like it didn’t rain at your place. In the monsoon, even the firewood gets damp—grain is grain, after all.”

Saying this, he went outside. Sona had spoiled his game.

Sona asked Jhunia, “What did Matadeen come for?”

Jhunia frowned and said, “He was asking for a rope. I told him there’s no rope here.”

“That’s just an excuse. He’s a bad man.”

“I think he’s a good man. What’s wrong with him?”

“You don’t know? He keeps Silia the Chamarin.”

“So that makes him a bad man?”

“What else makes a man bad?”

“Your brother brought me here too. Is he a bad man as well?”

Without answering, Sona said, “If he ever comes to my house again, I’ll drive him away.”

--- Page 150 ---
“And what if you end up marrying him?”

Sona blushed. “You’re my bhabhi, and you tease me like that!”

“Why, what’s so bad about it?”

“If he so much as speaks to me, I’ll scorch his mouth.”

“So, will you marry a god, then? Who else in the village is as handsome and strapping as he is?”

“Then you go with him—you’re a hundred times better than Siliya.”

“Why should I go? I’ve already gone with one man, for better or worse.”

“Then I’ll go with whomever I’m married to, for better or worse.”

“And what if you’re married to an old man?”

Sona laughed. “Then I’ll cook him soft, tender rotis, grind and sift his medicines, help him up by the hand, and when he dies, I’ll cover his face and weep.”

“And what if you’re married to a young man?”

“Then your head—yes, that’s what!”

“Tell me truly, do you prefer an old man or a young one?”

“Whoever loves me is young; whoever doesn’t is old.”

“God grant you marry an old man, then I’ll see how much you love him. Then you’ll wish, if only this wretch would die, I could settle down with a young one.”

“I’d just feel sorry for that old man.”

This year, a sugar mill had opened nearby. Its agents and brokers would go from village to village, buying standing sugarcane from the farmers. It was the same mill that Mr. Khanna had opened. One day, its agent came to this village too. When the farmers bargained with him, they realized there was no profit in making jaggery themselves—if they could get the same price at home, why go through the trouble of crushing the cane? The whole village was ready to sell their crop, even if the price was a little less—it didn’t matter. At least they’d get the money right away. One needed to buy oxen, another had to pay off debts, someone else wanted to redeem a pawned item from the mahajan. Hori needed to buy fodder for his oxen. This year’s sugarcane crop hadn’t been good, so there was also the fear that the produce might go to waste. And when jaggery would fetch the same price as the mill’s sugar, who would buy jaggery at all? Everyone had taken advances. Hori hoped for at least a hundred rupees. There would be a small deduction, but what could one do about the mahajans? Datadin, Mangru, Dulari, Jhinguri Singh—all of them were after him. If he started paying the mahajans, even the interest wouldn’t leave him with a hundred rupees. He kept looking for some clever way to get the money for the sugarcane without anyone finding out. But once the oxen were brought home, what could he do? When the cart was loaded, the whole village would see; the expenses at the weighbridge would be known. It was likely that Mangru and Datadin would stick to him like shadows. Seeing this, he felt trapped.

In the evening, Girdhar asked, “Kaka, when will your sugarcane go?”

Hori replied evasively, “Nothing’s certain yet, brother. What about yours?”

Girdhar also hedged, “Mine’s not certain either, Kaka.”

The others were just as cagey, and no one trusted anyone else—otherwise, someone would gobble up everything for himself. And when the next day the agent came again—

--- Page 151 ---
So, a new document, a new offering, a new petition. The next day, Shobha came and said, “Dada, do something so that Jhinguri gets cholera. May he fall so hard that he never rises again.”

Hori smiled and said, “Why? Doesn’t he have children?”

“Should I care for his children or my own? He keeps two maids in comfort, while here, even one can’t get dry bread. He’ll take all the earnings. He won’t let a single paisa come home.”

“My situation is even worse, brother. If the money slips from my hands, I’ll be ruined. Without the ox, I can’t manage.”

“It’ll take two or three more days to haul the sugarcane. As soon as all the cane is delivered, we’ll tell the overseer, ‘Brother, take some, but weigh the cane quickly, pay us later.’ Meanwhile, we’ll tell Jhinguri we haven’t gotten the money yet.”

Hori thought for a moment and said, “Jhinguri Singh is many times shrewder than you or me, Shobha! He’ll go straight to the munim and get the money from him. We’ll be left staring. The Khanna Babu who owns the mill is the same Khanna Babu who owns the moneylender’s shop. Both are one and the same.”

Shobha said, disappointed, “Who knows if we’ll ever be free from these mahajans?”

Hori replied, “There’s no hope of that in this lifetime, brother! We don’t want power, we don’t want luxury, we just want to wear rough clothes, eat simple food, and live with dignity. Even that’s not possible.”

Shobha said slyly, “Dada, this time I’ll trick them all. I’ll bribe the overseer to make us run around for the money. How long can Jhinguri keep up?”

Hori laughed and said, “None of this will happen, brother! The only wisdom is to plead with Jhinguri Singh. We’re caught in a net. The more you struggle, the tighter it gets.”

“You sound like an old man, Dada. Just sitting in the trap is cowardice. If the noose tightens, so be it, but you have to try to break free. At worst, Jhinguri will auction off our homes—let him. I wish no one would lend us money, let us starve, let us be kicked, not a paisa in debt. But if the moneylenders don’t lend, where will they get their interest? One files a claim against us, another traps us in his net with a loan at a lower rate. I’ll only go to collect the money when Jhinguri is gone somewhere.”

Hori’s heart was unsettled too. “Yes, that’s right.”

“We’ll have the cane weighed. We’ll collect the money when the time is right.”

“Yes, yes, that’s the way.”

The next morning, several men from the village began cutting the sugarcane. Hori too arrived at his field, sickle in hand. Shobha came to help him. Puniya, Jhunia, Dhaniya, Sona—all reached the field. Some cut the cane, some stripped it, some bundled it. When the moneylenders saw the cane being cut, their stomachs churned with anxiety. From one side came Dulari running, from another Mangru Sah, from a third Matadin and Pateshwari and Jhinguri’s men. Dulari, with thick silver bangles on her wrists and ankles, gold earrings in her ears, kohl in her eyes, her old age painted with colors, came and said, “First give me my money, then I’ll let you cut the cane. The more I swallow my grief, the bolder you become. For two years you haven’t paid a single coin of interest—fifty at least is owed to me in interest alone.”

Hori pleaded, “Bhabhi, let us cut the cane. If we get the money, as much as possible—”

--- Page 152 ---
“I will pay you too, as soon as I can. I cannot run away from the village, nor is death coming for me so soon. Won’t the standing sugarcane in the field yield any money?”

Dulari snatched the sickle from his hand and said, “Your intentions have become so rotten, that’s why you people never prosper.”

It had been five years since Hori had borrowed thirty rupees from Dulari. In three years, that sum had grown to a hundred rupees, and then a stamped agreement was written. In the next two years, fifty more rupees of interest had accrued.

Hori said, “Sahuain, I have never harbored ill intentions, and if God wills, I will repay every last paisa. But these days I am in dire straits, say what you will.”

No sooner had the Sahuain left than Mangru Sah arrived. Dark-skinned, his belly drooping below his waist, two large teeth jutting out in front as if ready to bite, a cap on his head, a shawl around his neck. He was not yet over fifty, but walked with the help of a stick. He suffered from gout. He also had a cough. Leaning on his stick, he stood and scolded Hori, “First give me my money, Hori, then cut your sugarcane. I lent you money, it wasn’t charity. It’s been three years, neither interest nor principal has been paid. But don’t think you’ll get away with swallowing my money. I’ll recover it even from your corpse.”

Shobha, ever the jester, said, “Then why worry, Sahji? You’ll recover it from his corpse. No, in a year or two, you’ll both reach heaven together. There, settle your accounts before God himself.”

Mangru hurled abuses at Shobha—calling him a cheat, a scoundrel, and more. “When it’s time to borrow, you wag your tails, but when it’s time to repay, you play tricks. I’ll have your house sold, I’ll auction your oxen and bullocks.”

Shobha teased him again, “All right, tell me honestly, Sah, how much did you originally lend, for which you now demand three hundred rupees?”

“When you don’t pay the yearly interest, it keeps growing, doesn’t it?”

“How much did you lend at first? Only fifty rupees, right?”

“Look how many years it’s been.”

“Five or six years, perhaps.”

“Ten years, a full ten, the eleventh is running.”

“And you feel no shame taking three hundred rupees for a loan of fifty?”

“What shame? I lent money, not begged for charity.”

Hori managed to send him off too, after much pleading and supplication. Datadin had farmed in partnership with Hori, providing the seeds and claiming half the crop. At this moment, it would be against custom to quarrel with him. Jhinguri Singh had already spoken with the manager of the mill. His men were loading the sugarcane onto carts and taking it to the boats. The river was half a mile from the village. One cart could make seven or eight trips in a day, and a single boat could carry the load of fifty carts in one crossing. This was a great saving. By arranging this convenience, Jhinguri Singh had placed the entire region under his debt.

As soon as the weighing began, Jhinguri Singh took his seat at the mill gate. He weighed everyone’s sugarcane, collected the payment slip, received the money from the cashier, deducted his own dues, and handed the remainder to the debtor. No matter how much the debtor wept or pleaded, he listened to none. That was the master’s order. What could he do?

Hori received one hundred and twenty rupees. Out of this, Jhinguri Singh deducted all his dues with interest, and handed Hori about twenty-five rupees.

--- Page 153 ---
Hori looked at the money with an air of indifference and said, “What will I do with this, Thakur? You take it too. I’ll get plenty of work as a laborer.”

Jhinguri threw the twenty-five rupees on the ground and said, “Take it or leave it, as you wish. Because of you, I’ve had to endure the landlord’s scoldings, and now Raisahab is breathing down my neck, demanding the payment for the land. Out of pity for your poverty, I’m giving you this much—otherwise, I wouldn’t have given you a single coin. If Raisahab gets stricter, I’ll have to pay even more from my own pocket.”

Hori quietly picked up the money and stepped outside, when Nokheram called out to him. Hori went over and placed the twenty-five rupees in his hand, and without saying a word, hurried away. His head was spinning. Shobha had received only this much as well. When he stepped outside, Pateshwari blocked his path.

Shobha changed his tone and said, “I have no money, do whatever you want.”

Pateshwari, growing angry, said, “You sold the sugarcane, didn’t you?”

“Yes, I sold it.”

“Didn’t you promise to pay me after selling the sugarcane?”

“Yes, I did.”

“Then why don’t you pay me? Haven’t you paid everyone else?”

“Yes, I have.”

“Then why not me?”

“What little I have left is for my wife and children.”

Pateshwari, now furious, said, “You will pay me, Shobha, and with folded hands, today itself. Take as much as you want right now. For one report, you’ll go to jail for six months—full six months, not a day less, not a day more. This gambling you do every day will all come out in one report. I am not a servant of the zamindar or the mahajan—I am a servant of Sarkar Bahadur, who rules the whole world and is the master of both your mahajan and zamindar.”

Pateshwari Lala moved on. Shobha and Hori walked silently for a while, as if this humiliation had left them senseless. Then Hori said, “Shobha, give him the money. Just think of it as if the sugarcane field caught fire. That’s how I’ve consoled myself.”

Shobha replied in a wounded voice, “Yes, I’ll give it, Dada. If I don’t, where will I go?”

From ahead, Girdhar came swaying, drunk on toddy. Seeing them, he said, “Jhinguriya took everything, Hori Kaka! Didn’t leave a single coin for chivda. The murderer! I cried, I begged, but the sinner showed no mercy.”

Shobha said, “You’re drunk on toddy, and still you say you didn’t get a single coin?”

Girdhar showed his belly and said, “It’s evening now, and if even a drop of water has passed my throat, it’s as much as beef. I had hidden a two-anna coin in my mouth. I drank toddy with that. I thought, I’ve sweated for a whole year, so at least today I’ll have some toddy. But I swear, I’m not drunk. What intoxication can there be in one anna’s worth? Yes, I’m swaying so people will think I’m thoroughly drunk. It’s good, Kaka, that everything’s out in the open now. Took twenty, paid back a hundred and sixty—there’s a limit to everything!”

When Hori reached home, Rupa ran up with water. Sona brought a filled chilam, Dhania set out chivda and salt, and all of them looked at him with hopeful eyes. Even Jhunia came and stood at the threshold. Hori sat there, dejected. How could he wash his face, how could he eat the chivda? He felt so ashamed and remorseful, as if he had just committed a murder.

--- Page 154 ---
Dhaniya asked, “How much did the weighing come to?”

“One hundred and twenty, but all of it was lost there, not a single coin left.”

From head to toe, Dhaniya felt scorched. Such a storm of emotion rose within her that she wanted to claw at her own face. She said,  
“Why did God create such a blockhead as you? If I ever met Him, I would ask. My whole life has turned bitter with you, and God won’t even grant me death to free me from this misery. You just handed over all the money to your brothers-in-law. Now, what income is left from which we’ll get a cow? Will you yoke me to the plough, or will you plough yourself? I tell you, you’ve grown old, and yet you haven’t learned enough sense to at least set aside the money for a cow! No one would have snatched it from your hands. This cold of Paush, and not a rag on anyone’s back. If you can, go drown yourself in the river. Better to die once than to die every day with sobs. How long will we spend our nights buried in straw? And even if we do, how long can we survive eating straw? If you wish, eat grass yourself, but I cannot eat grass.”

Saying this, she broke into a smile. By now, she had begun to understand that when the mahajan sits on your head, and you have money in hand, and the mahajan knows it, how can the debtor save himself?

Hori, head bowed, was mourning his fate. He did not see Dhaniya’s smile. He said,  
“We’ll get wages. We’ll work for hire and eat.”

Dhaniya asked, “Where is there work for hire in this village? And how will you show your face to do it? Aren’t you a Mahto?”

Hori took a few puffs from his chilam and said,  
“There’s no sin in working for wages. If you become a laborer, you become a peasant. If a peasant falls, he becomes a laborer. If working for wages was not in my fate, why would all these calamities come? Why would the cow die? Why would the boy turn out worthless?”

Dhaniya looked at her daughter-in-law and daughters and said,  
“Why are you all standing around? Go, do your own work. There are others who, when they return from the market, bring something for their children with a few coins. Here, you must be hoping to get the money changed, lest a single coin be lost. That’s why there’s no blessing in his earnings. Those who spend, receive. Those who can’t eat or wear, why should they get money? To bury it in the ground?”

Hori laughed and asked, “Where is that buried treasure?”

“Wherever you put it, it must be there. The real sorrow is that even knowing this, you die for money. If you had brought home something worth a few coins for the children, you wouldn’t have ended up in this mess. If you’d told Jhinguri, ‘Give me one rupee, or else I won’t give you a single coin, go get it from the court if you want,’ he would have surely given it.”

Hori felt ashamed. If, in his anger, he hadn’t handed over all the money to Nokhe Ram, what could Nokhe have done? At most, he would have taken a few annas as interest on the debt, but now the mistake was done.

Inside, Jhuniya said to Sona,  
“I feel so sorry for Dada. The poor man comes home exhausted after a day’s work, and Amma starts scolding him. The mahajan had him by the throat—what else could he do?”

“So where will the oxen come from?”

“The mahajan wants his money. What does he care for your family’s troubles?”

“If Amma had been there, she would have taught the mahajan a lesson. The wretch would have been left crying.”

Jhuniya teased,  
“So is there any shortage of money here? Just smile at the mahajan and say a word, and see if he doesn’t let go of all the money. Truly, all of Dada’s miseries would vanish.”

Sona pressed her mouth shut with both hands and said,  
“Enough, just keep quiet, or else—”

--- Page 155 ---
If I go and reveal all of Matadeen’s secrets to Amma, you’ll be the one crying.  
Jhunia asked, “What would you even tell Amma? There’s nothing to say. When he comes into the house on some pretext, what should I do—tell him to leave? And does he ever take anything from me? He only leaves something of his own behind. All he gets from me are sweet words. And I know how to sell my sweet words at a high price. I’m not so naïve as to fall for anyone’s tricks. Yes, if I ever learn that your brother has taken up with someone else over there, then I won’t hold back. Then no one’s bonds will restrain me. For now, I believe he is mine, and because of me, he has to wander from street to street, suffering blows. Laughing and talking is one thing, but I will not betray him. Whoever belongs to more than one person belongs to no one at all.”

Shobha came and called out to Hori, placed Pateshwari’s money in his hand, and said, “Brother, you go and give this money to the Lala. I don’t know what came over me at that moment.”

Hori had just stood up with the money when the sound of a conch shell reached his ears. At the far end of the village lived a Thakur named Dhyan Singh. He was a soldier in the army and, after ten years, had recently returned home on leave. He had traveled all over—Baghdad, Aden, Singapore, Burma. Now he was obsessed with getting married. That’s why he wanted to please the Brahmins by performing rituals and worship.

Hori said, “It seems the seventh chapter is over. The aarti is happening.”

Shobha replied, “Yes, it seems so. Come, let’s go and receive the aarti.”

Hori said with a troubled look, “You go ahead. I’ll come in a little while.”

The day Dhyan Singh had arrived, he’d sent a seer of sweets as a gift to every household. Whenever he met Hori on the road, he would inquire about his well-being. To attend his katha and not offer anything during the aarti would be an insult. The aarti plate would be in his hands. How could Hori, in front of him, accept the aarti empty-handed? It would be better not to go to the katha at all. Among so many people, would he even notice that Hori hadn’t come? No one was sitting there with a register, keeping track of who came and who didn’t. So Hori went and lay down on his cot.

But his heart ached with longing. He didn’t have a single coin—not even a copper paisa! He had no thought for the merit or spiritual power of the aarti. It was only a matter of social custom. He could only accept Thakurji’s aarti as an offering of devotion, but how could he break the code of conduct, how could he bear to be looked down upon in everyone’s eyes?

Suddenly, he sat up. Why should he be a slave to custom? Why should he forgo the merit of the aarti for the sake of social propriety? Let people laugh if they want to. He didn’t care. May God keep him from wrongdoing, that was all he wished for. He set off toward the Thakur’s house.

:18:

Khanna and Govindi do not get along. Why they don’t get along is hard to say. According to astrology, there is some conflict in their stars, though at the time of their marriage, their horoscopes and constellations were carefully matched. According to the Kama Shastra, there may be some other secret to this discord, and the psychologists might find yet another reason. All we know is that they do not get along. Khanna is wealthy, charming, sociable, handsome, well-educated, and one of the city’s distinguished men. Govindi may not be an apsara, but she is certainly beautiful. Wheat-colored skin, modest eyes that rise to meet yours only to fall again, cheeks without a flush but with a certain smoothness, a soft body, limbs arranged with grace—

--- Page 156 ---
Rounded, full arms, a face marked by a certain distaste, in which a glimmer of pride also shone, as if she regarded the dealings and transactions of the world as contemptible.

Khanna lacked none of the outward trappings of luxury: a first-class bungalow, first-class furniture, a first-class car, and boundless wealth. Yet, in Govindi’s eyes, these things held no value. In this briny sea, she lay parched with thirst. The upbringing of her children and the small, everyday tasks of the household were everything to her. She was so absorbed in these that she paid no heed to pleasure. What was attraction, and how could it arise—she had never pondered such things. She was not a man’s plaything, nor an object for his enjoyment; then why should she strive to make herself alluring? If a man lacked the eyes to see her true beauty and wandered after other women, that was his misfortune. She continued to serve her husband with the same love and devotion as ever, as though she had conquered feelings like resentment and attachment. And this immense wealth seemed only to crush her soul. Her heart constantly yearned to be free from these pretensions and hypocrisies. She dreamed daily of how happy she could have been in a simple, natural life. Then why did Malti become an obstacle in her path? Why were there gatherings of courtesans, why did suspicion, pretense, and unrest become thorns along her journey? Long ago, when she was a student at the girls’ school, she had been afflicted with the malady of poetry, where pain and suffering were the very essence of life, and wealth and luxury existed only to be sacrificed, for they led man toward falsehood and unrest. Even now, she sometimes composed poetry, but to whom could she recite it? Her poetry was not merely the ripple of her mind or the flight of feeling—each word carried the agony of her life and the cold sting of her tears. She longed to settle in some place where, far from hypocrisy and desire, she could enjoy simple happiness in her peaceful cottage. If Khanna ever saw her poems, he would mock them, and sometimes even tear them up and throw them away.

And the wall of wealth grew higher day by day, driving the couple ever farther and farther apart. The sweeter and more courteous Khanna was with his clients, the harsher and more insolent he became at home. Often, in anger, he would hurl abuses at Govindi; courtesy, for him, was merely a means to deceive the world, not a refinement of the heart. At such times, Govindi would retreat to her solitary room and spend the whole night weeping, while Khanna listened to courtesans’ performances in the drawing room or squandered his nights at the club, drinking. Yet, for all this, Khanna was still her everything. Even when downtrodden and humiliated, she remained his bondswoman. She would quarrel, burn with pain, weep, but she would remain his. She could not even imagine a life apart from him.

Today, Mr. Khanna must have seen the face of some ill-omened man upon waking. As soon as he opened the morning paper, he saw that the prices of several of his stocks had fallen, costing him thousands. The workers at the sugar mill had gone on strike and were on the verge of rioting. He had bought silver in hopes of profit, but its price had fallen even further today. The deal he was making with Rai Sahab, from which he had expected a handsome gain, now seemed to be postponed for some days. On top of that, his head was heavy and his body ached from drinking too much the previous night. The chauffeur had reported some trouble with the car’s engine, and news had arrived that a civil suit had been filed against his bank in Lahore. He sat, fuming inwardly, when at that very moment Govindi came and said—

“Bhishma’s fever has still not gone down. Please call a doctor.”

--- Page 157 ---
Bhishma was their youngest son, and because he had been frail since birth, he always had one complaint or another. Today it was a cough, tomorrow a fever; sometimes his ribs ached, sometimes he suffered from greenish-yellow diarrhea. Though he was ten months old, he looked no more than five or six. Khanna had come to believe that this child would not survive, and so he remained indifferent towards him. But Govindi, for this very reason, loved him more than any of the other children.

Khanna, displaying the affection of a father, said, “It isn’t right to make children dependent on medicines, and you have a habit of dosing them at the slightest thing. The moment something happens, you call for the doctor. Wait another day—this is only the third day. Perhaps it will subside on its own.”

Govindi insisted, “It hasn’t gone down in three days. I’ve tried every home remedy and failed.”

Khanna asked, “Very well, I’ll call someone. Whom should I call?”

“Call Dr. Nag.”

“All right, I’ll call him. But understand this—just having a name doesn’t make someone a good doctor. Nag may charge whatever fee he likes, but I’ve never seen anyone recover with his medicine. He’s famous for sending his patients straight to heaven.”

“Then call whomever you wish. I only suggested Nag because he’s come several times before.”

“Why not call Miss Malti? Her fee is lower, and as a lady doctor, she’ll understand children’s ailments in a way no male doctor can.”

Govindi retorted sharply, “I don’t consider Miss Malti a doctor.”

Khanna looked at her with piercing eyes and said, “So she went to England just to dig up grass? And the thousands of people she’s saved—does that mean nothing to you?”

“Perhaps. I simply don’t trust her. Let her cure men’s hearts if she likes. She has no medicine for anyone else.”

That was it—the quarrel had begun. Khanna thundered, Govindi poured down her anger. The mere mention of Malti’s name between them was like an ultimatum for war.

Khanna threw all his papers to the floor and said, “Life with you has become bitter.”

Govindi replied in a sharp tone, “Then go and marry Malti! What’s stopping you, if things work out there?”

“What do you take me for?”

“I think Malti wants to keep men like you as her slaves, not as her husband.”

“You think so little of me?”

And he began to offer proof to the contrary. Malti respected him more than perhaps anyone else. She wouldn’t give the time of day to Rai Sahib or Raja Sahib, but if a single day passed without meeting him, she would complain...

Govindi blew away these arguments with a single breath. “That’s because she thinks you’re the biggest fool of all—she can’t make fools of others so easily.”

Khanna boasted, “If I wished, I could marry Malti today. This very day, right now...”

But Govindi was utterly unconvinced. “Even if you rubbed your nose on the ground for seven lifetimes, she would never marry you. You’re her pony—she’ll feed you grass, sometimes stroke your face, run her hand over your flanks, but only so she can ride you. A thousand fools like you are in her pocket.”

--- Page 158 ---
Today, Govindi was greatly agitated. It seemed as if she had come prepared for a confrontation. The doctor’s summons was merely an excuse. How could Khanna tolerate such a grave accusation against his competence, skill, and manhood?

“In your opinion, I am a fool and an idiot, then why do thousands come to my door, groveling? Which raja or taluqdar is there who does not bow before me? I have made fools of hundreds.”

“That is precisely Malti’s specialty—she shaves those who shave others with a straight razor, with the back of her knife.”

“You may malign Malti as much as you wish, but you are not even the dust at her feet.”

“In my eyes, she is even worse than prostitutes, for she hunts from behind a veil.”

Both had unleashed their fiery arrows. Khanna could have said something even harsher to Govindi, and it would not have hurt her so deeply. But this vile comparison with Malti was unbearable even to her forbearance. Likewise, whatever Govindi might have said to Khanna, he would not have been so incensed, but he could not endure this insult to Malti. Both knew each other’s tender spots. Both their arrows struck true, and both were left writhing. Khanna’s eyes reddened with anger. Govindi’s face flushed crimson. In a fit of rage, Khanna stood up, seized her by both ears, twisted them hard, and slapped her three times. Govindi, weeping, withdrew into her room.

A little while later, Dr. Nag arrived, then Civil Surgeon Mr. Todd, and the learned physician Neelkanth Shastri, but Govindi, holding her child, remained seated in her room. She neither knew nor cared what was said, what diagnosis was made. The calamity she had feared had now descended upon her. Today, it was as if Khanna had severed all ties with her, as if he had cast her out and barred the door. The woman who sits in the marketplace of beauty, whose very shadow Govindi never wished to fall upon herself… that woman would now rule over her, even indirectly? Never. Khanna was her husband; he had the right to admonish her, even to strike her, and she could accept that. But Malti’s rule? Impossible! Yet, until the child’s fever subsided, she could not move. Even pride must bow its head before duty.

The next day, the child’s fever had abated. Govindi summoned a tonga and left the house. Where she was treated with such contempt, she could no longer stay. The blow had been so harsh that even her attachment to the children was broken. She had fulfilled her duty toward them. What remained was Khanna’s responsibility. Yes, the child in her arms—him she could not leave under any circumstances. He was her very life. From this house, she would take only her own soul; nothing else belonged to her. They claimed to be her providers—Govindi would show them that she could live even without their shelter. The three older children were out playing at the time. Govindi felt a pang, a desire to embrace them once, but she was not running away. If the children loved her, they would come to her, play in her home. Whenever she felt it necessary, she would come and see them herself. She simply did not wish to depend on Khanna’s protection.

Evening had fallen. The park was lively. People lounged on the green grass, enjoying the breeze. Govindi, passing through Hazratganj, had just turned toward the zoo when she saw Malti and Khanna approaching in a car. She thought she saw Khanna gesture toward her and Malti smile. No, perhaps it was only her imagination. Khanna would not slander her to Malti, but…

--- Page 159 ---
How shameless she is. I have heard she has a good practice, her family is well-off, and yet she goes about selling herself like this! Who knows why she does not marry, but then, who would marry her? No, that’s not it—there are plenty of men who would consider themselves blessed to have her, but would Malti herself choose anyone? And what happiness is there in marriage anyway? She does well not to marry. Right now, everyone is her slave. If she were to become someone’s wife, she would be reduced to a servant of one. She is doing the right thing. Even this gentleman here worships at her feet. If she were to marry him, he would start ruling over her—but why would she marry him? And it’s good that there are a few women like her in society, to keep men on their toes.

Today, Govindi felt a great sympathy for Malti. By accusing her, she was doing her an injustice. Wouldn’t Malti’s eyes be opened, seeing my own condition? If, witnessing the misery of married life, she chooses not to fall into this trap, what wrong is she doing?

A hush had fallen over the zoo. Govindi stopped the tonga and, carrying her child, walked toward the green grass. But she had barely taken two or three steps when her sandals sank into water. The lawn had just been watered, and beneath the grass, water was flowing. In her haste, without looking back, she took another step forward, and her feet were covered in mud. She looked down at her feet. Where would she find water here to wash them? All her inner turmoil vanished, replaced by a new worry—how to clean her feet. Her train of thought was broken. Until her feet were clean, she could think of nothing else.

Suddenly, she noticed a long pipe hidden in the grass, with water flowing from it. She went over, washed her feet, her sandals, her hands and face, scooped a little water in her palms to drink, and then sat down on the dry ground beyond the pipe. Sadness always brings thoughts of death. What if she were to die sitting here? The tonga driver would immediately go and inform Khanna. Khanna would light up at the news, but for the world’s sake, he would cover his eyes with a handkerchief. For the children, toys and amusements are dearer than their mother. Such is her life, for which not even a few tears would be shed. Then she remembered the days when her mother-in-law was alive and Khanna was not yet so indifferent. Back then, her mother-in-law’s constant scolding had seemed unbearable. Now, she felt as if that anger had been laced with affection. In those days, she would sulk, and her mother-in-law would coax her with love. Now, she could sulk for months, and who would care?

Suddenly, her mind flew to her mother’s feet. Oh! If only Amma were alive, would she be in such a wretched state? Her mother had nothing else, but she had a lap full of affection, a loving veil in which she could bury her face and weep. But no, she would not cry, she would not make that goddess unhappy in heaven. Her mother had done all she could for her. It was not in her power to share her fate. And why should she cry? She was no longer dependent on anyone. She could earn her own living. Tomorrow itself, she would start selling goods from the Gandhi Ashram. What was there to be ashamed of? At most, people would point and say—there goes Khanna’s wife. But why should she stay in this city? Why not go to another city, where no one knew her? Earning ten or twenty rupees was not so difficult. At least she would eat what she earned with her own sweat, and then no one could lord it over her. This gentleman only acts so high and mighty because he supports me. Now I will support myself.

Suddenly, she saw Mehta coming toward her. She felt uneasy. At this moment, she wanted complete solitude. She had no desire to speak to anyone, and yet here was another gentleman approaching.

--- Page 160 ---
He too had begun to weep.

Mehta approached her in surprise and asked, “How have you come here at this hour?”

Govindi, soothing the child, replied, “In the same way that you have come.”

Mehta smiled and said, “Don’t bring up my case. Like the washerman’s dog—belonging neither to home nor to the riverbank.”

“Here, let me quiet the child for you.”

“When did you learn this art?” she asked.

“I wish to practice it. There will be an examination, after all.”

“Oh! The day of the examination is near, then?”

“That depends on my preparation. When I am ready, I shall sit for it. For small degrees we study so hard we nearly lose our sight. But this is the examination of life itself.”

“Very well, I too shall see in what grade you pass.”

Saying this, she handed the child over to him. He tossed the child up a few times, and the child quieted down. Boasting like a child himself, he said, “Did you see? With the force of my magic, I made him quiet. Now I too will bring a child from somewhere.”

Govindi teased, “Will you bring only the child, or his mother as well?”

Mehta shook his head with playful despair and said, “Such a woman is nowhere to be found.”

“Why not? Isn’t Miss Malti there? Beautiful, educated, virtuous, charming—what more do you want?”

“There is not a single quality in Miss Malti that I would wish for in my wife.”

Govindi, enjoying this disparagement, said, “What fault do you find in her? She is always surrounded by admirers. I’ve heard that nowadays men prefer such women.”

Mehta, protecting his mustache from the child’s hands, replied, “My wife will be of a different sort. She will be someone I can worship.”

Govindi could not restrain her laughter. “So you do not want a wife, but an idol! Such a woman is hardly to be found.”

“No, such a goddess exists in this very city.”

“Truly? I too would like to see her, and try to become like her.”

“You know her well. She is the wife of a millionaire, yet she considers luxury trivial. She endures neglect and disrespect, yet never wavers from her duty. She sacrifices herself on the altar of motherhood, for whom renunciation is the highest right, and who is worthy of being made into an idol and worshipped.”

A tremor of joy passed through Govindi’s heart. Though she understood, she pretended not to, and said, “You praise such a woman! But to me, she is an object of pity. She is the ideal woman, and whoever can be an ideal woman can also be an ideal wife.”

Mehta said in surprise, “You insult her.”

“But that ideal is not for this age.”

“That ideal is eternal and immortal. Man, by distorting it, is bringing about his own ruin.”

Govindi’s heart blossomed. Such feelings had never arisen there before. Of all the people she knew, Mehta held the highest place. To receive this encouragement from his lips intoxicated her.

In that very intoxication, she said, “Then come, let me see her.”

--- Page 161 ---
Mehta, hiding his face in the child’s cheeks, said, “She is sitting right here.”

“Where? I don’t see her.”

“I am speaking to that very goddess.”

Govindi burst out laughing—“You are determined to tease me, aren’t you?”

Mehta, bowing with reverence, replied, “Devi-ji, you do me an injustice, and more than me, you do yourself an injustice. There are very few people in this world for whom I feel true reverence. You are one of them. Your patience, your sacrifice, your virtue, your love—they are unparalleled! The greatest happiness I can imagine in my life is to serve at the feet of a goddess like you. The womanhood I hold as my ideal—you are its living embodiment.”

Tears of joy welled up in Govindi’s eyes. Clad in this armor of reverence, what calamity could she not face? It was as if a gentle music rose from every fiber of her being. Suppressing the delight of her womanhood, she said, “Why did you become a philosopher, Mehta-ji? You should have been a poet.”

Mehta laughed simply. “Do you think anyone can be a poet without first being a philosopher? Philosophy is but a stage along the way.”

“So you are still on the path to poetry, but you also know that a poet never finds happiness in this world.”

“What the world calls sorrow, that is happiness for the poet. Wealth and splendor, beauty and strength, learning and intellect—these treasures may enchant the world, but they hold no attraction for the poet. His joy and fascination lie in extinguished hopes, faded memories, and the tears of broken hearts. The day he ceases to love these things, he ceases to be a poet. Philosophy only toys with the mysteries of life; the poet dissolves into them. I have read a few of your poems, and the thrill, the trembling, the sweet agony, the maddening ecstasy that brings one to tears—I alone know how deeply I have felt them. Nature has done us a great injustice in not creating another goddess like you.”

Govindi replied wistfully, “No, Mehta-ji, that is your illusion. You will find such women in every street, and I am less than all of them. A woman who cannot keep her husband happy, who cannot make herself the queen of his heart—can she even be called a woman? Sometimes I think I should learn this art from Malti. Where I have failed, she has succeeded. I cannot even win myself over, and she wins over others. Is that not to her credit?”

Mehta made a face and said, “If wine drives people mad, should it be considered better than water, which quenches thirst, sustains life, and brings peace?”

Govindi, taking refuge in jest, replied, “Whatever you say, I see that water wanders about in search of takers, while homes and hearths are sold for wine. And the stronger and more intoxicating the wine, the better it is considered. I hear you too are a devotee of wine?”

Govindi had reached that state of despair where even truth and righteousness begin to seem doubtful, but Mehta did not notice. His attention was fixed on the last part of her sentence. He felt more shame and regret at his own cleverness today than he ever had after hearing great sermons. He had answers, sharp and ready, for arguments, but to this sweet jibe, he could find no reply. He regretted ever bringing up the analogy of wine.

--- Page 162 ---
Malti had compared it to alcohol. The blow fell upon his own head. Ashamed, he said, "Deviji, I admit that I have this attachment. I will not try to justify my sin by claiming it is necessary for me, or by citing its stimulating qualities—such excuses are worse than the sin itself. Today, in your presence, I vow that not a single drop of liquor shall pass my lips again."

Govindi, stunned, said, "What have you done, Mehtaji? I swear by God, this was not my intention. I am sorry for it."

"No, you should be pleased that you have redeemed a man."

"I have redeemed you? I am myself about to beg for my own redemption."

"From me? What good fortune!"

Govindi said in a voice full of anguish, "Yes, for I see no one but you to whom I can tell my story. Please, keep this matter to yourself, though I know I need not remind you. My life has become unbearable. I have practiced as much self-restraint as I could, but I can endure no more. Malti is destroying me completely. I cannot conquer her with any weapon I possess. She holds you in great esteem—perhaps more than any other man. If you could somehow rescue me from her clutches, I would be indebted to you for life. My good fortune is being plundered at her hands. If you can protect me, please do. I left home today resolved never to return. I tried with all my might to break every bond of attachment, but a woman's heart is so weak, Mehtaji! Attachment is her very life. To break it while living is impossible for her. Until today, I kept my pain to myself, but now I come to you, spreading my veil, begging for alms. Save me from Malti. I am being destroyed by this enchantress."

Her voice was drowned in tears. She broke down, sobbing uncontrollably.

Mehta had never felt so exalted in his own eyes—not even when his work had been declared the finest of the century by the French Academy, and he had received their congratulations. The very idol he worshipped with a true heart, whom he regarded in his mind as his chosen goddess, from whom he hoped for guidance in the tangled moments of life, was today begging him for help. He felt such strength within himself that he could split mountains, swim across oceans. A kind of intoxication overtook him, as if a child, riding a wooden horse, imagines he is flying through the air. He did not consider how difficult the task was, nor how many of his own principles he might have to sacrifice. In a tone of reassurance, he said, "I did not know you suffered so much because of her. It is my fault—my lack of understanding, my blindness, my failure of imagination—what more can I say? Otherwise, why should you have had to endure such pain?"

Govindi grew doubtful. She said, "But remember, it is not easy to snatch prey from a lioness."

Mehta replied firmly, "A woman's heart is like the earth—it can yield both sweetness and bitterness. It all depends on the strength of the seed sown within."

"You must be regretting meeting me today."

"If I say that today, for the first time, I have tasted the true joy of life, perhaps you will not believe me."

"I have placed such a heavy burden on your shoulders."

--- Page 163 ---
Mehta said in a voice sweet with reverence, “You embarrass me, Deviji! I have already said, I am your servant. If my life itself were to be lost for your sake, I would consider it my good fortune. Do not mistake this for a poet’s emotional excess; this is the truth of my life. I cannot restrain myself from telling you what my ideal in life is. I am a devotee of nature, and I wish to see man in his natural form—one who laughs when happy, weeps when sorrowful, and, in anger, can even kill. Those who suppress both joy and sorrow, who consider weeping a weakness and laughter frivolity, I have no kinship with them. For me, life is a joyful play—simple, free—where there is no place for malice, envy, or jealousy. I do not worry about the past, nor do I care for the future. For me, the present is everything. Worry for the future makes us cowards; the burden of the past breaks our backs. The vitality of our lives is so meager that, stretched between past and future, it becomes even more feeble. We load ourselves with unnecessary burdens, crushed beneath the debris of customs, beliefs, and histories, unable to rise, our strength all but gone. The energy, the vigor that should have been devoted to fulfilling our human duties—in cooperation, in brotherhood—is sacrificed to settling old scores and repaying ancestral debts. And as for this obsession with God and salvation, it makes me laugh. This salvation and worship are the pinnacle of egotism, which destroys our very humanity. Where there is life, play, song, and love—there alone is God. To make life happy is worship, is salvation. The wise say, let not a smile come to your lips, nor tears to your eyes. I say, if you cannot laugh or weep, then you are not human, but stone. That knowledge which crushes humanity is not knowledge, but a millstone. But forgive me, I have ended up giving a whole speech. Now it is getting late; come, let me see you home. The child has fallen asleep in my arms.”

Govindi said, “But I have brought a tonga.”

“I will send the tonga away from here.”

When Mehta returned after paying the tonga fare, Govindi asked, “But where will you take me?”

Mehta, startled, replied, “Why, I will take you to your home.”

“That is not my home, Mehtaji.”

“Then is it Mr. Khanna’s home?”

“Is that even a question? That house is no longer mine. Where there is only humiliation and reproach, I cannot call it my home, nor can I think of it as such.”

Mehta, his voice heavy with pain, every word springing from his heart, said, “No, Deviji, that house is yours, and will always be yours. You created that home, you gave life to its people. And just as the soul animates the body—if the soul departs, what becomes of the body? Motherhood is a position of great honor, Deviji! And where, in a position of honor, is there not humiliation, reproach, and scorn? A mother’s task is to give life. She who holds such immeasurable power in her hands, why should she care who is angry with her, who is displeased? Just as the body cannot exist without the soul, so too is the body the soul’s most fitting abode. What lesson can I give you about duty and sacrifice? You are their living embodiment. I can only say that—”

Govindi interrupted impatiently, “But I am not only a mother, am I? I am a woman too.”

After a moment’s silence, Mehta replied, “Yes, you are. But I believe that a woman is, above all, a mother, and whatever else she is, is but an extension of her motherhood. Motherhood is the world’s—”

--- Page 164 ---
The greatest discipline, the greatest penance, the greatest sacrifice, and the most glorious victory—if I were to sum it up in a single word, I would call it harmony: of life, of personality, and of womanhood as well. As for Mr. Khanna, you must understand only this much: he is not in his senses. Whatever he says or does, he does in a state of frenzy, but this frenzy will not take long to subside, and the time will soon come when he will see you as his chosen goddess.

Govindi gave no reply. She walked slowly toward the car. Mehta stepped forward and opened the car door. Govindi got in. The car drove off, but both were silent.

When Govindi reached her doorway and stepped out of the car, Mehta saw in the electric light that her eyes were moist.

The children came running out of the house, crying, “Amma! Amma!” and clung to their mother. A radiant, proud glow of motherhood shone on Govindi’s face.

She said to Mehta, “Thank you very much for this trouble,” and lowered her head. A single tear fell onto her cheek.

Mehta’s eyes, too, filled with tears—how sorrowful is this woman’s heart, even amid all this wealth and luxury!

:19:

Mirza Khushend’s compound is a club, a courthouse, and a wrestling arena all in one. All day long, it is bustling with people. There was no space in the neighborhood for a wrestling ground, so Mirza had a thatched roof put up and made one. Every day, a hundred and fifty young wrestlers gather there. Mirzaji himself wrestles with them. The neighborhood panchayats are also held here. Disputes between husband and wife, mother-in-law and daughter-in-law, brother and brother—all are settled here. This is the center of the neighborhood’s social life, and of political activity as well. Meetings are held here every other day. Here the volunteers gather, here their programs are planned, and from here the city’s political affairs are managed. At the last gathering, Malti was elected president of the City Congress Committee. Since then, the place has become even more lively.

It has been a year since Gobar came to live here. He is no longer the simple, naïve village youth. He has seen much of the world now, and has begun to understand its ways. At heart, he is still a rustic—he holds onto every penny, never lets go of self-interest, never shirks hard work, nor ever loses courage—but the city’s air has touched him too. In the first month, he worked as a laborer, ate half his fill, and managed to save a little money. Then he started selling boiled taro, peas, and curd fritters from a basket. Seeing more profit in this, he left his job. In the summer, he set up a sherbet and ice stall, and then began selling hot tea. Now, his daily earnings are never less than two and a half to three rupees. He has had his hair cut in the English fashion, wears fine dhotis and pump shoes. He has bought a red woolen shawl and has developed a taste for betel and cigarettes. By attending meetings, he has gained some political awareness. He has begun to understand the meaning of nation and class. The prestige of social customs and the fear of public censure have greatly diminished in him. The frequent panchayats have made him bold. The very thing for which he lives here, far from home, hiding his face, happens here every day—often even more disgraceful things—and no one runs away. So why should he be so afraid and ashamed?

In all this time, he has not sent a single penny home. In matters of money, he is...

--- Page 165 ---
I do not consider them so clever. The moment they get the money, they will start soaring in the sky. Dada will be seized with the urge to go on a pilgrimage to Gaya at once, and Amma will be possessed by the desire to have jewelry made. He has no money for such useless things. Now he is a small-time moneylender. He lends money at interest to the tonga drivers and washermen in the neighborhood. In just these ten or eleven months, through his hard work, thrift, and determination, he has made a place for himself, and now he is thinking of bringing Jhunia here to live with him.

It is late afternoon. He has just returned from bathing at the roadside tap and is boiling potatoes for the evening meal when Mirza Khurshid arrives and stands at the door. Gobar is no longer his servant, but he still shows him the same respect and is ready to lay down his life for him. Going to the door, he asks, “What are your orders, Sarkar?”

Mirza, standing there, says, “If you have some money, give it to me. My bottle has been empty for three days now, and I am feeling very restless.”

Gobar had given Mirzaji money two or three times before, but had not been able to recover it so far. He was afraid to ask for it, and Mirzaji did not know how to return money once he had taken it. Money never stayed in his hands. It came in one moment, vanished the next. Gobar could not bring himself to say, “I won’t give you money,” or “I don’t have any money,” so he began to criticize drinking instead: “Why don’t you give it up, Sarkar? Does drinking do you any good?”

Mirza, sitting on the cot inside the room, said, “Do you think I don’t want to give it up, that I drink for pleasure? I cannot live without it. Don’t worry about your money—I will pay back every last paisa.”

Gobar remained unmoved. “I swear, Malik, if I had money right now, would I refuse you?”

“Can’t you even give me two rupees?”

“Not at the moment.”

“Take my ring as security.”

Gobar’s heart was tempted, but how could he change his word now?

He said, “What are you saying, Malik? If I had the money, I would have given it to you. What need would there be for the ring?”

Mirza, filling his voice with a pitiful insistence, said, “I will never ask you again, Gobar! I can’t even stand up anymore. Because of this liquor, I have squandered a fortune of lakhs and become a beggar. Now I am determined that, even if I have to beg, I will give it up.”

When Gobar refused again, Mirza Sahib left in disappointment. He had thousands of acquaintances in the city. Many had prospered because of him. He had helped many in their time of need, but he did not care to meet such people. He knew thousands of tricks to pile up money when he needed it, but he had no value for money itself. Money seemed to sting his hands; his mind would only be at peace once he had found some excuse to get rid of it.

Gobar began peeling potatoes. In just one year, he had become so shrewd and so skilled at saving money that it was astonishing. The room he lived in had been given to him by Mirza Sahib. He could easily get five rupees a month as rent for this room and the verandah. Gobar had been living there for almost a year, but Mirza had never asked for rent, nor had Gobar ever paid it. Perhaps it had never even occurred to Mirza that any rent could be collected for this room.

--- Page 166 ---
After a little while, an ekka-driver came to ask for money. His name was Aladdin—his head was shaved, he had a patchy beard, and he was blind in one eye. His daughter was getting married. He needed five rupees. Gover lent him one rupee at an anna’s interest.

Aladdin thanked him and said, “Brother, now call for your wife and children. How long will you keep pounding away alone?”

Gover began lamenting the expenses of the city, “How can a household run on such meager earnings?”

Lighting a bidi, Aladdin replied, “Allah will provide for the expenses, brother! Just think, how much comfort you’ll have. I tell you, the same money you spend alone will suffice for a family. There’s great blessing in a woman’s hands. By God, when I lived here alone, no matter how much I earned, it was all spent—food, drink, everything gone. I didn’t even have money for a bidi or tobacco. And on top of that, the bother—come home tired and you have to cook and serve yourself, then run to the baker’s shop. It was exhausting. Since my wife arrived, the same earnings feed both of us, and I get to rest as well. After all, a man earns for comfort. If, after toiling your life away, you don’t get any peace, what’s the point of living? I say, your earnings will even increase, brother! In the time you spend boiling potatoes and peas, you could sell two or four cups of tea. Now tea sells all year round. At night, when you lie down, your wife will massage your feet. All your fatigue will melt away.”

These words took root in Gover’s heart. He felt restless. Now he would bring Jhunia home, no matter what. The potatoes were left boiling on the stove, and he began preparing to leave for home. But then he remembered Holi was approaching, so he should buy supplies for the festival as well. That tendency among the frugal to spend freely on festivals awakened in him too. After all, this was the very day for which he had been saving every penny. He would take a sari each for his mother, sisters, and Jhunia. For Hori, a dhoti and a sheet. For Sona, a bottle of oil and a pair of slippers. For Rupa, Japanese bangles, and for Jhunia, a basket containing combs, vermilion, and a mirror. For the child, a cap and a frock, ready-made from the market. He took out his money and went to the bazaar. By noon, he had gathered everything. The bedding was packed up, and the news spread among the neighbors—Gover was going home. Several men and women came to see him off. Entrusting his home to them, Gover said, “I leave everything to you all. If God wills, I’ll return the day after Holi.”

A young woman smiled and said, “Don’t come back without your wife, or you won’t be allowed in the house.”

An older woman advised, “Yes, indeed, you’ve tended the stove alone long enough. Now you’ll get proper meals.”

Gover bade everyone farewell. There were Hindus and Muslims alike, all shared bonds of friendship, all companions in each other’s joys and sorrows. Those who kept the fast observed the roza, those who kept the ekadashi, observed the ekadashi. Sometimes, in jest, they would poke fun at each other—Gover would call Aladdin’s namaz his ‘up-and-downs,’ and Aladdin would call the hundreds of small and large shivalings under the peepal tree ‘clods of earth.’ But there was not even a trace of communal hatred. Gover was going home, and everyone wanted to see him off with smiles and happiness.

Just then, Bhure arrived with his ekka. He had just returned from a day’s work. When he heard Gover was leaving, he turned his ekka that way. The horse protested, so he gave it a few lashes. Gover loaded his things onto the ekka, and it set off. Those seeing him off accompanied him to the turn of the lane, and then Gover—

--- Page 167 ---
He greeted everyone with a “Ram-Ram” and climbed onto the ekka.  
The ekka sped swiftly down the road. Gobar was intoxicated with the joy of returning home. Bhurey, too, was delighted at the thought of bringing him back, and the horse was full of spirit. The horse trotted on. In no time, they reached the station.

Gobar, beaming, took a rupee from his waistband and held it out to Bhurey. “Here, buy some sweets for your family.”

Bhurey looked at him with grateful reproach. “Do you take me for a stranger, bhaiya? Just because you sat in my ekka for a day, should I take a reward from you? Where your sweat falls, I am ready to shed my blood. My heart is not so small. And even if I did take it, would my wife let me live in peace?”

Gobar said nothing more. Ashamed, he unloaded his luggage and went off to buy his ticket.

: 20 :

Phagun had arrived, bringing with it the treasures of new life. The mango trees, with both hands, were spreading the fragrance of their blossoms, and the koel, hidden among the branches, was secretly gifting her song.

In the villages, the cane harvest had begun. The sun had not yet risen, but Hori was already in the fields. Dhaniya, Sona, and Rupa were hauling bundles of soaked cane from the pond to the field, while Hori chopped the stalks with his sickle. Now he worked as a laborer for Dattadin. No longer a farmer, but a hired hand. His old relationship with Dattadin, that of priest and patron, was gone; now it was master and laborer.

Dattadin came and scolded him, “Move your hands faster, Hori! At this rate, you won’t finish even by the end of the day.”

Hori replied, wounded pride in his voice, “I am working, Maharaj. I’m not sitting idle.”

Dattadin was harsh with his laborers, which is why none stayed long in his employ. Hori knew his nature, but where else could he go?

The pandit stood before him and said, “There’s a difference in how you work. One way, the job is done in an hour; the other, you can’t finish a single load in a whole day.”

Swallowing this bitter draught, Hori began to work even harder. For months now, he had not eaten his fill. Most days, he survived on dry crusts. Even for the second meal, sometimes he ate only half his fill, sometimes nothing at all. He wished desperately that his hands would move faster, but his body was failing him. And on top of that, Dattadin was breathing down his neck. If only he could pause for a moment to catch his breath, he would feel refreshed—but how could he? The fear of rebuke loomed over him.

Dhaniya and the two girls, their saris soaked and muddied, came in carrying bundles of cane, dropped them, and paused to catch their breath. But Dattadin barked at Dhaniya, “What are you standing around for? Go, do your work. Money doesn’t come for free. You’ve brought only one load in all this time. At this rate, you won’t finish even by the end of the day.”

Dhaniya frowned and replied, “Will you not let us rest even for a moment, Maharaj? We are people too. Working for you hasn’t turned us into oxen. Try carrying a bundle on your own head, then you’ll know what it’s like.”

Dattadin grew angry. “I pay you to work, not to rest. If you want to rest—”

--- Page 168 ---
Then go home and take some rest.

Dhaniya was about to say something when Hori scolded her, “Why don’t you go, Dhaniya? Why are you arguing?”

Picking up her bundle, Dhaniya replied, “I am going, but you shouldn’t prod a moving ox with the goad.”

Datadeen’s eyes reddened with anger. “Seems your temper still hasn’t cooled, that’s why you’re left wanting for every grain.”

Why should Dhaniya stay silent? “I don’t go begging at your door.”

Datadeen retorted in a sharp tone, “If this goes on, you’ll end up begging too.”

Dhaniya had a ready answer, but Sona pulled her away towards the pond, or the quarrel would have escalated. Yet, out of earshot, she let out the burning in her heart, “You beg, you who belong to a beggar’s caste. We are laborers; wherever we work, we’ll earn our few coins.”

Sona reproached her, “Amma, let it go. You never look at the time, always ready to fight over every little thing.”

Hori, like a man possessed, kept raising the sickle high above his head, chopping the sugarcane stalks into piles. It was as if a fire raged inside him. Some supernatural strength had come over him. The water accumulated over generations within him now seemed to turn to steam, giving him a blind, mechanical power. Darkness began to cloud his eyes. His head spun like a top. Yet his hands moved with the rhythm of a machine, tireless, unceasing. Sweat streamed down his body, spittle frothed from his mouth, his head throbbed with a pounding sound, but it was as if some spirit had possessed him!

Suddenly, a dense darkness enveloped his eyes. It felt as if he was sinking into the earth. He tried to steady himself, stretching his hands into the void, and lost consciousness. The sickle slipped from his hand, and he fell face down to the ground.

At that very moment, Dhaniya arrived, carrying a bundle of sugarcane. She saw a group of men standing around Hori. One ploughman was saying to Datadeen, “Malik, you shouldn’t say such things that wound a man’s heart. A man dies only when his spirit is broken.”

Dhaniya flung down her bundle and ran to Hori like a madwoman, cradling his head in her lap and began to wail, “Where are you going, leaving me behind? Sona, run and bring water, and go tell Sobha that your father is in a bad way. Oh God! Where shall I go now? Whose will I be? Who will call me Dhaniya now...?”

Lala Pateshwari came running, speaking with affectionate sternness, “What are you doing, Dhaniya? Get a hold of yourself. Nothing has happened to Hori. He’s only fainted from the heat. He’ll come to his senses soon. If you let your heart break like this, how will you manage?”

Dhaniya clutched Pateshwari’s feet and wept, “What can I do, Lalaji? My heart won’t listen. God has taken everything from me. I bore it all, but now I can’t bear it anymore. Oh, my Hira!”

Sona brought water. Pateshwari sprinkled it on Hori’s face. Several men fanned him with their cloths. Hori’s body had grown cold. Even Pateshwari began to worry, but he kept encouraging Dhaniya.

Dhaniya, restless, said, “This has never happened before, Lala, never.”
