Pateshwari asked, “Did he eat anything last night?”

--- Page 169 ---
Dhaniya said, “Yes, I had made rotis, but are you unaware of what we have been going through these days? For months, we haven’t had a full meal. I keep telling you, work carefully, take care of yourself, but rest is simply not written in our fate.”

Suddenly, Hori opened his eyes and glanced around with a fleeting look.

Dhaniya seemed to come alive. Overwhelmed, she threw her arms around his neck and said, “How are you feeling now? My soul had nearly left my body.”

Hori replied in a feeble voice, “I’m better. I don’t know what came over me.”

Dhaniya, her words soaked in affection and reproach, said, “You have no strength left in your body, yet you work as if you have a life to spare. It was the girls’ fate, otherwise you would have drowned us all.”

Pateshwari laughed and said, “Dhaniya was weeping and wailing.”

Hori asked anxiously, “Did you really cry, Dhaniya?”

Pushing Pateshwari aside, Dhaniya said, “Let him babble. Ask him why he left his papers and came running home?”

Pateshwari teased, “You were the one crying, calling out for Hira. Now you’re too shy to admit it. You were beating your chest.”

Hori looked at Dhaniya with moist eyes. “She’s a fool, what else? Who knows what happiness she wants to keep me alive to see.”

Two men supported Hori and brought him home, laying him on the cot. Datadeen was grumbling that the sowing was getting delayed, but Matadeen was not so heartless. He ran home and brought hot milk, and also fetched a bottle of rosewater. After drinking the milk, life seemed to return to Hori.

Just then, Gobar appeared, carrying his belongings on a laborer’s head.

The village dogs at first ran toward him, barking, then began wagging their tails. Rupa exclaimed, “Bhaiya has come!” and ran clapping her hands. Sona too took a few steps forward, but suppressed her excitement within herself. In the span of a year, her youth had become even more reserved. Jhunia, too, stood at the doorway, her face veiled.

Gobar touched his parents’ feet and lifted Rupa into his arms with affection. Dhaniya blessed him and pressed his head to her chest, as if she had received the reward of her motherhood. Her heart swelled with pride. Today, she was a queen. Even in these tattered circumstances, she was a queen. Let anyone look into her eyes, see her face, her heart, her gait—any queen would be humbled. Gobar had grown so tall, and dressed so decently, he looked like a fine young man. Dhaniya had never harbored any ill omen in her heart. She always believed Gobar was safe and happy. Now, seeing him with her own eyes, it was as if she had found the lost jewel in the dust and turmoil of her life. But Hori turned his face away.

Gobar asked, “What’s wrong with Dada, Amma?”

Dhaniya did not want to sadden him with the troubles of the house. She said, “It’s nothing, son, just a little headache. Come, take off your clothes, wash your hands and face. Where have you been all these days? Is this any way to run away from home? And not even a single letter? After a whole year, you finally remembered us. Our eyes grew weary waiting for you. I kept hoping for the day I’d see you again. Some said you’d run off to Mirzapur, others said you’d gone to Damra Island. Hearing all this, my heart would shrivel up. Where have you been all this time?”

--- Page 170 ---
Gobar said shyly, “I didn’t go far, Amma. I was right here in Lucknow.”

“And even being so close, you didn’t write a single letter?”

Meanwhile, Sona and Rupa were inside, opening Gobar’s luggage and sorting through his things, but Jhunia stood apart, at a distance. Today, a playful flush of pride colored her face. After all that Gobar had done to her, today she would have her revenge. Like a moneylender eager to recover even those rupees he had written off as bad debt, she was ready to settle her score.

The child was grabbing at the things, wanting to put everything into his mouth, but Jhunia would not let him down from her lap.

Sona said, “Bhaiya has brought a mirror and comb for you, Bhabhi!”

Jhunia replied with indifference, “I don’t want a mirror or a comb. Let him keep them for himself.”

Rupa took out a shiny cap for the child. “Oh! This is Chunnu’s cap.” She placed it on the child’s head.

Jhunia took off the cap and threw it aside. Suddenly, seeing Gobar enter, she picked up the child and went into the inner room. Gobar saw all his things spread out. His heart longed to go to Jhunia first and beg her forgiveness, but he did not have the courage to go inside. He sat down where he was and began handing things to Rupa. But Rupa was sulking because there were no slippers for her, and Sona started teasing her, “What will you do with slippers? Go play with your doll. We don’t cry when we see your doll, so why do you cry when you see my slippers?” 

Dhaniya took it upon herself to distribute the sweets. After so many days, her son had returned home safe and sound. She would share the news and the sweets with the whole village. One gulab jamun for Rupa was like a cumin seed in a camel’s mouth—she wanted the whole pot set before her so she could jump and eat to her heart’s content.

Now the trunk was opened, and out came the saris. All had borders, like those worn in Lala Pateshwari’s house, but they were very light. How long would such fine saris last? Let the rich wear as fine saris as they like—their women have nothing to do but sit and sleep. Here, there is field and farm work to be done. Ah! For Hori, there was not only a dhoti but also a scarf.

Dhaniya said happily, “You did well, son! His scarf was completely worn to threads.”

By now, Gobar had taken in the condition of the household. Dhaniya’s sari was patched in several places. Sona’s sari was torn at the head, her hair peeking through. Rupa’s dhoti was fringed all around. All their faces were rough, not a sign of softness on anyone’s body. Everywhere he looked, poverty reigned.

The girls were delighted with the saris. Dhaniya’s concern turned to her son’s meal. There was a little barley flour saved for the evening meal, but that was just enough for a thin gruel. But Gobar was no longer the same Gobar—would he eat barley flour now? Who knows what he had eaten and drunk in the city? She went to Dulari’s shop and borrowed wheat flour, rice, and ghee. For a month, the shopkeeper’s wife had not given her a single thing on credit, but today she did not even ask once when the money would be paid.

She asked, “Gobar must have brought back a lot of money, right?”

Dhaniya replied, “Nothing’s been opened yet, Didi! I haven’t thought it proper to say anything yet. Yes—”

--- Page 171 ---
He has brought bordered saris for everyone. By your blessings, I have returned safely; for me, that is enough.

Dulari blessed him, “May God keep you safe wherever you are. What more does a mother want? The boy is sensible and not wasteful like other youngsters. If you can’t return our money now, at least pay the interest. The burden keeps growing day by day.”

Meanwhile, Sona was dressing Chunnu in his frock, cap, and shoes, making him into a little king. The child preferred to play with these things in his hands rather than wear them. Inside, Gobar and Jhunia were engaged in a drama of reconciliation.

Jhunia looked at him with eyes full of reproach and said, “You brought me here and then left for a distant land. No word, no news—whether I lived or died. Only after a year have you remembered me. What a deceiver you are! I kept thinking you were right behind me, but you flew away and returned only after a year. Who can trust men? You must have had your eye on someone else. You thought, ‘Let there be one woman at home, and another outside as well.’”

Gobar defended himself, “Jhunia, I swear before God, I have never even looked at another woman. I did run away from home out of shame and fear, but not for a single moment did you leave my thoughts. Now I have decided to take you with me—that’s why I’ve come. Your family must have been very angry?”

“Dada was ready to kill me.”

“Really?”

“All three of them came here. Amma scolded them so fiercely they were left speechless. But they took away our two bullocks.”

“So much force, and Dada said nothing?”

“How could Dada fight everyone alone? The villagers wouldn’t let them take the bullocks, but Dada gave in out of goodness—what could the others do?”

“So how is the farming going these days?”

“All the farming has fallen apart. A little is left in partnership with Pandit Maharaj. We didn’t even sow any sugarcane.”

At that moment, Gobar had two hundred rupees tucked into his waistband. Even otherwise, his temper was not mild; hearing this, his blood boiled.

He said, “Then I’ll go and settle matters with them first. How dare they take bullocks from my door? This is robbery, open robbery! All three of them will go to jail for three years. If they don’t return them, I’ll take them to court. I’ll break their pride completely.”

He was about to storm out in this fury when Jhunia caught his arm and said, “Go if you must, but what’s the hurry? Rest a while, eat something. The whole day is ahead. There were big panchayats here. The panchayat fined us eighty rupees, and thirty maunds of grain on top of that. That’s what made things even worse.”

Sona came in, having dressed the child in his clothes and shoes. Wearing them, he truly looked like a little king. Gobar took him in his arms, but at this moment, he found no joy in the child’s affection. His blood was boiling, and the money at his waist only stoked the fire. He would settle accounts with each and every one of them. By what right had the panchayat fined him? Who were they to interfere in his affairs? So he had taken in a woman—what business was it of the panchayat’s fathers? If for this reason they brought a criminal case against him, what harm had he done to the panchayat’s ancestors? If for this reason they brought a criminal case—

--- Page 172 ---
If she does, people will end up in shackles. The entire household has been thrown into chaos. What do these people think of her?

The child stirred slightly in her lap, then suddenly let out a loud wail, as if he had seen something frightening.

Jhunia took the child from her lap and said, “Now go and bathe. What are you lost in thought about? If you start fighting with everyone here, you won’t last a single day. The one who has money is the great man, the good man. If you have no money, everyone lords it over you.”

“It was my own foolishness that I ran away from home. Didn’t I see how someone would snatch away a single paisa?”

“You’ve breathed the air of the city, that’s why you talk like this. Otherwise, why would you have run away from home?”

“I just feel like picking up a stick and beating Pateshwari, Datadin, Jhinguri—all those scoundrels—and wringing the money out of their bellies.”

“Seems you’re burning with the fever of money. Come on, let’s see—show me what you’ve earned in all this time.”

She put her hand on Gobar’s waist. Gobar stood up and said, “What have I earned yet? Once you come with me, then I’ll earn. It took me a whole year just to learn the ways of the city.”

“Only if Amma lets me go, right?”

“Why wouldn’t Amma let you? What’s it to her?”

“Oh! I won’t go without her consent. You just left and went your way, but who did I have here? If she hadn’t let me into the house, where would I have gone? As long as I live, I’ll sing her praises. And will you keep wandering in foreign lands forever?”

“And what would I do sitting here? Work and die—that’s all there is here. If a man has a little sense and isn’t afraid of hard work, he can’t starve there. Here, sense is of no use at all. Why is Dada sulking?”

“Count your blessings that he just sulked and let it go. You caused such a big uproar—if he’d caught you in his anger, he would have turned red with rage.”

“So he must have scolded you plenty too?”

“Never, not even by mistake. Amma was upset at first, but Dada never said a word. When he calls me, it’s with great affection. If I have a headache, he gets restless. When I see my own father, I think Dada is a god. He always tells Amma, ‘Don’t say anything to the daughter-in-law.’ He’s scolded you hundreds of times for leaving me at home and wandering off. These days, we’re short of every paisa. The money from the sugarcane just vanished. Now we have to work as laborers. Today, poor Dada fainted in the field. There was a great commotion. He’s been lying there ever since.”

After washing his face and hands and combing his hair neatly, Gobar set out to conquer the village. He went to both his uncles’ houses and greeted them. Then he met his friends. There was no great change in the village—except that Pateshwari had built a new sitting room, and Jhinguri Singh had dug a new well at his doorway. In Gobar’s heart, the spirit of rebellion beat even louder. Everyone he met treated him with respect, and the young men made him their hero, ready to go with him to Lucknow. In just a year, how much he had changed!

Suddenly, he came upon Jhinguri Singh bathing at his well. Gobar passed by, but neither greeted him nor spoke a word. He wanted to show the Thakur that he didn’t care for him at all.

Jhinguri Singh himself called out, “When did you come, Gobar? Hope you’re well? Where were you staying in Lucknow?”

--- Page 173 ---
Gobur replied with swagger, “I did not go to Lucknow to be a servant. Service is servitude. I was in trade.”

Thakur looked him up and down with curious eyes. “How much did you make a day?”

Gobur brandished his knife like a spear and answered, “About two and a half to three rupees. Sometimes, if luck favored me, I’d make four. Never more than that.”

Even after much scraping and saving, Jhinguri could never earn more than twenty-five or thirty rupees a month. And here was this rustic lad, making a hundred rupees. His head bowed in shame. Now, with what authority could he assert himself over him? By caste, he was certainly superior, but who cared for caste anymore? This was not a moment for rivalry, but for supplication—perhaps something could be gained from him. He said, “That’s not a small earning, son, if you can manage your expenses. In the village, you can’t even get three annas. If you could find some work for Bhawaniya—my eldest son—I’d send him along. He’s neither educated nor literate, always up to some mischief. If there’s a vacancy for a clerk, let me know, or take him along with you. He’s your friend. Even if he’s not clever, it doesn’t matter. Just see if there’s a little extra earning.”

Gobur replied with a proud laugh, “It’s this taste for illicit income that ruins a man, Thakur. But we people have become so spoiled that unless we cheat, we can’t fill our bellies. In Lucknow, one can find a clerk’s job, but every moneylender wants an honest, vigilant man. I could tie Bhawani to someone’s neck, but if he’s caught with his hand in the till, they’ll come after me. In this world, it’s not learning that’s valued, but honesty.”

With this slap, Gobur moved on. Jhinguri was left seething inside. The boy speaks with such arrogance, as if he’s an incarnation of virtue itself.

In the same manner, Gobur gave Datadin a taste of his tongue. Datadin was about to eat, and seeing Gobur, he said cheerfully, “Are you well, Gobur? I heard you’ve found a good place there. Can’t you get Matadin in by some means? There’s nothing to do here but sit around chewing bhang.”

Gobur retorted, “What is your house lacking, Maharaj? Whichever patron’s door you stand at, you’ll get something. You take at birth, at death, at weddings, at funerals. You farm, you lend, you broker deals, and if someone makes a mistake, you slap a fine and loot his house. Isn’t all that enough to fill your belly? What will you do with so much wealth—have you found a way to take it with you?”

Datadin saw how brazenly Gobur spoke, as if he’d forgotten all respect and decorum. Perhaps he didn’t yet realize that his own father was in Datadin’s debt. It’s true, a small stream floods quickly. But Datadin did not let his face betray any anger. Like elders who laugh even when children pull their mustaches, he took the rebuke in good humor and said playfully, “You’ve become quite clever after breathing the air of Lucknow, Gobur! Come, what have you brought back? Show us. Truly, Gobur, we missed you a lot. Will you stay a while now?”

“Yes, I’ll stay for some days. I have to make a claim against those panchayat members who, under the pretense of a fine, swallowed up my hundred and fifty rupees. Let’s see who dares to cut me off from water and fire, and how the community tries to outcaste me.”

With this challenge, he strode ahead. His bravado had set his young admirers aflame. One said, “File a complaint, Gobur bhaiya! Chudda is a black snake—no charm can cure his bite. You gave them a good scolding. Warm up the patwari’s ears too. The old man’s a sly one. There’s fire between father and son.”

--- Page 174 ---
Set fire, set fire between brothers. He conspires with the steward to cut the throats of the tenants. Plow your own fields last, first plow his. Water your own crops later, first water his.  

Gobar twisted his mustache and said, "Why are you telling me, brother? Do you think I’ve forgotten in a year? If I didn’t have to live here, I’d make each and every one of them dance to my tune. This Holi, celebrate with great pomp, and when you put on the Holi play, soak all these bigwigs thoroughly."  

Plans for Holi began to take shape. There would be plenty of bhang—milky and salty—and along with the colors, soot would be prepared too, to smear on the faces of the village elders. Who can object during Holi? Then the play would be performed and the panchayat would be thoroughly mocked. Money was no concern—Gobar had returned with his earnings.  

After eating, Gobar went to meet Bhola. He would not rest until he brought back his pair of oxen and tied them at his own door. He was ready to fight and die for it.  

Hori said in a pleading voice, "Don’t escalate the quarrel, son! Bhola took the cow, may God bless him, but he was owed the money anyway."  

Gobar, agitated, replied, "Dada, don’t interfere. His cow was worth fifty. Our cow cost a hundred and fifty. We plowed with her for three years. Even then, she was worth at least a hundred. If he wanted his money, he could have filed a claim, got a decree, done whatever he wished. Why did he come to our door and untie our oxen? And what can I say about you? On one side, we lose the cow; on the other, we pay a hundred and fifty rupees in fines. This is the reward for owning a cow! If he had tried to take the oxen in front of me, I would have shown him. I would have laid all three of them on the ground right here, and I wouldn’t have spoken a word to the panchayat. Let’s see who would dare to ostracize me from the biradari. But you just sat there, watching."  

Hori hung his head like a guilty man, but how could Dhaniya tolerate such injustice? She said, "Son, you’re being unreasonable too. If our water and fire were cut off, could we survive in the village? We have a young daughter sitting at home—don’t we have to arrange her marriage? In life and death, a man needs his community..."  

Gobar interrupted, "We had water and fire, we had respect in the biradari, then why wasn’t I married? Tell me! Because there was no bread in the house. If you have money, neither water and fire nor caste and community matter. The world runs on money; no one cares about water and fire."  

Hearing the child cry, Dhaniya went inside, and Gobar too left the house. Hori sat, lost in thought. It was as if the boy’s mind had opened up. How bluntly he spoke! His sharp reasoning had defeated Hori’s sense of duty and morality.  

Suddenly Hori asked him, "Shall I come with you?"  

"I’m not going to pick a fight, Dada, don’t worry. The law is on my side, why would I start a quarrel?"  

"Is there any harm if I come too?"  

"Yes, there’s a lot of harm. You’ll ruin everything."  

Hori fell silent, and Gobar went on his way.  

Barely five minutes had passed when Dhaniya came out, holding the child, and said, "Has Gobar gone, alone? I wonder if God will ever grant you any sense. Do you think Bhola will give up the cow so easily? All three of them will pounce on him like hawks. May God protect him. Now whom should I tell to run after Gobar and stop him? I give up on you."  

Hori picked up his stick from the corner and hurried after Gobar. He ran out of the village. A faint line could be seen merging with the horizon. In just that short time...

--- Page 175 ---
He was gone. Hori’s soul began to reproach him. Why hadn’t he stopped Gobar? If only he had scolded him, told him not to go to Bhola’s house, Gobar would never have gone. And now, even if he tried, he could not run after him. Defeated, he sat down right there and said, “Protect him, Mahavir Swami.”

When Gobar reached the village, he saw a few men sitting under the banyan tree, gambling. Seeing him, the men thought he was a police constable. They quickly gathered their cowries and fled, when suddenly Jangi recognized him and said, “Oh, this is Gobardhan!”

Gobar saw Jangi peeking from behind the tree. He called out, “Don’t be afraid, Jangi bhaiya, it’s me. Ram-Ram! I just arrived today. I thought I’d come and meet everyone—who knows when I’ll return again. By your blessings, brother, I’ve been doing very well. The king I work for told me, ‘If you find one or two good men, bring them along. We need them for watchman’s duty.’ I said, ‘Sarkar, I’ll bring you such men that they’d sooner give up their lives than abandon their post. If you wish, come with me. It’s a good place.’”

Seeing Gobar’s fine appearance, Jangi was filled with envy. He had never even owned a pair of leather shoes, and here Gobar stood in shining boots. A clean, striped shirt, neatly combed hair—he looked every bit the babu sahib. There was a vast difference between the ragged Gobar of old and this refined Gobar. Whatever resentment remained in Jangi’s heart had already faded with time, and what little was left now vanished completely. He was a gambler by nature, addicted to ganja, and it was always a struggle to get money at home. His mouth began to water. He said, “Why wouldn’t I come? I’m just sitting here swatting flies. How much will I get paid?”

With great confidence, Gobar replied, “Don’t worry about that. Everything is in our own hands. Whatever you want, it can be arranged. I thought, why look outside when we have our own people at home?”

Jangi asked eagerly, “What kind of work will I have to do?”

“Either watchman’s duty or collecting debts. Collecting debts is the best. You go to the debtor, make a deal, and then tell the master he wasn’t home. If you like, you can easily make eight annas a day.”

“Is there a place to stay?”

“There’s no shortage of space. There’s a whole palace. Running water, electricity, nothing is lacking. Where’s Kamta? Has he gone somewhere?”

“He’s gone to deliver milk. No one lets me go to the market. They say, ‘You just smoke ganja.’ But now I smoke much less, brother—though I still need a couple of coins a day. Don’t say anything to Kamta. I’ll come with you.”

“Yes, yes, come without worry. After Holi.”

“Then it’s settled.”

Talking thus, the two of them reached Bhola’s doorway. Bhola was sitting, spinning twine. Gobar rushed forward and touched his feet, his throat truly choked with emotion. He said, “Kaka, forgive me for any mistakes I have made.”

Bhola stopped spinning and replied in a stony voice, “Gobar, you acted in such a way that even if I cut off your head, it would not be a sin. But now that you have come to my door, what can I say? God will punish you for what you did to me. When did you arrive?”

Gobar, with plenty of embellishment, recounted the story of his good fortune and asked Bhola’s permission to take Jangi with him. To Bhola, it was as if an unexpected blessing had come. Jangi, who was always causing some trouble at home...

--- Page 176 ---
He kept at it. If he goes out, at least he’ll earn a few coins. Even if he gives nothing to anyone, at least he’ll bear his own burden.

Govar said, “No, Kaka, if God wills and he manages to stay there, in a year or two he’ll become a man.”

“Yes, if he manages to stay there.”

“When trouble falls on one’s head, a man learns to take care of himself.”

“So, when are you thinking of leaving?”

“I’ll go after Holi. Once I’ve set the farming here in order again, I’ll be at ease.”

“Tell Hori to sit and chant Ram-Ram now.”

“I do tell him, but when does he ever sit still?”

“You must know some vaid there. This cough is troubling me a lot. If possible, send me some medicine.”

“There’s a renowned vaid living next door to me. I’ll tell him your condition and send you the medicine he prepares. Does the cough get worse at night or during the day?”

“No, son, at night. I can’t sleep. If there’s any way there, I’ll come and live there too. Here, nothing matters anymore.”

“The kind of work there is in Lucknow, Kaka, what is there here? Here, no one even asks for ten seers of milk for a rupee. You have to beg the halwais to take it. There, if you want, you can sell maunds of milk in an hour at five or six seers to the rupee.”

Jangi had gone to make milk sherbet for Govar. Seeing they were alone, Bhola said, “And, brother, now I’m tired of this entanglement. You see Jangi’s condition. Kamta takes the milk. I have to do all the feeding, watering, tying and untying. Now I just wish I could eat a meal in peace somewhere and lie down. How long can I keep sighing? Every day, quarrels and fights. How many people’s feet can I massage? The cough comes, I can’t get up at night, but no one even asks for a single lota of water. The peg for the cattle broke, but no one cares. It’ll only get fixed if I do it myself.”

With affection, Govar said, “Come to Lucknow, Kaka. Sell five seers of milk, cash in hand. I know many rich men there. I’ll take responsibility for selling a maund of milk. I have a tea shop too. I myself take ten seers of milk daily. You won’t have any trouble.”

Jangi brought the milk sherbet. Govar drank a glass and said, “Kaka, if you just sit at my tea shop morning and evening, you’ll earn a rupee a day, no doubt.”

After a minute, Bhola said hesitantly, “Son, in anger a man becomes blind. I brought your cow here. Take her with you. What farming is there here anyway?”

“I’ve already arranged for a new cow, Kaka!”

“No, no, what will you do with a new cow? Take this one.”

“Then I’ll send you the money.”

“The money isn’t going anywhere, son, it’s right here at home. It’s just the farce of the biradari. Otherwise, what difference is there between you and me? To tell the truth, I should have been happy that Jhunia is in a good home, she’s a fire! And I became thirsty for her blood.”

In the evening, when Govar left, the cow was with him, along with two pots of curd.
