:21:

In the villages, for six months of the year, some festival or another fills the air with the sounds of drums and manjiras. From a month before Holi to a month after, the air is alive with the songs of Phag. As soon as Ashadh arrives, the singing of Alha begins, and in Sawan and Bhadon, the Kajliyan are sung. After the Kajliyan, the singing of the Ramayana commences. Semari is no exception. The threats of the moneylender and the words of the zamindar’s steward cannot disrupt these festivities. There may be no grain in the house, no clothes on the body, not a coin in the pocket—no one cares. The joy of living cannot be suppressed; one cannot live without laughter.

Thus, during Holi, the main center for singing and music was Nokheram’s chaupal. There, bhang was prepared, colors were thrown, and dancing took place. In this festival, the steward sahib would spend five or ten rupees. Who else had the means to host such a gathering at their own door?

But this time, Gobar has drawn all the young men of the village to his own doorstep, and Nokheram’s chaupal lies empty. At Gobar’s house, bhang is being ground, betel leaves are being prepared, colors are being mixed, carpets are spread out, songs are being sung—and the chaupal is shrouded in silence. The bhang is there, but who will grind it? The drums and manjiras are all present, but who will sing? Whoever you see is rushing towards Gobar’s house. Here, the bhang is fragrant with rosewater, saffron, and almonds. Yes, yes—a whole seer of almonds, brought by Gobar himself. One sip, and your body is refreshed, your eyes wide open. He has brought the finest khamira tobacco, especially from Biswan. Even the colors are scented with kewra. He knows how to earn money, and he knows how to spend it. If you hoard it, who will see? This is the true glory of wealth. And it’s not just the bhang—he is also the leader of all the singers, and in the village, there is no shortage of dancers, singers, or actors. Shobha imitates the lame so well that no one can match him, and when it comes to mimicking voices, he has no equal. Name anyone, and he’ll speak in their voice—man or animal. Girdhar is unmatched in mimicry. He can imitate the vakil, the patwari, the thanedar, the peon, the seth—everyone. True, the poor fellow doesn’t have the proper props, but this time Gobar has arranged everything for him; his performances will be worth seeing.

The news spread so far that from evening itself, crowds began to gather to watch the spectacle. Groups of spectators started arriving from neighboring villages. By ten o’clock, three or four thousand people had gathered, and when Girdhar appeared, dressed as Jhinguri Singh with his troupe, there was not even standing room left. The same bald head, the same large mustache, and the same paunch. He sits, eating his meal, while the first Thakurain sits fanning him.

The Thakur, looking at the Thakurain with playful eyes, says, “Even now, you have such youthfulness that if any young man were to see you, he would be smitten.” The Thakurain, swelling with pride, replies, “That’s why you brought a new bride.”

“I brought her only to serve you. How could she ever compare to you?”

The younger wife overhears this and walks away with a pout.

In the next scene, the Thakur is lying on a cot, and the younger daughter-in-law sits on the floor, turned away. The Thakur, repeatedly and unsuccessfully trying to turn her face toward him, says, “Why are you angry with me, my darling?”

“Go to your darling, wherever she is. I am just a servant, here to wait upon others.”

--- Page 178 ---
"I have come."

"You are my queen. That old woman is there to serve you."

The first Thakurain overhears this, and, grabbing a broom, storms into the house and lands several blows on them. Thakur Sahib flees for his life.

Then came the second skit, in which the Thakur, after drawing up a document for ten rupees, hands over only five, deducting the rest for gifts, the written deed, customary fees, and interest.

The peasant comes and clutches the Thakur’s feet, weeping. Only with great difficulty does the Thakur agree to give him the money. When the document is written and the five rupees are placed in the debtor’s hand, he, bewildered, asks:

"But these are only five, master!"

"Not five, ten. Count them at home."

"No, sahib, these are five."

"One rupee for the gift, was it not?"

"Yes, sahib."

"One for the paper?"

"Yes, sahib."

"One for the customary fee?"

"Yes, sahib."

"One for the interest?"

"Yes, sahib."

"Five in cash—does that not make ten?"

"Yes, sahib! Now, please keep these five as well, from my side."

"What kind of fool are you?"

"No, sahib, one rupee is a gift for the younger Thakurain, one for the elder Thakurain. One rupee for the younger Thakurain’s betel leaves, one for the elder Thakurain’s betel leaves. The last one left, that is for your funeral rites."

In this way, Nokheram, Pateshwari, and Dattadin—each in turn—were all lampooned. Even if the jokes were not new and the skits were old, Girdhar’s manner was so comical, and the audience so simple-hearted, that they laughed at even the slightest thing. The farce continued all night, and the oppressed hearts, finding revenge in their imagination, were delighted. When the final skit ended, the crows were already cawing.

At dawn, whoever you met had the songs of the night, the same skits, the same jests on their lips. The village headmen became the butt of the show. Wherever they went, a few boys would follow behind, repeating the same lines. Jhinguri Singh, being a jester himself, took it in good humor, but Pateshwari had a bad habit of getting annoyed. And Pandit Dattadin was so short-tempered that he was ready to fight; he was used to being respected by all. As for the karinda, there was no question— even the Rai Sahib would bow his head upon seeing him. That he should be mocked like this, and in his own village, was unbearable for him. If he had possessed the power of a Brahmin’s curse, he would have incinerated these scoundrels. He would have uttered such a curse that all their interest would be wiped out, but in this Kaliyug, curses have lost their power. So he drew out another weapon. He came to Hori’s door, eyes blazing, and said—Now I will...

--- Page 179 ---
Won’t you come to work, Hori? You’re well enough now. You don’t think about how much loss I’ve suffered because of you.

Gobur had gone to bed late. He had just woken up and was rubbing his eyes as he stepped outside, when he heard Datadeen’s voice. Forget about offering his respects, he answered back with even more insolence—

He won’t work for you anymore. We have our own sugarcane to sow.

Datadeen, chewing his tobacco, said—How can he not work? You can’t leave work in the middle of the year. If you want to leave, do it in Jeth; leave if you must. But not before that.

Gobur yawned and said—He hasn’t signed himself into your servitude. He worked as long as he wished. Now he doesn’t want to, so he won’t. No one can force him.

“So Hori won’t work?”

“No.”

“Then return my money with interest. Three years’ interest comes to a hundred rupees. Including the principal, that’s two hundred. I thought I’d deduct three rupees a month as interest, but if you don’t wish to work, then don’t. Just give me my money back. If you want to act like a Dhanna Seth, then behave like one.”

Hori said to Datadeen—When have I ever refused your service, Maharaj? But we have our own sugarcane waiting to be sown.

Gobur scolded his father—What service, and whose service? No one here is anyone’s servant. We are all equals. What a joke! You lend someone a hundred rupees and then make him work for you all his life as interest, while the principal remains untouched! This isn’t moneylending, it’s bloodsucking.

“Then give me my money, brother, why quarrel? I charge others a full rupee in interest. I gave it to you at half a rupee, thinking you’re from the same village.”

“We’ll pay one rupee per hundred. Not a penny more. Take it if you want, or else get it from the court. One rupee per hundred is no small amount.”

“Seems like money has gone to your head.”

“It goes to the heads of those who turn one into ten. We are laborers. Our heat leaves us through sweat. I remember, you gave thirty rupees for a bullock. That became a hundred. And now a hundred has become two hundred. This is how you people have looted the farmers and turned them into laborers, while you became the owners of their land. Thirty becomes two hundred! Is there no limit? How many years ago was that, Dada?”

Hori said in a pleading voice—It must have been eight or nine years.

Gobur, placing his hand on his chest, said—In nine years, thirty rupees become two hundred! At the rate of one rupee per hundred, how much should it be?

He scratched out the calculation on the ground with a stick and said—In ten years, thirty-six rupees. Add the principal, that’s sixty-six. Take seventy rupees. Not a penny more will I give.

Datadeen, turning to Hori, said—Do you hear Hori, Gobur’s verdict? I should leave my two hundred and take seventy, or else go to court. If this is how things are done, how will the world go on? And you just sit there and listen. But remember, I am a Brahmin. If you swallow my money, you’ll never find peace. Even if I leave these seventy rupees and don’t go to court, just wait. If I am truly a Brahmin, I’ll get my full two hundred out of you. And you’ll come to my door, hands folded, to return it.

Datadeen, fuming, turned and left. Gobur remained seated where he was. But inside Hori, a storm of dharma was raging. If the money belonged to a Thakur or a Baniya, he wouldn’t have worried so much. But a Brahmin’s money! If even a single paisa of it was withheld, it would break his bones to get it back. God forbid that—

--- Page 180 ---
May the wrath of a Brahmin fall upon someone—there remains not a drop of water-bearer in his lineage, not a soul to light a lamp in his house. Hori’s conscience, fearful of religion, grew troubled. He rushed forward, clasped Panditji’s feet, and pleaded in a voice choked with anguish, “Maharaj, as long as I live, I will repay every single paisa I owe you. Do not heed the words of the boys. The matter is between you and me. Who is he to interfere?”

Datadeen’s tone softened a little. “Just look at his insolence! He says, ‘Take seventy rupees for two hundred, or go to court.’ He hasn’t yet tasted the air of the courts, that’s why. Once he falls into someone’s clutches, all this bravado will vanish. Spent just a few days in the city, and now he acts like a tyrant.”

“I say, Maharaj, I will repay every paisa I owe you.”

“Then from tomorrow, you’ll have to come work for me.”

“I have to harvest my own sugarcane, Maharaj. Otherwise, I would work for you.”

“I have to harvest my own sugarcane, Maharaj. Otherwise, I would work for you.”

When Datadeen left, Gobar looked at Hori with eyes full of scorn and said, “Went to appease the deity, did you? It’s you people who have spoiled these savs. Gave him thirty rupees, now he’ll take two hundred, and on top of that, he’ll scold you, make you work for him, and work you to death.”

Hori, standing by what he believed to be right, said, “One should never forsake righteousness, son. Each man’s deeds are his own. The interest on which we took the money, that we must pay. And then, he is a Brahmin. Will we be able to digest a Brahmin’s money? Such wealth is only digested by people like them.”

Gobar frowned. “Who’s telling you to forsake righteousness? And who’s telling you to swallow a Brahmin’s money? I’m only saying we won’t pay so much interest. The moneylenders in the market take twelve annas per hundred. You take one rupee. Or do you want to rob someone?”

“And what if he’s hurt by it?”

“Let him be. Should we dig our own graves out of fear of hurting him?”

“Son, as long as I am alive, let me walk my own path. When I am gone, do as you wish.”

“Then you pay it. I won’t strike my own foot with an axe. It was my stupidity to speak up between you two. You took the money, you pay it. Why should I risk my life?”

Saying this, Gobar went inside. Jhunia asked, “Why did you quarrel with father so early in the morning?”

Gobar narrated the whole incident and concluded, “This is how their burden of debt keeps growing. How long can I keep paying? They’ve spent their lives filling others’ homes. Why should I fall into the pit they’ve dug? He didn’t ask me before taking the loan. Nor did he take it for me. I am not responsible for it.”

Meanwhile, among the village elders, a plot was being hatched to put Gobar in his place. If this boy is not brought to heel, he will stir up chaos in the village. Ever since he became a peon, he’s grown insolent. Who knows where he learned so much law? He says, “I won’t pay more than one rupee interest per hundred. Take it if you want, otherwise go to court.” Last night, he gathered all the village boys and caused such a commotion. But even among the elders, there was no lack of envy. All were delighted at the ridicule of their equals. Pateshwari and Nokheram were talking. Pateshwari said, “But the savs know every detail of every household. They gave Jhinguri Singh such a thrashing, don’t even ask. Listening to the talk of the two Thakurainis, people laughed till they rolled on the ground.”

Nokheram, laughing heartily, said, “But the imitation was spot on. I’ve seen his younger begum several times…”

--- Page 181 ---
He saw the boys standing at the doorway, laughing and joking.

“And the elder Rani, with her kohl, vermilion, and red dye, always keeps herself looking young.”

“The two of them are always at odds, day and night. Jhinguri is utterly shameless. Anyone else would have gone mad by now.”

“I heard she did a terrible imitation of you. Had you locked up in the chamar’s house and beaten.”

“I escaped, but I’ll fix her by filing a claim for the unpaid rent. Then she’ll remember what it means to cross someone like me.”

“But hasn’t she already paid the rent?”

“But I haven’t given her a receipt, have I? What proof does she have that she’s paid? And who checks the accounts here anyway? I’ll send the bailiff today itself and summon her.”

Hori and Gobar were both irrigating the field to sow sugarcane. There had been little hope of cultivating sugarcane this season, so the field had lain fallow. But now that the oxen had arrived, why not plant sugarcane?

Yet the two of them were at odds, barely speaking or even looking at each other. Hori was driving the oxen, while Gobar was drawing water with the Persian wheel. Sona and Rupa were both running water through the field when they began to quarrel. The subject of their dispute was whether Jhinguri Singh’s younger Thakurain ate first and then fed her husband, or fed her husband first and then ate herself. Sona insisted that she ate first. Rupa disagreed.

Rupa argued, “If she eats first, then why isn’t she fat? Why is the Thakur fat? If the Thakur were to fall on her, the Thakurain would be crushed.”

Sona retorted, “You think eating well makes people fat? Eating well makes people strong, not fat. People get fat from eating grass and leaves.”

“So the Thakurain is stronger than the Thakur?”

“Of course! Just the other day, they had a fight, and the Thakurain pushed the Thakur so hard that his knees were scraped.”

“So you’ll eat first and then feed your husband too?”

“Why not?”

“But Amma always feeds Dada first.”

“That’s why Dada is always scolding her. I’ll become strong and keep my husband under control. Your husband will beat you, break your bones.”

Rupa, on the verge of tears, said, “Why would he beat me? I’ll never do anything to deserve a beating.”

“He won’t listen to anything. If you say even a word, he’ll hit you. He’ll skin you alive with his beatings.”

Angry, Rupa tried to tear Sona’s sari with her teeth, and when she failed, she started pinching her.

Sona teased her even more, “He’ll cut off your nose too.”

At this, Rupa bit her sister’s arm. Sona’s arm started to bleed. She shoved Rupa hard, and Rupa fell, then got up and started crying. Seeing the bite marks, Sona too began to cry.

Hearing their wailing, Gobar came over, furious, and gave each of them two hard blows. Both girls, still crying, left the field and headed home. The irrigation work came to a halt. This led to an argument between father and son.

--- Page 182 ---
A quarrel broke out.

Hori asked, "Who will water the fields? Why did you run off and not bring them back?"

"You are the one who has spoiled everyone."

"If you beat them like this, they will only become more shameless."

"Stop giving them two meals a day, and they will come to their senses."

"I am their father, not a butcher."

Once you stub your toe, for some reason it keeps getting hurt again and again. Sometimes the toe swells up and causes pain for months. The bond between father and son had been wounded in just this way, and now a third blow had fallen upon it.

Gobardhan went home and took Jhunia with him to water the fields. Jhunia went to the field, carrying her child.

Dhaniya and her two daughters watched silently. Even the mother found Gobar’s insolence hard to bear. If he had beaten Rupa, she would not have minded so much, but to strike a grown girl—this was unbearable for her.

That very night, Gobar decided to return to Lucknow. He could not stay here any longer. When he had no respect in his own home, why should he remain? He could not speak in matters of money and accounts. He had struck the girls just a little, and everyone had become so enraged, as if he were an outsider. Then why should he stay in this inn?

Both had finished their meal and stepped outside when Nokheram’s peon arrived and said, "Come, the karinda sahib has summoned you."

Hori replied with pride, "Why does he call me at night? I have already paid all my dues."

The peon said, "I have orders only to summon you. Whatever you wish to say, come and say it there."

Hori did not wish to go, but he had no choice. Gobar sat there, detached. Half an hour later, Hori returned and began to fill his chilam and smoke. Gobar could not restrain himself any longer. He asked, "Why did he call you?"

Hori replied in a hoarse voice, "I have paid every last paisa of the rent. Yet they say I still owe two years. Just the other day I sold the sugarcane and gave them twenty-five rupees right there, and today they claim I owe two years’ rent. I told them, I will not give a single coin more."

Gobar asked, "You must have a receipt, don’t you?"

"Where do they ever give receipts?"

"Then why do you give them money without a receipt?"

"How was I to know they would cheat me? This is all the result of your actions. You mocked them that night, and this is your punishment. You cannot live in water and make an enemy of the crocodile. With interest, they have made it seventy rupees in arrears. Where will that come from?"

Gobar defended himself, "If you had taken a receipt, I could have mocked them all I liked, and they couldn’t have touched a hair on your head. I don’t understand why you are not careful in money matters. If they won’t give a receipt, send the money by post. At most, you’ll lose a rupee or two in fees, but at least there wouldn’t be this kind of swindle."

"If you hadn’t started this fire, none of this would have happened. Now all the headmen are angry. Eviction—"

--- Page 183 ---
...are threatening us. God knows how we’ll get through this.”

“I’ll go and ask them.”

“You’ll just make things worse.”

“If I have to set fire to things, I’ll set fire to them. Let them try to evict us. I’ll make them swear on the Ganga-jal in court. You can sit here with your tail between your legs. I’ll fight this with my life. I don’t want a single paisa of anyone else’s, nor do I want to lose a single paisa of my own.”

He got up at once and went straight to Nokheram’s chaupal. He saw that all the village elders were gathered there, like a cabinet in session. As soon as they saw Gobar, everyone grew alert. The air was thick with the tension of conspiracy.

Gobar asked in a heated voice, “What is this, Karinda Sahib? Dada has paid up the rent till now, and yet you’re claiming two years’ arrears? What kind of trickery is this?”

Nokheram, reclining on his cushion, replied with feigned sorrow, “As long as Hori is here, I don’t want to discuss any dealings with you.”

Gobar said, wounded, “So I am nothing in my own house?”

“You may be everything in your own house. Here, you are nothing.”

“Very well, go ahead and file for eviction. In court, I’ll make you swear on the Ganga-jali that you received the money. I’ll bring a hundred witnesses from this very village to prove you never give receipts. The simple farmers here don’t speak up, so you think they’re all fools. Rai Sahib may scare everyone else in the village, but not me. I’ll tell the whole truth and see how you manage to extract money from me a second time.”

There was the force of truth in his words. Among the timid, even truth becomes mute. The same cement that turns to stone when laid on brick, turns to dust when laid on earth. Gobar’s fearless candor pierced the armor of injustice with which Nokheram’s feeble soul had clothed itself, imagining itself mighty.

Nokheram, as if trying to recall something, said, “Why are you getting so worked up? What’s there to get angry about? If Hori paid the money, it must be recorded somewhere. I’ll check the papers tomorrow. Now that you mention it, I do vaguely remember that perhaps Hori did pay. Don’t worry, if the money has come here, it can’t go anywhere else. You wouldn’t lie for a few rupees, and I won’t become rich from this money.”

Gobar came back from the chaupal and scolded Hori so harshly that the poor, self-serving old man was left on the verge of tears. “You’re worse than children, who start screaming at a cat’s meow. How many places am I supposed to protect you? I’m giving you seventy rupees. If Datadin comes, pay him and get a receipt. If you pay even a paisa more, don’t expect a single paisa from me again. I haven’t gone to the city just so you can keep getting robbed and I keep paying for it. I’m leaving tomorrow, but let me tell you this: don’t borrow a single paisa from anyone, and don’t lend anything to anyone. Mangru, Dulari, Datadin—all of them will charge a rupee per hundred as interest.”

Dhaniya, having finished her meal, came out as well. She said, “Why are you leaving so soon, son? Stay a few more days, help with the cane harvest, settle some accounts, then go.”

Gobar replied, with a touch of pride, “I’m losing two or three rupees a day here, do you understand? Here, at most, I earn four annas a day, and this time, I’ll be taking Jhunia with me as well...”

--- Page 184 ---
I will go. There, I have great difficulty finding food and drink.

Dhaniya said timidly, “As you wish, but how will she manage the house alone there? How will she take care of the child?”

“Should I look after the child, or think of my own convenience? I cannot tend the hearth anymore.”

“I am not stopping you from taking her, but living in a foreign land with wife and children, with no one ahead or behind, think how troublesome it will be.”

“In a foreign land, companions are found, Amma, and this is a world of self-interest—whoever shares your troubles for a few coins, he is your own. Empty-handed, even a mother’s brother does not ask after you.”

Dhaniya understood the barb. She felt as if fire blazed from head to toe. She said, “You have counted even your mother’s brother among those friends of money?”

“I see it with my own eyes.”

“You do not see. A mother’s brother’s heart is not so hard. Yes, it is true, as soon as boys start earning a few coins, they turn their eyes away from their mother’s brother. In this very village, not one or two, but ten or twenty examples I can give. For whom do mothers and brothers take loans and debts? For their sons and daughters, or for their own pleasure?”

“How do I know for whom you took a loan? I never saw a single paisa.”

“You grew so big without being raised?”

“What did you spend on raising me? When I was a child, you fed me milk. Then you left me to fend for myself like an orphan. Whatever everyone ate, I ate. There was no special milk for me, no butter set aside. And you want, and Dada wants, that I should repay all the debt, pay the rent, arrange the girls’ marriages—as if my whole life is meant just to pay your dues. I have my own wife and children too, don’t I?”

Dhaniya was struck silent. In a single moment, the gentle dream of her life seemed to shatter. Until now, she had been content in her heart, thinking that now her sorrows and poverty had all gone away. Since Gobar had come home, a glow of happiness had lit her face. Her voice had grown gentle, her manner generous. God had shown her mercy, so she should walk with her head bowed in gratitude. The quiet peace within had turned into kindness. But these words fell upon her heart like burning sand, scorching every hope like roasted grain. All her pride was crushed. After hearing this, what joy was left in life? The boat on which she had hoped to cross this ocean of existence had broken—then what was left to live for, for happiness?

But no. Her Gobar could not be so selfish. He had never answered back to his mother, never insisted on anything. Whatever dry crust he got, he ate without complaint; always simple, always gentle and affectionate—why was he speaking such heart-breaking words today? No one had gone against his wishes. Both his mother and father always tried to please him. If there was talk of settling accounts, who would tell him to pay off his parents’ debts? Was it not happiness enough that he, with respect and honor, could at least, if possible, help his parents a little? If not, would his parents complain? If he wanted to take Jhunia with him, let him take her gladly. Dhaniya had only thought of his comfort, that taking Jhunia there would make things easier for him—was there anything in that to make him so angry?

Perhaps someone has been poisoning his mind, reciting spells into his ears as he sits. Here, hundreds...

--- Page 185 ---
One must do some work or the other. There, she’ll have money in her hands, eat rich food with pleasure, wear fine clothes, and sleep with her legs stretched out. What does it take to cook for two people? There, money is what matters. I’ve heard you can get ready-made bread in the market. She’s the one who started all this trouble—she’s even lived in the city for some time, got a taste for city life. Here, no one asked after her. She found this fool, trapped him. When she came here five months pregnant, how pitifully she mewled! If she hadn’t found shelter here then, she’d be begging on the streets today. And this is how she repays that kindness! Because of this witch, I had to pay a fine, was disgraced in the community, the farm was ruined, everything fell apart. And now this witch is poking holes in the very plate she eats from. The moment she saw money, her eyes lit up. She struts around with her nose in the air, her moods never matching ours. Now that the boy has started earning a little, she doesn’t even ask after us. If I didn’t speak to her for days, she’d come running with oil to massage her mother-in-law’s feet. This witch wants to snatch away the treasure of my life from my hands.

In a pained voice she said, “Who’s putting these ideas in your head, son? You weren’t like this. Your mother and father are yours, your sisters are yours, this house is yours. Who here is an outsider? And will we stay forever? If you keep the honor of the house, it will be for your own good. Does a man earn for strangers or for his own family? Even a pig can feed itself. I never imagined that Jhuniya would turn into a serpent and bite us.”

Gobur snapped, “Amma, I’m not so naïve that Jhuniya can cast a spell on me. You’re blaming her for nothing. I can’t carry the whole burden of your household. I’ll help as much as I can, but I can’t shackle myself.”

Jhuniya too stepped out of the room and said, “Amma, don’t take out your anger on the innocent. I’m not a child that I can be bullied. Everyone knows what’s good and bad for themselves. A person isn’t born to spend his whole life in penance and die empty-handed. Everyone wants some happiness in life, everyone longs to have a little money in hand.”

Dhaniya ground her teeth and said, “Enough, Jhuniya, don’t spout too much wisdom. Now you’re old enough to think of your own good and bad? When you came here crying at my feet, did you think of your own good then? If I’d thought of myself at that time, who knows where you’d be now.”

After this, a battle broke out. Taunts and jibes, abuse and insults—nothing was left unsaid. Gobur too kept stinging from the sidelines. Hori, sitting in the verandah, heard everything. Sona and Rupa stood in the courtyard with bowed heads. Dulari, Puniya, and several other women came to intervene. Amidst the thunder, sometimes a few drops of rain would fall. Both women were lamenting their fates. Both were cursing God, and both were trying to prove their innocence.

Jhuniya was digging up old grievances. Today, she felt a special kinship with Heera and Shobha, whom Dhaniya had never spared. Dhaniya had never gotten along with anyone, so how could she get along with Jhuniya? Dhaniya tried to justify herself, but for some reason, public opinion was on Jhuniya’s side. Perhaps because Jhuniya was now the wife of an earning man, it was more prudent to keep her happy.

Then Hori came into the courtyard and said, “I beg you, Dhaniya, be quiet. Don’t blacken my face with shame. Yes, if you’re not satisfied yet, then say more.”

--- Page 186 ---
Dhaniya flared up and rushed forward—“You too are trying to take the thick branch. I alone am to blame, am I? She’s showering flowers on me, is she?”

The battlefield shifted.

“He who stoops to argue with the lowly becomes lowly himself.”

By what logic could Dhaniya accept that Jhunia was lowly?

With a pained voice, Hori said, “Fine, she’s not lowly, she’s greater, if you wish. But if someone does not wish to stay, will you bind them here? They have hands and feet now. What do you want—that they bring fodder and feed us? A parent’s duty is wholly to their children. Children have not even a sixteenth of that duty toward their parents. If someone leaves, bid them farewell with your blessings. Our fate is in God’s hands. Whatever is destined, we shall endure. Forty, forty-seven years have passed in tears and sorrow. A few more years remain—they too will pass the same way.”

Meanwhile, Gobar was preparing to leave. Even the water of this house was forbidden to him now. When a mother speaks such words, he would not even look at her face again.

In no time, his bedding was packed. Jhunia too donned her chunari. Munnu, dressed in his cap and frock, looked like a little prince.

With a choked voice, Hori said, “Son, I have no right to say anything to you, but my heart cannot bear it. Would it be so wrong to go and touch the feet of your unfortunate mother? The mother whose womb bore you, whose blood nourished you—can you not do even this much for her?”

Turning his face away, Gobar said, “I do not consider her my mother.”

Tears welled up in Hori’s eyes. “As you wish. Wherever you go, may you be happy.”

Jhunia went to her mother-in-law and touched her feet with the end of her sari. Not a single word of blessing escaped Dhaniya’s lips. She did not even raise her eyes to look. Gobar, carrying the child in his arms, walked ahead. Jhunia, clutching the bedding, followed behind. A chamar boy carried their box. Several men and women from Nand’s family accompanied Gobar to the edge of the village.

Dhaniya sat weeping, as if someone were sawing through her heart. Her mind felt like a house set ablaze, everything inside turned to ashes. There was not even a place left to sit and weep.

For some days now, there had been no talk of Rai Sahib’s daughter’s marriage.  
The election too was at hand, but above all, he had to pursue the Eden litigation, whose court fee alone was fifty thousand rupees, not to mention the other expenses.  
He was the sole master of his estate, yet in the prime of his youth, he had been ousted.  
And Rai Sahib, on behalf of his young son, wished to reclaim that estate by seeking refuge in the courts. His wife’s cousins had seized the estate and refused to give up any share. Rai Sahib, with great sorrow, had offered to let them keep a suitable allowance and leave. He was even willing to forgo the estate’s income, but the cousins would not agree to any compromise.  
By force, they had begun a campaign of collection and suppression in the estate. Rai Sahib’s situation had become so dire that there was no way left but to go to court. The litigation had already cost lakhs.

--- Page 187 ---
There was no property. The lawyers had assured him that he would certainly win the case. Who could let such an opportunity slip by? The difficulty was that all three matters had come together at once, and none could be postponed. His daughter had reached the age of eighteen, and only the lack of money had delayed her marriage until now. The estimated expense was a lakh of rupees. Whomever he approached, each opened his mouth wide, but just then, a very good opportunity presented itself. Kunwar Digvijay Singh’s wife had succumbed to tuberculosis, and Kunwar Sahib was eager to restore life to his desolate home as soon as possible. The match was settled on favorable terms, and lest the prey slip from his grasp, it was imperative that the wedding take place in this very marriage season.

Kunwar Sahib was a storehouse of vices. There was no intoxicant—wine, ganja, opium, madak, charas—that he did not indulge in. And debauchery, after all, is the ornament of a nobleman. What sort of aristocrat is he who is not a libertine? How else is one to make use of wealth? Yet, despite all these vices, he was so talented that even the learned acknowledged his worth. In music, drama, palmistry, astrology, yoga, stick-fighting, wrestling, marksmanship—there was none to match him. Along with this, he was bold and fearless. He supported the national movement with an open heart, though secretly. The authorities were not unaware of this, yet he enjoyed great prestige, and once or twice a year even the Governor Sahib would be his guest. He was not yet more than thirty or thirty-two, and his health was such that he could digest an entire goat by himself.

Raisahib thought, “The heavens have smiled upon me.” Kunwar Sahib had hardly completed his mourning period when Raisahib began negotiations. For Kunwar Sahib, marriage was merely a means to increase his influence and power. Raisahib was already a member of the council, and influential in his own right. He had also earned respect by demonstrating sacrifice in the national struggle. There could be no obstacle to the match, and it was settled.

Then there was the election—like a golden sickle, impossible to swallow or spit out. He had already been elected twice, and each time it had cost him a lakh of rupees, but this time a Raja Sahib from the same region had entered the fray and publicly declared that even if he had to give every voter a thousand rupees, even if his fifty-lakh estate was reduced to dust, he would not allow Rai Amarpal Singh to enter the council. The authorities had also assured him of their support. Raisahib was thoughtful, understood profit and loss, but he was a Rajput, and a nobleman of old lineage. How could he withdraw from the field after being challenged? If Raja Suryapratap Singh had come to him and said, “Brother, you have already been to the council twice, let me go this time,” perhaps Raisahib would have welcomed him. He no longer craved the council seat, but in the face of such a challenge, there was no path but to stand his ground. There was another consideration as well. Mr. Tankha had assured him, “You stand for election, and later you can withdraw after taking a lakh-rupee purse from Raja Sahib.” He had even said that Raja Sahib did not want to lose the honor of defeating Raisahib, and the main reason for this was the marriage alliance between Raisahib’s daughter and Kunwar Sahib. The union of two influential houses was seen as harmful to his own prestige. On the other hand, Raisahib hoped to gain property from his in-laws. This thorn pricked Raja Sahib as well. If that property were to fall into Raisahib’s hands—and the law was on his side—then a rival would rise up against Raja Sahib. Therefore, it was his duty to crush Raisahib and reduce his prestige to dust.

--- Page 188 ---
The poor Rai Sahib found himself in great distress. He began to suspect that Mr. Tankha had deceived him merely to serve his own interests. News had reached him that Tankha was now supporting Raja Sahib. This was like salt rubbed into Rai Sahib’s wounds. He had summoned Tankha several times, but either Tankha was never at home, or he would promise to come and then forget. At last, deciding to meet him in person, Rai Sahib went to his house. By chance, Mr. Tankha was at home, but Rai Sahib had to wait a full hour for him. This was the same Mr. Tankha who used to present himself at Rai Sahib’s door every day. Now, he had grown so haughty. Rai Sahib sat there, seething. As soon as Mr. Tankha entered the room, impeccably dressed, a cigar clenched between his lips, and extended his hand, Rai Sahib burst out, “I have been sitting here for an hour, and you have only just deigned to appear. I consider this an insult.”

Mr. Tankha sat down on a sofa and, blowing smoke with an air of nonchalance, replied, “I regret that. I was engaged in an important matter. You should have called me to fix a time.”

This was like pouring oil on fire, but Rai Sahib restrained his anger. He had not come to quarrel. This was a moment to swallow the insult. He said, “Yes, that was my mistake. It seems you have very little free time these days.”

“Yes, very little. Otherwise, I would certainly have come.”

“I came to ask you about that matter. There seems to be no hope of a settlement. On the other side, preparations for battle are in full swing.”

“You know Raja Sahib—he’s a stubborn man, utterly eccentric. Some obsession or other always possesses him. Right now, he’s obsessed with humiliating you, Rai Sahib. And when he gets an idea into his head, he listens to no one, no matter the cost. He’s already burdened with some four million rupees in debt, yet he’s as extravagant as ever, spending lavishly. He pays no heed to money. The servants’ wages have been pending for six months, but the Heera Mahal is still being built. The floors are of marble, the inlay work so dazzling the eyes cannot linger. Gifts are sent daily to the officials. I hear he’s planning to hire an English manager.”

“Then why did you say you could arrange a settlement?”

“I did everything in my power. What more could I do? If a man is determined to burn through a couple of lakhs, what can I do?”

Now Rai Sahib could no longer control his anger—especially when, out of those lakhs, he suspected ten or twenty thousand might find their way into Tankha’s own pocket.

Why should Mr. Tankha be cowed? He replied, “Rai Sahib, let’s not mince words. Neither of us is a saint here. We’re all out to make something for ourselves. You are just as eager for blind fools and full purses as I am. I proposed you stand for election; you agreed, tempted by the lure of a lakh. Had the move succeeded, today you would have been master of a lakh, gained a connection with Kunwar Sahib without taking a penny in debt, and the case would have been filed. But by your misfortune, that move failed. When you yourself were left high and dry, what was there for me? In the end, I had no choice but to grab onto their coattails. One way or another, I have to cross this Vaitarni.”

Rai Sahib was so inflamed that he felt like shooting this scoundrel. It was this rogue who had lured him with false promises, and now he was justifying himself. He would not even let dust settle on his back, but circumstances had sealed Rai Sahib’s lips.

--- Page 189 ---
“So now, nothing can be done by you?”

“Consider it so.”

“I am ready to settle even at fifty thousand.”

“Raja Sahab will not agree under any circumstances.”

“At least he will agree at twenty-five thousand?”

“There is no hope. He has made it clear.”

“Has he said so, or are you saying it?”

“You think I am lying?”

Raisahab replied in a gentle tone, “I do not think you are lying, but I do believe that if you had wished, the matter could have been settled.”

“So you think I did not let the settlement happen?”

“No, that is not what I mean. I only wish to say that if you had wanted, the deal would have been done, and I would not have been caught up in this mess.”

Mr. Tankha glanced at his watch and said, “Well, Raisahab, if you want me to speak plainly, then listen. If you had placed a cheque for ten thousand in my hand, today you would certainly be the master of one lakh. Perhaps you thought that after you got the money from Raja Sahab, you would give me a thousand or two, but I am not so naïve. You would have taken the money from Raja Sahab, locked it in your safe, and shown me the door. Then what could I have done to you? Tell me. I could not even file a complaint anywhere.”

Raisahab looked at him with wounded eyes, “You think I am dishonest?”

Tankha rose from his chair and said, “Who calls this dishonesty? Nowadays, this is cleverness. The successful policy is to make fools of others, and you are a master of it.”

Raisahab clenched his fist, “Me?”

“Yes, you. In the first election, I advocated for you with all my heart and soul. You gave me five hundred rupees after much pleading and tears. In the second election, you got rid of your obligation by giving me a broken-down, battered car. Once burnt, even buttermilk is sipped with caution.”

He left the room and ordered his car to be brought.

Raisahab’s blood was boiling. Was there any limit to such insolence! First, he had been made to wait an hour, and now, after such discourtesy, he was being practically thrown out of the house. If he had believed he could best Mr. Tankha in a fight, he would not have hesitated, but Tankha was physically stronger than him. When Mr. Tankha honked his horn, Raisahab too went out and got into his own car, heading straight to Mr. Khanna.

It was nine o’clock, but Khanna Sahab was still enjoying the sweetness of sleep. He never slept before two in the night, so sleeping until nine was only natural. Here too, Raisahab had to wait half an hour, so when Mr. Khanna finally emerged at half past nine, smiling, Raisahab scolded him, “Well! So His Excellency has finally awakened at half past nine! You must have collected all your money, that’s why you are so carefree. If you were a taluqdar like me, by now you would be standing at someone’s door. Just sitting makes my head spin.”

Mr. Khanna, offering him his cigarette case with a cheerful face, said, “I slept very late last night. Where are you coming from at this hour?”

--- Page 190 ---
Please provide the text you would like me to translate.

--- Page 191 ---
A single misstep can plunge you into the abyss. You must tread very carefully at this moment.

Raisahib drew his hand closer and said, “I understand all this very well, my friend! But what is the tragedy of life, if not this—that you are forced to do what your soul does not wish to do? At this moment, you must arrange at least two lakhs for me.”

Khanna sighed deeply. “My God! Two lakhs—impossible, absolutely impossible!”

“I will dash my head at your door and give up my life, Khanna, remember that. It is on your promise alone that I have made all these plans. If you disappoint me, I may have to swallow poison. I cannot kneel before Suryapratap Singh. My daughter’s marriage can be postponed for a few months. There is still time to file the lawsuit. But this election is upon us, and that is my greatest worry.”

Khanna, astonished, asked, “So you will spend two lakhs on the election?”

“It is not a matter of the election, brother, it is a matter of honor. In your opinion, is my honor not worth even two lakhs? If my entire estate is sold, I do not care, but I will not let Suryapratap Singh win so easily.”

After exhaling smoke for a minute, Khanna said, “I have already told you the state of the bank. The bank has practically stopped all transactions. I will try to secure a special concession for you, but you know—business is business. But what will my commission be? I will have to make a special recommendation for you. You know how much influence Raja Sahib has over the other directors. I will have to form a faction against him. You must understand that the matter will proceed solely on my responsibility.”

Raisahib’s face fell. Khanna was among his closest friends—his schoolmate, his companion—and yet he expected a commission from him, so devoid of sentiment? After all, for whom had he been currying Khanna’s favor all these years? Whenever fruit ripened in his orchard, or vegetables were harvested, the first basket was sent to Khanna. If there was a festival or a gathering, Khanna was the first to be invited. And this was his response? With a heavy heart, he said, “As you wish. But I always considered you my brother.”

Khanna replied with gratitude, “That is your kindness. I too have always regarded you as my elder brother, and still do. I have never kept anything from you, but business is a different realm. Here, no one is a friend, no one is a brother. Just as I cannot ask you, as a brother, to give me a higher commission than others, you too should not press me for a concession in my commission. I assure you, I will grant you every concession I can. Come to the office tomorrow during working hours and we will put everything in writing. That’s it, business concluded. Have you heard the latest? These days, Mehta Sahib is utterly smitten with Malti. All his philosophy has evaporated. He visits her once or twice a day without fail, and in the evenings they often go for walks together. It was my pride that I never once went to Malti’s door to pay my respects. Perhaps she is now making up for that. There was a time when, whatever she needed, it was Mr. Khanna she turned to. If there was any work, she would run to me. If she needed money, a note would arrive in my name. And now, she turns her face away when she sees me. I—”

--- Page 192 ---
He had specially ordered a watch from France for her. I took it to her with great enthusiasm, but she refused to accept it. Only yesterday, I sent her a basket of dried fruits, brought from Kashmir, and she returned it. I am astonished—how can a person change so quickly!

Raisahab was secretly pleased at her indifference, but, showing sympathy, said, “Even if we accept that she has fallen in love with Mehta, there is no reason to break off all relations.”

Khanna replied in a pained voice, “That is exactly what grieves me, brother! I knew from the very beginning that she could never be mine. I tell you the truth, I never deluded myself into thinking that Malti loved me. I never even hoped for something like love from her. I was only a worshipper of her beauty. We know there is poison in a snake, yet we feed it milk. What creature is more cruel than a parrot? Yet, enchanted by its beauty and voice, people keep it and place it in a golden cage. Malti was just such a parrot to me. My only regret is—why did I not realize this sooner? I have wasted thousands of rupees on her, brother. Whenever her note arrived, I sent money at once. Even today, my car is at her disposal. For her, I have ruined my household, brother. All the sweetness in my heart rushed so violently towards her that the garden on the other side was left utterly barren. It has been years since I spoke openly with Govindi. Her service, her affection, her sacrifice—all became as distasteful to me as sweets to a man with indigestion. Malti made me dance as a juggler makes a monkey dance, and I danced with joy. She insulted me, and I laughed happily. She ruled over me, and I bowed my head. She never showed me any affection, I admit that. She never encouraged me, that too is true. Yet, like a moth, I was willing to give my life for the flame of her face. And now, she cannot even treat me with common courtesy. But, brother, let me tell you—Khanna is not a man to sit quietly. I have all her notes safe with me. I will recover every single penny from her. And as for Dr. Mehta, I will not rest until I drive him out of Lucknow. I will make it impossible for him to stay here…”

Just then, the sound of a horn was heard, and in a moment, Mr. Mehta appeared.

Fair-skinned, the flush of health glowing on his cheeks, a shining face, a short achkan, tight-fitting pajama, golden spectacles—he looked like a deity of gentleness.

Khanna stood up and shook his hand. “Come in, Mr. Mehta, we were just talking about you.”

Mehta shook hands with both gentlemen and said, “I must have left home at a very auspicious hour, to find both of you here together. You may have seen in the letters—there is an initiative to open a gymnasium for women here. Miss Malti is the chairperson of that committee. It is estimated that the school will require two lakh rupees. You both know better than I how much the city needs such a place. I would like your names to be at the top of the list. Miss Malti herself was to come, but her father is unwell today, so she could not make it.”

He placed the donation list in Raisahab’s hand. The first name was Raja Suryapratap Singh, with a sum of five thousand rupees beside it. Next was Kunwar Digvijay Singh, with three thousand rupees. After that, several more sums, some as large, some a little less. Malti had given five hundred rupees, and Dr. Mehta, one thousand.

Raisahab, astonished, said, “You have already collected nearly forty thousand.”

Mehta replied with pride, “It is all due to your kindness. And this is only the fruit of your efforts—”

--- Page 193 ---
Gaja Suryapratap Singh had hardly ever participated in any public endeavor, but today, without a word, he wrote out a cheque. There is an awakening in the country. The people are ready to support any noble cause—only, they must have faith that their donation will be put to good use. I have great hopes from you, Mr. Khanna!

Khanna replied with indifference, “I do not involve myself in such useless activities. Who knows how far you people will go in your slavish imitation of the West? As it is, women are losing interest in their homes. Now, if they become obsessed with physical exercise, they will be lost altogether. A woman who does her household work needs no exercise. And as for the woman who does nothing at home and indulges only in luxury, I consider it a sin to donate for her exercise.”

Mehta was not at all discouraged. “In that case, I will not ask you for anything. To support an endeavor in which one does not believe is, in truth, a sin. Rai Sahib, you do not agree with Mr. Khanna, do you?”

Rai Sahib was lost in deep thought. Suryapratap’s five thousand had left him disheartened. He started, “Did you say something to me?”

“I was asking, you do not consider it a sin to support this cause, do you?”

“Whether the work you are involved in is righteous or unrighteous, I do not concern myself with that.”

“I would like you to consider for yourself, and if you find this endeavor beneficial to society, then support it. I quite liked Mr. Khanna’s policy.”

Khanna said, “At least I speak plainly, and that is why I have a bad name.”

Rai Sahib replied with a feeble smile, “I have no power to think for myself. I consider it my duty to follow in the footsteps of gentlemen like you. Whatever you say, I will write.”

“Then write a good amount.”

“As you wish.”

“Then, will you not write at least two thousand?”

Rai Sahib said, in a wounded tone, “Is that all I am worth in your eyes?”

He picked up the pen and, writing his name, put down five thousand. Mehta took the list from his hand, but felt such remorse that he even forgot to thank Rai Sahib. By showing him the donation list, he had done a great wrong—this thought began to trouble him.

Mr. Khanna looked at Rai Sahib with a mixture of pity and mockery, as if to say, “What a great fool you are!”

Suddenly, Mehta embraced Rai Sahib and exclaimed with a free voice, “Three Cheers for Rai Sahib! Hip Hip Hurrah!”

Khanna said, with a touch of irritation, “These people are princes and kings—if they don’t give to such causes, who will?”

Mehta said, “I consider you the king of kings. You rule over them. Their mansions are in your hands.”

Rai Sahib was pleased. “You have spoken a most telling truth, Mehtaji! We are kings in name only. The real kings are our bankers.”

Mehta now turned to flatter Khanna, “I have no complaint against you, Khannaji! If you do not wish to join this cause now, so be it. But someday, you surely will—”

--- Page 194 ---
They will come. It is thanks to the wealthy that our great institutions run. Who kept the national movement going with such pomp for two or three years? Who is building so many dharamshalas and pathshalas? Today, the reins of the world are in the hands of bankers. The government is their plaything. I am not disappointed in you either. A person who can go to jail for the nation will not find it difficult to spend two or four thousand rupees. We have decided that the foundation stone of this school will be laid by Govindi Devi. Raja Sahab and other gentlemen also thought that Lady Wilson should lay the foundation, but in the end, it was decided that this auspicious task should be done by one of our own sisters. “When wealth exceeds one’s needs, it seeks an outlet for itself. If it cannot find one, it will go to gambling, to horse racing, to bricks and mortar, or to debauchery.”

It was eleven o’clock. The time for Khanna Sahib’s office had come. Mehta left. Gaya Sahib also rose, but Khanna caught his hand and made him sit down again. “No, please sit for a while. You see, Mehta has trapped me so thoroughly that there is no way out. Govindi will lay the foundation stone for the world! In such a situation, would it not be ridiculous for me to stay away? How Govindi became so poor, I cannot understand, and how Malti endured it is even harder to fathom. What do you think—is there some mystery here or not?”

Rai Sahib spoke with warmth, “In such matters, a woman should always consult her husband.”

Khanna looked at Rai Sahib with grateful eyes. “It is for such things that Govindi blames me, and people criticize me for it. Just think, what do I have to do with these quarrels? Let those get involved who have spare money, spare time, and a hunger for fame. It always ends up that two or four gentlemen become secretary, under-secretary, president, and vice-president, host dinners for others, become favorites of the authorities, and gather university girls to give speeches—these exercises are only for show. This always happens in such institutions, and it is we and our brothers, those called wealthy, who are responsible for all this because of Govindi.

He stood up from his chair, then sat down again. His resentment toward Govindi began to show. Holding his head in both hands, he said, “I do not understand what I should do.”

Rai Sahib, with a tone of sympathy, said, “What are you telling Govindi Devi? Don’t refuse the Seth’s offer, stand firm. I too am caught in the fire at home. Why should you be?”

Khanna considered this suggestion for a moment and said, “But Govindi, on whom the violence is being inflicted, will also have to bear her share. It will become a matter that will cause an uproar throughout the city. This is all a mistaken notion. She has no right to say anything to me, nor should she ask me.”

--- Page 195 ---
“You must leave town the day before the foundation-laying ceremony.”

“That will be difficult, Rai Sahib! There will be nowhere left for me to show my face. Even if I were struck with cholera that day, I would still have to go.”

Rai Sahib, still holding onto hope, promised to return the next day and departed. No sooner had he left than Khanna, agitated, went straight to Govindi and confronted her sharply, “Why did you agree to lay the foundation stone of this gymnasium?”

How could Govindi confess that she was overjoyed at receiving this honor, that she had been preparing her speech with such devotion for this occasion, and had composed a poem brimming with passion? In her heart, she had believed that by accepting this proposal, she would please Khanna. His honor was her own. She had never imagined he might object. For the past few days, seeing her husband’s gentler demeanor, her spirits had soared. She had dreamed of enchanting everyone with her speech and her poetry.

But now, hearing his question and seeing his stern expression, her heart began to pound. In a guilty tone, she replied, “Dr. Mehta insisted, so I accepted.”

“If Dr. Mehta asked you to jump into a well, I doubt you’d agree so eagerly.”

Govindi fell silent.

“When God hasn’t given you any sense, why didn’t you ask me? Mehta and Malti are both scheming to squeeze a few thousand rupees out of me, but I’ve decided not to give them a single coin. Today itself, you must write to Mehta and decline.”

Govindi thought for a moment and said, “Then why don’t you write yourself?”

“Why should I? You made the promise, and I should write?”

“If Dr. Sahib asks for a reason, what shall I tell him?”

“Tell him to bang his head against the wall, what else? I won’t give a single paisa to this den of debauchery.”

“Who’s asking you to give anything?”

Khanna bit his lip and said, “How thoughtless you are! If you lay the foundation stone there and give nothing, what will people say?”

Govindi, as if at the point of a bayonet, replied, “Very well, I’ll write.”

“You must write today.”

“I said I would, didn’t I?”

Khanna went outside and began to check the mail. If he was late leaving for the office, the peon would deliver the mail to his house. The price of sugar had risen. Khanna’s face lit up. He opened another letter. The committee that had convened to set the price of wheat had decided that such regulation was impossible. Blast it! This was exactly what he had been saying, but Agnihotri had made a fuss and forced the committee to meet. In the end, it was like slapping a child’s face. This was a matter between the mill owners and the farmers. What business did the government have interfering in it?

Suddenly, Miss Malti stepped out of her car—radiant as a lotus, glowing like a lamp, the very image of vitality and joy—fearless, untroubled, as if she was certain that the world had opened its doors of respect and happiness for her. Khanna came out to the veranda and greeted her.

Malti asked, “Was Mehta here?”

“Yes, he was.”

“Did he say anything? Where is he going?”

“That was nothing you said.”

“Who knows where you disappeared! I searched all around. Did you make a donation for the gymnasium?”

Khanna replied in a guilty tone, “I didn’t understand the matter at all.”

Malti glared at him with her large eyes, as if wondering whether to pity him or be angry.

“What was there to understand? You could have figured out the details later—at this moment, it was only a matter of giving something. I pushed Mehta to come here. The poor man was afraid you might give some strange answer. Do you know what will be the result of your stinginess? You’ll get nothing from the merchant community here. Perhaps you’ve decided to humiliate me. Everyone advised to keep Lady Willman’s union. You understand the intricacies of banking, but such a simple thing escaped you! This can only mean that you wish to embarrass me. Very well, let it be so!”

Malti’s face flushed red. Khanna grew anxious, his bravado vanished, but at the same time he realized that if he was caught in thorns, Malti was stuck in a quagmire; if his purse was in danger, Malti’s reputation was at stake—which was far more valuable than any purse. So why shouldn’t he take some pleasure in Malti’s predicament? He had put her in a difficult position, and though he had lost the courage to anger her, he did not want to miss the chance to say a few sharp words. He also wanted to show that he was not a complete simpleton. Blocking her path, he said, “I am surprised, Malti, at how compassionate you have become towards me!”

Malti frowned and said, “I don’t understand your meaning.”

“Is your behavior with me now the same as it was some days ago?”

“I see no difference in it.”

“But I see a difference as vast as the sky and the earth.”

“Fine, suppose your suspicion is correct—then what? Have I come to you to help in a good cause, or to put my own conduct to the test? And if you think you can gain anything from me besides appreciation and thanks, you are mistaken.”

Khanna was defeated. He was trapped in such a narrow corner that there was no way out. Could he dare to say to her, “Have all the favors I have done for you so far deserved this reward?” Shame sealed his lips, as if he had lost his very self. Stammering, he said, “That was not my intention, Malti, you have completely misunderstood me.”

“If I ever do misunderstand you, I’ll run from even your shadow. I know you. You are my best friend. It was my grace that, just as I repay others’ favors, I accepted yours too with thanks. And as long as I—unless in your intoxication with wealth you attach some other meaning to it—this is not an exception to the nature of men, but know this: neither of us has ever triumphed over the other, nor ever will.”

Khanna stood there, shaken by every word.

--- Page 197 ---
He had no spirit left. Ashamed, he said, "Malti, I fall at your feet, please do not humiliate me any further. If nothing else, at least let our friendship remain."

Saying this, he took out his checkbook from the drawer, wrote a thousand, and, trembling, handed it to Malti.

Malti took the check and said with merciless irony, "Is this the price of my affection, or a donation to the gymnasium?"

With tearful eyes, Khanna replied, "Now spare me, Malti. Why are you blackening my face like this?"

Malti burst out laughing, "Look, I scolded you and still managed to collect a thousand rupees. Now you’ll never dare such mischief again, will you?"

"Never, never in my life."

"Hold your ears."

"I hold my ears, but now, have mercy and go. Let me sit alone and think—and weep. Today you have taken all the joy from my life..."

Malti laughed even louder, "Look Khanna, you are insulting me greatly, and you know, beauty cannot tolerate insult. I did you a kindness, and you take it as an offense."

Khanna looked at her with eyes full of rebellion and said, "You have done me a kindness, or have you slit my throat with a blunt knife?"

"Why, was I robbing you to fill my own house? You have been saved from that robbery."

"Why are you rubbing salt into my wounds, Malti? I am a human being too."

Malti looked at Khanna as if she wanted to decide whether he was truly a human or not.

"I see no sign of it yet."

"You are a complete riddle, today it is proven."

"Yes, I am a riddle for you, and I shall remain so."

Saying this, she flitted away like a bird, and Khanna, placing his hand on his head, began to wonder—is this a play, or is it her true self?
