After Gobar and Jhunia left, the house became desolate. Dhaniya was constantly reminded of Chunnu. The child's mother was Jhunia, but it was Dhaniya who had raised him. She would rub him with scented paste, apply kohl to his eyes, put him to sleep, and whenever she found a moment free from work, she would shower him with love. This intoxication of motherhood alone kept her oblivious to her misfortunes. Seeing his innocent, butter-soft face, she would forget all her worries, and her heart would swell with loving pride. That foundation of her life was no longer there. Seeing his empty cot, she would break down in tears. The armor that had protected her from all anxieties and disappointments had been snatched away. The witch had come and turned her golden home to dust. Gobar had not even replied to any of her letters. That wretched woman had bewitched him, and who knows what dances she would make him perform there. Here, she hardly cared for the child. She could never tear herself away from her hair parting, her kohl, her braids. How would she care for the child? The poor thing must be lying alone on the ground, crying. The poor soul had not known a single day of happiness...

--- Page 198 ---
Sometimes it was a cough, sometimes dysentery, sometimes one thing, sometimes another. Thinking about all this, he would grow angry at Jhunia. He still felt the same tenderness for Gobar as ever. But this witch had fed him something and brought him under her spell. If she were not such an enchantress, how could she have cast her magic? No one used to care for her. She was kicked around by her sisters-in-law. Now that she had found this fool, she had become a queen.

Hori said irritably, “You always blame Jhunia for everything. Why don’t you understand—if our gold is impure, what fault is it of the goldsmith’s? If Gobar hadn’t taken her, would she have gone on her own? The city’s food and water have changed the boy’s eyes—why can’t you see that?”

Dhaniya burst out, “All right, be quiet. You’re the one who put that widow on your head. Otherwise, I would have swept her out of the house the very first day.”

The threshing floor was already crowded with bundles. Hori was about to go and thresh the grain with the oxen. Turning his head, he said, “Suppose the daughter-in-law has really won Gobar over—why does it trouble you so much? She’s done what everyone does, what Gobar has done too. Now she has her own children. Why should she trouble herself for my children, why should she take our burden onto her own head?”

“You’re the root of all this trouble.”

“Then throw me out too. Take the oxen, thresh the grain. I’ll smoke the hookah.”

“You go and grind at the mill, I’ll thresh the grain.”

In jest, their sorrow vanished. That was their remedy. Dhaniya, now cheerful, sat down to comb out little Rupa’s hair, which was completely tangled, and Hori went off to the threshing floor. The playful spring was scattering fragrance, delight, and the riches of life with both hands, with an open heart. The koel, hidden among the mango branches, was awakening hopes with her sweet, melodious, soul-touching call. On the mahua trees, the mynas sat in a row like a wedding procession. The neem, siris, and karonda filled the air with their intoxicating scent. When Hori reached the mango orchard, the ground beneath the trees was strewn with star-shaped blossoms. His troubled, despairing heart too began to sing in the midst of this vast beauty and vigor:

“My heart burns day and night.  
The koel sings in the mango grove,  
But peace never comes.”

Coming from the other side was Dulari Sahuain, dressed in a pink sari. Thick silver anklets adorned her feet, a heavy gold necklace hung around her neck, her face was withered but her heart was green as ever. There was a time when Hori used to tease her in the fields and threshing floor. She was his bhabhi by relation, he the devar, and so there was always playful banter between them. Since Sahji had died, Dulari had stopped leaving the house. She sat at the shop all day, gathering all the news of the village from there. If there was ever a quarrel, Sahuain would surely arrive to mediate. She never lent money at less than eight percent interest. And though, out of greed for interest, she never got back the principal—the one who borrowed would simply eat it up and sit idle—yet her rate of interest remained unchanged. Poor woman, how could she recover her money? She was not one to file complaints or go to the police station; her only strength was her tongue. But as the years passed, her tongue grew sharper, but its sting lessened. Now people would laugh at her curses and joke, “What will you do with all that money, Kaki? You won’t be able to take a single penny with you. Feed the poor, earn as much merit as you can—that’s what will help you in the next world.” And Dulari would fume at the mention of the next world.

Hori teased her, “Today, bhabhi, you truly look young.”

--- Page 199 ---
Sahuain, delighted, said, “Today is Tuesday, an auspicious day—don’t cast an evil eye on me! That’s why I don’t wear anything fancy. The moment I step out of the house, everyone starts staring as if they’ve never seen a woman before. Pateshwari Lal still hasn’t let go of his old habits.”

Hori paused; an amusing scene was unfolding. The bullocks had gone ahead.

“These days, he’s become quite the devotee. Don’t you see? Every full moon, he listens to the Satyanarayan katha, and he goes to the temple for darshan twice a day.”

“All these rakes, when they grow old, turn into devotees. They must atone for their misdeeds, after all. Tell me, now that I’m an old woman, what’s there to laugh at with me?”

“How can you be old already, Bhabhi? To me, even now—”

“All right, keep quiet, or I’ll shower you with a hundred and fifty abuses! The boy’s gone off to earn in a distant land, and not once did he invite me for a meal. Yet you’re ready to make me your Bhabhi for nothing.”

“Swear on me, Bhabhi, if I’ve touched even a single paisa of his earnings! Who knows what he brought, where he spent it—I know nothing. All I got was a pair of dhotis and a turban.”

“Well, at least he’s started earning. If not today, then tomorrow, he’ll take care of the household. May God keep him happy. And do keep giving us our money back, bit by bit. Only the interest keeps piling up.”

“I’ll pay you every last paisa, Bhabhi, just let me get my hands on some money. And if we do end up spending it, it’s not as if we’re outsiders—we’re your own, after all.”

Sahuain was always disarmed by such playful flattery. Smiling, she went on her way. Hori hurried to catch up with the bullocks, led them into the threshing floor, and began making them walk in circles. This was the only threshing ground in the entire village. Somewhere, grain was being threshed; somewhere else, it was being winnowed; elsewhere, it was being weighed. The nai-bari (barbers), carpenters, blacksmiths, purohits, bards, beggars—all had gathered to collect their customary shares. Under a tree, Jhinguri Singh sat on a cot, collecting his dues. Several baniyas stood haggling over the price of grain. The whole threshing floor had the bustle of a marketplace. A khatkin woman was selling wild berries and nightshade, while a hawker wandered about with oil-fried sweets and jalebis. Pandit Datadin, too, had come to get his share of grain from Hori, and was sitting on the cot with Jhinguri Singh.

As he kneaded his tobacco, Datadin said, “Have you heard? The government is telling the mahajans to lower the interest rates, or else they won’t get decrees.”

Jhinguri, chewing tobacco, replied, “Pandit, I know one thing. When you’re in need, you’ll come to us for loans a hundred times, and we’ll charge whatever interest we please. If the government doesn’t arrange loans for the tenants, this law won’t affect us at all. We’ll write down a lower rate, but we’ll deduct twenty-five out of every hundred right at the start. What can the government do about that?”

“That’s true, but the government understands these tricks well. They’ll come up with some way to stop it, you’ll see.”

“There’s no way to stop it.”

“All right, but what if they make it a rule that unless the village headman or the karinda signs the stamp paper, the loan won’t be valid? What will you do then?”

“The tenant will be desperate a hundred times over—he’ll beg the headman, touch his feet, and get the signature. We’ll still deduct a quarter.”

“And what if you get caught? Falsify the accounts, and you could get fourteen years.”

Jhinguri Singh laughed loudly. “What are you saying, Pandit? Do you think the world is going to change? The law—”

--- Page 200 ---
And justice belongs to him who has money. The law says that the moneylender must not deal harshly with the debtor, that no zamindar should oppress a tenant farmer, but what happens in reality? You see it every day! The zamindar has Musak Bandhwa beaten, and the mahajan speaks with kicks and shoes. The farmer who stands his ground, neither the zamindar nor the mahajan dares speak to him. We seek out such men and, with their help, press down the necks of others. Even you owe five hundred rupees to Rai Sahib, but does Nokheram have the courage to say a word to you? He knows well that it is in his interest to remain on good terms with you. Does any debtor have the strength to run to court every day? The whole business will go on just as it always has. The courts and the law are always on the side of those with money. We people have nothing to fear.

Saying this, he made a round of the threshing floor, then came back and sat down on the cot, saying—So, what has happened about Matai’s marriage? My advice is, get him married, otherwise there will be great disgrace.

At this, Datadeen seemed as if stung by a wasp. He understood well enough what this criticism meant. Growing heated, he said—Let people say what they like behind my back, but if anyone dares say it to my face, I’ll uproot him. Let someone try to be as strict in observance as I am. I know many who pretend to be pious, but care nothing for dharma or karma, for stories or for the Puranas, yet call themselves righteous. Who will laugh at me, who has never, in his life, put a drop of water in his mouth before bathing and worshipping, even when traveling by express train? It is hard to maintain such discipline. If anyone says I have eaten anything from the bazaar, or drunk water from another’s hand, let me be cast out. Siliya cannot even cross our threshold; if she bows her head at the door, I turn her away. And if a woman is abandoned, it is a shameful act. I will say it openly: it is a shameful act to abandon a woman. Matai, too, is a worthy son, following in my footsteps with worship, vows, and ritual purity. As he grows older day by day, who would dare call him astray?

Jhinguri Singh, emboldened, said—I have heard much about Datadeen, that he has presented a long list of journeys through the Mahabharata and the Puranas, and even wrote a letter about it. And nowadays the Brahmins say there is nothing shameful in it!

Jhinguri Singh is his learned friend.

So, are you pretending to be a Sukul now?
‘The shadow of time must fall; it must be that of the true Guru.’
He said, but no one listens, because now you have begun to open your mouth.

--- Page 201 ---
“You ask for something? Ten bighas of land and, besides begging, what else do you have?”

Datadeen’s pride was wounded. Stroking his beard, he replied, “Maybe I have nothing, and yes, I do beg, but I gave five hundred rupees at each of my daughters’ weddings. So why shouldn’t I ask for five hundred for my son? If someone had married my daughter for nothing, I would have married my son for nothing too. As for status—if you consider priesthood to be begging, I see it as zamindari, as ancestral right. Zamindari may disappear, ancestral homes may fall, but priesthood will endure till the end. As long as the Hindu community exists, there will be Brahmins, and there will be priesthood. During the wedding season, I can easily earn a hundred or two just sitting at home. If luck favors, I make four or five hundred. Clothes, utensils, food—those are separate. Somewhere or other, there’s always a ritual or ceremony happening. Even if nothing else comes, I still get a plate or two of food and a few coins as dakshina. There’s no comfort like this in zamindari or moneylending. And then, as for Silia—what would I gain from marrying a Brahmin girl compared to her? She would just sit at home as a daughter-in-law, at most cook some bread. But here, Silia alone does the work of three people. And what do I give her except food? At most, a sari once a year.”

Under another tree was Datadeen’s own pile of straw. Four bullocks were threshing the grain. Dhanna Chamar was driving the bullocks, Silia was winnowing the grain with her feet, and Matadeen sat on the other side, rubbing oil into his staff.

Silia was a dusky, radiant, slender girl, not beautiful in the conventional sense, but still captivating. There was a wild joy in her laughter, her gaze, the play of her limbs, so that every part of her seemed to dance. From head to toe, she was covered in chaff, drenched in sweat, her hair half undone. She ran back and forth, winnowing the grain, as if she were playing some game with all her heart and soul.

Matadeen said, “Silia, not a grain should be left by evening! If you’re tired, I’ll come help.”

With a cheerful face, Silia replied, “Why should you come, Pandit? I’ll finish all the winnowing by evening.”

“Well, then I’ll carry the grain and store it. What can you do all alone?”

“Why worry? I’ll winnow it and carry it all myself. By midnight, not a single grain will be left here.”

Today, Dulari Sahua’in was out collecting her dues. On Holi, Silia had bought two paise worth of pink color from her shop, but hadn’t paid yet. Coming up to Silia, she said, “Well, Silia, it’s been a month since you took the color, and you still haven’t paid? Whenever I ask, you just walk away. Today I won’t leave without my money.”

Matadeen quietly slipped away. Though he had taken both Silia’s body and soul, he did not wish to give anything in return. Now, in his eyes, Silia was nothing but a machine for work, nothing more. He skillfully manipulated her affection. Silia looked up, but Matadeen was nowhere to be seen. She said, “Don’t shout, Sahua’in, here, take this. Instead of two, take four paise worth of grain—will you take my life for it? It’s not as if I would have died.”

She scooped out about a seer of grain from the pile and poured it into the outstretched end of Sahua’in’s sari. Just then, Matadeen emerged angrily from behind the tree, grabbed Sahua’in’s sari, and said, “Put the grain back, Sahua’in, this isn’t a robbery.”

Then, glaring at Silia with bloodshot eyes, he scolded, “Why did you give away the grain? Who gave you permission? Who are you to give away my grain?”

Sahua’in put the grain back on the pile, and Silia stood stunned, staring at Matadeen’s face.

--- Page 202 ---
She began to look around, as if the branch on which she had been sitting so securely had suddenly snapped, and now she was falling, unsupported, into the void below. With a crestfallen face and tears welling in her eyes, she pleaded with the sahuain,  
“I will return your money, sahuain! Have mercy on me today.”

The sahuain looked at her with eyes full of compassion, then turned a look of reproach upon Matadin before walking away.

Then, as Silia sifted the grain, she asked with wounded pride,  
“Have I no right at all over your things?”

Matadin glared at her and replied,  
“No, you have no right. You work here, you eat here. If you want to eat, eat; if you want to squander, squander—but not here. If you don’t like it here, go find work elsewhere. There’s no shortage of laborers. We don’t keep you for free; you get food and clothing.”

Silia looked at Matadin like a bird whose owner had clipped its wings and thrown it out of the cage. Whether there was more pain or reproach in that gaze, it was hard to say. But just like that bird, her heart fluttered, longing to soar in the open sky, but finding herself powerless, she wished only to return to that same cage—even if it meant suffering, thirst, or dying from beating her head against the bars. Silia wondered, what other home did she have now? Though not wedded in ritual, in custom, in feeling, she was as much a wife as any other. Now, whether Matadin beat her or cast her out, she had no other refuge, no other support. She remembered all the days gone by—and it had not even been two years—when this same Matadin would rub her feet and, holding his sacred thread in his hand, say,  
“Silia, as long as there is breath in my body, you will never be turned out.”

He would follow her, lovesick, through the fields, the orchards, along the riverbank. And today, this heartless behavior! All for a handful of grain.

She gave no reply. It felt as if a lump of salt was stuck in her throat, and with limp hands, she resumed her work.

At that very moment, her mother, father, both brothers, and several other cousins came and dragged her from her bedding, cursing,  
“Wretch! If you were going to labor anyway, why did we bring you back home? If you live with a Brahmin, then live like a Brahmin, day by day. If you were to remain a chamarin, what did you come here for—did you think you’d get ghee and sweets here? Where do you think you belong?”

Jhinguri Singh and Datadin both rushed over and stood between the women, trying to calm their shrieks. Jhinguri Singh whispered in Silia’s ear,  
“Is there trouble?”

Silia’s father, Harkhu, was an old man of sixty-seven, but despite his age,  
“We will see this through, either by making her a part of their household or by leaving our own home—  
If she goes back to their house, we have nothing to say. We are ready to accept your kinship. Once she understands that we—  
Eat, drink, sit with us. We are not afraid, we—”

Datadin struck his staff on the ground and said,  
“Understand this, we—”

--- Page 203 ---
Take her wherever you wish. We have not kept her bound. She worked here, she took her wages. There is no shortage of laborers here.

Silia’s mother, wagging her finger, retorted—Bravo, Pandit! What justice you dispense! If your own daughter had run off with a chamar, and you spoke like this, I would see then. Is it because we are chamars that we have no honor? We will not take Silia alone; we will take Matadeen with her—the one who ruined her honor. You are so pious and righteous. You will sleep with her, but you will not drink water from her hands. And this wretch, she endures it all. If it were up to me, I would feed such a man poison.

Harkhu called out to his companions—Did you hear what these people said? Why are you still standing there, gaping?

At this, two chamars sprang forward and seized Matadeen by the hands; a third rushed up and snapped his sacred thread. Before Datadeen and Jhinguri Singh could ready their staffs, two more chamars forced a large piece of bone into Matadeen’s mouth. Matadeen clenched his teeth, but the vile thing touched his lips nonetheless. He felt nauseated, his mouth opened involuntarily, and the bone lodged deep in his throat. By now, all the people from the threshing floor had gathered, but, astonishingly, not one dared oppose these plunderers of religion. Everyone disliked Matadeen’s conduct. He preyed upon the village’s daughters and daughters-in-law, so in their hearts, all were pleased at his humiliation. Outwardly, however, they tried to assert their authority over the chamars.

Hori said—Enough now, Harkhu. If you wish for goodwill, then leave this place.

Harkhu replied fearlessly—You have daughters too, Hori Mahto, remember that. If the village’s honor is allowed to be destroyed like this, no one’s dignity will remain.

Having achieved complete victory over their enemy in a moment, the attackers thought it wise to withdraw. Public opinion can change in an instant. It is best to avoid its wrath.

Matadeen was retching. Datadeen, rubbing his back, said—If I don’t have each of them sent to jail for five years, then say I’m not a man. I’ll have them grinding grain for five years.

Harkhu replied with swagger—That means nothing to us. Who sits around enjoying leisure like you? Wherever we work, we’ll get half a bellyful of grain.

After vomiting, Matadeen lay on the ground like a lifeless thing, as if his back were broken, as if he were searching for a handful of water to drown himself in. The dignity that had fueled his pride, his gallantry, his sense of manhood, was now destroyed. That piece of bone had defiled not only his mouth, but his very soul. His religion was founded on these notions of food, purity, and pollution. Today, the root of that religion was cut. No matter how much penance he performed, no matter how much cow dung he ate or Ganga water he drank, no matter how many alms, pilgrimages, or fasts he undertook, his dead religion could not be revived. If this had happened in secret, it might have been concealed, but here, his religion was plundered before all. Now, his head would be bowed forever. From today, even in his own home, he would be considered untouchable. His loving mother would shrink from him, and such was the death of religion in this world that so many people merely stood by and watched. Not one uttered a word. A moment ago, people would have greeted him with folded hands; now, they would turn their faces away. He would not be allowed into any temple, nor touch anyone’s vessels or utensils. And all this happened because of that unfortunate Silia.

Silia stood where she had been winnowing grain, her head bowed, as if it were her own disgrace.

--- Page 204 ---
Suddenly, her mother came and scolded her—“Why are you standing there staring? Go straight home, or I’ll cut off your braids one by one! You’ve already brought enough shame to your ancestors’ name—what more do you want to do now?”

Siliya stood motionless, like a statue. She felt anger towards her parents and brothers. Why did they interfere in her life? She wanted to live as she pleased—what business was it of others? They said she was being insulted here, but would any Brahmin eat food she had cooked? Would they even drink water from her hands? Just a little while ago, her heart had been wounded by Matadin’s cruel behavior, but now, the oppression of her own family and community transformed her sorrow into a fierce resolve.

With rebellion in her voice, she said, “I will not go anywhere. Will you not let me live even here?”

The old woman snapped harshly, “You won’t go?”

“No.”

“Go quietly!”

“I will not.”

Immediately, both her brothers grabbed her hands and began to drag her away. Siliya sat down on the ground. Even then, her brothers did not let go—they kept dragging her. Her sari tore, the skin on her back and waist was scraped raw, but she would not agree to leave.

Then Harkhu said to the boys, “Fine, leave her now. We’ll consider her dead. But if she ever comes to my doorstep again, I’ll drink her blood.”

Risking her life, Siliya replied, “Yes, if I ever come to your door, then do it.”

In a frenzy of rage, the old woman kicked Siliya several times, and if Harkhu had not pulled her away, she might have killed her then and there.

The old woman lunged again, but Harkhu pushed her back, saying, “Are you a murderer too, Kaliya? Will you kill her?”

Siliya clung to her father’s feet and cried, “Kill me, Dada, all of you together—kill me! Oh, Mother, are you so merciless? Did you raise me with your milk for this? Why didn’t you strangle me in the cradle itself? Alas! You’ve even corrupted the Pandit because of me. What did you gain by ruining his dharma? Now even he will not look at me. But whether he does or not, I will stay with him. He may keep me hungry, he may beat me, but I will not leave his side. Should I let him suffer because of me? I will die, but I will not become faithless. Once someone has held my arm, I belong to him alone.”

Kaliya bit her lip and said, “Let the wretch go. She thinks he’ll keep her. But if he doesn’t throw her out today itself, I’ll never show my face again.”

Even her brothers felt pity. They all left Siliya there and went away. Then, slowly, limping and moaning, she made her way to the threshing floor, sat down, and covered her face with her veil, weeping.

Datadin vented his anger on his beard—“Why didn’t Siliya go with them? What is she waiting for now? Even after ruining me, her hunger isn’t satisfied?”

Siliya raised her tear-filled eyes. There was a glint of defiance in them.

“Why should I go with them? I will stay with the one who held my arm.”

Panditji threatened, “If you set foot in my house, I’ll speak to you with my feet.”

Siliya replied boldly, “Wherever he keeps me, I will stay—be it under a tree or in a palace.”

Matadin sat there, senseless. Noon was approaching. Sunlight filtered through the leaves onto his...

--- Page 205 ---
...was falling across his face. Sweat dripped from his brow. Yet he sat there, silent and motionless.

Suddenly, as if coming to his senses, he said, “What do you say for me now, Dada?”

Datadeen placed his hand on his head, comforting him, and said, “What can I say for you now, son? Go, bathe, eat, and then whatever arrangement the pandits decide, that shall be done. Yes, there is one thing—you will have to renounce Siliya.”

Matadeen looked at Siliya with bloodshot eyes. “I will never see her face again. But if I perform penance, then there will be no fault left, will there?”

“After penance, there is no sin or fault left.”

“Then go to the pandits today itself.”

“I will go today, son!”

“But if the pandits say that penance is not possible, then?”

“As they wish.”

“Then will you drive me out of the house?”

Datadeen, overcome with fatherly affection, said, “How could that ever be, son? Wealth may go, religion may go, social standing may go, but I cannot abandon you.”

Matadeen picked up his stick and followed his father home. Siliya, too, rose and, limping, followed behind him.

Matadeen turned back and said in a harsh voice, “Don’t come with me. I have nothing to do with you. Even after all this suffering, you are not satisfied?”

Siliya, with boldness, caught his hand and said, “How can you say there’s nothing between us? There are men in this village richer than you, handsomer than you, more respected than you. Why don’t I go to them? Why has this misfortune come upon you today? The rope that has fallen around your neck—you may wish a thousand times, but you cannot cast it off. And neither will I leave you and go anywhere. I’ll work as a laborer, beg if I must, but I will not leave you.”

Saying this, she let go of Matadeen’s hand and went back to the threshing floor to winnow grain. Hori was still there, piling up the grain. Dhaniya had come to call him for food. Hori untied the bullocks and led them to a tree, then said to Siliya, “You too, go and eat something, Siliya. Dhaniya is waiting here. The sari on your back is stained with blood, girl! Be careful, lest the wound fester. Your family is very cruel.”

Siliya looked at him with sorrowful eyes. “Who here is not cruel, Dada? I have not found anyone compassionate.”

“What did the pandit say?”

“He says he has nothing to do with me.”

“Is that so? He says that?”

“He must think he’ll save face this way, but how will he hide what the whole world knows? If my bread is too heavy for them, let them not give it to me. What do I care? I worked as a laborer before, and I’ll do it again. If I ask you for a place the size of a sleeping mat to lie down, will you not give it to me?”

Dhaniya, moved with pity, said, “Is there any shortage of space, daughter? Come, stay at my house.”

Hori said in a pleading voice, “She invites you, but you don’t know the pandit.”

Dhaniya replied fearlessly, “If he gets angry, he’ll just eat one more roti, what else will he do? Am I his servant? He took her honor, got her cast out from the community, and now he says he has nothing to do with her?”

--- Page 206 ---
No. Is he a man or a butcher? This is the fruit of his own intentions. He never thought of it before, and so he went on living as he pleased. Now he says, “What have I to do with it?”

In Hori’s mind, Dhania was in the wrong. The way Siliya’s family had disgraced Matai—this was not right. Whether they took Siliya away by force or with affection, she was their daughter. Why did they have to disgrace Matai?

Dhania rebuked him sharply—“Let it be, don’t act so righteous. All men are the same. When Matai disgraced her, no one thought it was wrong. Now that Matai himself has been disgraced, why does it hurt? Is Siliya’s honor not honor at all? She’s a chamarin, so now they pretend to be so pious and righteous! Harkhu Chaudhary did the right thing. This is the only punishment for such ruffians. Come, Siliya, to my house. Who knows what kind of cruel parents she has, that they’ve beaten the poor girl’s back bloody. You go and send for Sona. I’ll take her with me.”

Hori went off, and Siliya fell at Dhania’s feet, weeping.

:24:

Sona was in her seventeenth year, and this year it was necessary to get her married. Hori had been worrying about this for two years, but with empty hands, he could do nothing. Still, this year, come what may, her marriage must be arranged, even if it meant taking on debt or mortgaging the fields. If it had been up to Hori alone, the marriage would have happened two years ago. He wanted to manage with thrift. But Dhania would say, “No matter how much you tighten your belt, it will take at least two or two and a half hundred rupees.” Ever since Jhunia had come into the house, their standing in the biradari had fallen, and without giving at least a hundred or two hundred rupees, no respectable groom could be found. Last year, at Chaiti, nothing came of it. There was Pandit Datadin’s half-share, but Panditji gave such an account of the seeds and labor that Hori was left with barely a quarter of the grain. And the full rent still had to be paid. The crops of sugarcane and hemp were ruined—hemp because of too much rain, and sugarcane because of termites. But this year’s Chaiti harvest was good, and the sugarcane was thriving. There was grain for the wedding, and if two hundred rupees could be raised, he would be freed from the debt of the daughter’s marriage. If Gobar could help with a hundred rupees, Hori could manage the rest easily. Both Jhinguri Singh and Mangru Sah had softened a bit now. Since Gobar was earning in the city, their money could not be lost.

One day, Hori suggested going to Gobar for two or three days.

But Dhania had not forgotten Gobar’s harsh words. She did not want to take a single paisa from Gobar, not in any way.

Hori said, exasperated, “But how will things work out, tell me that?”

Dhania shook her head and replied, “Suppose Gobar had never gone to the city, what would you have done then? Do that now.”

Hori was silenced. After a moment he said, “I’m only asking you.”

Dhania evaded, “That’s for men to think about.”

Hori was ready with his answer—“Suppose I were not here, you were alone, what would you do then? Do that.”

Dhania looked at him with scornful eyes—“Then, even if I gave away a kusha-daughter, there would be no one to laugh.”

--- Page 207 ---
He could have given a kusha-girl as well. That would have been auspicious for him, but how could he abandon family honor? At the weddings of his sisters, three hundred baraatis had come to the door. The dowry had been generous. There had been music and dance, bands and processions, elephants and horses—all had been present. Even today, his name carried weight in the community. He was on familiar terms with people from ten villages. If he gave a kusha-girl, how would he show his face to anyone? Better to die than to suffer such disgrace. And why should he give a kusha-girl? He had cattle, land, and a little bit of reputation as well. If he sold even one bigha, he could easily get a hundred rupees, but for a farmer, land is dearer than life itself, and he only had three bighas in total. If he sold one, how would he continue farming?

Several days passed in this turmoil. Hori could not come to any decision.

It was the time of the Dussehra holidays. The sons of Jhinguri, Pateshwari, and Nokheram had all come home for the break. All three were studying English, and though each was now twenty years old, none showed any sign of going to university. They lingered in the same class for two or three years at a time. All three were already married. Pateshwari’s son, Vindeshwari, was even the father of a child. The three of them spent their days playing cards, drinking bhang, and strutting about like dandies. Several times a day, they would pass by Hori’s doorway, and by some strange coincidence, every time they did, Sona would also appear at the door for one reason or another. Watching this, Hori’s blood ran cold, as if yellow clouds full of hail were gathering in the sky, ready to destroy his crops.

One day, the three of them went to bathe at the same well where Hori was running the pulley to irrigate his fields. Sona was drawing water. Today, Hori’s blood boiled. That very evening, he went to Dulari the moneylender’s wife. He thought, “Women have compassion; perhaps her heart will soften and she’ll lend me money at a lower interest.” But Dulari began to lament her own troubles. There was not a single household in the village that did not owe her something—even Jhinguri Singh owed her twenty rupees—but no one showed any intention of repaying. Poor woman, where was she to get the money?

Hori pleaded, “Bhabhi, it would be a great act of charity. If you don’t give me the money, you’ll be loosening the noose around my neck. Jhinguri and Pateshwari have their eyes on my fields. I think to myself, this is the only legacy left by my forefathers—if I lose this, where will I go? A true son increases the family’s wealth, but will I become such a worthless one that I squander all that my ancestors earned?”

Dulari swore, “Hori, I swear on Thakurji’s feet, I have nothing right now. Those who borrowed from me refuse to return it—what am I to do? You’re not a stranger. Sona is like my own daughter, but tell me, what can I do? Your own brother Hira took twenty-five rupees for a bullock. He’s nowhere to be found, and if I ask his wife, she’s ready to fight. Shobha seems simple enough, but he doesn’t know how to repay a loan. And the truth is, no one has anything to give—where would they get it from? I see everyone’s condition, and that’s why I hold back. People are barely managing to feed themselves, what more can they do? I won’t advise you to sell your land. Even if you have nothing else, at least you have your honor.”

Then, in a conspiratorial whisper, she added, “Pateshwari Lala’s boy circles your house a lot. All three of them are the same. Be careful with them. They’ve become city-bred, they don’t understand the bonds of village brotherhood. There are other boys in the village, but they have some decency, some respect, some fear. These ones are like stray bulls. My Kausalya came home from her in-laws’, and seeing the way these boys behaved, I called her father-in-law and sent her back right away. Who knows how far things might go?”

Seeing Hori smile, she said with playful reproach, “If you laugh, Hori, then shall I say something? You always find some excuse to come to my shop—”

--- Page 208 ---
…but I never even glanced at him.

Hori replied with gentle protest, “That’s not true, Bhabhi. No one comes without tasting a little sweetness. A bird only returns to the courtyard once it’s found some grain there.”

“Liar!”

“You may not have looked with your eyes, but your heart was always watching—calling, even.”

“Oh, enough! Don’t act like you can read my mind. I only felt pity seeing you hovering around so often; it’s not as if you were some dashing young man.”

Just then, Huseni came to buy a paisa’s worth of salt, and their banter was interrupted. When Huseni left with his salt, Dulari spoke again, “Why don’t you go to Gobar? You’ll get to see him, and perhaps he’ll give you something.”

Hori replied with a despondent heart, “He won’t give anything. As soon as sons start earning a little, their eyes turn away from their parents. I was ready to swallow my pride, but Dhaniya wouldn’t allow it. If I left without her consent, she’d make it impossible for me to live in the house. You know her nature.”

Dulari teased him, “You’ve become your wife’s slave.”

“What could I do if you never asked?”

“If you’d asked me to make you my servant, I would have written it down—truly.”

“Well, what’s stopping you now? Write it down. I’ll sell myself for two hundred rupees—not a high price.”

“But you won’t say a word to Dhaniya, will you?”

“No, I swear I won’t.”

“And if you do?”

“Then cut out my tongue.”

“Alright, then go and put your house in order. I’ll give you the money.”

With tears in his eyes, Hori fell at Dulari’s feet. Overcome with emotion, he could not speak.

The sahuain pulled her feet away, saying, “Now, this mischief I do not like. Within a year, I’ll take my money back with interest, and I’ll make you swear to it. You’re not so reliable in business, but I trust Dhaniya. I’ve heard the Pandit is very angry with you. He says, ‘If I don’t drive him out of the village, I’m no Brahmin.’ Why don’t you throw Silia out? You’ve brought this trouble upon yourself for nothing.”

“Dhaniya keeps her here—what can I do?”

“I heard the Pandit went to Kashi. There’s a renowned scholar there. He’s asking five hundred rupees to perform the purification rites. Is there any justice in this? When religion is lost, do a thousand purifications, what difference does it make? No one will drink water touched by you, no matter how many rites you perform.”

Hori left for home, his heart leaping with joy. He had never felt such happiness in his life. On the way, he stopped at Shobha’s house and invited him to come for the engagement ceremony. Then the two of them went to Dattadin to ask for an auspicious date for the engagement. Returning home, they began making preparations at the doorway.

Dhaniya came out and said, “It’s already past midnight—aren’t you going to eat? Sit down and have your meal. There’s the whole night left for gossiping.”

Hori, wishing to include her in the discussion, said, “I want your advice here…”

--- Page 209 ---
"That’s good. Now tell me, what all needs to be brought? I have no idea."

"If you have no idea, then why are you sitting here for advice? Do you even have the money, or are you just savoring sweets in your mind?"

Hori replied with pride, "Why does that concern you? Just tell me what all things we’ll need to bring."

"Well, I don’t eat such imaginary sweets."

"Then just tell me, what all was brought for our sisters’ weddings?"

"First tell me, have you got the money?"

"Yes, I have. Or do you think I’ve been chewing on bhang?"

"Then come and eat first. We’ll discuss it afterwards."

But when she heard that he had spoken with Dulari, she wrinkled her nose and said, "Has anyone ever managed to pay off a debt to her? That witch charges such steep interest."

"But what else could I do? Who else would lend?"

"Why don’t you just admit you went there for a chance to chat her up? You’ve grown old, but that habit hasn’t left you."

"Dhaniya, sometimes you talk just like a child. Would someone like her laugh and joke with a pauper like me? She doesn’t even speak plainly."

"Who else would go to her except people like you?"

"Even the high and mighty rub their noses at her door, Dhaniya. What would you know? She has Lakshmi on her side."

"The moment she gave you the slightest hope, you ran around spreading the good news."

"She didn’t just give hope, she made a firm promise."

When Hori went to eat his meal and Shobha returned to his own home, Sona came out with Siliya. She had been standing at the door, listening to everything. The fact that two hundred rupees were being borrowed from Dulari for her engagement churned inside her like fresh lime thrown into water. A small lamp burned at the door, blackening the wall above the shelf. Both oxen were eating fodder from the trough, and the dog sat on the ground, waiting for scraps. The two young women came and stood by the oxen’s manger.

Sona said, "Did you hear? Dada is borrowing two hundred rupees from the moneylender woman for my engagement."

Siliya knew every detail of the household. She replied, "If there’s no money at home, what else can they do?"

Sona, gazing at the dark trees ahead, said, "I don’t want to do anything that forces my parents into debt. Where will they get the money, poor things? They’re already weighed down by debt. If they take two hundred more, won’t the burden only grow heavier?"

"Do big people’s weddings ever happen without dowry, silly? Without dowry, you’d only get an old man. Will you go with an old man?"

"Why should I go with an old man? Was Bhaiya old when he brought Jhunia home? Who gave him any dowry?"

"That brought shame to the family name."

"I’ll tell the Sonari family straight out—if you take even a single penny as dowry, I won’t marry you."

Sona’s marriage had been arranged with the son of a wealthy farmer from Sonari.

--- Page 210 ---
“And if he says, ‘What can I do? Your father gives, my father takes, what power do I have in this?’”

The weapon Sona had thought to be a magic arrow now seemed nothing more than a reed stick. Despairing, she said, “I just want to try speaking to him once. If he says he has no power, then is Gomti so far from here? I’ll drown myself. My mother and father raised me with so much hardship—am I to repay them by leaving their house and burdening them with more debt? If God had blessed my parents with wealth, I would not object to whatever they wished to give their daughter. But when they are struggling for every penny, when the mahajan could file a complaint and auction our home any day, and tomorrow we might have to work as laborers—then the only righteous path for a daughter is to drown herself. At least the land and home would be saved, there would still be bread to eat. My parents will mourn me for a few days, but they will find solace. Better that than marrying me off and making them grieve for a lifetime. In three or four years, the two hundred rupees will double—where will Dada get that kind of money?”

It seemed to Siliya as if a new light had dawned in her eyes. In a rush of emotion, she embraced Sona and said, “Where did you learn such wisdom, Sona? You seem so simple and innocent!”

“What wisdom is there in this, you witch? Do I not have eyes, am I mad? They’ll take two hundred for my marriage. In three or four years, that will double. Then for Rupi’s marriage, they’ll ask for another two hundred. Whatever little land we have will be auctioned off, and we’ll be left to beg from door to door. Is that it? Wouldn’t it be better if I just ended my own life? Go at dawn to Sonari and call him—but no, I can’t do the calling. I’d be too ashamed to speak to him myself. You give him my message. Let’s see what he says. Who’s far? He’s just across the river. Sometimes he brings his cattle here. Once his buffalo strayed into my field, and I cursed him soundly. He folded his hands in apology. Tell me, have you met Matai lately? I heard the Brahmins are not accepting him back into the biradari.”

Siliya replied with contempt, “Why wouldn’t they take him back? The old man just doesn’t want to spend the money. If he gets the cash, he’ll swear a false oath on the Ganga. The boy spends his days now loafing about outside the house.”

“Why don’t you leave him? Sit with someone from your own biradari and live in peace. He wouldn’t insult you.”

“Why not, indeed? After all the misery that poor fellow has suffered because of me, should I leave him now? Whether he becomes a pandit or a god, to me he’s still the same Matai who once bowed at my feet. Even if he becomes a Brahmin and marries a Brahmin woman, no one will serve him as I have. For now, he may leave me for the sake of honor and reputation, but you’ll see—he’ll come running back.”

“He’s already come, Siliya. If he could have you, he’d devour you whole.”

“Who’s going to call him? Each to their own dharma. If he’s breaking his, why should I break mine?”

At dawn, Siliya set off towards Sonari, but Hori stopped her. Dhaniya had a headache and someone had to tend the vegetable beds in her place. Siliya couldn’t refuse. She was only free at noon, and then she went to Sonari.

That afternoon, when Hori returned to the well, there was no sign of Siliya. Annoyed, he muttered, “Where has Siliya flown off to? She stays here, but then wanders off somewhere, as if she has no heart for any work.”

--- Page 211 ---
Did you see? Do you know where Sona has gone?

Sona made an excuse—“I don’t know anything. She said she was going to the washerwoman’s house to fetch the laundry, she must have gone there.”

Dhaniya got up from the cot and said, “Come on, I’ll water the beds myself. Why are you getting upset, as if you pay her wages?”

“Doesn’t she live in our house? Aren’t we being maligned in the whole village because of her?”

“Alright, let it be. She’s lying in some corner—are you going to charge her rent for that?”

“She’s not lying in a corner, she’s taken up an entire room.”

“So the rent for that room must be fifty rupees a month?”

“Even a single paisa would be fair. She lives in our house; wherever she goes, she should ask permission. If she comes today, I’ll have a word with her.”

The group began to move. Hori did not let Dhaniya go. Rupa was watering the garden beds, and Sona was carrying the soil. Rupa was shaping the damp earth into stoves and pots, while Sona, with anxious eyes, kept glancing towards Sonari. There was doubt in her heart, but also hope. The doubt was greater, the hope less. She thought, “If those two are getting money, why would they let go? Those who have money, value it even more. And Gauri Mahto is greedy above all. Mathura has compassion, has a sense of right and wrong, but whatever his father wishes, that’s what he’ll have to accept. But Sona will scold them so sharply that they’ll remember it. She’ll say plainly, ‘Go marry some rich man’s daughter; I cannot live with a man like you.’ If by chance Gauri Mahto agrees, she’ll wash his feet and drink that water. She’ll serve him in a way she never even served her own father, and she’ll treat Siliya to sweets until she’s full. The rupee that Gobar had given her, she still kept safe. At this tender thought, her eyes sparkled and a faint blush ran across her cheeks.”

But why hasn’t Siliya come yet? It’s not as if it’s far. They must not have let her come. Ah! There she is, but she’s coming so slowly. Sona’s heart sank. The unlucky ones must not have agreed, otherwise Siliya would have come running. Then Sona’s marriage is as good as done. Wash your face and be ready.

Siliya did come, but instead of coming to the well, she began to water the beds in the field. She was afraid—if Hori asked where she had been all this time, what would she say? Sona passed those two hours with great difficulty. As soon as the group dispersed, she ran to Siliya.

“Where had you gone, did you die somewhere? My eyes are sore from looking for you!”

Siliya felt offended—“Did you think I was sleeping there? You can’t have such conversations in the open. You have to wait for the right moment. Mathura had gone to graze the cattle by the river. I searched him out and gave him your message. He was so happy, what can I say? He fell at my feet and said, ‘Sillo, ever since I heard Sona is coming to my house, I haven’t slept a wink.’ His curses have come back to haunt me, but what can I do about his father? He listens to no one.”

Sona interrupted, “Then let him not listen. Sona is stubborn too. What I’ve said, I’ll do. Then they’ll be left wringing their hands.”

“Right then, he left the cattle there and took me straight to Gauri Mahto. Mahto has four households. The well is his too. He has ten bighas of sugarcane. When I saw Mahto, I had to laugh. He looked like a grass-cutter. Yes, he is the master of his fortune. There was a fierce argument between father and son. Gauri Mahto said, ‘What does it matter to you, whether I take something or not? Who are you to speak?’ Mathura said, ‘What concern—

--- Page 212 ---
“If that is so, then don’t arrange my marriage—I will marry as I wish.” The quarrel escalated, and Gauri Mahto took off his wooden sandals and beat Mathura soundly. Any other boy, after such a thrashing, would have rebelled. Mathura, if he had landed even a single punch, would have flattened Mahto for good. But the poor fellow, after receiving dozens of blows, said nothing. With tears in his eyes, he looked at me like a helpless soul and walked away. Then Mahto turned his anger on me. He hurled hundreds of abuses, but why should I listen? What did I have to fear from him? I said plainly, “Mahto, two or three hundred rupees is not a great sum, and Hori Mahto will not be sold for so little. You will not become rich, and all that money will be squandered on amusements. But you will not find such a daughter-in-law again.”

With moist eyes, Sona asked, “Mahto started beating him over such a small matter?”

Siliya had kept this incident hidden. She did not want to let such a humiliating thing reach Sona’s ears, but on hearing this question, she could not restrain herself. She said, “It was the same old story about the cow dung. Mahto said, ‘A man eats what is impure only if it is sweet. Only silver can wash away a stain.’ At this, Mathura replied, ‘Uncle, which house is free from blemish? Some are exposed, some are hidden. Gauri Mahto himself was once involved with a Chamarin. He has two sons from her.’ No sooner had these words left Mathura’s mouth than it was as if a demon possessed the old man. As greedy as he is, he is just as quick to anger. He will not rest until he gets his way.”

The two women headed home. Sona carried a heavy load of the yoke, rope, and plough on her head, but at that moment, it felt lighter than a flower. It was as if a spring of joy and energy had burst forth within her. Mathura’s brave image stood before her, and she felt as though she had seated him in her heart and was washing his feet with her tears. It seemed as if the goddesses of the sky had lifted her in their arms and were carrying her away in the crimson glow of the heavens.

That very night, Sona was seized by a high fever.

On the third day, Gauri Mahto sent a letter through the barber:

“Blessings and greetings to the most esteemed Hori Mahto from Gauriram. Regarding the dowry discussions that took place between us, we have reflected calmly and realized that such exchanges burden both families of the bride and groom. Now that our alliance is settled, we should act in a way that offends no one. Do not worry about dowry—we swear it to you. Whatever simple fare you can arrange for the wedding guests, serve them that. We will not ask for more. We have made arrangements for provisions ourselves. Whatever hospitality you extend out of happiness, we will accept it with bowed heads.”

Hori read the letter and rushed inside to read it to Dhaniya. He was so overjoyed he could have leapt for joy, but Dhaniya sat lost in thought. After a moment, she said, “This is Gauri Mahto’s goodness, but we too must uphold our honor. What will the world say? Money is but dust in the hand. One does not forsake family honor for it. We will give whatever we can, and Gauri Mahto must accept it. Write this as your reply. Does a daughter have no right to her parents’ earnings? No, there is no need to write—come, I will send the message with the barber myself.”

Hori stood bewildered in the courtyard while Dhaniya, moved by the generosity Gauri Mahto’s decency had awakened in her, dictated the message. Then she gave the barber some sweet drink and sent him off with a proper farewell.

When he had gone, Hori said, “What have you done, Dhaniya? I have never understood your ways. You move forward and backward at the same time. First, you argued that—”

--- Page 213 ---
Do not borrow even a single paisa from anyone; there is no question of giving or taking anything. And when God, sitting inside Gauri, made her write that letter, you started singing the tune of family honor. Only God knows your true intentions.

Dhaniya said, “A betel leaf is offered after seeing the face, don’t you know that? Back then, Gauri used to show her pride, now she shows her decency. A stone should answer a brick, but a greeting should not be answered with an insult.”

Hori wrinkled his nose and said, “Then show your decency. Let’s see, where will you bring the money from?”

Dhaniya’s eyes flashed as she replied, “It’s not my job to bring money, it’s yours.”

“I’ll take it from Dulari alone.”

“Go ahead, take it from her. Everyone will charge interest. If you’re going to drown anyway, what difference does it make whether it’s in a pond or the Ganga?”

Hori went outside and began to smoke his chilam. How easily he could have freed himself from this burden, but Dhaniya would never let him be. Whenever you look, she’s always going against the grain. It’s as if she’s possessed by some spirit. Even after seeing the state of the house, her eyes refuse to open.

Bhola, meanwhile, had brought home a second wife. Life without a woman was barren for him. When Jhunia was there, she would serve him his hookah and water, call him to meals on time. Now he was like an orphan. The daughters-in-law never got a break from housework—how could they attend to him? So, a new marriage had become absolutely necessary. By chance, he found a young widow whose husband had died only three months before. She had a son as well. Bhola’s mouth watered. He quickly seized his prey. Until the marriage was settled, he dug up her house.

Until now, whatever was in his house belonged to the daughters-in-law. They did as they pleased, lived as they liked. Ever since Jangi had taken his wife to Lucknow, Kamta’s wife had become the mistress of the house. In just five or six months, she had managed to squirrel away thirty or forty rupees for herself. She would secretly sell a seer or half-seer of milk and curd. Now, her stepmother-in-law became the new mistress. The daughter-in-law resented this control, and quarrels broke out between them every day. Things escalated to the point that even Bhola and Kamta began to argue because of the women. The fight grew so heated that it came to the brink of separation, and it is an ancient custom that at the time of separation, there must be a beating. That custom was followed here as well.

Kamta was a young man. Whatever authority Bhola had over him was only as a father, but after bringing home a new wife, he had no right to expect respect from his son. At least, Kamta would not accept it. He threw Bhola down and kicked him several times, then threw him out of the house. He wasn’t allowed to touch any of the household things. Not a single villager took Bhola’s side. The new marriage had made him a laughingstock. He spent the night somehow under a tree, and as soon as morning came, he went to Nokheram and poured out his complaint. Bhola’s village was also in Nokheram’s territory, and whatever authority there was in the whole area, Nokheram held it. Nokheram felt no pity for Bhola, but when he saw the lively, colorful woman with him, he immediately agreed to give them shelter. He gave them a room to live in where his cows were tied. Suddenly, he felt the need for an experienced man to look after his animals and fodder. He hired Bhola as a servant for three rupees a month and a seer of grain daily.

Godan: 204

--- Page 214 ---
Nokheram was a short, stout, balding man, with a long nose and small, dark eyes. He wore a large turban, a short kurta, and in winter, would wrap himself in a quilted blanket when going outside. He took great pleasure in having oil massages, which was why his clothes were always dirty and stained. His family was large—seven brothers and all their children depended on him. Yet his own son studied English in the ninth grade, and his daughter-in-law’s job at the office was not an easy one. From the Rai Sahib, he received only twelve rupees as salary, but he spared no effort in claiming every possible allowance for himself. So if a debtor ever fell into his clutches, he would not let go until he had squeezed every last paisa from him. Earlier, when his salary was only six rupees, he was not so greedy with the tenants. But ever since it had been raised to twelve, his hunger had only grown, and that was why he never got promoted.

In the village, everyone acknowledged his authority in some form or another—even Jhinguri Singh flattered him. Only Pateshwari was wary of him. Nokheram was proud of being a Brahmin and believed he could never be put in the dock, while Pateshwari took pride in being a Kayasth, a master of the pen. “What can he do to me?” he thought. “I am not the zamindar’s servant, but a servant of the government. He has no power over me.” If Nokheram observed the Ekadashi fast and presided over the panchayat, Pateshwari would listen to the Satyanarayan Katha every full moon and put his faith in it. His eldest son was already married; if his younger son passed his tenth standard, he would find him a job as a copyist somewhere too. He always greeted people with a show of respect. There was, however, one rumor about him—that he kept his widowed kaharin as his mistress.

Now, it seemed, the opportunity had come to settle old scores.

He said to Bhola, trying to reassure him, “Why have you come here? If you need anything, just come to me. I’ll find some work for you—maybe storing grain in the barns or something.”

Bhola pleaded, “Sarkar, I have a petition. Is this how we should be treated? We built the houses, and now we are being thrown out. Is this not injustice? The panchayat should decide this.”

Nokheram explained, “If someone has done wrong, God will punish him. Why else is this world called a place of suffering? God knows every grain of truth. He is called the knower of hearts. So don’t be worse than him. Get up from here and send them away.”

He heard the voices of children—his own daughter, the daughter of his cowherd, whose parents had died. For her dowry, he was offering two hundred rupees.

--- Page 215 ---
Kamta did drive the old man out, but now he began to realize just how hardworking the old fellow had been. Rising at dawn to feed and water the cattle, milking the cows, then taking the milk to the market, returning to feed and water the cattle again, and milking once more—within a fortnight, Kamta’s whole appearance had changed. Quarrels broke out between husband and wife. His wife said, “I did not come to your house to give up my life. If my bread is too heavy for you, I’ll return to my father’s home.” Kamta grew afraid—if she left, there would be no one to cook, and he would have to pound the grain himself. In the end, he hired a servant, but that did not work out either. The servant began stealing chaff and bran to sell. He was dismissed. Again, husband and wife quarreled. This time, his wife left in anger for her parental home. Kamta was at his wits’ end. Helpless, he went to Bhola and pleaded, “Dada, forgive whatever mistakes I have made. Please come back and manage the house. I will live just as you wish.”

Bhola found it galling to live here as a laborer. The hospitality he had received in the first month or two was gone. Now Nokheram would sometimes even ask him to fill the hookah or lay out the cot. Poor Bhola would swallow his resentment like poison. Even if there were quarrels and strife in his own home, at least he would not have to wait on anyone.

When his wife, Nohri, heard of this proposal, she retorted haughtily, “You want to go back to the place you were kicked out of? Have you no shame?”

Bhola replied, “And am I sitting on a throne here?”

Nohri tossed her head and said, “If you want to go, go. I’m not going.”

Bhola knew Nohri would object, and with good reason. Here, no one cared for him, but Nohri was treated with great respect. Even the constables and officials deferred to her. Hearing her reply, Bhola grew angry, but what could he do? If he had the courage to leave her behind, Nohri would have had no choice but to follow him. Nokheram did not have the nerve to keep her alone under his roof; he was a man who hunted from behind a screen, but Nohri was well acquainted with Bhola’s nature.

Bhola pleaded, “Listen, Nohri, don’t make trouble. There are no daughters-in-law left there now. Everything will be in your hands. Think of the disgrace in the community from working as a laborer here.”

Nohri, waving him off, said, “If you want to go, go. I’m not stopping you. Maybe you love your son’s kicks, but I don’t. I am content with my own labor.”

Bhola was forced to stay, and Kamta, after much cajoling, managed to persuade his wife to return. Meanwhile, rumors swirled about Nohri—Nohri wore a pink sari today. Now, what is there to ask? She could wear a new sari every day if she wished. “Now that her beloved is the kotwal, what has she to fear? Has Bhola gone blind?” Shobha was a great joker, the village clown, even a Narad of sorts. He was always sniffing out gossip. One day, he came across Nohri at home and made some joke. Nohri complained to Nokheram, and Shobha was summoned to the village square and scolded so harshly he would never forget it.

One day, misfortune befell Lala Pateshwariprasad. It was summer. Lala was having mangoes plucked in his orchard. Nohri, dressed up, happened to pass by. Lala called out, “Nohri Rani, come here, take some mangoes—they are very sweet.”

Nohri thought Lala was mocking her. She had grown proud now. She wanted people to see her as the zamindar’s wife and treat her with respect. The proud are often suspicious, and when there is guilt in the heart, suspicion grows even more. Why did he laugh looking at me?
