She would scold even the most respectable tenants. Not just the tenants—now she had begun to assert herself even over the clerk sahib.

Bhola no longer wished to remain dependent on her. In his eyes, there was no work more disgraceful than living off a woman’s earnings. He received only three rupees a month, and even that never reached his hands. Nohri would spend it all before he saw a paisa. He could not scrape together even a farthing for tobacco, while Nohri chewed betel worth two annas every day. Whoever saw him, would order him about. The bailiffs made him fill their hookahs, chop wood; the poor man, exhausted from a day’s labor, would come home and collapse on a string cot under the tree at the door. There was not a soul to offer him even a cup of water. He had to eat stale bread from the afternoon for dinner, and that too with only salt, or water and salt.

At last, defeated, he decided to return home and live with Kamta. Whatever may come, at least he would get a hard piece of bread, and it would be his own home.

Nohri said, “I will not go there to be anyone’s servant.”

Bhola steeled himself and replied, “I’m not telling you to go. I’m talking about myself.”

“You’ll leave me and go? Aren’t you ashamed to say that?”

“I’ve swallowed my shame.”

“But I haven’t given up my own shame. You cannot leave me.”

“You follow your own will—why should I be your slave?”

“I’ll call a panchayat and blacken your face, remember that.”

“Is there any less shame on me already? Do you still want to keep me deceived?”

“You’re putting on airs as if you buy me jewelry every day! Well, Nohri is not one to tolerate anyone’s airs.”

Bhola, exasperated, got up and took a stick from beside his pillow, but Nohri leapt and grabbed his wrist. It was impossible for Bhola to break free from her strong grip. He sat down quietly, like a prisoner. There was a time when he could make women dance to his tune, and now he was bound in the snare of a single woman, unable to escape. He did not wish to expose his weakness by struggling to free his hand. He had realized his own limits, but why could he not speak to her fearlessly and say, “You are not right for me, I renounce you.” She threatens him with the panchayat? What is the panchayat but a bogeyman? If she is not afraid of the panchayat, why should he be?

But he did not have the courage to give these feelings voice. It was as though Nohri had cast a spell over him.

: 26 :

Lala Pateshwari was the very embodiment of the virtues of the patwari community. He could not bear to see any tenant encroach even an inch upon another brother’s land. Nor could he tolerate a tenant withholding a mahajan’s money. The protection of the interests of all the village folk was his highest duty. He had no faith in compromise or conciliation—those were signs of lifelessness. He was a worshipper of conflict, which is the mark of vitality. He was always seeking to inject excitement into this life. He would set off one firecracker or another every day. These days, Mangru Sah was the special object of his attention.

Mangru Sah was the richest man in the village, but took no part at all in local politics. He had no craving for authority or status. His house, too, was outside the village, where he had an orchard and...

--- Page 218 ---
He had built a well and a small Shiva temple. He had no children, so he had reduced his moneylending and spent most of his time in worship and prayer. Many tenants had swallowed his money, but he never filed complaints or made demands. Hori too owed him about a hundred and fifty rupees, including interest, but neither was Hori anxious to repay the debt, nor was he eager to recover it. He had asked a few times, even scolded and threatened, but seeing Hori’s condition, he fell silent. Now, by chance, Hori’s sugarcane crop was the best in the village. People estimated that, at the very least, he would make two to two and a half hundred rupees from it. Pateshwari Prasad suggested to Mangru that if a claim was filed against Hori at this time, all the money could be recovered. Mangru was not so much kind as he was lazy. He did not want to get involved in any hassle, but when Pateshwari assured him that he would not have to go to court even for a day, nor suffer any other trouble, and that he would get his decree while sitting at home, he gave permission to file the case and even provided money for court expenses.

Hori had no idea what was brewing behind his back. He did not know when the case was filed, when the decree was passed. He only found out when the bailiff came to auction his sugarcane crop. The whole village gathered at the edge of the field. Hori ran to Mangru Sah, and Dhaniya began cursing Pateshwari. Her simple intuition told her that this was Pateshwari’s doing, but Mangru Sah was busy with his prayers and could not be found, and even after showering Pateshwari with abuses, Dhaniya could do nothing to him. Meanwhile, the sugarcane was auctioned off for a hundred and fifty rupees, and the bid was won by Mangru Sah himself. No one else dared to bid. Even Dattadin did not have the courage to listen to Dhaniya’s curses.

Dhaniya, agitated, said to Hori, “Why are you just sitting there? Why don’t you go and ask the patwari? Is this your sense of duty towards the people of your own village?”

Hori replied meekly, “You have a mouth to ask, don’t you? Haven’t they heard your curses?”

“Whoever acts in a way to deserve curses will surely get them.”

“You’ll curse them and still maintain brotherhood?”

“I’ll see who dares come near my field!”

“The buyers will come and cut the crop. What will you do, and what will I do? You may satisfy the itch of your tongue by cursing.”

“Will anyone dare to cut my crop while I’m alive?”

“Yes, yes, even if you and I are alive, the whole village together couldn’t stop them. That crop is no longer ours, it belongs to Mangru Sah now.”

“Did Mangru Sah toil in the blazing sun of Jeth, irrigating and weeding?”

“You did all that, but now the crop belongs to Mangru Sah. Aren’t we indebted to him?”

The sugarcane was gone, but with it came a new problem. Dulari had agreed to lend money only against this crop. Now, on what security would she lend? Hori already owed her two hundred rupees. She had thought that once the old debt was repaid from the sugarcane, a new account could begin. In her eyes, Hori’s credit was good only up to two hundred rupees. Lending more would be risky. The wedding season was upon them. The date was fixed. Gauri Mahto must have completed all the preparations. Now, postponing the marriage was impossible. Hori felt such rage that he wanted to strangle Dulari. He had pleaded and begged as much as he could, but the stone-hearted woman was unmoved. At last, folding his hands as he was leaving, he said, “Dulari, I won’t run away with your money. Nor am I about to die so soon. I have fields, trees, a house—”

--- Page 219 ---
She would scold even the most respectable tenants. Not only the tenants, now she had begun to assert herself even over the clerk sahib.

Bhola did not wish to remain dependent on her. In his eyes, there was no work more degrading than living off a woman’s earnings. He received only three rupees a month, and even that never reached his hands. Nohri would take it all before he could touch it. He could not even afford a pinch of tobacco, while Nohri would spend two annas daily on betel leaves. Whoever you saw, everyone would boss him around. The orderlies would make him fill their hookahs, chop wood; the poor fellow, exhausted after a day’s labor, would come home and lie down on a string cot under the tree at the door. There was not even a soul to bring him a cup of water. He had to eat stale bread from the afternoon for dinner, and that too with nothing but salt, or water and salt.

At last, defeated, he decided to go back home and live with Kamta. If nothing else, he would at least get a piece of bread; after all, it was his own home.

Nohri said, “I will not go there to be anyone’s servant.”

Bhola steeled himself and said, “I’m not telling you to go. I’m talking about myself.”

“You’ll leave me and go? Aren’t you ashamed to say that?”

“I have swallowed my shame.”

“But I have not given up my own shame. You cannot leave me.”

“You follow your own mind, so why should I be your slave?”

“I’ll call a panchayat and blacken your face, remember that.”

“Is there not enough blackening already? Do you still want to keep me deceived?”

“You act as if you buy me jewelry every day! Nohri is not one to tolerate anyone’s airs.”

Bhola, exasperated, got up and picked up a stick from beside his pillow, but Nohri sprang up and grabbed his arm. It was impossible for Bhola to break free from her strong grip. He sat down quietly, like a prisoner. There was a time when he could make women dance on his fingertips; now he was bound in the snare of a single woman and could not escape. He tried to free his hand, but did not want to pull back the curtain. He had realized his own limits, but why could he not boldly say to her, “You are of no use to me, I renounce you. You threaten me with the panchayat? Is the panchayat some bogeyman? If you are not afraid of the panchayat, why should I be?”

But he could not muster the courage to put these feelings into words. It was as if Nohri had cast a spell over him.

Lala Pateshwari was the very embodiment of the virtues of the patwari community. He could not bear to see any tenant encroach even an inch upon another brother’s land. Nor could he bear to see a tenant cheat a mahajan out of his money. To protect the interests of all the villagers was his highest duty. He had no faith in compromise or reconciliation; those were signs of lifelessness. He was a devotee of conflict, which is the mark of vitality. He was always trying to stir up excitement in this life. Every now and then, he would set off some spark. These days, his special favor rested on Mangru Sah.

Mangru Sah was the richest man in the village, but he took no part at all in local politics. He had no desire for power or authority. His house, too, was outside the village, where he had an orchard and...

--- Page 220 ---
"Will he send it? This is the second year, not a single letter."

Just then, Sona came in, carrying a bundle of fresh grass on her head for the cattle. She moved with the innocence of a young girl, trying to hide her budding youth beneath her veil. She dropped the bundle there and went inside.

Nohri said, "The girl has grown up so much."

Dhaniya replied, "A girl's growth is like the rise of a flood. But really, how many days has it been?"

"The groom is settled, isn't he?"

"Yes, the groom is settled. If we can arrange the money, we’ll have the wedding this very month."

Nohri was not small-hearted. The little money she had managed to save was burning in her pocket. If she could give some for Sona’s wedding, what honor it would bring her! The whole village would talk about her. People would be astonished and say, "Nohri gave so much money. She’s a great lady." Hori and Dhaniya would sing her praises from house to house. Her respect in the village would increase so much. She would silence those who pointed fingers at her. Who would dare laugh at her or taunt her then? Right now, the whole village was her enemy. Then, the whole village would become her friend. This thought brought a glow to her face.

"If a little will help, take it from me. When you have the money in hand, return it."

Both Hori and Dhaniya looked at her. No, Nohri was not joking. In their eyes was surprise, gratitude, doubt, and a touch of shame. Nohri was not as bad as people thought.

Nohri spoke again, "Your honor and mine are one. If you are shamed, am I not shamed too? Whatever happens, now you are our in-laws."

Hori said hesitantly, "Your money is as good as in our house. When we need it, we’ll take it. A man relies on his own, but if we can arrange from outside, why touch the money at home?"

Dhaniya agreed, "Yes, of course."

Nohri showed her affection, "When there’s money at home, why beg from outsiders? You’ll have to pay interest, sign papers, bring witnesses, pay commissions, flatter people. But if my money is tainted, that’s another matter."

Hori reassured her, "No, no, Nohri, if we can manage at home, why stretch a hand outside? But it’s a matter between us. Farming is uncertain. If you need money urgently and we can’t arrange it, you’ll feel bad and we’ll be in trouble too. That’s why I said it. After all, the girl is yours too."

"I don’t need the money urgently."

"Then we’ll take it from you. Why should the merit of the daughter’s wedding go outside?"

"How much do you need?"

"How much do you need?"

"How much can you give?"

"Will a hundred be enough?"

Hori was tempted. If God has showered money upon them, why not take as much as possible?

"That will be more than enough. We have grain at home, but Thakurain, let me tell you today, I never thought you were such a Lakshmi. In these times, who helps whom, and who has anything to give? You have saved me from drowning."

--- Page 221 ---
The time for lighting lamps had come. A chill was settling in. The earth had draped itself in a blue sheet.

Dhaniya went inside and brought out the brazier. Everyone began to warm themselves. In the straw-lit glow, Chabili, Rangili, the fallen woman Nohri, sat before them like a blessing. At this moment, how much kindness shone in her eyes, how much modesty on her cheeks, how much noble inspiration on her lips!

After some idle conversation, Nohri stood up and, saying, “It’s getting late now. Come tomorrow and collect the money, Mahto,” she started for home.

“Let me walk you home,” Hori offered.

“No, no, you stay. I’ll go by myself.”

“My heart wishes to carry you on my shoulders,” he said.

Nokheram’s chaupal was at the other end of the village, and the path outside was clear. Both set out along that way. Now, all around, there was silence.

Nohri said, “You should talk some sense into Rawat. Why does he always quarrel with everyone? If we have to live among these people, shouldn’t we live so that a few become our own? But he fights with everyone, argues with everyone. When you can’t keep me in purdah, when I have to work for others, how can it be that I neither laugh nor speak with anyone, nor anyone looks at me or smiles? All that is only possible in purdah. Tell me, if someone looks at me or stares, what am I to do? I can’t gouge out his eyes. Besides, a hundred things are accomplished through friendliness. Behave according to the times. Once, an elephant swayed in your courtyard—what use is that to you now? Now you’re a laborer for three rupees. We used to have a buffalo at home, but now I’m a laboring woman. But he understands nothing. Sometimes he thinks of living with the children, sometimes of going to live in Lucknow. He’s made my life miserable.”

Hori tried to appease her. “This is pure foolishness on Bhola’s part. He’s grown old; he should understand by now. I’ll talk to him.”

“Then come in the morning, I’ll give you the money.”

“Shouldn’t we write something down...?”

“I know you won’t swallow my money.”

Her house arrived. She went inside. Hori returned home.

:27:

When Gobar came to the city, he found that another vendor had taken the spot where he used to set up his cart, and the customers had now forgotten him. The house, too, now felt like a cage. Jhuniya would sit alone inside and weep. The boy was used to playing all day in the courtyard or at the door. Here, there was no place for him to play. Where could he go? There was barely a yard-wide path at the entrance. Foul smells drifted in. In the heat, there was nowhere to sit or lie outside. The boy would not leave his mother for even a moment. And when there was nothing to play with, what else could he do but eat or drink milk? At home, sometimes Dhaniya played with him, sometimes Rupa, sometimes Sona, sometimes Hori, sometimes Puniya. Here, Jhuniya was alone, and she had to do all the housework.

And Gobar, intoxicated with youth, was lost in his unfulfilled desires, longing to drown in the sea of sensual pleasures. He could not settle his mind to any task. He would take out his cart, but return within an hour.

--- Page 222 ---
There was nothing else for entertainment. The neighboring laborers and tonga drivers played cards and gambled through the night. He, too, used to play at first. But now, for him, amusement meant only laughter and dalliance with Jhunia. In a few days, Jhunia grew weary of this life. She longed to go somewhere secluded, to sit in peace, to lie down and sleep without care, but such solitude was nowhere to be found. Now, she grew angry with Gobar. He had painted such an alluring picture of city life, and here, there was nothing but this dark dungeon. She even grew irritated with the child. Sometimes she would beat him and throw him outside, then bolt the door from within. The boy would cry himself hoarse.

On top of all this, she was expecting another child. No one to turn to, neither ahead nor behind. She often suffered from headaches. She had lost all appetite for food. She would lie in a stupor, silent in a corner, wishing no one would speak to her—but here, Gobar’s relentless affection kept knocking at her door. There was not a drop of milk left in her breasts, yet Lallu clung to her chest. Her body had grown weak, and so had her spirit. Whatever resolve she made, she would break at the slightest insistence. She would be lying down, and Lallu would come and forcibly climb onto her chest, taking her nipple in his mouth and chewing. He was now two years old, with sharp teeth. When no milk came, he would, in anger, bite her breast, but Jhunia no longer had the strength even to push him away. She felt as if death stood before her at every moment. She felt no affection for husband or son. Everyone was a friend only for their own gain.

During the rainy season, when Lallu began to suffer from diarrhea and stopped drinking milk, Jhunia felt as if a great calamity had been lifted from her head. But when, after a week, the child died, his memory, now alive with motherly love, began to make her weep.

And when, only a week after Lallu’s death, Gobar again began to press his demands, she burned with anger and said, “How much of an animal are you?”

Now, Lallu’s memory was dearer to Jhunia than Lallu himself had ever been. When he was before her, the pain he caused her far outweighed the joy. Now, Lallu dwelled within her heart—calm, steady, gentle, smiling. In her imagination, there was now a bliss tinged with pain, without the dark shadow of reality. The Lallu outside had been only a reflection of the Lallu within. The reflection was gone, which was false, unstable. The true form was within her, alive with her hopes and good wishes. In place of milk, she was now raising him on her own blood. She was no longer aware of the closed room, the foul air, or the daily burning in smoke. That memory, seated within her, kept giving her strength. In life, he had been a burden; in death, he had merged into her soul. All her motherly love had turned inward, leaving her indifferent to the world outside. Whether Gobar came home late or early, whether he ate with relish or not, whether he was happy or sad—none of it mattered to her anymore. How much Gobar earned or how he spent it—she cared nothing for it. Her life, whatever it was, was within her; outwardly, she was but a shell.

By sharing in her sorrow, by entering her inner life, Gobar could have come close to her, could have become a part of her existence, but he only came to the barren shore of her outer life and returned, unfulfilled.

One day, in a harsh voice, he said, “So, how long will you go on mourning for Lallu? It’s been four or five months now.”

Jhunia sighed deeply and replied, “You cannot understand my pain. Go about your work. Let me be as I am.”

--- Page 223 ---
“Will Lallu return just because you keep crying?”

Jhunia had no answer to this. She rose and went to boil potatoes in the pot for kachalu. She had never thought Gobar’s heart could be so stone-like. This heartlessness made Lallu even dearer to her. Lallu was hers alone, no one else had any claim, no one else had any share in him. Until now, Lallu had existed partly outside her heart as well, and Gobar’s heart had held some light for him. But now, he was entirely hers.

Disheartened by his failure with the handcart, Gobar had taken up a job at the sugar mill. Mr. Khanna had already— Disheartened by his failure with the handcart, Gobar had taken up a job at the sugar mill. Mr. Khanna had already— After a day’s work, when Gobar returned home at dusk, there was not a trace of fatigue in his body. Sometimes, he would even laugh and chat. Then, out in the open fields, beneath the boundless sky, it was as if all his losses were restored. However much his body worked there, his mind remained free. Here, though the labor was less, the tumult, the constant motion, and the stormy noise seemed to weigh on him like a stupor. There was always the fear that he might be scolded at any moment. This was the condition of all the workers. All of them drowned their bodily exhaustion and mental despair in toddy or liquor. Gobar too developed a taste for drink. He would come home drunk, late at night, and find some excuse to hurl abuses at Jhunia, threaten to throw her out of the house, and sometimes even beat her.

Now, Jhunia began to suspect that she was nothing but a kept woman, and that was why she was being insulted so. Had she been a wedded wife, would Gobar have dared to treat her this way? The community would have punished him, cut him off from water and hookah. How great a mistake she had made, running away from home with this deceitful man. She had become the laughingstock of the world, and gained nothing in return. She began to see Gobar as her enemy. She cared neither for his food nor her own. When Gobar beat her, such rage would seize her that she felt like slitting his throat with a knife. As her pregnancy advanced, her anxiety grew—who would care for her? And if Gobar continued to beat her like this, her life would become hell.

One day, when she went to the well to fetch water, a neighbor woman asked, “How many months along are you?”

“I haven’t really counted,” Jhunia replied.

The woman was short, dark-skinned, stout, with large breasts and an ungainly appearance. Her husband worked as a porter, and she herself ran a firewood shop. Jhunia had bought wood from her several times. That was the extent of their acquaintance.

The woman smiled and said, “Seems to me your time is up. It’ll happen today or tomorrow. Have you arranged for a midwife?”

Jhunia answered in a frightened voice, “I don’t know anyone here.”

“What sort of husband is yours, sitting with oil in his ears?”

“What does he care for me?”

“Yes, I can see that. You’ll be in confinement, but you need someone to help you.”

“For me, everyone is dead.”

She brought the water back and began to wash the dirty dishes, her heart pounding with the fear of childbirth. She began to wonder—What will happen, O God? Hmph! What will happen? I’ll die, that’s all. That would be for the best.

--- Page 224 ---
I will go.

In the evening, she began to feel pain in her stomach. She understood—the hour of misfortune had arrived. Clutching her belly with one hand, drenched in sweat, she lit the stove, put on some khichdi, and, tormented by pain, lay down right there on the floor. Around ten at night, Gobar returned, reeking of toddy. His tongue slurred, he rambled incoherently—“I don’t care about anyone. Whoever needs me a hundred times over, let them stay; if not, let them leave. I can’t stand anyone’s arrogance. I never tolerated my own parents’ pride, those who gave me birth, so why should I tolerate it from others? The jamadar tries to glare at me. There’s no one here who will put up with anyone’s swagger. If people hadn’t held me back, I would have drunk his blood, his blood! Tomorrow I’ll see that boy—so what if I’m hanged? I’ll show them how a man dies. Let me go to the gallows laughing, proud, twirling my mustache! And women—how unfaithful they are! She’s just thrown on some khichdi and gone to sleep, legs stretched out. Whether anyone eats or not, what does she care? She enjoys her fulkas, but for me—khichdi! Torment me as much as you can, but God will torment you, He who is just.”

He didn’t wake Jhunia. He said nothing to her. Quietly, he served himself some khichdi in a plate and, swallowing a few mouthfuls, lay down in the verandah. In the last quarter of the night, he felt cold. When he went into the room to fetch a blanket, he heard Jhunia groaning. His drunkenness had faded. He asked, “How are you feeling, Jhunia? Are you in pain somewhere?”

“Yes, there’s a sharp pain in my stomach.”

“Why didn’t you say so before? Where am I to go at this hour?”

“Who was I to tell?”

“Was I dead?”

“What do you care whether I live or die?”

Gobar panicked—where could he find a midwife now? Why did she have to come at this hour? There was nothing in the house either. If this witch had told him earlier, he could have borrowed two or four rupees from someone. Once, he always had a hundred or fifty rupees in his hands, four people would flatter him. But ever since this ill-fated woman arrived, it was as if Lakshmi herself had turned away. Now he was reduced to begging for every penny.

Suddenly, someone called out, “Is that your wife groaning? Is she in pain?”

It was the same stout woman with whom Jhunia had spoken earlier that day. She had gotten up to feed the horse and, hearing Jhunia’s moans, had come to inquire.

Gobar went to the verandah and said, “She has a stomachache. She’s writhing in pain. Is there a midwife around here?”

“I guessed as much when I saw her today. The dai lives in Kacchi Sarai. Run and fetch her. Tell her to come quickly. I’ll stay here in the meantime.”

“I haven’t seen Kacchi Sarai. Which way is it?”

“All right, you keep fanning her, I’ll go and bring her. That’s what they say—an inexperienced man is good for nothing. A full belly, yet no news of the midwife.”

Saying this, she left. People called her Chuhiya to her face—that was her name—but behind her back, they called her Motalli, the Fat One. If she ever heard someone call her Motalli, she would curse their ancestors for seven generations.

Gobar had barely sat for ten minutes when she returned and said, “How are the poor supposed to survive in this world? The widow says she’ll come only if she gets five rupees, and eight annas a day. And on the twelfth day, a sari. I told her, I’ll burn your mouth! Go jump in the stove! I’ll see to it myself. On the twelfth day, a sari! I told her, I’ll burn your mouth! Go jump in the stove! I’ll see to it myself. On the twelfth—”

No, I am not done yet. You come outside, Govardhan, I will take care of everything. In times of need, only a human stands by another human. You’ve given birth to four children, and now the midwife sits in the forest.

She went and sat beside Jhunia, placed Jhunia’s head on her lap, and gently stroked her belly.  
Chuhia said, “The moment I saw you today, I understood. To tell you the truth, I couldn’t sleep all night because of this worry. Who do you have here who is truly yours?”

Jhunia, clenching her teeth in pain and hissing, replied, “I won’t survive this, didi. Oh! I never went to ask God for this. I raised one child and He took him away, so what was the need for another? If I die, mother, please have mercy on my child. Raise him, look after him. God will bless you.”

Chuhia, untangling her hair with affection, said, “Have patience, daughter, have patience. The pain will pass in a moment. You’ve kept so quiet about it. What’s there to be ashamed of? If you had told me, I would have brought a talisman from Maulvi Sahib—the same Mirzaji who lives in this compound.”

After that, Jhunia lost consciousness. When she came to her senses at nine in the morning, she saw Chuhia sitting with the baby in her arms, while she herself lay there in a clean sari. She felt so weak, as if there was not a drop of blood left in her body.

Every morning, Chuhia would come and cook harira and halwa for Jhunia, and several times during the day she would come to massage the baby with ubtan and feed it cow’s milk. Today was the fourth day, but Jhunia’s breasts had not produced any milk. The baby would scream itself hoarse, for the cow’s milk did not agree with its stomach. Not for a moment would it be quiet. Chuhia would offer her own breast to the child; the baby would suck for a moment, but when no milk came, it would start wailing again. When even by the evening of the fourth day Jhunia’s milk had not come in, Chuhia grew anxious. The child was wasting away. There was a pensioner doctor living in the Nakhass. Chuhia brought him over. After examining Jhunia, the doctor said, “There’s no blood in her body, so how can there be milk? The problem is complicated. It will take months of medicine and care to restore her blood, and only then will her milk come. By then, this lump of flesh will be gone.”

It was late at night. Gobar, having drunk toddy, was lying in the veranda. Chuhia was trying to quiet the baby by putting her breast in its mouth, when suddenly she felt as if her breast had filled with milk. Delighted, she said, “Here, Jhunia, now your child will live. My milk has come in.”

Jhunia asked in astonishment, “You have milk?”

“Yes, truly!”

“I don’t believe it!”

“See for yourself!”

She pressed her breast and a stream of milk spurted out.

Jhunia asked, “Your youngest daughter must be at least eight years old.”

“Yes, she’s in her eighth year, but I always had plenty of milk.”

“But you haven’t had any children recently.”

“That same girl was the last. My breasts had completely dried up, but it’s God’s miracle, what else!”

From then on, Chuhia would come four or five times a day to feed the baby. The child had been born weak, but drinking Chuhia’s healthy milk, he began to flourish. One day, Chuhia went to bathe in the river. The baby, tormented by hunger, began to writhe. Chuhia returned at ten o’clock to find Jhunia rocking the baby in her arms, while he continued to cry. Chuhia took the baby from her lap and tried to feed him, but...

Jhunia rebuked her, “Let it be. Better that the unfortunate one dies. At least we won’t have to accept anyone’s charity.”

Chuhia began to plead. After much persuasion, Jhunia finally placed the child in her lap.

But even now, there was no harmony between Jhunia and Gobar. In Jhunia’s heart, it was firmly settled that he was a truly selfish, unfeeling man, who saw her only as an object of pleasure. Whether she lived or died, so long as his desires were satisfied, he cared not at all. He must think, “If she dies, I’ll bring another,” but don’t be so sure, mister. It was only my own foolishness that I fell into your trap. Back then, you would have laid your head at my feet. But ever since we came here, I don’t know why, it’s as if his whole temperament has changed. Winter had arrived, yet there was neither a quilt nor a blanket. Whatever few rupees were saved from bread and lentils were squandered on toddy. There was only one old quilt; both of them slept under it, but even so, there was a distance of a hundred leagues between them. They spent the night lying back to back.

Gobar longed to take the child in his arms and play with him. Sometimes, at night, he would get up just to gaze at the child’s sweet face, but his heart was pulled away by Jhunia’s coldness. Jhunia, too, would neither speak to him nor serve him in any way, and the bitterness between them, like rust on iron, only grew deeper, firmer, and harder with time. Each would twist the other’s words to the worst possible meaning, the kind that would only inflame their mutual resentment. For days, they would nurse a single remark in their hearts, feeding it with their own blood, preparing to pounce on each other like hunting dogs.

Meanwhile, at Gobar’s factory, there was some new uproar every other day. In this year’s budget, a duty had been imposed on sugar. The mill owners found a perfect excuse to reduce wages. If the duty caused a loss of five, cutting wages brought a profit of ten. For months, this very issue had been raging in the mill. The workers’ union was ready to strike at a moment’s notice. As soon as wages were cut, a strike would erupt. Gobar would not accept even a single paisa’s reduction in wages. When, in these times of inflation, wages had not increased by even a paisa, why should he now share in the loss?

Mirza Khushend was the president of the union, and Pandit Omkarnath, editor of ‘Bijli’, was the secretary. Both were determined to organize such a strike that the mill owners would remember it for years. That the workers, too, would suffer from the strike, that thousands would be left without bread—this aspect did not concern them at all. And Gobar was at the very forefront of the strikers. He was rebellious by nature, only needed to be provoked. He was not afraid of life or death. One day, Jhunia, mustering her courage, tried to reason with him, “You have a wife and child now. It’s not right for you to leap into the fire like this.”

At this, Gobar flared up, “Who are you to interfere in my affairs? I don’t ask your advice.”

The quarrel escalated, and Gobar beat Jhunia soundly. Chuhia came and pulled Jhunia away, then began to scold Gobar. But Gobar was possessed by a demon. His eyes blazing red, he shouted, “Don’t come to my house anymore, Chuhia! There’s no reason for you to come here.”

Chuhia replied with sarcasm, “If I don’t come to your house, how will I earn my bread? When I come and ask around, only then does the griddle get hot. If it weren’t for me, brother, this sister of mine wouldn’t be sitting here today to take your kicks.”

Gobar, raising his fist, shouted, “I told you, in my house—”

His temper was sky-high.

But Chuhia stood her ground, fearless, and said—

--- Page 227 ---
Beating a woman is no great act of manliness. What do you do for her that she should endure your blows? Is it just because you feed her a single piece of bread? Count yourself lucky that you have such a gentle, cow-like wife. If it were another woman, she would have swept you out of the house with a broom.

The people of the neighborhood gathered, and from all sides, Gobar was showered with rebukes. The very same men who beat their own wives daily at home now stood as paragons of justice and compassion. Chuhiya, emboldened, began to complain. Daadhijar said, “Don’t come to my house. A man who keeps a wife and children must know that raising a family takes real courage. Ask him—if I were not here, where would this child, wailing like a calf, be today? He shows his manhood by beating his wife. If I were not your wife, I’d have picked up this shoe and slapped you across the face, locked you in the room, and barred the door from outside. You’d be left begging for a morsel of grain.”

Irritated, Gobar went off to his work. If Chuhiya were a man instead of a woman, he would have taught her a lesson. What’s the point of arguing with a woman?

Meanwhile, clouds of discontent were thickening at the mill. The workers carried copies of “Bijli” in their pockets, and whenever they found a moment, two or three would gather to read it together. The newspaper’s sales soared. The labor leaders would sit in the “Bijli” office until midnight, devising strike strategies, and when the news appeared in bold letters in the morning edition, the public would rush to buy it, and copies would sell at double or triple the price.

On the other side, the company directors were also lying in wait. A strike was in their interest. There was no shortage of men; unemployment was rampant, and similar workers could be easily found at half the wages. Production costs would be immediately cut in half. If work was disrupted for ten or five days, it hardly mattered. Finally, it was decided to announce a reduction in wages. The date and time were fixed, and the police were informed. The workers had no inkling. They were waiting for the right moment—to strike only when the warehouse was nearly empty and demand was at its peak.

Suddenly, one evening, as the workers were leaving after their shift, the directors’ announcement was read out. At that very moment, the police arrived. Against their wishes, the workers were forced to strike then and there, when the warehouse was so full that even with soaring demand, the stock would not be cleared for six months.

When Mirza Khushend heard the news, he smiled, as if a proud warrior had been captivated by the enemy’s strategy. After a moment’s reflection, he said, “Very well. If this is what the directors want, so be it. The circumstances favor them, but we have the strength of justice. They want to hire new men and keep the work going. Our effort must be to ensure they do not get a single new worker. That will be our victory.”

That very night, a crisis meeting was held at the “Bijli” office. The executive committee was formed, officers were elected, and at eight o’clock, a long procession of workers marched through the streets. By ten, the entire program for the next day was set, with strict instructions that there must be no rioting or disorder.

But all efforts proved futile. When the strikers saw a swarm of new workers standing at the mill gates like a plague of locusts, their violent instincts could not be contained. They had thought that a hundred or fifty men would come each day to be hired, and they could be persuaded or threatened to leave. But seeing the sheer number of strikebreakers—

--- Page 228 ---
The new men might have been frightened on their own, but here the situation was entirely different. If all these men joined, there would be no hope left for the strikers to reach a settlement. It was decided that the new workers would not be allowed to enter the mill at any cost. There was no other way but to use force. The new group, too, was ready to fight to the death. Most of them were so famished that they could not afford to let go of this opportunity. To die of hunger themselves, or to watch their children die of hunger, was far worse than to fight against this situation and die in the struggle. A violent clash broke out between the two groups. The editor of Vijli managed to escape, but poor Mirzaji was beaten, and Govar, who tried to protect him, was badly injured as well. Mirzaji was a wrestler, a seasoned fighter, and did not allow any serious blow to land on him. Govar, on the other hand, was a simple villager. He knew how to strike hard, but not how to defend himself—something far more important in a fight. The bone in one of his arms was broken, his head was split open, and in the end, he collapsed right there. Countless sticks had rained down on his shoulders, leaving every limb of his body shattered. When the strikers saw him fall, they fled. Only ten or twelve determined men remained, standing around Mirzaji. The new workers, waving the flag of victory, entered the mill, while the defeated strikers began carrying their wounded to the hospital. But there was no room for so many people at the hospital. Mirzaji was admitted, but after bandaging Govar’s wounds, he was sent home.

When Jhunia saw Govar’s lifeless, motionless body, her womanhood was awakened. Until now, she had seen him as a master, someone who ruled over her, scolded her, beat her. Today, he was crippled, helpless, pitiable. Jhunia bent over the cot, her eyes brimming with tears, and looked at Govar. Thinking of the state of the household, a jealous anger rose within her toward him. Govar knew there was not a single paisa in the house. He also knew there was no hope of getting even a paisa from anywhere. Even knowing this, and despite her repeated pleas, he had brought this calamity upon himself. How many times had she told him, “Don’t get involved in this quarrel. The ones who start the fire will walk away, and the poor will be left to suffer.” But when did he ever listen to her? She was his enemy. His friends were those who now rode around comfortably in their motorcars. In her anger, there was a kind of satisfaction, like when we see a child fall from a chair after repeatedly being warned not to stand on it, and we exclaim, “Good, very good—why shouldn’t your head split in two?”

But in the very next moment, hearing Govar’s pitiful cries, her whole being shuddered. In his pain, these words escaped his lips: “Oh, oh! My whole body is crushed. No one had the slightest pity.”

She kept looking at Govar’s face for a long time. She wanted to find some sign of life in his fading hope, but with every moment, her patience sank like the setting sun, and the darkness of the future began to engulf her.

Suddenly, Chuhia arrived and called out, “How is Govar, bahu? I just heard about it. I ran here straight from the shop.”

Jhunia’s restrained tears burst forth; she could not speak. She looked at Chuhia with frightened eyes.

Chuhia looked at Govar’s face, placed her hand on his chest, and said reassuringly, “He’ll be better in four days. Don’t worry. You’re fortunate—your husband’s fate is strong. Several men died in that riot. Do you have any money in the house?”

--- Page 229 ---
Jhunia bowed her head in shame.

“I’ll bring it for you. Just warm a little milk and give it to him.”

Jhunia grasped her feet and said, “Didi, you are my mother. I have no one else.”

The winter evening, already somber, seemed even more desolate today. Jhunia lit the hearth and began to boil the milk. Chuhiya sat in the veranda, feeding her child.

Suddenly, Jhunia spoke in a heavy voice, “I am so unfortunate, Didi! It feels as if all this has happened because of me. When the heart is troubled, sorrow is bound to come, and then curses escape the lips, words of anger. Who knows if my curses…”

She could say no more. Her voice was swept away in a torrent of tears. Chuhiya wiped her tears with the edge of her sari and said, “Why do you think such things, daughter? It is the fate of your sindoor that he survived. But yes, if there is a quarrel between you, say whatever you must, but do not harbor bitterness in your heart. If the seed is sown within, it will surely sprout.”

Jhunia asked in a trembling voice, “What should I do now, Didi?”

Chuhiya comforted her, “Nothing, child! Take the name of God. He alone protects the poor.”

At that moment, Govar opened his eyes and, seeing Jhunia before him, spoke in a feeble, pleading voice, “I have been badly hurt today, Jhunia! I didn’t say a word to anyone. They all beat me for no reason. Forgive me for what I said and did. I tormented you, and this is my punishment. I am only a guest for a short while longer. I will not survive. My whole body feels as if it is being torn apart by pain.”

Chuhiya came inside and said, “Lie quietly. Don’t try to move or talk. You will not die, I guarantee it.”

A glimmer of hope appeared on Govar’s face. He asked, “Do you really mean it? I won’t die?”

“Yes, you will not die. What has happened to you? You have a slight injury on your head and your arm is dislocated. Men suffer such wounds every day. No one dies from these.”

“I will never beat Jhunia again.”

“Perhaps you’re afraid Jhunia might beat you now.”

“Even if she does, I will not protest.”

“When you are well, you will forget all this.”

“No, Didi, I will never forget.”

At this moment, Govar spoke like a child. For five or ten minutes, he would lie there, almost unconscious. Who knows where his mind wandered? Sometimes he saw himself drowning in a river, and Jhunia coming into the water to save him. Sometimes he saw a demon sitting on his chest, and a goddess with Jhunia’s face protecting him. Again and again, he would start and ask, “I won’t die, will I, Jhunia?”

For three days, he remained in this state, and Jhunia, staying awake at night and standing by him during the day, seemed to guard him from death itself. Chuhiya took care of the child. On the fourth day, Jhunia brought a cart, and together they carried Govar to the hospital. When they returned, Govar learned that he would truly recover. With tears in his eyes, he said, “Forgive me, Jhunia!”

In these three or four days, Chuhiya had spent three or four rupees, and now Jhunia felt embarrassed to take more from her. She was not wealthy herself. She would give Jhunia the money she earned from selling firewood. At last, Jhunia decided to find some work. It would still be months before Govar fully recovered.

--- Page 230 ---
She would need money—for food and drink, for medicine and remedies. She would have to earn at least enough to feed herself. Since childhood, she had learned to tend cows and cut grass. But where were cows here? Yes, she could cut grass. Many men and women from the neighborhood would go together outside the town to cut grass and earn eight or ten annas. At dawn, she would wash Gobar’s hands and face, entrust the child to him, and set out to cut grass, working hungry and thirsty until late afternoon. Then she would take the grass to the market, sell it, and return home in the evening.

At night, she would sleep and wake with Gobar’s rhythms, yet despite such hard labor, her heart remained so light, as if she were sitting on a swing, singing. All along the way, she would joke and banter with the men and women walking with her. Even while cutting grass, laughter and teasing would continue among them. There was no lamenting fate, no complaints about hardship. The joy that lies in the meaning of life, in making the hardest sacrifices for one’s own, in the freedom of selfless service—its radiance seemed to shine from every limb of her body. Just as a child, standing on its own feet, claps with delight, so did she feel, as if a spring of happiness had burst open within her soul! And when the mind is healthy, how can the body remain unwell? In that single month, it was as if she had been transformed. There was no more weakness in her limbs—now there was agility, suppleness, delicacy. The pallor had left her face; now there was a rosy glow of blood. Her youth, which had withered in the closed room under the weight of humiliation and quarrels, now seemed to blossom, revived by fresh air and sunlight. Now, nothing could make her angry. Where once she would lose her temper at the child’s slightest cry, now it seemed her patience and love knew no end.

In contrast, even as Gobar’s health improved, he remained somewhat downcast. When we inflict cruelty upon a loved one, and when misfortune gives us the strength to truly feel their pain, then an awakening stirs in our soul, and we become ready to atone for our unjust actions. Gobar, too, was restless with the urge for such atonement. Now his life would take on a new form, where harshness would be replaced by gentleness, pride by humility. He now understood that the chance to serve is a great blessing, and he would never again forget this opportunity.

:28:

To Mr. Khanna, the workers’ strike seemed utterly unjustified. He had always tried to remain on the side of the people. He considered himself a man of the people. During the recent nationalist movement, he had shown great enthusiasm. He had been a leading figure in the district, had gone to jail twice, and had suffered losses of several thousand rupees. Even now, he was ready to listen to the workers’ grievances; but it could not be that he would neglect the interests of the shareholders of the sugar mill. He might be willing to sacrifice his own interests if his higher ideals were touched, but to fail to protect the shareholders’ interests—that would be wrong. This was business, not a charitable institution, to be handed over entirely to the workers. Money had been raised from the shareholders with the assurance that this venture would yield a profit of fifteen or twenty percent. If they did not even receive ten percent, they would consider the directors—and especially Mr. Khanna—nothing but frauds. And how could he reduce his own salary? He had already set his salary low, compared to other companies. He took only one thousand rupees a month. He received some commission as well, but if he took that much, he also managed the operations of the mill.

--- Page 231 ---
Laborers work only with their hands. The director works with his intellect, his education, his talent, his influence. The value of these two powers can never be equal. Why are the laborers not content with the fact that it is a time of recession, that unemployment is spread everywhere, and men have become cheap? Even if they get three-quarters of a man’s wage in place of a full one, they should be satisfied. To tell the truth, they are satisfied. It is not their fault. They are simpletons, like calves’ uncles. The real mischief is the doing of Omkarnath and Mirza Khurshid. These are the people who, for the sake of a little money and fame, are making those poor fellows dance like puppets. They do not think how many homes will be ruined by their sport. If Omkarnath’s newspaper did not circulate, what could poor Khanna do? And if one day his paper gained a hundred thousand subscribers, and he began to profit five lakhs from it, would he then take only his own share for subsistence and distribute the rest among his workers? Impossible! And that ascetic Mirza Khurshid—he too was once a millionaire. Thousands of laborers were his employees. Did he, taking only his own needs, distribute everything among the workers? No, with that very sum for his own upkeep, he would frolic with European girls, feast with high officials, drink thousands of rupees’ worth of liquor each month, and travel to France and Switzerland every year. Today, his heart bursts at the plight of the workers.

Khanna had no concern for these two leaders. He had full doubts about the purity of their intentions. Nor did he care for Raisahib, who always echoed Khanna’s words and supported every one of his opinions. Among his acquaintances, there was only one person whose impartial judgment Khanna trusted completely, and that was Dr. Mehta. Ever since Dr. Mehta had begun to grow close to Malti, Khanna’s respect for him had greatly diminished. For years, Malti had been the queen of Khanna’s heart, but he had always regarded her as a plaything. There was no doubt that the plaything was very dear to him. If it were lost, broken, or taken away, he would weep bitterly—and indeed, he had wept—but still, she was only a plaything. He never truly trusted Malti. She had never been able to pierce his outer shell of luxury and reach his heart. Even if Malti herself had proposed marriage to Khanna, he would not have accepted. He would have found some excuse to avoid it.

Like many other beings, Khanna’s life too was double-faced or two-sided. On one side, he was a devotee of sacrifice, public service, and charity; on the other, of self-interest, luxury, and dominance. Which was his true nature? It was hard to say. Perhaps the nobler foundation of his soul was built on service and sympathy, the lesser half on self-interest and pleasure. But between the noble and the base, there was always a struggle, and the base, by its obstinacy and insistence, would overpower the gentle and peaceful noble side. His baser self leaned toward Malti, his nobler self toward Mehta, but now the noble had become one with the base. He could not understand how an idealist like Mehta could become enamored of a restless, pleasure-loving woman like Malti. Try as he might, he could not see Mehta as a victim of passion, and sometimes he even suspected that Malti had another aspect, one he could not see, or one he lacked the capacity to perceive.

After considering all aspects, for and against, he concluded that in this situation, only Mehta could give him light.

Dr. Mehta was intoxicated by work. He would sleep at midnight and rise again in the dead of night. No matter what the work was, he would find time for it from somewhere. Whether it was playing hockey or a university debate, rural organization or the offering at a wedding, he had enthusiasm for every task.

--- Page 232 ---
He was there, and it was the appointed hour. He wrote articles for journals and had been working for several years on an extensive tome, which was now nearing completion. Even at this moment, he was engaged in a scientific experiment. Seated in his garden, he was testing the effects of electrical currents on plants. Recently, at a learned society, he had demonstrated that crops could be grown in a very short time using electricity, that yields could be increased, and even out-of-season produce could be cultivated. These days, he devoted two or three hours every morning to such experiments.

After hearing Mr. Khanna’s story, he looked at him sternly and said, “Was it necessary to reduce the workers’ wages just because a duty was imposed? You should have lodged a complaint with the government. And if the government did not listen, why should the punishment fall upon the workers? Do you believe that laborers are paid so well that cutting a quarter of their wages would not cause them hardship? Your workers live in hovels—filthy, stinking hovels—where you could not bear to stay even a minute without feeling sick. The clothes they wear, you would not even use to wipe your shoes. The food they eat, your dog would refuse. I have shared in their lives. You wish to snatch away their bread to fill the bellies of your shareholders…”

Khanna, growing impatient, said, “But not all our shareholders are wealthy. Many have sacrificed everything for this mill, and apart from its profits, they have no other means of livelihood.”

Mehta replied in a tone that made it clear he placed no value on this argument. “A man who invests in a business is not destitute, that he should consider its profits the foundation of his life. Perhaps, if profits decrease, he may have to let go of a servant, or cut down on his butter and fruit, but he will not go naked or hungry. Those who toil with their lives have a greater right than those who merely invest money.”

This was the very point Pandit Omkarnath had made. Mirza Khursheed had offered the same advice. Even Govindi had sided with the workers, but Khannaji had paid no heed to any of them. Yet, hearing the same words from Mehta, he was moved. He considered Omkarnath selfish, Mirza Khursheed irresponsible, and Govindi incompetent. But Mehta’s words carried the weight of character, study, and goodwill.

Suddenly, Mehta asked, “Did you seek your Devi-ji’s opinion on this matter as well?”

Khanna, somewhat embarrassed, replied, “Yes, I did.”

“And what was her opinion?”

“The same as yours.”

“I expected as much. Yet you consider that wise woman incompetent.”

At that moment, Malti arrived and, seeing Khanna, said, “Ah, so you are here? I have invited Mehtaji to dinner today. I have cooked everything myself. I invite you as well. I will have Govindi Devi forgive you for this offense.”

Khanna was curious. Now Malti had taken to cooking with her own hands? Malti, the very same Malti who never even put on her own shoes, who never so much as pressed the electric switch herself, whose life was all luxury and amusement.

Smiling, he said, “If you have cooked, I will certainly eat. I could never have imagined that you were skilled in the culinary arts as well.”

--- Page 233 ---
Malti spoke without hesitation, “He has beaten me into becoming a vaidya. How could I disobey his orders? After all, he is the lord and master.”

Khanna, enjoying the sarcasm, glanced at Mehta and said, “Men were never such objects of reverence for you.”

Malti did not feel embarrassed. Understanding the meaning behind this remark, she replied with spirited fervor, “But now they are. Because the image of man I had seen within the circle of my acquaintances was nothing compared to this. So beautiful, so tender-hearted…”

Mehta looked at Malti with a pleading expression and said, “No, Malti, have mercy on me, or I will run away from here.”

These days, whoever met Malti would hear her singing Mehta’s praises, as if a new convert were loudly proclaiming her newfound faith. She paid no heed to discretion. Poor Mehta would be left inwardly wounded. He would listen to harsh and bitter criticism with great relish, but upon hearing his own praise, he would become utterly foolish, his lips pursed as if he had been stung by a biting jest. And Malti was not one of those women who could keep things inside. She could only exist outwardly—before, as now—in both action and thought. She did not know how to keep anything in her heart. Just as, upon receiving a beautiful sari, she would grow impatient to wear it, so too, if a beautiful thought entered her mind, she could not rest until she expressed it.

Malti came closer, placed her hand on Mehta’s back as if to shield him, and said, “Alright, don’t run away, I won’t say anything more. It seems you prefer criticism. Then listen to criticism—Khannaji, this gentleman has cast the net of his love over me…”

The chimney of the sugar mill was clearly visible from here. Khanna looked toward it. That chimney stood tall in the sky like a pillar of his achievements. Pride gleamed in Khanna’s eyes. At that very moment, he was supposed to go to the mill’s office. There would be an urgent meeting of the directors, and he would have to understand the situation and devise a new solution to the problem.

But where was this smoke rising from near the chimney? In the blink of an eye, the whole sky was filled with smoke like a balloon. All of them looked anxiously in that direction. Could there be a fire? It certainly looked like one.

Suddenly, on the road ahead, thousands of people could be seen running toward the mill. Khanna stood up and called out loudly, “Where are you all running?”

A man stopped and replied, “Why, the sugar mill is on fire! Don’t you see?”

Khanna looked at Mehta, and Mehta looked at Khanna. Malti rushed into the bungalow and put on her shoes. There was no time for regret or complaint. Not a word escaped anyone’s lips. In danger, our awareness turns inward. Khanna’s car was already waiting. The three of them, anxious and alarmed, got in and sped toward the mill. When they reached the crossroads, they saw the entire city surging toward the mill. There is a strange magic in fire that draws people to it. The car could go no further.

Mehta asked, “The fire insurance—was it done?”

Khanna drew a long breath and replied, “No, brother, the paperwork was still underway. How could I have known this calamity would strike?”

--- Page 234 ---
The car was left there with Ram-Asrey, and the three men pushed their way through the crowd to reach the front of the mill. They saw a sea of fire surging up into the sky. The frenzied waves of flame rose one after another, gnashing their teeth, licking their tongues, as if they would swallow the very heavens. Beneath this ocean of fire, a dense smoke hung, as if the monsoon clouds, bathed in soot, had descended to earth. Above it, it seemed as though a trembling, boiling Himalaya of fire stood. In the compound, a crowd of hundreds of thousands had gathered, along with the police. The sprays from the fire brigade vanished as if swallowed by the fiery sea. Bricks were burning, iron girders were ablaze, and streams of molten sugar flowed in every direction. Even the ground itself seemed to be spewing flames.

From afar, Mehta and Khanna had wondered why so many people were merely watching the spectacle, why they were not helping to put out the fire. But now they too realized that, apart from watching, there was nothing else within anyone’s power to do. To go within fifty yards of the mill walls was to risk one’s life. Chunks of brick and stone were cracking off and flying through the air. Whenever the wind shifted in their direction, panic would break out.

The three men stood at the back of the crowd, at a loss for what to do. How had the fire started? And how had it spread so quickly? Had no one seen it at first? Or, seeing it, had no one tried to put it out? Such questions arose in everyone’s mind, but whom could they ask? Surely, there must be mill employees present, but it was impossible to find them in the crowd.

Suddenly, a fierce gust of wind swept the flames low and sent them leaping toward the crowd, as if a tidal wave had surged in the sea. People ran for their lives, stumbling over one another, as if a tiger had sprung upon them. The tongues of flame seemed alive, animated, as if Sheshnag himself were spewing fire from his thousand mouths! Many people were trampled in the stampede. Khanna fell flat on his face; Mehta held Malti tightly with both hands—otherwise, she too would surely have been crushed. The three of them stopped under a tamarind tree by the compound wall. Khanna stood staring at the mill chimney, lost in a kind of numb, vacant absorption.

Mehta asked, “Are you badly hurt?”

Khanna gave no reply. He kept staring in the same direction. There was a void in his eyes, the sign of madness.

Mehta took his hand and asked again, “We are standing here for no reason. I’m afraid you’re badly hurt. Come, let’s go back.”

Khanna looked at him and, as if possessed, said, “I know very well who is behind this. If this is what gives them satisfaction, then may God bless them. I don’t care, I don’t care, I don’t care. If I wish, I can build a new mill today. Yes, an entirely new mill. What do these people think of me? The mill did not make me—I made the mill. And I can make it again. But those who did this, I will grind them to dust. I know everything, every last detail.” 

Mehta saw his face and his agitation, and, alarmed, said, “Come, let me take you home. You are not well.”

Khanna burst out laughing, “I am unwell because the mill burned down? I could open such a mill in a snap of my fingers. My name is Khanna—Chandraprakash Khanna. I invested everything I had in this mill. In the first mill, we made a twenty percent profit. Encouraged, I opened this one. Half the money in it is mine. I put two lakhs from the bank into this mill. I can—”

--- Page 235 ---
An hour ago, I was a man worth a million. Yes, a million—but at this moment, I am destitute—no, I am bankrupt. I owe two hundred thousand to the bank. The house I live in is no longer mine. The very utensils I eat from are no longer mine. I will be thrown out by the bank. The Khanna whom people envied, that Khanna is now reduced to dust. I have no place in society now; my friends will no longer see me as worthy of their trust, but as an object of pity. My enemies will not envy me, they will laugh at me. You do not know, Mr. Mehta, how many of my principles I have murdered. How many bribes I have taken. What sort of men I hired to weigh the peasants’ sugarcane, what false weights I kept. What can you do, hearing all this? But why should Khanna remain alive to witness his own ruin? Let whatever is to happen, happen. Let the world laugh as much as it wants, let friends grieve as much as they wish, let people hurl as many insults as they please. Khanna will not live to see and hear it with his own eyes and ears. He is not shameless, not without honor.

Saying this, Khanna began to beat his head with both hands and wept loudly.

Mehta embraced him and said in a sorrowful voice, “Khannaji, please, have patience. How can you, a wise man, let your heart grow so small? The respect a man receives for his wealth is not his own respect, but the respect for his wealth. Even in poverty, you can remain worthy of your friends’ trust—and your enemies’ as well; in fact, then you will have no enemies left. Come, let us go home. A little rest will calm your mind.”

Khanna gave no reply. The three men reached the crossroads. The car was waiting. In ten minutes, they arrived at Khanna’s mansion.

Khanna got down and said in a calm voice, “You take the car. I have no need for it now.”

Malti and Mehta also got down. Malti said, “You go and rest for a while. We’ll sit and chat. There’s no hurry to go home.”

Khanna looked at her with gratitude and said in a voice choked with emotion, “Forgive me, Malti, for all the wrongs I have done. You and Mehta—apart from you two, I have no one in this world. I hope you will not let me fall in your eyes. Perhaps in a few days, I will have to leave even this house. How cruelly fate has betrayed me!”

Mehta said, “I tell you the truth, Khannaji, today you have more respect in my eyes than ever before.”

The three entered the room. As soon as the door opened, Govindi came in and said, “Have you just come back? Maharaj has brought terrible news.”

A storm of uncontrollable, overwhelming emotion rose in Khanna’s heart. He fell at Govindi’s feet and wished to wash them with his tears. In a heavy voice, he said, “Yes, beloved, we are ruined.”

His lifeless, despairing, wounded soul was desperate for consolation—true, love-filled consolation—like a patient, whose thread of life is fraying, looks at the physician’s face with hope-filled eyes. The same Govindi, whom he had always wronged, always insulted, always betrayed, always considered a burden to his life, whose death he had always wished for—she, at this moment, seemed to shower him with blessings, good fortune, and reassurance, as if her very life’s heaven lay at his feet, as if by placing her hand on his unfortunate head she could send life flowing again through his lifeless veins. In this weakness of spirit, in this dire calamity, it was as if she stood ready to gather him to her breast.

--- Page 236 ---
While boating, the very rocks we consider perilous—those we wish someone would cast away—are the ones we cling to when our boat is wrecked.

Govindi seated him on a sofa and spoke in a voice soft with affection, “Why do you belittle yourself so? For money, which is the root of all evil? What happiness does it bring us? Night after night, some new worry, the ruin of one’s soul. You spoke of giving up your job, yet you hardly found time to write letters to your own relatives. Has this world, since time immemorial, ever worshipped anything but wealth? You have often said yourself: when Lakshmi is with you, she wags her tail before you, but tomorrow, with equal speed, she will turn to someone else and not even glance your way. The truly virtuous do not bow before wealth; they look to see what is within you. If you have truth, justice, sacrifice, and strength, they will embrace you. If not, they will turn away, seeing you as a thief, even become your enemy. This is what I tell you.”

Mehta, as if startled from a heavenly dream, said, “What are you saying?”

After experiencing the purest truths of life, the great souls have spoken thus. The meaning of life—

Govindi, addressing Mehta, said, “No one ever considers who truly becomes wealthy. Only he who can outwit others with his cunning…”

Khanna interrupted, “No, Govindi, we have lived together long enough to know—wealth is not gained by cunning alone. For that, one must devote body, mind, intellect, even the grace of God. All our skill and divine power—wealth is the name of their harmony.”

Govindi, not opposing but mediating, said, “I do not deny it, but even so, the importance we attach to it in life is not justified. I am happy that this burden has been lifted from your shoulders. Such things—selfishness and pride—are not the purpose of life. The true aim is not to become an idol of selfishness and arrogance, but to serve the suffering. Do not take it amiss, but what has been the meaning and purpose of your life until now? Deprived of that means, you have had to endure more pain and sorrow, but do you not see? This has given you the chance to burn with the injustice of the world. To suffer is sometimes nobler. If by losing wealth we could save our lives, it would be no loss. There is honor in fighting as a soldier for justice, but there is even greater honor in enduring suffering. If your silent simplicity has gone to waste, if it has all been for nothing—”

Mehta looked at her with eyes full of devotion, his face heavy with sorrow. He struggled to speak, and Malti was pained in her heart. In Govindi’s eyes, Mehta’s soul was vast, his life so radiant.

:29:

Nohri was not among those women who, after doing a good deed, become entangled in pride. On the contrary, she would boast of her actions and shower as much love as she could, even if it meant stamping her feet and making a scene. Such a person deserves not praise, but only disgrace and infamy.

Godan: 29

--- Page 237 ---
It is not a matter of disgrace. If one does not have the desire or the means, no one can blame them for it. But when we do a good deed and then begin to remind others of our favor, the very person for whom we did the kindness becomes our enemy and wishes to erase our favor. That same kindness, if it remains in the heart of the doer, is truly a virtue; but if it is brought out into the open, it becomes a vice. 

Nohri went about telling everyone, “Poor Hori was in great trouble. He was about to mortgage his land for his daughter’s wedding. When I saw his condition, I felt pity. I never liked Dhaniya; that widow is so proud, she walks as if she doesn’t even touch the earth. Poor Hori was wasting away with worry. I thought, let me help him in this crisis. After all, it is only humans who help other humans. And Hori is no longer a stranger to us—whether he admits it or not, he is now our relative. I took out the money and gave it to him, otherwise the girl would still be sitting unmarried.”

But Dhaniya was not one to listen quietly. “Was the money given as charity? As if you are some great benefactor! The mahajan will take interest, and so will you. What favor is this? If you had given it to someone else, even the principal would have disappeared, let alone the interest. Since we have taken it, we will return it the moment we have the money in hand. Were we the ones who ate up your household’s honor and never acknowledged it? No one here would even let you stand at their door. We have preserved your dignity, kept the color in your cheeks.”

It was ten o’clock at night. The dark monsoon clouds had gathered. The whole village was shrouded in darkness. Hori, after his meal, smoked his hookah and was about to go to bed when Bhola came and stood before him.

Hori asked, “How are you, Bhola Mahto? If you are to live in this village, why not build a small house of your own? People in the village say all sorts of things—do you like hearing such talk? Don’t take it the wrong way, but since we are now related, I cannot bear to hear your name maligned. Otherwise, what business is it of mine?”

Just then, Dhaniya came in, bringing a lota of water to place by Hori’s bedside. Hearing this, she said, “If it were another man, he would have cut off such a woman’s head.”

Hori scolded her, “Why do you talk nonsense? Put the water down and go to sleep. If you ever stray from the path, should I cut off your head? Would you let me?”

Dhaniya splashed a little water at him and said, “Let your sister stray, why would I? I am only speaking the truth of the world, and you start abusing me. Now your mouth must be sweetened by her words. No matter which path a woman takes, the man just sits and stares. I do not call such a man a man.”

Hori felt wounded inside. Bhola must have come to share his sorrows, but instead, Dhaniya turned on him. Irritated, he said, “You do whatever you please all day, what can I do about it? If I say anything, you pounce on me. Think about that.”

Dhaniya had never learned to flatter. She replied, “If a woman spills a pot of ghee or sets the house on fire, a man will bear it, but no man will tolerate her straying from the path.”

Bhola spoke in a pained voice, “You are right, Dhaniya! I should have cut off her head, but I no longer have that kind of strength. Go and reason with her, I have tried everything and failed.”

“If you didn’t have the power to keep a woman in line, why did you get engaged in the first place? For this disgrace? Did you think she would come and press your feet, fill your pipe again and again, and nurse you when you were ill? Only a woman who has shared the joys of youth with you would do that. I just don’t understand how you became so infatuated with her. You should have at least checked what kind of nature she had, what her ways were. You acted like a hungry jackal—”

--- Page 238 ---
They fell upon him in such a way. Now your only dharma is to take up the axe and cut off her head. At worst, you’ll be hanged. Hanging is better than this disgrace.’

A surge of energy coursed through Bhola’s blood. He said, ‘So that’s your advice?’

Dhaniya replied, ‘Yes, that’s my advice. You’re not going to live another hundred years. Accept it—this was all the life you were meant to have.’

This time Hori scolded her sharply, ‘Be quiet! You think you’re some great saint, coming from there. Even a wild bird won’t stay in a cage—how can a person? Let her go, Bhola. Consider her dead, and go live in peace with your children. Eat your bread and remember Ram’s name. The joys of youth are over. That woman is fickle; you’ll get nothing from her but disgrace and jealousy.’

For Bhola to leave Nohri—impossible! Even now, Nohri seemed to be glaring at him with eyes full of reproach. But no, Bhola would leave her now. She would reap what she had sown.

Tears welled up in his eyes. He said, ‘Hori bhaiya, only I know the torment I’ve suffered because of this woman. It was because of her that I fought with Kamta. I had to bear this stain in my old age, and now it’s there. She taunts me every day—“Your daughter ran away.” My daughter may have run away, but at least she’s with her husband, sharing his joys and sorrows. I’ve never seen a woman like this one. She laughs with others, but when she sees me, her face turns sullen. I’m a poor man, earning three or four annas a day. Where am I to get milk, curds, meat, fish, sweets, and cream?’

Bhola left from there, vowing to himself that he would now live with his sons. He had suffered enough. But the next morning, when Hori saw him, Bhola was coming away from Dulari the moneylender’s shop, tobacco in hand.

Hori thought it best not to call out to him. In attachment, a man is not master of himself. Returning home, he said to Dhaniya, ‘Bhola is still there. Nohri really has bewitched him.’

Dhaniya wrinkled her nose and said, ‘She’s shameless, and so is he. A man like that ought to drown himself in a handful of water. Where’s all his pride now? When Jhunia came here, he was running after her with a stick, afraid his honor would be ruined. Now his honor doesn’t matter!’

Hori felt pity for Bhola. The poor man was ruining his life, caught in the snare of this wanton woman. But how could he leave her? How easy is it to abandon a wife like that? This witch wouldn’t let him live in peace even then. She’d call a panchayat, or make a scene over food and clothing. For now, only the villagers knew. People hesitated to say anything outright; they just made sly remarks. But then the whole world would blame Bhola. People would say, ‘When the man left her, she blackened his face.’

Two months later, one day, news spread through the village that Nohri had shaved her head in mourning for her dead husband.

The rains had ended, and preparations for the rabi sowing were underway. She had gone off to the corner of the village. She couldn’t get money for sugarcane seed, and without it, there would be no cane crop. On top of that, the Harijan’s bullock had died, and without a new bullock, the work could not go on.

Since the bullock had died, things had become even more difficult. That day, a handful of...

--- Page 239 ---
One day, in Hori’s field. The plowing was never quite as it should be. Hori took the plow and went to the field, but his mind remained troubled by thoughts of Bhola. In his whole life, he had never heard of a woman striking her husband with a shoe. Not just with a shoe—even a slap or a punch, he could not recall any such incident. And today, Nohri had beaten Bhola with shoes, and everyone stood by and watched the spectacle! How will that unfortunate man ever be free of this woman? Now Bhola ought to drown himself somewhere. When life holds nothing but disgrace and misery, it is better for a man to die. Who is there to mourn Bhola’s name? His sons may perform the rites, but out of social obligation; not a tear will come to anyone’s eyes. Driven by desire, a man ruins his own life in this way. When there is no one left to weep for you, what attachment can there be to life, and what fear of death?

There is this Nohri, and then there is that Chamarin, Siliya! In every way, she is a thousand times better than Nohri. She may eat only after feeding two others, and roam about like a Radhika, but she works for wages, starves, and sits waiting in the name of Matai, and that merciless man does not even ask after her. Who knows, if Dhaniya had died, Hori’s fate might have been the same today. The very thought of her death sent a shiver through Hori. Dhaniya’s image stood before his mind’s eye—a goddess of service and sacrifice, sharp-tongued, but with a heart as soft as wax, one who would give her life for every paisa, but ready to sacrifice everything for the sake of honor. In her youth, she was not lacking in beauty. What is Nohri compared to her? When she walked, she looked like a queen. Whoever saw her, could not look away. Back then, Pateshwari and Jhinguri were young men. Both would place a hand on their chest when they saw Dhaniya. They would circle the doorway a hundred times a day. Hori kept watch on them, but never found an excuse to confront them. In those days, there was great scarcity of food in the house. There had been a frost, and the fields yielded no fodder. People survived by eating wild grasses. Hori had to go work at the famine camp, earning six paise a day. Dhaniya stayed alone at home, but no one ever saw her glance at any young man. Pateshwari once tried to tease her. She gave such a sharp retort that he has not forgotten it to this day.

Suddenly, he saw Matadin approaching. The butcher’s son, what a tilak he wore, as if he were a true devotee of God. A painted jackal! Who would bow to such a Brahmin?

Matadin came near and said, “Your right ox is getting old, Hori. It won’t last through the next irrigation. You must have brought it five years ago?”

Hori placed his hand on the right ox’s back and said, “Not five, brother, this is its eighth year! My heart wants to give it a pension, but a farmer and a farmer’s ox—only Yama, the god of death, grants them retirement. My heart aches to put the yoke on its neck. The poor thing must think, ‘Still no rest? Must I plow even these old bones?’ But I have no choice. And you, how are you? Are you well now?”

For the past month, Matadin had been suffering from malarial fever. One day, his pulse had even stopped. He had been taken off the cot and laid on the ground. Since then, he felt that he had been punished for his wrongdoing toward Siliya. When he had driven Siliya from his house, she had been pregnant. He had not felt the slightest pity. Even with her full pregnancy, she continued to work for wages. Had Dhaniya not shown her compassion, she would have died. What trials she had endured to survive! In such a condition, she could not even work for wages. Now, ashamed and remorseful, Matadin had come to give Siliya two rupees through Hori’s hand, hoping that if Hori would give her the money, he would consider it a great favor.

Hori said, “Why don’t you give it to her yourself?”
