Matadeen said humbly, “Don’t send me to her, Hori Mahto! With what face could I go? I am afraid she might scold me when she sees me. Have some mercy on me. I can barely walk, but for this money, I ran a whole kos to a yajman’s house. I have suffered enough for my deeds. I can no longer bear the weight of this disgrace. Whatever sin you commit in secret, no one will say a word. But openly, nothing can be done—otherwise, a blot will fall on the family. Please explain to her, Dada, that she should forgive my offense. The bonds of dharma are very tight. One must uphold the honor of the society in which one is born and raised. If the dharma of any other caste is corrupted, it brings no special harm, but if a Brahmin’s dharma is corrupted, he is left with nothing. His dharma is the wealth earned by his ancestors. It is from that he earns his bread. For this atonement, I have already lost three hundred. If I am to remain outcast, then whatever I must do, I will do openly. If a man has some duty to society, he also has some duty as a human being. By upholding social dharma, one earns the respect of society, but by upholding human dharma, one earns the pleasure of God.”

In the evening, when Hori timidly gave Silia the money, it was as if she had received the boon of her penance. She could bear the weight of sorrow alone, but the weight of happiness cannot be borne alone. Whom could she share this good news with? She could not confide her heart’s secrets to Ghuniya. There was no one else in the village with whom she was close. Her stomach was rumbling with hunger. Sona was her only companion. Silia became restless to meet her. How could she wait through the night? A storm was rising in her heart. Now she was no longer an orphan. Matadeen had once again taken her arm. On the path of life, there was no longer a dark, terrifying chasm before her, but a lush, green field where streams sang and deer played. The love that had turned away from her was now wild with joy.

How much she had cursed Matadeen in her heart! Now she would ask his forgiveness. She had truly made a great mistake by insulting him before the whole village. She was a chamarin, of a low caste—what had she lost? Today, if she offered a meal to the biradari with ten or twenty rupees, she would be accepted back. But the poor man’s dharma was lost forever. That honor could never be his again. How blind with rage she had been, going around proclaiming his love for her to all! His dharma was ruined—he had reason to be angry, but why had she been possessed by such madness? If she had simply gone to his house, what harm would there have been? No one would have bound her there. The country worships Matadeen because he lives by rules and dharma. When that dharma was destroyed, why would he not thirst for her blood?

Just a little while ago, she had blamed Matadeen for everything, and now she blamed herself entirely. Compassion had awakened compassion. She hugged her child close and showered him with love. Now, looking at him, she felt no shame or remorse. He was no longer merely an object of her pity. Now he was worthy of all her motherly love and pride.

The silvery Kartik moonlight lay over nature like a sweet melody. Silia left her house. She would go to Sona and share this joyful news. She could not wait any longer. It was only evening. She would find a boat. She walked on, step by step. When she reached the river, she saw the boat was on the other side, and there was no sign of the boatman. The moon seemed to be melting and flowing into the river. She stood for a moment, thinking. Then she waded into the water. How deep could the river be? What was this river compared to the ocean of joy within her? At first, the water reached her knees, then her waist, and—

--- Page 241 ---
In the end, the water reached up to her neck. Siliya grew afraid—what if she drowned, what if there was a sudden pit? Yet, risking her life, she pressed forward. Now she was in the middle of the current. Death danced before her eyes, but she did not falter. She knew how to swim. In her childhood, she had swum in this very river countless times, had even crossed it standing upright. Still, her heart pounded in her chest. But now, the water began to recede. There was no more fear. She hurriedly crossed the river, wrung out her clothes on the bank, and, shivering from the cold, moved ahead. All around her was silence—not even the cry of jackals could be heard. The sweet anticipation of meeting Sona carried her forward like a breeze.

But upon reaching the village, she hesitated to go to Sona’s house. What would Mathura say? What would his family think? Sona herself might be angry—why have you come so late at night? In the countryside, the weary farmers, exhausted from the day’s labor, fall asleep soon after dusk. The whole village was wrapped in sleep. The doors of Mathura’s house were shut. Siliya could not bring herself to knock. What would people say, seeing her in this state? There, by the door, the fire in the hearth was still glowing. Siliya sat by it to warm her clothes. Suddenly, the door creaked open and Mathura stepped out, calling, “Hey! Who’s sitting by the fire?”

Siliya quickly pulled her veil over her head and came closer, replying, “It’s me, Siliya.”

“Siliya! How did you come at such an hour? Is everything well at home?”

“Yes, all is well. My heart was restless. I thought I’d come and see everyone. There’s never any time during the day.”

“So, did you come after bathing in the river?”

“How else could I come? The water was no less.”

Mathura led her inside. The vestibule was dark. He took Siliya’s hand and drew her toward him. Siliya jerked her hand away and said sternly, “Listen, Mathura, if you try to tease me, I’ll tell Sona. Remember, you’re my younger brother-in-law. Seems like you and Sona aren’t getting along.”

Mathura slipped his arm around her waist and said, “You’re very cruel, Sillo. Who’s going to see us at this hour?”

“Am I more beautiful than Sona? You should count yourself lucky to have found such a fairy from Indra’s court. Now you want to play the bee? If I tell her, she’ll never look at your face again.”

Mathura was not a libertine; he truly loved Sona. But in that moment, the darkness, the solitude, and Siliya’s youth unsettled his heart. Chastened by her rebuke, he came to his senses. Letting go of Siliya, he said, “I beg you, Sillo, don’t tell her. Punish me however you wish right now.”

Sillo felt pity for him. She gently slapped his cheek and said, “Your punishment is this: never try such mischief with me again, nor with anyone else. Otherwise, Sona will slip from your hands.”

“I swear, Sillo, it will never happen again.”

There was pleading in his voice. Sillo’s heart was moved; her compassion grew tender.

“And if you do?”

“Then do whatever you wish.”

Sillo’s face was close to his, and their breath, their voices, their bodies trembled with emotion. Suddenly, Sona called out, “Who are you talking to out there?”

Sillo stepped back. Mathura went forward into the courtyard and said, “Sillo, from your village—”

It coursed through every vein in her body. Waves of humiliation rose within her, like the sting of a serpent’s bite. To go to bed hungry at home is one thing, but to be asked to leave the feast—such disgrace is enough to make one wish for death. For Siliya, even a moment’s stay here became unbearable, as if someone were strangling her. She could not ask anything. She sensed what was in Sona’s heart. She feared that the snake coiled in its burrow might emerge at any moment; she wanted to flee before that happened. But how could she escape, what excuse could she make? Why didn’t her life just end?

Mathura had picked up the storeroom key, intending to bring out something for Siliya to eat, but now he stood there, paralyzed with indecision. Meanwhile, Siliya’s breath hung suspended, as if a sword dangled above her head.

In Sona’s eyes, the greatest sin was for a man to covet another’s wife, or a woman to look at another’s husband. For such a crime, she had no forgiveness. Theft, murder, deceit—none of these were as terrible. She did not mind laughter and jest, if done openly; but secret flirtation, she considered contemptible. From childhood, she had learned and understood many customs. Whenever Hori was late returning from the market and Dhaniya found out he had been at Dulari the shopkeeper’s, even if only to buy tobacco, Dhaniya would not speak to him for days, nor do any work in the house. Once, over such a matter, she had run away to her natal home. This feeling was even stronger in Sona. Before her marriage, it was not so powerful, but after marriage, it became a vow for her. If the skins of such men and women were to be flayed, she would feel no pity. Outside of marriage, she saw no place for love. The duties between husband and wife—this alone, to her, was love. And then, there was her bond of sisterhood with Siliya. She loved Siliya, trusted her, and now this very Siliya was betraying her. Surely, Mathura and Siliya must have had an understanding from before. Mathura must have met her in the fields by the riverbank. And tonight, Siliya had crossed the river so late for this very reason. Had Sona not overheard their conversation, she would have known nothing. Mathura must have thought this the best time for their tryst—after all, the house was so quiet. Sona’s heart was restless to know everything, so she could devise some means of protecting herself. And why was Mathura still standing here? Would he not even let her speak?

She said angrily, “Why don’t you go outside? Or do you mean to stand guard here?”

Without a word, Mathura went out. His heart shriveled with fear that Siliya might reveal everything.

And Siliya’s heart shriveled with dread, as if the sword hanging above was about to fall.

Then Sona, in a grave voice, said to Siliya, “Listen, Siliya, tell me the truth—otherwise, right here before you, I’ll strike my own neck with the axe. Then you can rule as my rival wife. Look, the axe is lying right there. Two swords cannot rest in one scabbard.”

She sprang forward, picked up the axe lying in the courtyard, and holding it in her hand, continued, “Don’t think I’m just making empty threats. In anger, I can’t say what I might do. Tell me the truth, clearly.”

Siliya began to tremble. Each word fell from her lips as if a gramophone were playing—she could not hide a single thing. On Sona’s face played a terrible resolve, as if possessed by bloodlust.

Sona looked at her with eyes as piercing as a spear, and, as if striking with a dagger, said, “Are you telling the truth—exactly as it happened?”

“Absolutely true. I swear on my child.”

“You haven’t hidden anything?”

“If I have hidden even a grain, may I lose my sight.”

“Then why didn’t you kick that sinner? Why didn’t you bite him? Why didn’t you drink his blood, why didn’t you scream?”

What answer could Sillu give?

Sona, like a madwoman, her eyes blazing like embers, said, “Why don’t you speak? Why didn’t you bite off his nose? Why didn’t you strangle him with both hands? Then I would have bowed my head at your feet. Now, in my eyes, you are faithless, a mere harlot. If this was what you intended, why do you tarnish Matadeen’s name? Why don’t you just take someone and live with him, why don’t you go back to your own home? Isn’t this what your family wanted? You could have taken dung-cakes and grass to the market, brought back money, and your father would have drunk toddy from those very coins. Then why did you disgrace that Brahmin? Why did you stain his honor? Why do you sit here pretending to be a chaste woman? If you cannot live alone, why don’t you get engaged to someone? Why don’t you drown yourself in the river or pond? Why do you poison the lives of others? Today I tell you, if anything like this happens again and I find out, not one of the three of us will remain alive. Now, go, blacken your face and leave. From this day, there is no bond between you and me.”

Sillu slowly rose and stood up carefully. It felt as if her back had broken. For a moment she tried to gather her courage, but nothing came to her defense. Darkness swam before her eyes, her head spun, her throat was parched. Her whole body was numb, as if life was escaping through every pore. Step by step, as if there were a pit before her, she walked out and headed toward the river.

At the door stood Mathura. He asked, “Where are you going at this hour, Sillu?”

Sillu gave no reply. Mathura did not ask again.

The silvery moonlight still lay spread all around. The river’s waves still bathed in the moon’s rays, and Sillu, distraught, like a shadow in a dream, walked on toward the river.

They remain left behind. After the lamps are lit, they do not leave their offices and have begun to flatter the officers. Mirza Khushend’s authority is still as strong as ever, but Mirzaji, seeing no remedy for this deceit and its resolution, wishes in his heart that all of them should be dismissed. Yet, considering the suffering of the new men, he tells the inquirers, “Do as you wish.”

When Mr. Khanna saw that the old workers were again eager for employment, he became even more arrogant, though in his heart he knew that, at this wage, the old workers were far better than the new ones. Despite all their efforts, the new men could not match the work of the old. Most of the old workers had been accustomed to mill work since childhood and were highly skilled. The new workers were mostly distressed peasants from the villages, used to working in the open air and fields with the old wooden tools of bygone days. Inside the mill, they felt suffocated, and the rapidly moving parts of the machinery frightened them.

At last, when the old workers were thoroughly defeated, Khanna agreed to reinstate them. But the new workers were willing to work for even less pay, and now the directors faced the question: should they rehire the old workers, or keep the new ones? Half of the directors favored retaining the new men at reduced wages. The other half believed that the old workers should be kept at their current wages—though it would cost a little more, the work would be better. Khanna was the soul of the mill, in a sense its autocrat. The directors were mere puppets in his hands. The decision rested with Khanna, and he was consulting not only his friends but even his adversaries on the matter. First of all, he sought Govindi’s advice. Ever since he had lost hope in Malti, and Govindi had come to know how much a learned, experienced, and wise man like Mehta respected her and what kind of devotion he expected from her, affection had once again awakened between the couple. Do not call it affection, but companionship was certainly there. The jealousy and unrest between them had vanished. The wall that stood between them had crumbled.

Malti’s own ways and mannerisms were undergoing a transformation. Mehta’s life until now had been spent in self-study and contemplation, and after thoroughly examining all philosophies—activism and renunciation, self and non-self—he had come to the conclusion that the path of service, which lies between action and renunciation, whether you call it karma-yoga or something else, alone can give life meaning, alone can elevate and purify it. He had no faith in an all-knowing God. Though he did not openly declare his atheism, for he considered it impossible to hold any definite opinion on the subject, this conviction had become firm in his mind: that there is no divine ordinance in the birth and death, joy and sorrow, sin and virtue of living beings. He believed that man, in his arrogance, has made himself so important that every one of his actions is thought to be inspired by God. In the same way, perhaps locusts hold God responsible when, upon encountering the sea in their path, billions of them perish. But God’s decrees are so inscrutable that man cannot comprehend them—so what satisfaction can be gained by believing in them? In Mehta’s view, the only purpose of the concept of God was the unity of mankind.

He regarded monism, universal selfhood, or the principle of non-violence not from a spiritual, but from a material perspective. Though these principles have never dominated any period of history, they have nonetheless held a place of great importance in the cultural development of humanity.

--- Page 246 ---
Mehta had a firm belief in the unity of human society, yet he did not feel the need to accept the concept of a divine essence to support this belief. His love for humanity was not founded on the idea that a single soul resides in all living beings. To him, the philosophical debates of dualism and non-dualism held no significance beyond their practical, social value—and that value, in his eyes, was simply to bring humanity closer together, to erase mutual differences, and to strengthen the spirit of brotherhood. This unity, this sense of oneness, was so deeply rooted in his soul that, for him, creating any spiritual foundation for it seemed pointless. And once he had grasped this principle, he could not rest in peace. It became necessary for him to work as much as possible, free from self-interest. Without this, his mind could not find peace. The thoughts of fame, greed, or even duty never entered his heart; their pettiness was enough to keep him away from them. Service itself was becoming his only self-interest, and the influence of his generous nature was, in some subtle way, beginning to affect Malti as well.

Until now, all the men Malti had met had only awakened her desire for pleasure. Her spirit of sacrifice had been steadily weakening, but in Mehta’s company, her sense of selflessness had been reawakened. In all strong-willed souls, this feeling lies dormant, and when it finds light, it shines forth. If a person is still chasing after wealth or fame, it means he has not yet come into contact with a refined soul.

Now, Malti often went to the homes of the poor to see patients without charging any fee. A gentleness had come into her manner with her patients. Yet, she still could not detach her mind from adornment and beauty. To give up color and powder seemed to her far more difficult than any inner transformation.

Sometimes, the two of them would go out to the villages, spending a few hours with the peasants, passing the night in their huts, eating their simple food, and feeling blessed by the experience. One day, they reached Semi, and wandering about, they arrived at Belari. Hori was sitting at his doorway, smoking his chilam, when Malti and Mehta came and stood before him. Mehta recognized Hori at once and said, “Is this your village? Do you remember, we came to Rai Sahib’s house, and you played the gardener in the Dhanush-Yajna play?”

Hori’s memory stirred. He recognized them and went off towards Pateshwari’s house to fetch some chairs.

Mehta said, “There’s no need for chairs. We’ll just sit here on this cot. We haven’t come to sit on chairs, but to learn something from you.”

Both of them sat on the cot. Hori stood there, bewildered. How was he to show hospitality to such important people? What did he have that was worthy of offering them?

At last, he asked, “Shall I bring some water?”

Mehta replied, “Yes, I am thirsty.”

“Shall I bring something sweet as well?”

“Bring it, if you have any at home.”

Hori went inside to fetch some sweets and water. Meanwhile, the village children gathered around the two visitors, gazing at them as if some rare creatures from a menagerie had arrived.

Sillo, carrying her child, was passing by on some errand. Seeing the two strangers, she stopped out of curiosity.

Malti came over, took the child from Sillo’s arms, and, caressing it, said, “Who has—”

--- Page 247 ---
Is that so?  
Sillo wasn’t quite sure. Another woman replied, “He must be about a year old, isn’t he?”  
Sillo agreed.  
Malti joked, “He’s a lovely child. Give him to me.”  
Sillo swelled with pride and said, “He is yours, after all.”  
“Then may I take him with me?”  
“Take him. He’ll become a real man in your care.”  

Other village women gathered and led Malti into Hori’s house. Here, in the presence of men, they never had the chance to converse with Malti. Malti saw a cot laid out, with a rug spread over it, borrowed from Pateshwari’s house. Malti went and sat down. The conversation turned to child protection and infant care. The women listened with rapt attention.  

Dhanya said, “How can we maintain such cleanliness and discipline here, memsahib? We can’t even be sure of our next meal.”  

Malti explained, “Cleanliness costs nothing. With a little effort and cleverness, it can be managed.”  

Dulari Sahuain asked, “How do you know all these things, memsahib? You aren’t even married yet, are you?”  

Malti smiled and asked, “How do you know I’m not married?”  

All the women turned away, smiling. Dhaniya said, “Oh, can that be hidden, Miss Sahiba? One can tell just by looking at your face.”  

Malti, embarrassed, said, “That’s why I didn’t marry—how else could I serve you all?”  

All spoke in unison, “Bless you, memsahib, bless you!”  

Siliya began to massage Malti’s feet. “Memsahib has come from so far, you must be tired.”  

Malti pulled her feet back. “No, no, I’m not tired. I came by train. I want you all to bring your children, so I can see them and tell you how to keep them healthy and free from disease.”  

In a short while, twenty or twenty-five children gathered. Malti began to examine them. Several children had eye infections; she put medicine in their eyes. Most of the children were weak. The reason was that their parents did not get enough to eat. Malti was surprised to learn that very few homes had milk. As for ghee, they hadn’t seen it in years.  

Here too, Malti explained the importance of proper food, as she did in every village. It pained her: why didn’t these people eat well? She felt anger toward the villagers. Were they born only to toil and die, unable to eat what they produced? There was food for two or four bullocks, but not fodder for a single cow or buffalo? Why did these people not consider food the mainstay of life, but merely a means to keep body and soul together? Why didn’t they ask the government to give them loans at nominal interest, to save them from the clutches of the usurious mahajans? Whomever she asked, she found that most of their earnings went to pay off the mahajans’ debts. The disease of land division was also spreading. There was so much discord among them that rarely did two brothers live together. Much of their misery, Malti realized, was due to their own narrowness of mind.

--- Page 248 ---
And there was selfishness. Malati continued to talk with the women on these subjects. Watching their devotion, the inspiration to serve grew even stronger within her. How petty and artificial that life of luxury seemed before this life of sacrifice! Today, those silk garments embroidered with zari, that body fragrant with perfume, and that face adorned with powder, all began to shame her. The gold watch tied to her wrist seemed to be staring at her with unblinking eyes. The sparkling jeweled necklace around her neck felt as if it were choking her.

In the presence of these goddesses of sacrifice and devotion, she felt herself base in her own eyes. She knew many things more than these rural women, understood the pace of the times better, but could she live even a single day in the circumstances in which these poor women were making their lives meaningful? Where there was no trace of ego, where they worked all day, fasted, wept, and yet their faces remained so cheerful! Others had become so much their own that their very sense of self had disappeared. Their sense of belonging was found in their sons, their husbands, their relatives. By protecting this feeling—by expanding the sphere of this feeling—the ideal of future womanhood would be formed. Among the awakened women, the feeling of self-service has taken root—everything for oneself, for one’s own pleasure and luxury—compared to that, this simple state is better. Men are cruel, granted, but after all, they are the sons of these very mothers. Why did the mother not give her son such education that he would worship his mother, worship womankind? Because the mother does not know how to give this education, because she erased herself so completely that her very form was distorted, her personality destroyed.

No, erasing oneself will not do. For the welfare of society, woman must defend her rights, just as these peasants must sacrifice some of their divinity to defend themselves.

Evening had fallen. The women still surrounded Malati. It seemed they could never be satisfied with her words. Several women urged her to stay the night. Malati found their simple affection so endearing that she accepted their invitation. At night, the women would sing their songs for her. Malati made good use of her time by visiting each house and acquainting herself with its condition. Her sincere goodwill and sympathy were nothing less than a blessing from the goddess for those rustic women.

Meanwhile, Mehta Sahib sat cross-legged on a cot, watching the boys wrestle and regretting that he had not brought Mirzaji along—otherwise, he too would have found a match. He wondered how people who called themselves educated could be so heartless towards such sturdy and innocent children. Just as ignorance is simple, guileless, and open-hearted, so too can knowledge be. His faith in humanity was so firm, so profound, that he considered any behavior contrary to it inhuman. He forgot that rams have always answered the innocence of lambs with hooves and teeth. He created an ideal world for himself, populated it with ideal humanity, and remained absorbed in it. Reality—how inaccessible, how inscrutable, how unnatural it is—became difficult for him to contemplate. At that moment, sitting among these villagers, Mehta Ji was grappling with the question of why their defeat was so pitiable. He could not muster the courage to face the truth that their very divinity was the cause of their misery. If only they were more human and less divine, they would not be trampled so. Whatever happened in the country, even if revolution came, it mattered nothing to them. If any party appeared before them as the strong, they were ready to bow their heads before it.

--- Page 249 ---
Helplessness has reached the point of numbness, which only a harsh blow can awaken into action. It is as if their soul, despairing from all sides, has now withdrawn inward, folding its limbs and sitting still. It is as if the very consciousness of life has faded from them.

Evening had fallen. Those who had been working in the fields till now were also hurrying back. At that moment, Mehta saw Malti among several village women, so absorbed, holding a child in her arms, as if she were one of them. Mehta’s heart overflowed with joy. In a way, Malti had surrendered herself to Mehta. He now had no doubt about this, but still, an intense feeling for Malti had not yet arisen in his heart, without which proposing marriage would have seemed laughable to him. Malti had come to his door like an uninvited guest, and Mehta had welcomed her. There was no feeling of love in this, only a sense of manhood. If Malti considered him worthy enough to bestow her favor upon, Mehta could not refuse her grace. At the same time, he wished to remove Malti from Govindi’s path, and he knew that until Malti had firmly set her foot forward, she would not lift the one behind. He knew that by deceiving Malti, he was revealing his own baseness. For this, his conscience had continually reproached him, but the more closely he saw Malti, the more his attraction grew. The allure of beauty could have no effect on him; this was the attraction of character. He knew that what could truly be called love could only be born after being bound in a single bond. The love that comes before that is merely an infatuation with beauty, which does not last. But before that, it was necessary to decide whether the stone that would be placed on the lathe of companionship was even capable of being shaped. Not all stones become beautiful sculptures on the lathe. In all these days, Malti had cast her rays into different parts of his heart, but they had not yet converged into that flame which would set his entire inner being alight. Today, by mingling with the villagers and erasing all distinctions, Malti seemed to have concentrated those rays, and for the first time, Mehta felt a sense of unity with Malti. As soon as Malti returned from her round of the village, he set out with her towards the river. They decided to spend the night there.

For some reason, Malti’s heart was pounding today. On Mehta’s face, she saw a strange light and longing.

On the riverbank, it was as if a floor of silver had been spread, and the river, adorned with jeweled ornaments, singing in sweet tones, was showing her dance to the trees, who, with their heads bowed to the moon and stars, seemed to be sleeping. Mehta was intoxicated by the enchanting beauty of nature, as if his childhood had returned with all its playfulness. He rolled several times on the sand, then ran and stood in the river, water up to his knees.

Malti said, “Don’t stand in the water. You might catch a chill.”

Mehta splashed water and said, “I feel like swimming across to the other side of the river.”

“No, no, come out of the water. I won’t let you go.”

“Won’t you come with me to that deserted settlement, where dreams reign?”

“I don’t know how to swim.”

“All right, come, let’s make a boat and go sitting on it.”

He came out. All around, as far as the eye could see, stood a forest of reeds. Mehta took from his pocket...

--- Page 250 ---
Baku took out his knife and, cutting many branches, gathered them together. Along the embankment stood tall clusters of sarpat reeds. Climbing up, he cut a bundle of sarpat and, sitting right there on the sandy floor, began to twist a rope from the reeds. He was so cheerful, as if preparing for an ascent to heaven. Several times his fingers were cut, blood oozed out. Malti was growing irritated, repeatedly insisting they return to the village, but he paid her no heed. He had the same childlike joy, the same stubbornness. All his learning, all his science, were swept away in this current.

The rope was ready. A large raft of jhau branches was made, the branches at both ends bound tightly with the rope. The gaps were stuffed with more jhau twigs so the water would not seep in. The boat was ready. The night became even more dreamlike.

Mehta, launching the raft onto the water, took Malti’s hand and said, “Come, sit.”

Malti, uncertain, asked, “Will it bear the weight of two people?”

Mehta replied with a philosophical smile, “The raft on which we journey through life is far more fragile than this, Malti. What are you afraid of?”

“What is there to fear, when you are with me?”

“Do you mean that?”

“Until now, I have overcome every obstacle without anyone’s help. Now, I am with you.”

Both sat on the raft of jhau, and Mehta, with a single pole, began to steer it. The raft wobbled and floated out onto the water.

To distract herself from the raft, Malti asked, “You have always lived in cities—how did you become so adept at village life? I could never have made such a raft.”

Mehta looked at her with affectionate eyes and said, “Perhaps it is the imprint of a previous birth. The moment I touch nature, it is as if a new life awakens within me, every vein fills with vigor. Each bird, each animal, seems to invite me to joy, as if reminding me of forgotten happiness. I find this joy nowhere else—not even in the moving notes of music, nor in the lofty flights of philosophy. It is as if I find myself, as if a bird returns to its nest.”

The raft wobbled, sometimes tilting, sometimes straight, sometimes spinning as it moved along.

Suddenly, Malti asked in a plaintive voice, “And do I never enter your life?”

Mehta took her hand and said, “You do, again and again—you come like a waft of fragrance, like a shadow of imagination, and then vanish. I run to bind you in my embrace, but my hands remain empty, and you disappear.”

Malti, in a state of frenzy, said, “But have you ever thought about why? Tried to understand?”

“Yes, Malti, I have thought, many times, again and again.”

“And what did you find?”

“That the foundation on which I wish to build the house of my life is unstable. It is not some grand mansion, only a small, peaceful cottage, but even that needs a firm foundation.”

Malti, withdrawing her hand as if in protest, said, “That is a false accusation. You have always looked at me with the eyes of judgment, never with the eyes of love. Do you not know that a woman does not want to be tested, she wants to be loved? Judgment turns virtues into faults, beauty into ugliness.”

--- Page 251 ---
Love turns faults into virtues, the unlovely into the beautiful. I loved you, and I cannot even imagine that you could have any flaw. But you put me to the test, and, thinking me fickle, unstable, and who knows what else, you always kept running away from me. No, let me say what I wish to say. Why am I unstable and fickle? Only because I did not receive that love which would have made me steadfast and unwavering. If you had surrendered yourself to me as I did to you, you would not be making such accusations against me today.

Mehta, savoring Malti’s pride, said, “And you never tested me? Is that the truth?”

“Never.”

“Then you made a mistake.”

“I do not care.”

“Don’t be carried away by emotion, Malti! Before giving love, we all put the other to the test, and you did too, even if only indirectly. Today I will tell you plainly: I looked at you just as I look at thousands of women every day, only with a sense of amusement. If I am not mistaken, you too saw me as a new plaything, a diversion.”

Malti interrupted, “You are wrong. I never looked at you that way. From the very first day, I made you my deity and placed you in my heart…”

Mehta cut her off, “There you go again, with your sentimentality. I do not like sentimentality in matters of such importance. If you considered me worthy of such grace from the very first day, it can only be because I am more skilled than you at playing a role. Otherwise, from what I have seen of women’s nature, they are quite thorough in their scrutiny when it comes to love. Even in olden times, men were tested through the swayamvar. That tendency still exists, though its form may have changed. Since then, I have always tried to present myself to you in my entirety, and at the same time, to reach the depths of your soul. And the deeper I have gone into your innermost being, the more treasures I have found. I came for amusement, and today I stand as a worshipper. What you have found within me, I do not know.”

The other bank of the river had arrived. Both stepped out and sat down again on the sandy floor, and Mehta continued in the same flow, “And today, it is to ask you this that I have brought you here.”

Malti, her voice trembling, said, “Do you still need to ask?”

“Yes, because today I will show you a side of myself that perhaps you have not yet seen, and which I too have kept hidden. Very well, suppose I marry you and tomorrow I am unfaithful to you—what punishment would you give me?”

Malti looked at him in astonishment. She could not grasp the meaning of his question.

“Why do you ask such a thing?”

“To me, it is a matter of great importance.”

“I cannot imagine such a possibility.”

“There is nothing impossible in this world. Even the greatest saint can fall in a single moment.”

“I would seek the cause and try to remove it.”

“Suppose I cannot change my ways?”

“Then I do not know what I would do. Perhaps I would take poison and go to sleep forever.”

“But if you were to ask me the same question, I would give a different answer.”

--- Page 252 ---
Malati asked anxiously, “Tell me!”

Mehta replied with firmness, “First, I will give you precedence, then myself.”

Malati burst into loud laughter, and a shiver ran through her from head to toe. Her laughter was but a veil to conceal her trembling. Mehta asked, “Why are you laughing?”

“Because you do not seem to be such a violent man.”

“No, Malati, in this matter I am wholly a beast, and I see no reason to be ashamed of it. Spiritual love, sacrificial love, selfless love—where a man effaces himself and lives only for his beloved, delights in her happiness, and surrenders his soul at her feet—these are meaningless words to me. I have read such tales of love in books, where the lover gives his life for his beloved’s new suitors, but I would call that devotion, or service, never love. Love is not a gentle cow, but a ferocious lion that does not allow even a glance to fall upon its prey.”

Malati looked straight into his eyes and said, “If love is a ferocious lion, then I shall keep my distance from it. I have always thought of it as a cow. I consider love to be above suspicion. It is not a thing of the body, but of the soul. There is no place for doubt in it, and violence is but the offspring of doubt. It is complete self-surrender. In its temple, you may receive blessings only as a worshipper, not as an examiner.”

She stood up and walked swiftly toward the river, as if she had found her lost path. Never before had she felt such vigor. Even in her independent life, she had always sensed a weakness within herself, which kept her agitated, always unsettled. Her mind seemed to seek a refuge, a support on which she could rest, with which she could face the world. She did not find this strength within herself. Seeing the strength of intellect and character in others, she was drawn toward it, yearning. Like water, she would take the shape of any vessel. She had no form of her own.

Her disposition had always depended on some external source. In plays, love is always the central relationship, but she paid more attention to the conventions that had become tradition. Her first concern was to pass the examination; acquiring knowledge came afterward. If she learned that the examiner was kind or partial and would pass the students regardless, she would not even glance at the textbooks. Malati had to act reserved, to please Mehta. Her aim was to attain both love and knowledge, to understand their meanings, but like a student eager to display her merit, she was haunted by the fear of failure. She would not allow herself to be satisfied merely because the examiner would be pleased on his own.

But today, it was as if Mehta had, by hurting her, quelled her inner turmoil.

For the first time, she saw that he had never bent his head to her name, never mentioned her with deference. He appeared to her the most capable of all. In her godlike life, the sharp brilliance of intellect seemed the highest thing. Even those poems, he considered mere toys—he would break them apart. Astonished by Mehta’s play, she felt she must gather self-confidence, focus her vision on Mehta.

There is meaning, there is pride, in turning toward Mehta.

--- Page 253 ---
Since then, she had been shaping her own character. The inspiring force she needed had been found, and, in some mysterious way, it was giving her momentum and strength. The new ideal of life that had appeared before her—she was striving to draw herself closer to it, and, feeling a sense of success, she imagined the day when she and Mehta would become one. This vision made her even more resolute and devoted.

But today, when Mehta brought her hopes to the threshold and placed before her an ideal of love in which love was dragged down from the realm of soul and surrender to the material plane—where suspicion, jealousy, and desire reign—her refined intellect was wounded. The reverence she felt for Mehta suffered a jolt, as if a disciple had caught their guru in a base act. She saw that Mehta’s sharp intellect was being drawn toward the animality of love, turning its eyes away from its divinity, and, seeing this, her heart sank.

Mehta, somewhat abashed, said, “Come, let us sit a while longer.”

Malti replied, “No, I must return now. It is getting late.”

:31:

Raisahab’s star was in the ascendant. All three of his ambitions had been fulfilled. His daughter’s wedding had taken place with great pomp, he had won his lawsuit, and not only had he succeeded in the election, he had also become a Home Member. Congratulations poured in from all sides; telegrams arrived in a constant stream. By winning this case, he had secured a place among the first rank of taluqdars. His honor had never been less than anyone’s before, but now its roots had grown even deeper and stronger. His portrait and character sketches were appearing one after another in the contemporary journals. His debts had increased greatly, but Raisahab no longer cared. He could free himself from debt simply by selling a small piece of this new property.

He had reached heights of happiness beyond even his loftiest dreams. Until now, he had only a bungalow in Lucknow. Now, it became necessary to have a bungalow in each of Nainital, Mussoorie, and Shimla. It no longer befitted him to stay in hotels or in another raja’s bungalow when visiting these places. When Surya Pratap Singh had bungalows in all these locations, it was a matter of great shame for Raisahab not to have his own.

By chance, he did not even have to go through the trouble of building bungalows. Ready-made bungalows were found at cheap prices. For each bungalow, a gardener, watchman, steward, cook, and so on were hired, and the greatest stroke of fortune was that, on the occasion of His Majesty’s birthday, he was also granted the title of Raja. Now his ambitions were completely satisfied. That day, a grand celebration was held, and such a magnificent feast took place that all previous records were broken. When His Excellency the Governor bestowed the title upon him, a wave of royal loyalty surged through his heart, filling every fiber of his being with pride. This was life! Not like those rebels who, falling into the trap of sedition, earned only disgrace, went to jail, and fell from the good graces of the officers. The very DSP who had arrested him last time now stood before him with folded hands, perhaps seeking forgiveness for his offense.

But the greatest triumph of his life came when his old, defeated enemy, Surya Pratap Singh, sent a proposal for his daughter’s marriage to Raisahab’s eldest son, Rudrapal Singh.

--- Page 254 ---
Neither winning a lawsuit had brought him such joy, nor becoming a minister. All those things had appeared in his imagination, but this—this was not only beyond hope, it was beyond imagination. The same Suryapratap Singh, who for months had considered him lower than his own dog, now wished to marry his daughter to his son! How impossible it seemed! Rudrapal was currently studying for his M.A.—a bold, steadfast idealist, self-reliant, proud, a lover of beauty and pleasure, and a lazy youth, who found his father’s hunger for wealth and status distasteful.

Raisahab was in Nainital at the time. When he received this message, his heart leapt with joy. Though he did not wish to put any pressure on his son regarding marriage, he was confident that whatever he decided, Rudrapal would have no objection. To be allied with Raja Suryapratap Singh was such a stroke of fortune that the thought of Rudrapal refusing did not even cross his mind. He immediately sent word to Raja Sahib, and at once telephoned Rudrapal. Rudrapal replied, “I do not accept.”

Never in his life had Raisahab felt such disappointment, nor such anger. He asked, “Any reason?”

“You will know when the time comes.”

“I wish to know now.”

“I do not wish to say.”

“You will have to obey my command.”

“What my soul does not accept, I cannot accept, even at your command.”

Raisahab tried to reason gently, “Son, in your idealism you are striking an axe at your own fortune. Have you thought how much this alliance would elevate your position in society? Consider it the will of God. Had I found a poor girl from that family, I would have counted myself fortunate, but this is Raja Suryapratap’s daughter, the jewel of our region. I see her every day. You must have seen her too. In beauty, virtue, character, and temperament, I have never seen a young woman like her. My days are numbered, but your life lies ahead. I do not wish to pressure you. You know how liberal my views are regarding marriage, but it is also my duty to warn you if I see you making a mistake.”

Rudrapal replied, “I made my decision on this matter long ago. There can be no change now.”

Raisahab’s anger flared again at his son’s obstinacy. He thundered, “It seems you have lost your mind. Come and see me at once. Do not delay. I have already given my word to Raja Sahib.”

Rudrapal replied, “I am sorry, I do not have the time right now.”

The next day, Raisahab came himself. Both stood ready, armed with their own weapons—on one side, a lifetime’s experience, full of compromises; on the other, youth, stubborn, defiant, and unyielding.

Raisahab struck at the heart of the matter. “I want to know—who is she?”

Rudrapal replied, unmoved, “If you are so eager, then I will tell you. It is Saroj, my sister.”

Raisahab, wounded, fell silent. “Ah, her!”

“You have seen Saroj, haven’t you?”

--- Page 255 ---
“I have seen her well. Have you seen the princess or not?”  
“Yes, I have seen her, very well.”  
“And yet…”  
“I do not consider beauty to be of any importance.”  
“I feel sorry for your intelligence. Do you know Malti, what kind of woman she is? Would her sister be any different?”  
Rudrapal frowned and said, “I do not wish to say anything more to you on this subject. But if I marry, it will be Saroj.”  
“As long as I live, that can never happen.”  
“Then it will happen after you.”  
“So, these are your intentions?”  

And Rai Saheb’s eyes filled with tears, as if his whole life had been laid waste. The ministry, the estate, the title—all seemed as withered and joyless as stale flowers. All the striving of his life seemed futile. When his wife had died, he was not yet thirty-six. He could have remarried, could have enjoyed the pleasures of life. Everyone urged him to marry again, but he looked at the faces of his children and accepted the austerity of a widower’s life. He poured all the joys and pleasures of his life into these boys. Until today, he had given all the affection of his heart to his sons, and now this boy spoke with such cruelty, as if there was no bond between them. Then why should he give his life for property, honor, and rights? All that he did was for these boys, and when they had no regard for him, why should he continue this penance? How many days did he have left in this world anyway? He too knew how to live in comfort. His brothers and cousins twirled their moustaches and enjoyed life, roaming about in merriment. Why should he not also indulge in pleasure and luxury?  

He did not remember at this moment that the penance he undertook was not for his sons, but for himself—not only for fame, but because he was a man of action, and needed this for his own existence. He could not satisfy his soul by becoming indulgent and idle. He did not realize that some people are made in such a way that they cannot accept the crippling idleness of luxury. They are born to drink the blood of their own hearts, and will do so till their dying breath.  

But the reaction to this wound came at once. Even when we make sacrifices for others without expecting anything in return, we still wish to rule over their minds. Even if that rule is for their own good—though we so completely identify their good with our own that it ceases to be theirs and becomes ours. The greater the sacrifice, the stronger this desire for control becomes. And when, suddenly, we are faced with rebellion, we are shaken, and our sacrifice turns into a kind of vengeance. Rai Saheb was now determined that Rudrapal’s marriage to Saroj would never take place, even if he had to take the help of the police, even if he had to murder justice itself.  

He spoke as if drawing a sword: “Yes, only after I am gone—and that will not be for a long time yet.”  
Rudrapal fired back as if shooting a bullet: “May God grant you immortality. My marriage with Saroj has already taken place.”  
“Lies!”  
“Not at all. The certificate is here.”  

Rai Saheb, wounded, collapsed. Never had he seen such ruthless violence even in the eyes of an enemy.

--- Page 256 ---
He had never seen this before. An enemy could at most strike at his self-interest, his body, or his honor. But this blow had struck at that innermost core where the very inspiration of life was stored. It was a storm that had uprooted his existence from its very foundation. Now he was utterly powerless. Even with all the power of the police at his command, he was helpless. The use of force had been his last weapon. That weapon had now slipped from his grasp. Rudrapal was of age, Saroj was of age, and Rudrapal was the master of his estate. He had no hold over him. Ah! If only he had known that this boy would rebel in this way, why would he have fought for this estate at all? Two or two and a half lakhs had been squandered in litigation. His whole life had been destroyed. Now his only hope of saving face was to keep flattering this boy; if he put up the slightest resistance, his honor would be trampled in the dust. Even after sacrificing his life, he was no longer the master. Oh! His whole life was ruined. His entire life!

Rudrapal had left. Rai Saheb called for his car and set out to meet Mehta. If Mehta wished, he could persuade Malti. Saroj would not disregard his words either. Even if it took ten or twenty thousand rupees to halt the marriage, he was ready to pay. In his self-absorbed state, it did not occur to him at all that he was taking such a proposal to Mehta, who would never sympathize with him on this matter.

After hearing the whole story, Mehta began to humor him. Putting on a grave face, he said, "This is a matter of your prestige."

Rai Saheb did not catch on. Jumping in, he said, "Yes, only a matter of prestige. You know Raja Suryapratap Singh, don’t you?"

"I have seen his daughter as well. Saroj is not even the dust of her feet."

"But this boy has lost all sense."

Rai Saheb looked at Mehta’s face. He saw a faint smile there. He understood. In a pained voice, he said, "So, you too have chosen this moment to make fun of me. If only you wouldn’t stand there with your hands tied. I don’t know how true it is, but it’s said that Raja Sahab even greets the police inspector of his own estate—do you call that prestige? Saying this, he rose and walked slowly toward the door. The knot he had come to untangle had only grown more complex. The darkness had deepened. Mehta accompanied him to the car to see him off.

Rai Sahab went straight to his bungalow and had just picked up the daily paper when Mr. Tankha’s card was brought in. Rai Sahab shuddered. The same thought had crossed his mind, but he had not allowed it to take shape. Both men shared the same instincts. The cave-dweller lived on in both of them. Rai Sahab had covered him with the garments of civilization; in Raja Sahab, he stood naked. Rai Sahab could not let go of the chance to prove his own superiority.

Raja Sahab interrupted, “You carry humanity about with you, but you do not see that even today, man’s animal nature triumphs over his humanity. Otherwise, why would nations go to war? Wouldn’t all disputes be settled by panchayats? As long as man exists, so will his animality.”

A small debate broke out, which soon turned into a quarrel, and finally into a bitter dispute. Raja Sahab, angry, departed. The next day, Rai Sahab too left for Nainital. And a day later, Rudrapal set off for England with Saroj. Now, there was no longer any bond of father and son between them. They had become rivals. Mr. Tankha was now Rudrapal’s advisor and advocate. On Rudrapal’s behalf, he filed a claim for a settlement of accounts against Rai Sahab. A decree for one million rupees was passed against Rai Sahab.

It was not the decree itself that pained him so much, but the humiliation. More than the humiliation was the grief of seeing the cherished hopes of his life turned to dust. And the greatest sorrow of all was that it was his own son who had betrayed him. The pride of being the father of an obedient son had been cruelly snatched from his hands.

--- Page 260 ---
She went with the one to whom she was married, but there was no love between man and woman. Digvijay Singh was such a man, too, and Meenakshi suffered inwardly. She found solace in books and magazines. Digvijay was not more than thirty years old, educated as well, but exceedingly arrogant, always boasting of his family’s prestige, cruel by nature, and miserly. He would cast his eyes on the daughters and daughters-in-law of the lower castes in the village. His companions, too, were from the lower orders, whose flattery had made him all the more self-indulgent and depraved. Meenakshi could not truly respect such a man. Moreover, reading about women’s rights in journals had begun to open her eyes. She started frequenting the Ladies’ Club, where many educated women of high birth would gather. There, discussions abounded about votes, rights, independence, and women’s awakening, as if some conspiracy were being plotted against men. Most of these ladies were those who did not get along with their husbands, who, having received a modern education, wished to break free from old conventions. There were also several young women who had earned degrees and, considering married life detrimental to their self-respect, were seeking employment. Among them was Miss Sultan, who had returned from England as a barrister and now provided legal advice to purdah-observing women. On her advice, Meenakshi filed a claim for maintenance against her husband. She no longer wished to live in his house. Meenakshi had no need of the maintenance; she could have lived comfortably at her parental home, but she wanted to leave by blackening Digvijay Singh’s face in public disgrace. Digvijay Singh, in turn, accused her of immorality. Rai Sahib tried his utmost to quell this discord, but Meenakshi no longer wished to see her husband’s face. Although Digvijay Singh’s claim was dismissed and Meenakshi was awarded maintenance, the insult continued to rankle in his heart. She lived separately in a bungalow, took an active part in the socialist movement, yet the bitterness would not subside.

One day, in a fit of rage, she took a whip and went to Digvijay Singh’s bungalow. A crowd of loafers had gathered, and a courtesan was dancing. Like a war-goddess, she stormed into this assembly of fiends and created havoc. The whip lashed out, and people scattered in all directions. What could those lowly ruffians do before her fury? When Digvijay Singh was left alone, she began to strike him with the whip, blow after blow, until the Kunwar Sahib was left breathless and exhausted. The courtesan still stood cowering in a corner. Her turn came. Meenakshi raised her whip, ready to strike, when the courtesan fell at her feet and, weeping, pleaded, “Dulhinji, spare my life today. I will never come here again. I am innocent.”

Meenakshi looked at her with contempt and said, “Yes, you are innocent. Do you know who I am? Go. Never come here again. We women are nothing but objects of pleasure—there is no fault of yours!”

The courtesan placed her head at Meenakshi’s feet and, overcome with emotion, said, “May God keep you happy. You are just as I had heard you were.”

“What do you mean by ‘keep you happy’?” Meenakshi asked.

“Whatever you wish, Maharani ji.”

“No, you must tell me.”

The courtesan’s spirit shrank within her. She had come to offer a blessing, but now, having narrowly escaped with her life, she should have quietly gone her way. Yet, seized by the urge to offer a prayer, she now wondered how to save herself.

Trembling, she replied, “May your honor’s reputation and name grow ever greater.”

--- Page 261 ---
Meenakshi smiled—“Yes, that’s fine.”

She got into her car, went to the District Magistrate’s bungalow to report the incident, and then returned to her own house. From that moment, husband and wife became thirsty for each other’s blood. Digvijay Singh roamed about with a revolver in hand, lying in wait for her, and she, for her own protection, kept two wrestler Thakurs by her side. Rai Saheb, who had once built a paradise of happiness, now saw it crumbling before his very eyes, and, disillusioned with the world, his soul began to turn inward. Until now, desires had inspired him to live. But when that path was closed, his mind inclined towards devotion, which was a truth far greater than desires. The new estate for which he had taken loans had slipped from his hands before he could repay the debt, and that burden still weighed upon him. The ministry certainly brought in a handsome sum, but all of it was spent merely on maintaining the dignity of his office, and to uphold his princely grandeur, Rai Saheb was forced to impose extra levies, evictions, and tributes upon his tenants—practices he despised. He did not wish to inflict suffering upon his people. Their plight evoked his pity, but he was overwhelmed by his own needs.

The difficulty was that even in worship and devotion, he found no peace. He wished to renounce attachment, but attachment would not renounce him, and in this tug-of-war, he found no escape from humiliation, remorse, and unrest. And when the soul knows no peace, how can the body remain healthy? Despite every effort to keep well, some ailment or another always clung to him. All kinds of delicacies were prepared in the kitchen, yet for him, it was always only moong dal and dry chapatis. He saw his brothers, even more indebted, humiliated, and grief-stricken than himself, whose indulgence and splendor lacked for nothing, but such shamelessness was not in his nature. The noble values of his heart had not yet been destroyed. They could justify cruelty, cunning, shamelessness, and oppression as the pride and prestige of the zamindari, and thus appease their souls, and this was his greatest defeat.

Mirza Khushend, after leaving the hospital, had started a new venture. Resting idly was not in his nature. And what was this venture? He had formed a theater troupe of the city’s courtesans. In his better days, he had lived a life of indulgence, and during his recent days of suffering in the solitude of the hospital, his soul had grown devout. Remembering that former life filled him with deep anguish. If only he had possessed wisdom then, how much good he could have done for others, how many burdens of sorrow and poverty he could have lightened! But he had squandered his wealth in debauchery. It is no new discovery that it is in times of crisis that our souls awaken. Who, in old age, does not grieve over the follies of youth? If only that time had been spent in gathering knowledge or strength, in accumulating good deeds—how much peace the heart would have found today! It was then that he experienced, with painful clarity, that in this world, there is no one truly your own, no one to shed tears at your death.

Again and again, a memory from his life returned to him. In a village near Basra, when he was lying in a camp, stricken with malaria, a local villager had nursed him with such selfless devotion. When he recovered and tried to repay the villager’s kindness with money and jewelry, the man’s eyes filled with tears, he lowered his head, and refused to accept those gifts.

--- Page 262 ---
He had refused.

In the care of these veins, there was discipline, order, truth—but where was that love, that absorption, which had once shone in the clumsy, unpracticed ministrations of that other woman? The image of devotion had long since faded from his heart. He had promised to return to her, but never went back. In the intoxication of pleasure, he did not even remember her. And if he did, it was only with pity, not with love. Who knows what she must have endured then? But these days, her anxious, gentle, calm, and simple face hovered constantly before his eyes. If only he had married her, how much less sorrow there would have been in his life today! The pain of injustice done to her had made him extend his sympathy and service to that entire class. As long as the river was in flood, its muddy, swift, foaming current distorted the light. Now the current had become steady and calm, and the rays could reach its depths.

On this cool spring evening, when Mirza Sahib stepped out onto the veranda of his hut, he found the two ladies sitting and conversing. Just then, Mr. Mehta arrived. Mirza, with great enthusiasm, said, “I have been waiting for you, preparing for your hospitality!”

Both ladies smiled. Mehta felt embarrassed.

Mirza signaled to the two women to leave, and when they had gone, he said, “I was just about to come to you myself. I feel that the work I am doing cannot be completed without your help. You just keep urging me on—‘Yes, Mirza, go ahead, march on!’”

Mehta laughed and said, “Whatever work you undertake, you will need no help from me. You are older than I am. Who among us small, insignificant people has the power to influence others? God knows what I would have done myself.”

In a few words, Mirza Sahib laid his new scheme before him: In the bazaar, it is always those women who come—either those who cannot find work at home, or those forced by economic hardship. Very few women are thus degraded.

Mehta, like other thoughtful gentlemen, believed that the root cause lay chiefly in the conditioning of the mind and religious perspective. On this point, a debate arose between the two friends.

Mehta, clenching his fist and striking the air, said, “There are many crimes committed for the sake of livelihood. People want the best things in the world. If everything is to be blamed on circumstances, then there is no hope from such a society.”

Mirza twirled his moustache and said, “For every person, the question is of a hut to live in. A man does not want only bread; he needs medicine too. The question comes in many forms, but Dr. Mehta, if you do anything, it will not be just a play of words.”

--- Page 263 ---
He said, “Forgive me, Mirza Sahab, but as long as there are wealthy people in this world, there will also be courtesans. Even if your troupe succeeds—which I very much doubt—you will never be able to take in more than a handful of women, and that too only for a short time. Not every woman has the talent for acting, just as not every man can be a poet. And even if we suppose that the courtesans become permanent members of your troupe, their place in the bazaar will not remain empty. Unless the axe strikes at the root, plucking leaves will yield no result. Sometimes, among the wealthy, there emerge those who renounce everything and devote themselves to the remembrance of God, but the reign of wealth continues as ever. Not the slightest weakness has crept into it.”

Mirza felt saddened by Mehta’s obstinacy. Such an educated, thoughtful man, yet he speaks like this! Will the structure of society change so easily? That is a matter of centuries. Until then, should this injustice be allowed to continue? Why not lock the lion in a cage, so that even with its teeth and claws, it cannot harm anyone? Why sit idly by until the lion takes a vow of non-violence? Let the wealthy squander their riches as they please, build dharamshalas and mosques, they do not care. But let them not ruin the lives of helpless women. This, Mirza cannot bear to see. He would empty the marketplace of beauty so thoroughly that not even a beggar would spit on the gold coins of the rich. Did not even the most hardened drunkards quench the fire in their hearts with water when the liquor shops were picketed?

Mehta laughed at Mirza’s foolishness and said, “You should know that there are countries in this world where there are no courtesans. But even there, the wealth of the rich finds ways to create objects of desire.”

Mirza too laughed at Mehta’s naiveté. “I know, my friend, I know. Thanks to your blessings, I have seen the world. But this is Hindustan, not Europe.”

“Human nature is the same everywhere in the world.”

“But remember, every community has something you might call its soul. Chastity is the soul of Hindustani tahzeeb.”

“Go on, praise yourself as much as you like.”

“You speak so ill of wealth, yet you never tire of defending Khanna. Don’t deny it.”

Mehta’s sharpness faded. He spoke gently, “I defended Khanna only when he had freed himself from the clutches of wealth. And if you see his condition these days, you will feel pity. And what defense can I offer? I am tied to my books and college; at most, I can offer dry sympathy. It is Miss Malti who truly defended him, who saved Khanna. I had never realized how much strength for sacrifice and renunciation lies hidden in the depths of the human heart. You too should go and meet Khanna one day. You will be amazed. What he needs most right now is sympathy.”

Mirza, as if against his own will, said, “If you say so, I will go. I would not hesitate to go to hell with you. But you were supposed to marry Miss Malti, weren’t you? That was quite the hot news.”

Mehta, embarrassed, said, “I am practicing austerity. Let’s see when I am granted a boon.”

“Oh, she was madly in love with you.”

Mirza asked, “When shall we meet again?”

“This time, you’ll have to make the effort. Do visit Khanna!”

Dr. Mehta, once the examiner, now found himself the examined. Keeping his distance from Malti, he began to fear that he might lose her. For months, Malti had not come to see him, and when, in his disappointment, he went to her house, she was never there. In the days when Rudrapal and Saroj’s romance was unfolding, Malti would come to him almost daily for advice, sometimes even twice a day. But since both had left for England, her visits had ceased. Even at home, she was difficult to find. It seemed as if she was avoiding him, as if she were forcibly turning her mind away from him.

The book he was working on these days refused to progress, as though his concentration had deserted him. He had never been particularly skilled at managing the household. Altogether, he earned more than a thousand rupees a month, yet not a single paisa was ever saved. Beyond his daily bread and lentils, nothing remained in his hands. If there was any extravagance, it was his car, which he drove himself. Some money was spent on books, some on donations, some on supporting poor students, and some on decorating his home, for which he had a kind of love affair. Importing all sorts of plants and herbs from abroad and nurturing them—this was his mental indulgence, or perhaps his intellectual vanity.

But for several months now, even that garden had begun to lose its charm for him, and the state of the house had grown worse. He would eat two flatbreads, yet the expenses would exceed a hundred rupees. He had immovable property, but he had managed to get through the harshest winter with just that. There were always transactions of buying and selling new property. Sometimes, he had to eat lentils without ghee. He could not even remember when he had last sold any property, and how could he ask the cook? How would the cook understand what was happening?

At last, after three disappointments and four months had passed, Malti, seeing his condition, could bear it no longer. She said, “What is this, selling off your property? Aren’t you ashamed to wear such worn-out clothes?”

Though Malti was not his wife, she stood so close to him that she spoke as one would to a dear one.

Mehta replied without embarrassment, “What can I do, Malti? What can you do?”

Malti was surprised. “You are a man of property, and yet you don’t have money even for necessities? Where does all my income go?”

“I don’t spend a single extra paisa,” he said.

“Well then, take money from me and tell me what needs to be done.”

--- Page 265 ---
Mehta, embarrassed, said, “This time I will buy one. I swear it.”

“If you come here again, come as a proper man.”

“That’s a harsh condition.”

“Harsh, perhaps, but necessary. With people like you, nothing works without strictness.”

But there, the trunk was empty, and he did not have the courage to go to any shop without money.

How could he show his face at Malti’s house? His heart writhed in helplessness. Then, one day, a new calamity befell him. For several months, he had not paid the rent. Seventy-five rupees per month kept accumulating. The landlord, after repeated requests and still not recovering the money, finally sent a notice. But a notice is not some magic charm that conjures up money. The date on the notice passed, and still, no payment was made. The landlord, left with no choice, filed a suit. He knew Mehtaji was a gentleman and a charitable soul, but what more could he do than wait for six months? Mehta made no attempt to plead his case; a one-sided decree was passed. The landlord immediately had the decree executed, and the bailiff came to Mehta’s house to give prior notice, since his own son studied at the university and Mehta had even given him a scholarship. By chance, Malti was also present at that time.

She asked, “What is this attachment about? For what reason?”

The bailiff replied, “It’s the decree for the unpaid rent. I thought I should inform you. It’s a matter of four or five hundred rupees, not a huge sum. Even if you pay within ten days, there’s no problem. I’ll keep the landlord tangled up for ten days.”

When the bailiff left, Malti asked in a tone full of scorn, “So things have come to this? I’m amazed you write such thick tomes. The rent has been overdue for six months and you don’t even know?”

Mehta, hanging his head in shame, replied, “How could I not know? But there’s never any money left. I don’t spend a single paisa unnecessarily.”

“Do you keep any accounts?”

“Why wouldn’t I keep accounts? Whatever I receive, I record it all. Otherwise, the income tax people wouldn’t let me live.”

“And what about your expenses?”

“I don’t keep any record of those.”

“Why not?”

“Who would write it all down? It feels like a burden.”

“And yet you manage to write all those books?”

“That doesn’t require anything special. I just sit down with my pen. But I can’t sit down every moment with an account book for expenses.”

“So how will you pay the money?”

“I’ll borrow it from someone. If you have it, give it to me.”

“I can give it only on one condition: all your income must come into my hands, and all expenses must be made by me.”

Mehta, delighted, said, “Ah, if you take on this burden, what more could I want? I’ll beat the drums in celebration.”

Malti paid the amount for the decree, and the very next day forced Mehta to vacate that bungalow. In her own bungalow, she gave him two large rooms. She took charge of his meals and other needs.

--- Page 266 ---
The arrangements were made within their own household. Mehta did not possess much else, but he had cartloads of books. Both his rooms were filled with them. He certainly felt a pang at leaving his garden behind, but Malti gave him her entire compound, telling him to plant whatever flowers and shrubs he wished. Mehta was content, but Malti found it very difficult to manage his income and expenses. She saw that the income exceeded a thousand, but all of it vanished in payments. Twenty or twenty-five boys were studying at the school on his scholarships. The number of widows he supported was no less. She could not figure out how to reduce these expenses. All the blame would fall on her head, all the disgrace would be hers. Sometimes she grew irritated with Mehta, sometimes with herself, sometimes with the supplicants who, without the least hesitation, placed their burdens on a simple, generous soul. What irked her even more was that some of these recipients were not even deserving. One day, she confronted Mehta about it.

Hearing her reproach, Mehta replied calmly, “You have full authority. Give to whomever you wish, or refuse whomever you wish. There is no need to consult me. But you alone will have to answer for it.”

Malti retorted with annoyance, “Yes, of course. You take the credit, and heap the blame on me. I do not understand by what logic you support this practice of charity. This custom has made mankind more lazy and parasitic than even injustice has, and has wounded its self-respect more deeply. In fact, I believe injustice has at least awakened a spirit of rebellion in humanity, which has greatly benefited society.”

Mehta admitted, “I think so too.”

“You do not think so,” Malti insisted.

“No, Malti, I speak the truth.”

“Then why is there such a gulf between your thoughts and your actions?”

In the third month, Malti disappointed many. She gave some a direct refusal, demanded work from others, and even humiliated a few.

Mr. Mehta’s budget gradually came into order, but this brought him a certain regret. When Malti showed him a saving of three hundred in the third month, he said nothing to her, but in his eyes, her stature diminished somewhat. A woman ought to possess charity and self-sacrifice—this, to him, was her greatest virtue. Upon this foundation, society’s edifice stands. He regarded a mercantile spirit in a woman as an unspoken vice.

When Mehta’s new spectacles arrived, and a new watch as well, he was so embarrassed that he did not go out for several days. In his eyes, there was no greater crime than self-indulgence.

But the secret was that while Malti kept a tight rein on Mehta’s accounts, wishing to close the doors of his charity, she herself lavished the generosity of her heart with both hands, giving the gift of life. She would not visit the homes of the wealthy without a fee, but she saw the poor for free, and gave them medicine without charge. The only difference was that her charity extended both inside and outside the home, while Mehta’s was only for the outside; he did not wish to bring it into the house. Mehta’s path was clear—he wandered the open forest, unburdened by the ties of his own caste. Malti’s path was difficult, weighed down by duty and bonds she did not desire. She felt as if she were wandering in a dense jungle.

--- Page 267 ---
And if you close it off, it will rush to bite and tear. Even when all comforts are found within the cage, its soul will always yearn for the jungle. For Mehta, the domestic world was an unfamiliar realm, whose customs and codes he did not know.

He had seen the world from the outside and believed it to be filled only with deceit and treachery. Wherever he looked, he saw only vice. But when he delved deeper into society, he realized that beneath these vices there was also sacrifice, there was love, there was patience. Yet he also saw that though these virtues existed, they were rare. And when, amidst his doubts and suspicions, he saw Malti emerging from darkness in the form of a goddess, he rushed towards her with impatience, losing all self-restraint, wanting to hide her away so carefully that no other eye could fall upon her. He forgot that attachment is the root of destruction. Can something as merciless as love be bound by fear? It demands complete trust, total freedom, full responsibility. Its power to blossom lies within itself. It needs light and space. It is not a wall upon which bricks can be laid from above. It is alive, with an infinite power to expand.

Since Mehta had come to this bungalow, he found many opportunities to meet Malti every day. Their friends believed these were preparations for their wedding, that only the formalities remained. Mehta too kept dreaming this dream. If Malti had truly rejected him forever, why would she show him such affection? Perhaps she was giving him time to think, and after much thought he had come to the conclusion that without Malti, he was incomplete. Only she could lead him towards wholeness. Outwardly she was a woman of pleasure, but within, she was the very center of emotional strength. Yet the situation had changed. Earlier, Malti had been the one thirsting; now Mehta was tormented by longing. And once he had received an answer, he could not let go of the question.

Seeing Malti up close, his attraction only grew. From afar, the letters of a book seemed blurred and smudged, but up close, they became clear, filled with meaning and message.

Meanwhile, Malti had hired Gobar as the gardener for her garden. One day, as she was returning from seeing a patient, her car ran out of petrol on the road. She was driving herself. She grew anxious—how would she get petrol now? It was nine at night, and the cold of Magh had set in. The streets were deserted. There was no one in sight who could help push the car to the petrol station. She kept scolding her servant in frustration. “Useless fellow, always lying about somewhere, oblivious.”

By chance, Gobar happened to pass by. Seeing Malti standing there, he understood the situation and pushed the car for two furlongs to the petrol shop.

Malti asked cheerfully, “Will you work for me?”

Gobar accepted with gratitude. A salary of fifteen rupees was agreed upon. He liked the work of a gardener. It was what he had done before, and he enjoyed it. The wages at the mill were higher, but that work troubled him.

The next day, Gobar began working at Malti’s house. He was given a small room to stay in. Jhunia also came. When Malti visited the garden, she would find Jhunia’s child playing in the dust and dirt. One day, Malti gave him a sweet. From that day, the child became attached to her. As soon as he saw her, he would run after her and would not leave her side until he received a sweet.

One day, when Malti came to the garden, the child was nowhere to be seen. She asked Jhunia and learned that the child had a fever.

Malti said anxiously, “He has a fever, and you didn’t bring him to me? Come, let me see him.”

--- Page 268 ---
The child lay unconscious with fever on the cot. In that thatched-roof room, there was so much dampness, so much darkness, and even in these cold days, so many mosquitoes that Malti could not stay there even for a minute. She immediately took the thermometer and went back to check—the fever was one hundred and four. Malti grew fearful—what if it was smallpox? The child had not yet been vaccinated, and if he remained in that damp room, there was the danger that the fever might worsen.

Suddenly, the child opened his eyes and, seeing Malti standing there, looked at her with plaintive eyes and stretched out his hands for her lap. Malti picked him up and began to rock him gently. In Malti’s arms, the child seemed to experience a great comfort. With his burning fingers, he grasped the string of pearls around her neck and tried to pull it toward himself. Malti took off the necklace and put it around his neck. Even in this state, the child’s selfish nature was alert. Now that he had the necklace, he no longer needed to stay in Malti’s lap—here, there was the risk it might be taken away. Jhunia’s lap seemed safer at this moment.

Malti said with a smile, “What a clever boy! How quickly he ran off with the trinket!”

Jhunia said, “Give it back, son, it belongs to memsahib.”

The child clutched the necklace with both hands and looked at his mother with indignation.

Malti said, “You keep it, child, I won’t ask for it.”

That very moment, she returned to the bungalow and vacated her own sitting room, and Jhunia settled into the new room at once.
