Mangal looked upon that paradise with eyes full of wonder. There was a fan on the ceiling, colored bulbs, pictures on the walls. He gazed at these things for a long time, wide-eyed. Malti called out to him affectionately, “Mangal!”

Mangal smiled at her, as if to say, “Today, memsahib, I cannot laugh. What can I do? If you can do something, please do.”

Malti explained many things to Jhunia, and as she was leaving, she asked, “If there is another woman in your house, tell Gobar to bring her here for a few days. I am afraid of smallpox. How far is your home?”

Jhunia gave the name and address of her village. By her estimate, it was eighteen or twenty kos away.

Malti remembered Velari. She said, “Isn’t that the village which has a river half a mile to the west?”

“Yes, memsahib, that’s the one. How do you know?”

“We once went to that village. Stayed at Hori’s house. Do you know him?”

“He is my father-in-law, memsahib. You must have met my mother-in-law too?”

“Yes, yes, she seemed a very wise woman. She talked with me at length. So send Gobar to bring your mother here.”

“He will not go to bring her.”

“Why not?”

“There is some reason.”

Jhunia had to do all the housework herself—sweeping, cleaning, cooking, fetching water. During the day, both she and the child would get by with some roasted grains or parched rice. At night, when Malti came, Jhunia would cook her meal, and Malti would sit by the child. She wanted again and again to sit by the child, but—

--- Page 269 ---
Malti would not let him come in. At night, the child’s fever would rise, and he would grow restless, lifting both his hands in the air. Malti would take him in her arms and pace the room for hours. On the fourth day, smallpox broke out. Malti vaccinated everyone in the house, vaccinated herself, and even vaccinated Mehta. Gobar, Jhunia, Maharaj—no one was spared. On the first day, the pustules were small and scattered. It seemed to be a mild case. The next day, they blossomed and grew to the size of grapes, and then several pustules joined together to become as large as gooseberries.

Mangal, tormented by burning, itching, and pain, would moan in a plaintive voice and look at Malti with helpless, pleading eyes. Even his moaning was like that of an adult, and there was a maturity in his gaze, as if he had suddenly grown up. This unbearable suffering seemed to have erased his innocent childhood. It was as if his infant mind had awakened to the understanding that only Malti’s care could make him well. Whenever Malti left for some task, he would begin to cry; the moment she returned, he would fall silent. At night, his restlessness would increase, and Malti would have to sit by his side almost the entire night. Yet she never grew irritated or annoyed. Yes, sometimes she did get angry with Jhunia, for out of ignorance, Jhunia would do things she ought not to do.

Both Gobar and Jhunia had more faith in charms and exorcisms, but here, there was no opportunity for such things. Moreover, though Jhunia was the mother of two children, she did not know how to care for a child. When Mangal was troublesome, she would scold and curse him. If she found even a moment’s respite, she would lie down on the floor and would not rise before morning. Gobar, for his part, would enter the room as if afraid—Malti was sitting there, how could he go in? He would ask Jhunia about the child’s condition and then lie down after eating. After that injury, he had never fully recovered. Even a little work would tire him out. In those days when Jhunia used to sell grass and he could rest, he had regained some color, but in recent months, carrying loads and working with lime and mortar had ruined his health again. And here, there was so much work: drawing water to irrigate the entire orchard, weeding the beds, cutting grass, feeding and watering the cows, milking them—and how could he shirk his duties when the master was so kind? This sense of obligation would not let him sit idle for even a moment, and when Mehta himself took up the hoe and worked in the orchard, how could he rest? He himself was withering away, but the orchard was flourishing.

Mr. Mehta, too, had grown fond of the child. One day, Malti had the boy in her lap and had him pull at Mehta’s moustache. The little rascal had grabbed the moustache so hard, it seemed he would tear it out by the roots. Tears came to Mehta’s eyes.

Mehta, annoyed, had said, “What a mischievous boy!”

Malti had scolded him, “Why don’t you just shave off your moustache?”

“My moustache is dearer to me than life.”

“This time, if he grabs it again, he’ll pull it out for sure.”

“Then I’ll pull off his ears!”

Mangal had found great delight in pulling Mehta’s moustache. He had laughed heartily and pulled even harder, but perhaps Mehta, too, found some pleasure in having his moustache pulled, for nearly every day he would let the boy play tug-of-war with it once or twice.

Ever since Mangal had come down with smallpox, Mehta had grown deeply anxious. He would often go into the room and look at Mangal with troubled eyes. Imagining the child’s suffering would shake his tender heart. If by running about he could make the boy well, he would have run to the ends of the earth.

--- Page 270 ---
If spending money would have helped, even if it meant begging, he would have done it gladly; but here, there was nothing he could do. Even to touch him, his hands trembled, as if afraid the child’s fragile limbs might break. How gently Malti lifts him, carrying him on her shoulder as she paces the room, coaxing him with such affection to drink his milk. This motherly tenderness raises Malti so high in his eyes—Malti is not merely a beautiful woman, but a mother, and not just any mother, but in the truest sense, a goddess, a giver of life, who can make even another’s child her own, as if she had always been storing up her motherhood and now was pouring it out with both hands. Motherhood seemed to overflow from her every limb, as if this was her true form, and all her gestures, her adornments, were but veils to protect that sacred essence.

It was past one in the night. Hearing Mangal’s cries, Mehta started awake. He thought, Poor Malti must have stayed up till midnight; how hard it must be for her to get up now. If the door is open, I’ll quiet the child myself. He got up at once, came to the door of that room, and peered in through the glass. Malti was sitting with the child in her lap, and the child was crying for no apparent reason. Perhaps he had seen a dream, or was frightened by something else. Malti was soothing him, patting him, showing him pictures, walking with him in her arms, but the child would not stop crying. Seeing this unwavering affection, this indomitable maternal spirit, Mehta’s eyes filled with tears. His heart surged with such emotion that he wanted to go in and press Malti’s feet to his heart. From deep within, a flood of loving words rose up—Beloved, goddess of my heaven, my queen, darling...

And in that rapture of love, he called out, “Malti, please open the door.”

Malti came and opened the door, looking at him with questioning eyes.

Mehta asked, “Hasn’t Jhunia gotten up? He’s crying so much.”

Malti replied with compassion in her voice, “It’s the eighth day today, the pain must be worse. That’s why.”

“Then give him to me, I’ll walk him for a while, you must be tired.”

Malti smiled, “You’ll get angry in just a little while.”

It was true, but who admits their own weakness? Mehta insisted, “You think so little of me?”

Malti placed the child in his arms. As soon as he was in Mehta’s lap, he fell silent. That intuition children have told him: there’s no use crying now. This new person is not a woman, but a man, and men are quick to anger, even cruel, and might lay you on the bed or put you outside in the dark and go far away, not letting anyone come near.

Mehta said, with a touch of pride, “See, how quickly I quieted him?”

Malti teased, “Yes, you’re skilled in this art. Where did you learn it?”

“From you.”

“I am a woman, and not to be trusted.”

Mehta, embarrassed, said, “Malti, I beg you, forget those words of mine. In these past months, I have repented so much, felt so ashamed, so miserable—you cannot imagine.”

Malti replied simply, “I have forgotten, truly I have.”

“How can I believe that?”

“The proof is that we both live in the same house, eat together, laugh together...”

--- Page 271 ---
"May I not be permitted to make a request?"

He laid Mangal down on the cot, where the child curled up and slept, and looked at Malti with pleading eyes, as if everything depended on her consent.

Malti, her heart softened, replied, "You know, there is no one in this world closer to me than you. Long ago, I surrendered myself at your feet. You are my guide, my deity, my guru. You need not ask anything of me; you need only give a sign. Before I met you, before I recognized you, pleasure and self-service were the only aims of my life. You came and gave it purpose, gave it stability. I can never forget your kindness. I have tied your words by the riverside in the knot of my memory. My only sorrow was that you, too, saw me as any other man would, which I had never expected from you. I know the responsibility is mine, but to think that even after receiving your priceless love, I would remain unchanged—that was an injustice to me. You cannot understand the pride I feel at this moment. Now that I have your love and trust, nothing remains for me to desire. This blessing is enough to make my life meaningful. This is my fulfillment."

As she spoke, such affection welled up in Malti's heart that she longed to embrace Mehta. The feelings within her seemed to have come forth and become real. Every fiber of her being thrilled with joy. The happiness she had thought unattainable was so near, so easily within reach! And the radiance of her heart, rising to her face, lent her such beauty that Mehta saw in her an aura of divinity. Was this a woman, or an embodiment of auspiciousness, purity, and sacrifice?

Just then, Jhunia awoke and sat up, and Mehta went to his room. For two weeks after that, he had no opportunity to speak with Malti. She never met him alone. Malti's words echoed in his heart—how much comfort, how much humility, how much intoxication there was in them!

Within two weeks, Mangal recovered, though the marks of smallpox did not fade from his face. That day, Malti fed the neighborhood children sweets to their fill and fulfilled all the vows she had made. Now she truly felt the joy that comes from a life of sacrifice. The happiness of Jhunia and Govind seemed to be reflected within her. The delight and exhilaration she found in relieving the suffering of others was something she had never experienced in a life of pleasure and indulgence. That longing had faded now, like flowers that wither once they bear fruit. She had moved beyond the stage where one mistakes coarse pleasure for supreme happiness. Now, such pleasure seemed trivial, even repulsive, leading only to a base decline. What joy could there be in living in a grand bungalow, when the mud huts around it seemed to be wailing in misery? She no longer felt pride riding in her car. An innocent child like Mangal had brought so much light into her life, had opened the door to true happiness before her.

One day, Mehta was suffering from a severe headache. He lay on his cot, eyes closed, writhing in pain, when Malti came and placed her hand on his forehead, asking, "How long have you had this pain?"

It seemed to Mehta that those gentle hands had drawn all the pain away. He sat up and said, "The headache has been there since noon, and I have never had such pain before, but the moment you touched me, my head felt so light, as if there had never been any pain. There is some magic in your hands."

--- Page 272 ---
Malti brought him some medicine to take and, instructing him to rest quietly, was about to leave the room at once.

Mehta insisted, “Won’t you sit for just two minutes?”

Malti turned back at the door and said, “If you start talking now, the pain might return. Lie down and rest. These days, I always see you reading or writing something. Leave your books and papers aside for a few days.”

“Won’t you sit for even a minute?”

“I have to go see a patient.”

“Very well, go.”

A certain sadness spread over Mehta’s face, so Malti turned back, came before him, and said, “Alright, tell me, what is it?”

Mehta said, dispirited, “It’s nothing important. I was just wondering, at this hour of the night, which patient are you going to see?”

“That same girl, Rai Sahib’s daughter. Her condition had become very serious. She’s a bit better now.”

As soon as she left, Mehta lay down again. He could not understand why the pain had subsided the moment Malti placed her hand on him. Surely, she possessed some power, a blessing earned through her penance, her tireless humanity. Malti had reached that lofty ideal of womanhood where she shone like a star of light. Now, she was not an object of love, but of reverence. She had become rare, and rarity is the mantra of striving for noble souls. The happiness Mehta had imagined in love, reverence had made it deeper, more vital. In love, there is some pride, some sense of importance. Reverence erases the self and makes its own annihilation its highest aim. Love seeks possession, and whatever it gives, it also desires something in return. The supreme joy of reverence is surrender, in which the ego is destroyed.

Mehta’s great book, which he had been writing for three years and in which he had synthesized all the world’s philosophies, was now complete. He dedicated this work to Malti, and the day the copies arrived from England, he presented one to her. Seeing it dedicated in her name, she was both astonished and saddened.

She said, “What have you done? I don’t consider myself worthy of this.”

Mehta replied with pride, “But I do. This is nothing. If I could, I would lay it at your feet.”

“At my feet? I, who have known nothing but self-interest?”

“If I could obtain even a fragment of your self-sacrifice, I would consider myself blessed. Are you not a daughter?”

“A daughter of stone, why don’t you say that as well?”

“A daughter of sacrifice, of auspiciousness, of purity.”

“Then you have truly understood me! I and sacrifice? I tell you honestly, never has such a thought entered my mind. Whatever I do, directly or indirectly, it is not because I am sacrificing or helping suffering souls with my songs, but because it brings me joy. In the same way, whenever I write, it is to please myself. Perhaps the pride of the mind seems to you like self-sacrifice in me…”

--- Page 273 ---
“You make me into a goddess for no reason at all. Now all that’s left is for you to worship me with incense and lamps.”

Mehta replied in a plaintive voice, “I have been doing that for years, Malti, and I will continue until I receive a boon.”

Malti teased, “And after you receive your boon, perhaps you’ll throw the goddess out of the temple!”

Mehta answered carefully, “Then my separate existence would cease to be; the worshipper would merge into the worshipped.”

Malti grew serious. “No, Mehta, I have been thinking about this question for months, and at last I have decided that it is far happier to remain friends than to become man and woman. You love me, you trust me, and I am certain that if the moment ever came, you would give your life to protect me. In you, I have found not only my guide, but also my guardian. I, too, love you, I trust you, and there is no sacrifice I would not make for you. And it is my only prayer to the Almighty that He keeps me steadfast on this path throughout my life. For our fulfillment, for the growth of our souls, what more do we need? By confining our small household, our souls, within a tiny cage, by keeping our joys and sorrows to ourselves, can we ever come close to the infinite? That would only hinder our progress. There are a few rare souls who, even with these shackles on their feet, can still walk the path of growth, and do so. I also know that for true fulfillment, familial love, sacrifice, and renunciation are of great importance, but I do not find my soul strong enough. As long as there is no possessiveness, no sense of ‘mine’, there is no attachment to life, no force of selfishness. The day the mind succumbs to attachment and we become bound, in that instant the realm of our humanity will shrink, new responsibilities will arise, and all our strength will be spent in fulfilling them. I do not wish to imprison the soul of a thoughtful, talented man like you in this cage. Until now, your life has been a sacrifice, with little room for selfishness. I will not drag it downwards. The world needs seekers like you, who can expand their sense of belonging so much that the whole world becomes their own. The world is crying out against injustice, terror, and fear. The shadow of superstition, false religion, and selfishness looms everywhere. You have heard that anguished cry. If you do not listen, then who will? And like false beings, you cannot close your ears to it. That food would become a burden to you. Take your knowledge and wisdom, your awakened humanity, with even greater enthusiasm and strength along that same path. I will follow behind you. Make my life meaningful along with yours. That is all I ask of you. If ever your heart turns towards worldly life, even then, as far as I am able, I will turn you away from it—and God forbid that I should fail, but if I do, then I will part from you with a few tears, and I cannot say what will become of me, to what shore I will drift, but whatever that shore may be, it will not be the shore of this bondage. Now tell me, what do you command me to do?”

Mehta listened with bowed head. Each word seemed to open an inner eye within him, as it had never opened before. The emotions that had until now appeared before him like dream-images, now pulsed as living truths. He felt light and upliftment in every fiber of his being. In the presence of great resolutions, our childhood returns to our eyes. Sweet memories of his childhood came alive in Mehta’s eyes, when he would sit in his widowed mother’s lap and experience great happiness. Where is that mother now, to come and see her—

--- Page 274 ---
Bless this child’s good fortune. Give me your blessings. That stubborn child of yours is being born anew today.

He took Malti’s feet in both his hands and, trembling, said, “Your command is accepted, Malti.”

And the two became one, bound in a deep embrace. Tears streamed from both their eyes.

:34:

Siliya’s child was now two years old and ran all over the village. He had brought with him a strange language of his own and spoke only in that tongue, whether anyone understood him or not. In his speech, the sounds ‘ta’, ‘la’, and ‘gha’ abounded, while ‘sa’, ‘ra’ and others were missing. In that language, roti became ‘oti’, milk was ‘toot’, greens were ‘chhaag’, and cowrie was ‘tauli’. He imitated the voices of animals so well that people would clutch their stomachs with laughter. Someone would ask, “Ramu, how does a dog speak?” Ramu would reply gravely, “Bho-bho,” and pretend to chase and bite. “How does a cat speak?” And Ramu would go, “Myaun-myaun,” widening his eyes and scratching with his paws. He was a lively, mischievous boy. Whenever you saw him, he was lost in play, caring neither for food nor drink. He disliked being held in anyone’s lap. His happiest moments were those spent under the neem tree at the door, gathering heaps of dust, rolling in it, piling it on his head, making mounds, building little houses. He never got along with boys his own age—perhaps he did not consider them worthy playmates.

Someone would ask, “What is your name?”
He would promptly reply, “Lamu.”
“What is your father’s name?”
“Matadin.”
“And your mother’s?”
“Chhiliya.”
“And who is Datadin?”
“He is our chala.”  
No one knew who had told him of this relation to Datadin.

Ramu and Rupa got along splendidly. He was Rupa’s doll. She would rub him with ubtan, apply kohl to his eyes, bathe him, comb his hair, feed him morsel by morsel with her own hands, and sometimes fall asleep at night with him in her arms. Dhaniya would scold, “You’re always breaking the rules of purity and pollution!” But Rupa never listened to anyone. Her rag doll had taught her how to be a mother. Now, having a living, breathing child to nurture, she could no longer be satisfied with dolls.

Behind her house, where once her cattle-shed had been, Siliya had set up a thatched hut in the ruins of Hori’s house. Life could not be spent in Hori’s home. After spending several hundred rupees, Matadin was finally restored as a Brahmin by the pandits of Kashi. That day, a grand havan was performed, many Brahmins were fed, and countless mantras and shlokas were recited. Matadin had to eat and drink only pure cow dung and cow urine. The dung purified his mind; the urine killed the germs of impurity in his soul.

Yet, in a way, this penance truly did purify him. The blazing fire of the havan...

--- Page 275 ---
His humanity shone forth, and in the light of the sacrificial fire, he examined the pillars of religion closely. From that day, he grew weary of religion. He cast off his sacred thread and submerged his priesthood in the Ganges. Now, he was a true farmer. He also saw that although the scholars had accepted his Brahminhood, the common people still would not drink water from his hands. They would ask him for auspicious times, consult him about omens and rituals, even give him alms on festival days, but would not let him touch their utensils.

On the day Silia’s child was born, he drank twice his usual amount of bhang, and pride swelled his chest, his fingers repeatedly stroking his moustache. What would the child be like? Would he resemble him? How could he see him? His heart ached with longing.

On the third day, Rupa met him in the fields. She asked, “Rupiya, have you seen Silia’s boy?”

Rupiya replied, “Why wouldn’t I have seen him? He’s red-cheeked, plump, with big eyes and curly hair on his head, staring wide-eyed at everything.”

It was as if that child had taken up residence in Matadeen’s heart, kicking and moving about. A kind of intoxication clouded his eyes. He lifted the young Rupa into his arms, then set her on his shoulder, then took her down and kissed her cheeks.

Rupa, tending to the child, said boldly, “Come, I’ll show him to you from afar. He’s just in the veranda. But why does Silia always cry?”

Matadeen turned his face away. His eyes had grown moist, and his lips trembled.

That night, when the whole village slept and the trees were lost in darkness, he came to Silia’s door and listened with his whole being to the child’s cries, in which was contained all the music, joy, and sweetness of the world.

Silia would lay the child to sleep in Hori’s house and go off to work as a laborer. Matadeen, on one pretext or another, would come to Hori’s house, and, stealing glances at the child, would soothe his heart, his eyes, his soul.

Dhaniya would smile and say, “Why are you shy? Take him in your arms, love him. Is your heart made of wood? He’s just like you.”

Matadeen would throw a rupee or two for Silia and leave. With the child, his soul too was growing, blossoming, shining. Now his life had a purpose, a vow. He had gained restraint, gravity, a sense of responsibility.

One day, Ramu was lying on the cot. Dhaniya had gone somewhere. Rupa, hearing the boys’ shouts, had gone off to play. The house was empty. At that moment, Matadeen arrived. The child was waving his hands and feet, gazing up at the blue sky, bubbling with the joy of life that was still fresh within him. Seeing Matadeen, he laughed. Matadeen was overcome with affection. He picked up the child and pressed him to his chest. His whole body, heart, and soul thrilled, as if rays of light were trembling on the surface of water. In the child’s deep, pure, unfathomable, joy-filled eyes, he found the truth of his own life. A kind of fear gripped him, as if that gaze pierced his heart—how impure he was, how could he touch this gift of God? With a protective heart, he laid the child back down. At that moment, Rupa came in from outside, and he left.

One day, a heavy hailstorm fell. Silia had gone to the market with grass. Rupa was absorbed in her play. Ramu had begun to sit up now, even babble a little. In the courtyard, where hailstones were scattered...

--- Page 276 ---
He looked and understood—the seeds had been scattered by the wind. The child picked up a few and ate them, playing merrily in the courtyard. That night, he was seized by fever. The next day, it turned into pneumonia. On the third day, at dusk, the child breathed his last in Siliya’s lap.

But even in death, the child remained the center of Siliya’s life. Her breasts would fill with milk, soaking her blouse, and at that very moment, tears would stream from her eyes. Before, after finishing all her chores at night, she would clasp Ramu to her heart and offer him her breast, and it felt as if the child’s vitality flowed into her own soul. Then she would sing sweet songs, dream gentle dreams, and weave new worlds in her mind, with Ramu as their king. Now, after finishing her work, she would sit and weep in her empty hut, her spirit yearning to fly away to that world where, perhaps, her child was still playing. The whole village shared in her sorrow. Ramu had been so lively—whenever anyone called, he would run into their arms. Now, in death, beyond their reach, he seemed to cry out even more. His memory was lovelier, more enchanting, more alluring than ever.

On that day, Matadeen broke down completely. The curtain is meant for the wind, but the storm had torn it down so it would not be swept away. He lifted the small body in both his hands and carried it to the riverbank, where the stream hung, thin and narrow, after a mile-wide stretch of sand.

His arms would not straighten. That day, he felt no shame at all, absorbed in his grief, and no one said a word to him. In fact, everyone tried to share his sorrow.

Hori said, “This is the duty of a man. Whomever you hold by the hand, you must support.”

Dhaniya, rolling her eyes, said, “Don’t boast, I know what you’re up to.”

A month passed. Siliya began working as a laborer again.

A month passed. Siliya began working as a laborer again.

The moon had risen, smiling down. Siliya had gathered the leftover stalks from the harvested field in her basket and was about to head home when suddenly, a flood of memories broke within her. Her blouse was soaked with milk, and it was as if she found a strange pleasure in her own weeping.

Suddenly, at the sound of footsteps, she started.

A voice said, “How long will you go on weeping, Siliya?”

He himself began to cry.

Tears rose in Siliya’s throat. “How did you come here?”

Matadeen, his voice trembling, said, “You never even let him play with me.”

“No, Siliya, one day—”

“Truly?”

“Truly.”

“Where was I?”

“You had gone to the bazaar.”

“He cried in your arms?”

“No, Siliya, I cried.”

“Truly?”

--- Page 277 ---
"Truly."

"So, you only played with him for one day?"

"Yes, only one day, but I used to come watch him every day. I would see him playing on the cot, and leave with my heart in my throat."

"It was you who was fated for this."

"I regret ever taking him in my arms that day. This is the punishment for my sins."

There was forgiveness shining in Siliya’s eyes. She placed the basket on her head and started towards home. Matadeen walked alongside her.

Siliya said, "Now I sleep in Dhania kaki’s verandah. I don’t feel comfortable in my own house."

"Dhania used to always give me advice."

"Truly?"

"Yes, truly. Whenever she met me, she would start advising me."

As they neared the village, Siliya said, "Alright, now you go your own way from here. If the Pandit sees us together, it will be trouble."

Matadeen lifted his head and said, "I am not afraid of anyone anymore."

"If they throw you out of your house, where will you go?"

"I have built my own home now."

"Truly?"

"Yes, truly!"

"Where? I haven’t seen it."

"Come, I’ll show you."

They walked on, Matadeen in front, Siliya behind. Hori’s house appeared. Matadeen went around to the back and stood at the door of Siliya’s hut. "This is our home," he said.

With a voice full of disbelief, forgiveness, irony, and sorrow, Siliya said, "But this is Siliya Chamarin’s house."

Opening the reed mat at the door, Matadeen replied, "This is the temple of my goddess."

Siliya’s eyes began to shine. She said, "If it’s a temple, will you just pour a pot of water and leave?"

Taking the basket from her head, Matadeen said in a trembling voice, "No, Siliya. As long as I live, I will remain in your shelter. I will worship only you."

"You’re lying."

"No, I swear by touching your feet. Listen, the Patwari’s son Bhunesri was after you quite a lot. You scolded him well."

"Who told you that?"

"Bhunesri himself said so."

"Truly?"

"Yes, truly."

Siliya lit the lamp with a match. In one corner was an earthen pot, in another the hearth, where two or three brass and iron vessels were neatly washed and placed. In the middle, straw was spread out—Siliya’s bed. At the head of this bed, Ramu’s little cot seemed to be weeping, and right there—

--- Page 278 ---
Nearby, two or three clay elephants and horses lay broken and neglected. When their master was no more, who would care for them? Matadeen sat down on the straw. A pang rose in his chest. He felt as if he could weep his heart out.

Siliya placed her hand gently on his back and asked, “Did you ever think of me?”

Matadeen caught her hand, pressed it to his heart, and said, “You were always before my eyes. Did you ever think of me?”

“My heart used to burn for you.”

“And you never felt pity?”

“Never.”

“Then Bhunesri…”

“Enough, don’t abuse me. I am afraid—what will the villagers say?”

“Those who are good people will say, this was her true dharma. As for the wicked, their words are not worth hiding from.”

“And who will cook your meals now?”

“My queen, Siliya.”

“Then how will you remain a Brahmin?”

“I do not wish to be a Brahmin, but a chamar. Whoever turns away from the one who accepts him as family, he is the real chamar.”

Siliya put her arms around his neck.

:35:

Hori’s condition was worsening day by day. Yet, he never lost hope. Each defeat seemed to make him hungrier for life. He had reached his final state, when even if he could have remained steadfast, he would have wiped away a few tears. He had earned, but there was no vice he had not been accused of. The good days always remained a mirage, forever just out of reach. The green shimmer of false hope would appear, only to vanish. Like a defeated king, he had guarded his last fortress—his dignity—as dearly as his own life.

But now, even that fortress had fallen.

Pandit Nokheram had placed a claim on his body. The land had slipped from his hands. The desire to live! Even Raisahib had to evict more families than ever before. If there had been any happiness, it was in his son. But evening had fallen on that too. He was lost in these thoughts.

“Hori, about your eviction,” someone said, “there are still fifteen days left till the date.”

Hori had brought ruin upon himself for their sake.

--- Page 279 ---
Why does such misery befall us? We neither feast nor squander, yet if the crops fail, and what little grows sells for a pittance, what is a farmer to do?

“But you must save the ancestral land. How will you survive otherwise? This is all that remains of your forefathers’ legacy. If that is lost, where will you go?”

“It is God’s will; what power do I have?”

“There is one way, if you are willing.”

It was as if Hori had been granted a reprieve. Falling at his feet, he pleaded, “It would be a great act of dharma, Maharaj. Who do I have but you? I had lost all hope.”

“There is no need to despair. Just understand this: a man’s duty is one thing in times of happiness, and another in times of sorrow. In happiness, a man gives alms, but in sorrow, he may even beg. That, too, becomes his dharma. When our bodies are healthy, we will not even sip water without bathing and performing our rituals. But when we fall ill, we take our medicine without bathing or changing clothes, sitting on our beds. That is the dharma of that time. Here, there may be many differences between us, but in Jagannathpuri, there is no distinction—high and low all sit together in the same row to eat. In times of crisis, even Lord Ramchandra ate the leftovers of Sabari, and slew Bali by stealth. When even the greatest break with tradition in times of trouble, who are we to hold ourselves apart? You know Ramsevak Mahto, don’t you?”

Hori replied dispiritedly, “Yes, of course I know him.”

“He is my patron. He is doing very well for himself—his farming is separate, his moneylending is separate. I have never seen a man with such authority. His wife died several months ago. He has no children. If you are willing to marry Rupa to him, I can persuade him. He would never refuse me. The girl is grown now, and these are dangerous times. If something untoward were to happen, your family’s honor would be stained. This is a golden opportunity. The girl will be married, and your fields will be saved. You will be spared all the expenses.”

Ramsevak was only a few years younger than Hori. The very suggestion of marrying Rupa to such a man was an insult. Where was his flower-like Rupa, and where was that old stump of a man! Hori had endured many wounds in his life, but none cut deeper than this. Today, his fortunes had sunk so low that someone dared suggest he sell his own daughter, and he lacked even the courage to refuse. His head bowed in shame.

After a minute, Dattadin asked, “So, what do you say?”

Hori did not give a clear answer. He said, “I will tell you after I have thought about it.”

“What is there to think about?”

“I should ask Dhaniya as well.”

“Are you willing or not?”

“Let me think, Maharaj. Such a thing has never happened in our family. I must consider our honor as well.”

“Give me your answer within five or six days. Don’t spend so long thinking that the eviction comes.”

Dattadin departed. He had no doubts about Hori; his only concern was Dhaniya. She was stubborn—she would rather perish than surrender her honor.

But if Hori agreed, she would eventually accept, though not without tears and protest. After all, losing the fields would also bring disgrace.

--- Page 280 ---
Dhaniya came and asked, “Why did the Pandit come?”

“Nothing much, just talk about the eviction.”

“He must have come to wipe our tears. As if he would ever lend us a hundred rupees.”

“I don’t even have the face to ask.”

“Then why does he come here at all?”

“It was about Rupiya’s engagement.”

“With whom?”

“Do you know Ramsevak? With him.”

“I’ve never seen him, but I’ve heard his name for years. He must be an old man.”

“He’s not old, just middle-aged.”

“You didn’t scold the Pandit? If you’d told me, I’d have given him such a reply he’d remember it.”

“I didn’t scold him, but I did refuse. He said the wedding could happen without much expense, and the fields would be saved too.”

“Why didn’t you say clearly that he was asking us to sell our daughter? How could that old man even have the nerve?”

But the more Hori thought about this question, the less stubborn he felt. He was not lacking in concern for family honor, but when someone is afflicted by an incurable disease, do they care what they eat? In front of Dattadin, Hori had shown a feeling that could not be called acceptance, but inside, he was melting. Age is of no consequence. Life and death are matters of fate. Old men linger, young men depart. If happiness is written for Rupiya, she will find it there too; if sorrow is her lot, she will find no joy anywhere. And as for selling his daughter—there was no question of that. Whatever Hori would take from him would be a loan, and as soon as he had the money, he would repay it. There was no shame or dishonor in this. Of course, if he had the means, he would marry Rupiya to a young man from a good family, give a dowry, and spend lavishly on the wedding feast. But since God had not made him capable of that, what could he do except give away his daughter as she was? People would laugh, but those who only laugh and never help—why should he care for their laughter? The real problem was that Dhaniya would never agree. She was stubborn as a mule. She would keep dragging along with the same old sense of shame. This was not the time to uphold family prestige, but to save their own lives. If she was so concerned about honor, let her bring five hundred rupees—where would she find them?

Two days passed, and neither of them spoke about the matter. Yes, they did speak in hints and riddles.

Dhaniya would say, “There’s joy in a wedding only when the bride and groom are well-matched.”

Hori would reply, “A wedding is not about joy, foolish woman—it’s a kind of penance.”

“Fine, a penance then.”

“Yes, that’s what I say. If God puts a man in a certain situation, finding happiness in it—if that’s not penance, what is?”

The next day, Dhaniya thought of another aspect of marital happiness. “What joy is there in a household where there are no in-laws, no sisters-in-law or brothers-in-law? Let the girl enjoy being a new bride for a few days.”

Hori said, “That’s not the happiness of married life, it’s a punishment.”

Dhaniya flared up, “You always talk so strangely. How will a daughter-in-law live alone in a house, with no one—”

--- Page 281 ---
No one ahead, no one behind?

Hori said, “When you came into this house, there weren’t just one, but two devars, a mother-in-law, a father-in-law. Tell me, what happiness did you find here?”

“Are all households filled with such people?”

“What, do goddesses from the heavens descend into homes? The daughter-in-law is alone, and the whole house lords over her. Whom should the poor girl try to please? Whoever’s command she doesn’t obey becomes her enemy. It’s best to be alone.”

Still, the matter rested there, but Dhaniya’s heart grew lighter. On the fourth day, Ramsevak Mahto himself arrived. Riding a fine horse, accompanied by a barber and a servant, as if he were a great zamindar. He was over forty, his hair streaked with gray, but there was a sharpness to his face, his body sturdy. Hori looked utterly old before him. Ramsevak was on his way to plead a case in court and wanted to spend the afternoon here. The sun was blazing, the hot wind fierce! Hori brought wheat flour and ghee from the sahuain’s shop. Pooris were made. All three guests ate. Dattadin too came to give his blessings. Conversation began.

Dattadin asked, “What sort of case is it, Mahto?”

Ramsevak, putting on airs, replied, “There’s always some case or another, Maharaj! One cannot survive in this world by being a cow. The more you yield, the more people press you. The police station, the courts, the law—they’re all supposed to protect us, but no one actually does. There’s looting on all sides. Whoever is poor, whoever is helpless, everyone is ready to cut his throat. God forbid anyone should act dishonestly—that is a great sin. But to not fight for one’s rights and justice is an even greater sin. Tell me, how long can a man keep his head down? Here, the kisans are everyone’s easy prey. If you don’t give the patwari his nazrana and customary fees, it’s impossible to live in the village. If you don’t fill the bellies of the zamindar’s peons and agents, marriages can’t happen. The thanedar and constable act as if they’re our sons-in-law. When they visit the village, it’s the farmer’s duty to honor them, offer gifts—otherwise, with a single report, the whole village could be shackled. Sometimes the qanungo comes, sometimes the tehsildar, sometimes the deputy, sometimes the judge, sometimes the collector, sometimes the commissioner. The kisan must always stand before them with folded hands. He must arrange for their rations, fodder, eggs, chickens, milk, ghee. The same thing is happening to you too, Maharaj! Every day, new and newer officials keep appearing. One doctor comes to pour medicine into the wells. Another doctor comes now and then to check the cattle. There’s an inspector who examines the boys. Who knows how many departments have their officers? Separate ones for the canal, for the forest, for toddy and liquor, for village improvement, for agriculture. How many can I count? If a padre comes, he must also be given rations, or else he’ll file a complaint. And if you say that all these departments and officers do any good for the kisan, not even in name. Once, the zamindar levied a two-rupee per plough tax on the village. He was hosting a feast for a senior officer. The kisans refused to pay. So, he imposed a penalty on the whole village. The officials always side with the zamindar. They never think that kisans are people too, that they have children, that they too have honor and dignity. And all this is the result of our meekness. I had the village crier announce that no one should pay this extra rent, nor abandon their fields. If someone can convince me, I’ll pay the penalty, but if you expect us to be ground to dust without protest, that will not happen. The villagers listened to me, and everyone refused to pay the penalty. When the zamindar saw the whole village united, he was helpless. The fields—

--- Page 282 ---
If they evict us, then who will till the land? In these times, unless you stand firm, no one listens. Even a child does not get milk from its mother without crying.

Ramsevak left in the third quarter of the day, leaving an indelible impression on Dhania and Hori.

Datadin’s mantra was awakened.

He asked, “What do you say now?”

Hori, gesturing towards Dhania, said, “Ask her.”

“I am asking both of you,” said Datadin.

Dhania replied, “He is older in age, but if you all agree, then I accept as well. Whatever is written in fate will come to pass, but the man is good.”

And Hori had developed that trust in Ramsevak which the weak often feel towards men of vigor. He began to weave grand dreams like Sheikh Chilli. If such a man were to take his hand, his fortunes would surely change.

The date for the wedding was fixed. Gobar would have to be called as well. Write to him from our side, whether he comes or not is his choice. At least he should not be able to say, “When did you invite me?” Sona too must be called.

Dhania said, “Gobar was not like this, but ever since he brought Jhunia, he has changed. He went away to the city and forgot us so completely—no letters, no messages. Who knows how they are?” As she spoke, her eyes filled with tears.

When Gobar received the letter, he prepared to leave at once. Jhunia did not like the idea of going, but at such a time she could not object. How could a brother not attend his sister’s wedding? Was the shame of not going to Sona’s wedding not enough?

With a choked voice, Gobar said, “It is not right to remain estranged from one’s parents. Now that I have hands and feet of my own, I may quarrel with them, but they gave me birth, raised me, made me a man. If they say a few harsh words now, I should bear it. Lately, I remember Amma and Dada again and again. I don’t know why I was angry with them then. Because of you, I had to leave my parents too.”

Jhunia flared up, “Don’t lay that sin on my head! It was you who wanted to fight. I stayed with Amma for so long and never even breathed a word.”

“The quarrel happened because of you.”

“Fine, let it be because of me. I too left my home for you.”

“Who loved you in your home? Your brothers were angry, your sisters-in-law tormented you. If Bhola had got you, he would have devoured you raw.”

“All because of you.”

“This time, let us stay so that they too may have some happiness in life, let us not do anything against their wishes. Dada is so good, he never even scolded me. Amma has beaten me many times, but whenever she did, she would always give me something to eat. She would beat me, but until she made me laugh, she would not be at peace.”

Both spoke to Malti about it. Malti not only gave them leave, but also gifted a spinning wheel and a pair of bangles for the bride. She herself wished to go, but there were patients under her care whom she could not leave even for a day. However, she promised to come on the day of the wedding and heaped toys for the child. She kissed and caressed the child again and again, as if in that moment—

--- Page 283 ---
She wanted to take an advance, but the child, caring nothing for her affection, was happy to go home—the home he had never even seen. In his childish imagination, home was something greater than heaven itself.

When Gobar reached home and saw its condition, he was so disheartened that he felt like turning back at that very moment. One part of the house was on the verge of collapse. At the doorway, only a single bullock was tied, and even that one was half-dead. Dhania and Hori could hardly contain their joy, but Gobar’s heart was heavy. What hope was there now of saving this home? He worked as a servant, yes, but at least he ate his fill. He served only one master. Here, whoever you see, acts like a master. It’s servitude, but a dry one. You toil to grow grain, and whatever money you get, you hand over to others. All you can do is sit and chant the name of Ram. Only Grandfather has the heart to endure all this. Gobar felt he could not bear it even for a single day.

And this was not just Hori’s plight. The entire village was stricken by the same calamity. There was not a single man whose face was not sorrowful, as if pain itself had taken the place of their souls and was making them dance like puppets. They walked, they worked, they were ground down, they suffocated, simply because grinding and suffocating was written in their fate. There was no hope in their lives, no enthusiasm, as though the springs of their existence had dried up and all their greenery had withered.

It was the month of Jeth; the grain still lay in the threshing floors, yet no one’s face bore a trace of happiness. Much of it had already been claimed by the mahajans and the agents right there in the threshing yard, and what little remained was not truly theirs either. The future loomed before them like darkness. They could see no way out. All their senses had dulled. Mounds of refuse lay piled at their doorways, stinking, but their noses no longer smelled, nor was there any light left in their eyes. At dusk, jackals would begin to howl at the gates, but no one cared. Whatever coarse food came their way, they ate it, just as an engine consumes coal. Their bullocks would not touch the trough unless there was chuni or chaff in it, but the people themselves only needed something to fill their bellies. Taste meant nothing to them. Their palates were dead. The very idea of flavor had vanished from their lives. For a handful of grain, they would stoop to dishonesty, for a fistful more, they would take up sticks. It is the ultimate degradation when a man forgets even shame and honor.

Since childhood, Gobar had seen the villages in this state and had grown used to it, but today, after four years, it was as if he saw a new world. Living among decent people had awakened his mind; he had stood at the back of political gatherings and listened to speeches, and they had pierced him to the core. He had heard and understood that one must shape one’s own destiny, that with intelligence and courage, one must overcome these disasters. No god, no hidden power would come to help them, and a deep empathy had awakened within him. Now, the old arrogance and recklessness were gone. He had become humble and industrious. Why, under the sway of selfishness and greed, should you worsen the condition you are already in? Suffering has bound you all together with a single thread. Why break this divine bond of adversity with petty selfishness? Turn that bond into one of unity. Such thoughts had given wings to his humanity.

After seeing the highs and lows of the world, the generosity that comes to a guileless soul now seemed to flutter within him, eager to soar into the sky. Whenever he saw Hori working, he would push him aside and do the work himself, as if trying to atone for past misdeeds. He would say, “Dada, now don’t worry about anything. Leave all the burdens to me. I will send money every month. You have struggled and suffered so long—rest for a while now. Shame on me, that while I am alive, you have had to endure such hardship.”

--- Page 284 ---
He had to get up, and from every pore of Hori’s being, blessings flowed for his son. He felt a divine vigor in his worn-out body. Why should he, at this moment, burden his son’s blossoming youth with the weight of debts and worries? Let the boy eat and drink in peace, enjoy the pleasures of life. Hori himself was ready to toil and suffer. That was his life. He could not survive by chanting the name of Ram alone. He needed the spade and the hoe. Turning a rosary and repeating God’s name would not bring peace to his mind.

Gobardhan said, “If you wish, I can arrange to pay off the debt in installments, giving a little every month. How much would it be in total?”

Hori shook his head. “No, son, why should you trouble yourself? You don’t earn that much either. I’ll manage everything. Times won’t always be like this. Rupa will be gone soon. Then it’s only the debt left to repay. Don’t you worry about anything. Just be careful with your food and drink. Build up your body now, and you’ll always live in comfort. As for me—what of me? I’m used to hardship and struggle. I don’t want to yoke you to the plough just yet, son! You’ve found a good master. Serve him well for a while, and you’ll become a real man. She has come here herself, a living goddess.”

“She said she’ll come again for the wedding.”

“She’ll be most welcome. Living among such good people, even if you earn less, you gain wisdom and your eyes are opened.”

Just then, Pandit Datadin signaled to Hori and took him aside. Pulling two hundred-rupee notes from his waistband, he said, “You listened to my advice, and did well. Both matters are settled. You are free from the obligation to your daughter, and your ancestral land is saved. I have done all I could for you. Now it’s up to you to handle your affairs.”

When Hori took the money, his hand trembled. He could not lift his head. Not a word escaped his lips, as if he had fallen into a bottomless pit of humiliation and kept falling. After fighting life for thirty years, he was defeated—and so utterly defeated it was as if he had been made to stand at the city gates, and every passerby spat in his face. He wanted to cry out, “Brothers, I am an object of pity. I never knew what the heat of Jeth or the rains of Magh were like. Look inside this body, see how much life remains—how battered by wounds, how crushed by blows. Ask it, did you ever know rest, ever sit in the shade?” And now, on top of all that, this humiliation—and still he lives, coward, greedy, base. All his faith, once so deep it had become blind and heavy, seemed to shatter into pieces and scatter.

Datadin said, “Then I’ll be going. If you wish, you can go to Nokhe Ram right now.”

Hori replied humbly, “I will, Maharaj, but my honor is in your hands.”

For two days, the village was filled with celebration. There was music and singing, and Rupa, after much weeping, departed for her new home. But no one saw Hori leave the house. He hid himself away, as if his face were blackened with shame. With Malti’s arrival, the bustle only increased. Women from neighboring villages also came.

With his gentle nature and affection, Gobardhan had won over the entire village. There was not a single household where he had not left a memory of his sweet manners. Bhola even fell at his feet. Bhola’s wife...

--- Page 285 ---
She offered her betel leaf and gave her a rupee as a parting gift, and also asked for her address in Lucknow. If she ever came to Lucknow, she would surely visit her. She did not mention the matter of her money.

On the third day, when Gobar was about to leave, Hori, with tears in his eyes, confessed to Dhaniya the sin that had been tormenting his soul for many days, and wept as he spoke, “Child, out of attachment to this land, I have burdened myself with a bundle of sin. Who knows what punishment God will give me for this!”

Gobar did not become agitated in the least; there was no trace of anger on his face. With reverence, he said, “There is nothing sinful in this, Dada! Yes, Ramsevak’s money should be repaid. But what else could you have done? I am good for nothing, your farming yields nothing, no one will give us a loan, and there isn’t food in the house for even a month. In such a situation, what else could you have done? If you hadn’t saved the ancestral land, where would we have lived? When a man has no recourse, he can only surrender himself to fate. Who knows how long this injustice will go on? For one who cannot get even a morsel to eat, honor and respect are but empty pretenses. If you had, like others, strangled someone else’s throat, seized their savings, then you too would have been called a good man. You never abandoned your principles, and this is your punishment. If I were in your place, I would either be in jail or hanged by now. I could never have tolerated earning and filling everyone else’s bellies while sitting with my own children, my mouth tied shut. ”

Dhaniya did not agree to send her daughter-in-law with him. Jhunia too wished to stay a few more days. It was decided that Gobar would go alone.

The next morning, Gobar took leave from everyone and set out for Lucknow. Hori accompanied him to the edge of the village. Never before had he felt such love for Gobar. When Gobar bent to touch his feet, Hori broke down in tears, as if he would never see his son again. His soul was filled with joy, with pride, with resolve. Receiving such reverence and affection from his son, he felt radiant, he felt vast. The gloom that had settled over him days before—a darkness in which he had lost his way—was now replaced by enthusiasm and light.

Rupa was happy in her marital home. In the circumstances in which her childhood had passed, money was the most precious thing. There had been so many desires in her heart, which had withered away, unfulfilled. Now she was able to realize them, and Ramsevak, though middle-aged, had become youthful again. For Rupa, he was her husband; whether he was young, middle-aged, or old made no difference to her womanly feelings. Her love was not dependent on his looks or age; its foundation was much deeper, rooted in ancient traditions, which could be shaken only by an earthquake. Her youth was absorbed in itself; she adorned herself for her own sake and rejoiced in it. For Ramsevak, she had another aspect. Then she became the housewife, engaged in the work of the household. She did not wish to embarrass or trouble him by flaunting her youth. No sense of incompleteness ever entered her mind. The granaries filled with grain, the fields stretching from the village to the boundaries, the rows of cattle at the door—these left no room for any feeling of lack within her.

And her greatest desire was to see her family happy. How could she remove their poverty? The memory of that cow was still fresh in her heart—the cow that had come like a guest and left everyone weeping when she went away. That memory, after so many days, had become even more tender. Her sense of belonging had not yet settled in this new home. That old house was still her true home. The people there were her own, their sorrows her sorrows, their joys her joys. Seeing a whole herd of cattle at this doorstep could not bring her the happiness that seeing a single cow at her own door once did.

--- Page 286 ---
His grandfather’s longing had never been fulfilled. The day the cow had arrived, how elated he had been, as if some goddess had descended from the heavens. Since then, he had never had the means to bring home another cow, but she knew that even today, that same longing burned just as brightly in Hori’s heart. If this cow were to leave, she would surely take the white cow along with her. No, why not send it with her own man? All it took was to ask Ramsevak, and he agreed at once. The next day, through the help of an Ahir, Rupa sent the cow. She told the Ahir, “Tell Dada, I am sending it for Mangal to have milk.” Hori too had been thinking of getting a cow. He was not in any great hurry yet, but Mangal was here, and how could he live without milk? As soon as he had the money, the first thing he would do was buy a cow. Now, Mangal was not just his grandson, not just Govind’s son, but also Malti Devi’s darling. He must be raised accordingly.

But where was the money to come from? By chance, that very day, a contractor began digging stones from the village wasteland for the road. When Hori heard of it, he hurried there and began working for eight annas a day. If this work lasted even two months, he would have enough for a cow. After toiling all day in the scorching sun and heat, he would come home utterly exhausted, yet there was no trace of despair. With the same enthusiasm, he would go to work the next day. Even at night, after dinner, he would sit before the lamp and spin twine. Sometimes he would go to bed at eleven or twelve. Dhania too had become obsessed; instead of stopping him from working so hard, she would sit beside him and spin twine as well. They had to buy a cow, and Ramsevak’s money had to be repaid. Govind had promised. She was deeply anxious.

It was past eleven at night. Both of them sat spinning twine. Dhania said, “If you’re sleepy, go and rest. You have to work again in the morning.”

Hori looked up at the sky. “I’ll go. It can’t be more than ten yet. You go, get some sleep.”

“I nap for a bit in the afternoon.”

“I also eat some chivda and nap under a tree.”

“The heat must be terrible.”

“What heat? There’s good shade.”

“I worry you might fall ill.”

“Come now, only those fall ill who have the leisure for it. Here, the only worry is that when Govind returns, half of Ramsevak’s money will be gone. He’ll bring some too. If only this year we could be free of this debt, it would be a new life.”

“I miss Govind so much these days. How gentle he has become!”

“When he left, he fell at our feet.”

“Mangal was so healthy when he came from there. Here, he’s grown thin.”

“There, he had milk, butter—what didn’t he get? Here, if he gets bread, that’s enough. The doctor told me to get a cow.”

“The cow would have come long ago, but you never listen. I took on Puniya’s burden as well.”

“What could I do? I have my own dharma too. Hira deserted us, but someone had to look after things. Who else but me? If you and I hadn’t managed together, what would have become of us? Even after all this, what is our place in the world?”

“If you bury the money, will it grow into a tree?”

--- Page 287 ---
“What times are these? If farming puts food in your belly, that is enough. Who can afford to save anything?”

“Heera seems to have vanished from the world itself.”

“My heart tells me he will return, someday, somehow.”

Both lay down to sleep. When Hori rose before dawn, he saw Heera standing before him—his hair grown long, his clothes in tatters, his face gaunt, not a trace of flesh or blood left on his body, as if he had even shrunk in height. He ran and fell at Hori’s feet.

Hori embraced him and said, “You’ve wasted away, Heera! When did you come? I kept thinking of you all day today. Have you been ill?”

Today, in Hori’s eyes, this was not the Heera who had made his life bitter, but the Heera who had once been a small, motherless, fatherless child. The twenty-five or thirty years in between seemed to have vanished, leaving no trace.

Heera did not reply. He stood there, weeping.

Hori took his hand and said, his voice choked with emotion, “Why do you weep, brother? All men make mistakes. Where have you been all this time?”

Heera replied in a broken voice, “Where can I say, Dada? Just think that I was destined to see you again, and so I survived. The guilt of murder weighed on me. It felt as if that cow stood before me always—whether waking or sleeping, she never left my sight. I lost my mind, and spent five years in the madhouse. It’s been six months since I was released. I begged for food, wandered from place to place. I didn’t have the courage to come here. How could I show my face to the world? But in the end, my heart would not listen. I steeled myself and came. Have you… your children…”

Hori interrupted him, “You ran away for nothing. If you’d given the daroga a few rupees, the matter would have been settled. What else could have happened?”

“I will never die in debt to you, Dada!”

“I am not a stranger, brother.”

Hori was filled with joy. All the hardships and disappointments of life seemed to fall at his feet. Who could say he had been defeated in the struggle of life? Was this joy, this pride, this thrill, a sign of defeat? In these very defeats lay his victory. His battered, broken weapons were his banners of triumph. His chest swelled, his face shone with new light. In Heera’s gratitude, the whole success of his life had taken form. If his granary had been filled with two hundred maunds of grain, if his pots had been filled with thousands of rupees, could he have found this heavenly happiness?

Heera looked him up and down and said, “You too have grown so thin, Dada!”

Hori laughed and said, “Are these days for me to be fat? Only those are fat who have no worries about debt or honor. In these times, to be fat is shamelessness. A hundred must be made thin for one to be fat. What happiness is there in such fatness? Happiness is when all are well-fed. Did you meet Sobha?”

“I met him last night. You cared for your own, even those who turned against you, and kept your honor intact. He sold off his land and fields, and now only God knows how he will survive.”

Today, when Hori went out to dig, his body felt heavy. The fatigue of the night had not left him, but his steps were swift, and there was a proud, untroubled firmness in his gait.

By ten in the morning, the wind had begun to blow, and by noon, it was raining fire from the sky. Hori, picking his way among the stones…

--- Page 288 ---
He had been carrying baskets from the quarry to the road and loading them onto the cart. When the midday break came, he was utterly exhausted. He had never felt such fatigue before. His legs would not lift; his body seemed scorched from within. He neither bathed nor ate his chivda. In that same exhaustion, he spread his angoccha under a tree and lay down to sleep, but his throat was parched with thirst. It is not wise to drink water on an empty stomach. He tried to suppress his thirst, but with every moment, the burning inside him grew. He could bear it no longer. One laborer had filled a bucket and was eating his chivda. Hori drew a lota of water and drank it, then lay down again, but within half an hour he vomited, and his face took on a deathly pallor.

The laborer asked, “How are you feeling, Hori bhaiya?”

Hori’s head was spinning. He replied, “It’s nothing, I’m fine.”

Even as he spoke, he vomited again, and his hands and feet began to grow cold. Why was his head spinning? It was as if darkness was falling before his eyes. His eyes closed, and all the memories of his life began to rise up vividly in his heart, but out of order—future before past, past before future, mismatched, distorted, and disconnected like dream images. He saw his happy childhood, when he played marbles and slept in his mother’s lap. Then he saw Gobar coming and falling at his feet. The scene changed: Dhaniya, dressed as a bride in a red chunari, was feeding him. Then the image of a cow appeared before him, just like Kamadhenu. He milked her and was giving the milk to Mangal, when the cow turned into a goddess and...

The same laborer called out again, “The afternoon is waning, Hori, come lift your basket.”

Hori said nothing. His soul seemed to be flying through unknown worlds. His body was burning, his hands and feet were growing cold. He had been struck by the hot wind.

A man was sent running to his house. Within an hour, Dhaniya arrived, breathless. Shobha and Heera followed behind, bringing a makeshift litter.

Dhaniya touched Hori’s body, and her heart sank. His face had lost all luster. In a trembling voice, she asked, “How are you feeling?”

Hori looked at her with unsteady eyes and said, “You’ve come, Gobar? I’ve bought a cow for Mangal. She’s standing there, look.”

Dhaniya had seen the face of death before. She recognized it. She had seen it come on silent feet, and she had seen it come like a storm. She had watched her mother-in-law die, her father-in-law die, two of her children die, dozens of people in the village die. Her soul was shaken. The very foundation on which her life rested seemed to be slipping away. But no, this was a time for courage. Her fears were unfounded. It was only the hot wind—he had lost consciousness because of it.

Holding back her rising tears, she said, “Look at me, I am here. Don’t you recognize me?”

Hori’s consciousness returned. Death had drawn near, the fire was about to blaze. The smoke had cleared. He looked at Dhaniya with helpless eyes, and two tears slipped from the corners. In a faint voice, he said, “Forgive anything I have said or done, Dhaniya. Now I am going. The longing for a cow remained in my heart. Now whatever money is left here will go for the funeral rites. Don’t weep, Dhaniya. How much longer could you keep me alive? All misfortune has come to pass. Now let me die.”

And his eyes closed again. At that moment, Heera and Shobha arrived with the litter. They lifted Hori and laid him on it, and set off toward the village.

--- Page 289 ---
The news spread through the village like the wind. The entire village gathered. Hori lay on his cot, perhaps understanding everything, but his tongue had fallen silent. Yet, the tears streaming from his eyes revealed how hard it was to break the bonds of attachment. The sorrow of what one could not accomplish—this is what is called attachment. What attachment can there be to duties fulfilled or tasks completed? Attachment lies in leaving behind those orphans for whom we could not fulfill our responsibilities, in those unfinished dreams we could not realize.

But even understanding everything, Dhania clung to the fading shadow of hope. Tears fell from her eyes, but like a machine she kept running—sometimes roasting mangoes to make a drink, sometimes rubbing wheat bran on Hori’s body. What could she do? There was no money; otherwise, she would have sent for a doctor.

Heera, weeping, said, “Bhabhi, harden your heart. Arrange the godan. Dada is leaving.”

Dhania looked at him with eyes full of reproach. How much harder could she make her heart? Did she need to be told her duty towards her husband? Was it only her dharma to weep for the one who had been her companion in life?

Several other voices rose—“Yes, arrange the godan. Now is the time.”

Dhania stood up like an automaton, brought the twenty annas she had received from selling twine that day, placed the coins in her husband’s cold hand, and said to the priest, Dattadin, who stood before her, “Maharaj! There is no cow in the house, nor calf, nor money. This is all we have—these coins are his godan.”

And with that, she collapsed to the ground, overcome.
